                            GENERAL THETA FOUNDATIONS I:
                           CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT

                                                     QIAN QI


       Abstract. This is the current technical supplement to General Theta Foundations I. It contains
       the complete inherited positive-realization, contrast, nonabelian multiplicity, mean–contrast, spectral
       and arithmetic theory, the return-polytope approximation and sharp physical-dictionary law, and
       all independent causal, minimax, dual, finite-order measurement, two-stage reset, communication,
       discrimination and geometric results. The main article proves the shared-preparation, tied-action
       reset-memory and unrestricted-decoder certificates. The statements retained here keep their individ-
       ual hypotheses and full proof bodies. Cross-document references and the theorem map identify their
       actual logical roles; historical preservation is not an additional mathematical premise.



References to the main article use ordinary section numbers; statements numbered S belong to this
supplement. Native paired compilation resolves references in both directions. The first sections
supply the exact group and Fourier premises used by the shared-device theorems. The inherited
return geometry supplies the upper counterpart to the unrestricted-decoder lower. Subsequent
sections retain the complete independent developments. Relocation does not change a statement’s
hypotheses or priority; two explicit output-visible-core clarifications from the parallel author branch
are identified in the source-provenance and proof-audit records.

            S1. A permutation normal form with an exact memory charge
   Theorem 3.1 identifies when finite memory exists. We now determine the form of every possible
finite realization, retaining the cost of its prescribed pure initial label. The distinction between
a minimal linear representation and a positive realization remains essential. In particular, the
permutation representation carried by the observable space does not by itself provide a decoder
with values in the probability simplex.
   Let b be a compactly reversible response admitting a finite realization. Write G for its finite
observable group, d = dim Vb , and N for its residual cardinality. We use the chronological convention
g(u)g(v) = g(uv): the right action on Vb is f · g(w) = Lw f , where Lw f (z) = f (wz). Thus G in
this notation is the opposite of the group of operators with the usual convention for composition;
inversion identifies the two groups. No group cardinality, faithfulness, or representation-theoretic
statement depends on this convention. Every element has a representative in A∗ , including its
inverse, since a finite group generated by the command elements is already generated by their
positive words. The value b(g) = b(w) is consequently well defined.
   A probability factorization on a finite G-set consists of a finite set X, row permutation matrices
Pg with Pg Ph = Pgh , a probability row λ on X, and a row-stochastic matrix D with columns indexed
by C, such that
                                      b(g) = λPg D      (g ∈ G).                                (S1.1)
All rows of D are probability laws, including rows associated with states that are not distinguished
by the immediate terminal measurement. Unused orbits may be deleted. Define
                                      κG (b) = min{|X| : (S1.1) holds}.

  Date: October 2026.
                                                         1
2                                                QIAN QI


The residual realization shows that this minimum is finite and at most N . It permits a mixed λ
without charging for its preparation; the stochastic memory m(b) continues to charge every live
label, beginning at the specified point mass as in (3.1).
Theorem S1.1 (Reversible stochastic normal form). Every M -label stochastic realization of b gives
a faithful finite G-set X and a factorization (S1.1) satisfying
                                   |X| + 1{λ is not a point mass} ≤ M.                             (S1.2)
Conversely, every such factorization has a realization with the displayed number of labels. Its
transitions on X are permutations; if λ is mixed, its only additional label initializes the distribution
on the first command. Thus arbitrary continuing stochastic dynamics confers no advantage over one
initialization mixture followed by a permutation action.
Proof. Fix a realization (eT
                           1 , {Qa }, D0 ), and retain the reachable row space R and the surjection
π : R → Vb from the proof of Theorem 3.1. Write
                                       L = {w ∈ A∗ : g(w) = 1}.
These are identity words in the observable group; their matrices need not be identity matrices on
hidden states. Choose finitely many words ℓ1 , . . . , ℓs ∈ L whose positive matrix entries cover all
directed edges occurring in any Qℓ , ℓ ∈ L. There are at most M 2 distinct edges, so s ≤ M 2 is
possible. If no nontrivial choice is needed, the empty word suffices. Set
                                               I + Qℓ1 + · · · + Qℓs
                                       T =                           .
                                                       s+1
Its closed communicating classes C1 , . . . , Ck are closed for every Qℓ with ℓ ∈ L. The positive diagonal
makes the restriction of T to each such class aperiodic. The elementary convergence argument in
Lemma S16.1 gives a stochastic idempotent
                             E = lim T n ,      ∆M E = conv{µ1 , . . . , µk },
                                  n→∞
where µi is the unique stationary probability row of T on Ci . Their supports are disjoint, so this
image is a simplex with exactly k vertices. Every identity-word matrix preserves each Ci ; hence
                                        EQℓ E = E          (ℓ ∈ L).                                (S1.3)
This is a sandwiched equality. We do not assert Qℓ E = E, EQℓ = E, or common stationarity of the
µi under all identity words.
   Each T n is a convex combination of identity-word matrices. Consequently RE ⊆ R and π(rE) =
π(r) for r ∈ R. For a word u, put Fu = EQu E, considered as a stochastic map of ∆M E to itself.
Choose a word ū representing g(u)−1 . Replacing the middle E by the convex combinations T n and
using uℓū ∈ L gives
                                           Fu Fū = E = Fū Fu .
Thus Fu is an affine automorphism of that simplex. Such an automorphism permutes its vertices,
because both it and its inverse preserve the simplex and therefore preserve extremality. If g(u) = g(v),
the same inverse word shows Fu Fū = Fv Fū = E, so Fu = Fv . Next, EQu T n Qv E is a convex
combination of Fuℓv , all equal to Fuv . Passing to the limit proves Fu Fv = Fuv . We have obtained a
permutation action Pg on the vertices X = {µ1 , . . . , µk }.
   This action is faithful. If Fu is the identity on ∆M E, then rEQu E = rE for all rows r. For
r ∈ R, application of π shows Lu π(r) = π(r). Surjectivity gives Lu = I on Vb , and so g(u) = 1.
Write eT         i λi µi and set D(i, ·) = µi D0 . Intertwining on R gives, for every word w,

including the empty word. If the original initial label lies in Ci , its row under E is exactly µi . A
mixed λ therefore requires that the initial label lie outside the union of the closed classes. The k
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       3


nonempty disjoint classes then use at most M − 1 labels. This proves (S1.2); a pure λ only requires
k ≤ M.
   For the converse, when λ is a point mass its support point is the initial label in X. If it is mixed,
adjoin a label ⋆ with decoder D(⋆, ·) = λD. On command a, its transition row into X is λPg(a) ,
and no transition returns to ⋆. The rows on X are those of Pg(a) . Induction gives (S1.1) for every
nonempty word, and the decoder at ⋆ gives the empty word. The first input is applied during
initialization, so it is not lost or encoded in an uncounted preparatory clock.                       □
   The general structure of nonnegative idempotents and bounded groups of nonnegative matrices is
classical; see Brown [60], Schwarz [61], and Flor [62]. The proof above supplies the additional steps
required for an arbitrary semigroup realization: the identity-word projection is constructed before
passing to the observable quotient, the sandwiched action is proved to factor through that quotient,
and the prescribed pure start is charged. It does not assume that the original stochastic matrices
themselves form a group.
Theorem S1.2 (Exact memory formula). With randomized terminal readout allowed and the initial
label prescribed pure, the minimum stochastic memory is
                                                                 
                 m(b) = min            |X| + 1{λ is not a point mass} = min{N, 1 + κG (b)}.      (S1.4)
                         (X,λ,D)

The minimum ranges over precisely the probability factorizations (S1.1), not arbitrary linear factor-
izations.
Proof. The first equality is both directions of Theorem S1.1. A point-mass factorization gives a
deterministic-transition realization on X, so |X| ≥ N by Theorem 3.1. A mixed factorization costs
at least 1 + κG (b). Conversely, the residual machine has N labels, and a factorization attaining
κG (b) costs at most 1 + κG (b). These inequalities give the second equality, also when a minimum
factorization happens to have a pure start.                                                     □
Corollary S1.3 (Sharp group bounds). An M -label realization of a compactly reversible response
satisfies
                            |G| ≤ M !,     M < N =⇒ |G| ≤ (M − 1)!.
Both bounds are attained by families below. In particular, a stochastic memory saving over the
residual count requires a faithful permutation core on at most M − 1 points.
Proof. The core action in Theorem S1.1 embeds G into Sk . If M < N , its start cannot be pure,
since that would produce a deterministic machine with at most M labels. Thus k ≤ M − 1 in this
case.                                                                                       □
   Let νG (Vb ) be the least cardinality of a finite G-set whose real permutation module contains Vb ,
and let µ(G) be the least cardinality of a faithful finite G-set. All references to Vb here use the
right-action convention already fixed. A probability factorization gives Vb as a subquotient of RX :
take the cyclic span of λ, then its observable quotient. Averaging an inner product over finite G
splits every invariant subspace orthogonally, so this subquotient embeds in RX . We obtain
                d ≤ νG (Vb ) ≤ κG (b),         m(b) ≥ max{d, µ(G), min(N, 1 + νG (Vb ))}.        (S1.5)
These are lower bounds, not an identification of a linear module with a bounded decoder. Proposi-
tion S1.6 makes the distinction strict.
Corollary S1.4 (Rank rigidity). For a finite compactly reversible response,
                                          m(b) = d     ⇐⇒      N = d.
In that case every realization with d labels has permutation transition matrices. If N > d, then
m(b) ≥ d + 1.
4                                                   QIAN QI


Proof. Every M -label realization has d ≤ M . If M = d, the core in Theorem S1.1 has k ≥ d by its
linear rank, hence k = d and its start is pure. Thus N ≤ d, whereas N ≥ d because the residuals
span Vb . Conversely N = d gives a deterministic machine with d labels. For the assertion about the
original matrices, a d-label realization has R = Rd and the observable map π is an isomorphism. An
inverse word for g(a) therefore gives a stochastic inverse of Qa on all of Rd . A stochastic map with
a stochastic inverse is an affine automorphism of the full simplex and hence a permutation.        □

S1.1. Two commands and a factorial deterministic gap. Let k ≥ 3, let Pg be the natural
row permutation action of Sk , choose a nonconstant λ ∈ ∆k , and choose a nonconstant column
t ∈ [0, 1]k . Two commands are the k-cycle (1 2 · · · k) and the transposition (1 2); these generate Sk .
Supply the binary response
                                                               
                                     b(g) = λPg t, 1 − λPg t .                                    (S1.6)
If the distinct coordinate values of λ have multiplicities n1 , . . . , nj , put Nλ = k!/(n1 ! · · · nj !).
Proposition S1.5 (Exact symmetric-group family). The response (S1.6) has observable group Sk ,
dimension k, residual cardinality Nλ , and
                                 κSk (b) = k,       m(b) = min{Nλ , k + 1}.
When the coordinates of λ are pairwise distinct, the least stochastic memory is k + 1 and the least
deterministic-transition memory is k!. For a point-mass λ, both are k. If either λ or t is constant,
the response instead has one residual and one-label memory.
Proof. For any nonconstant vector, differences between it and its transposes yield a nonzero multiple
of some ei − ej . Conjugating by all permutations shows that the span of its orbit differences is 1⊥ .
The orbit of λ therefore spans Rk , because every row has mass one. The orbit of t also spans Rk :
its differences span 1⊥ , and its coordinate sum is positive, since a nonconstant nonnegative vector
is nonzero. Consequently the map
                                                                          
                                      r 7−→ h 7−→ (rPh t, r1 − rPh t)
is injective on Rk . It identifies the reachable row space with Vb , and identifies the observable action
with the faithful natural permutation action. Its residuals are precisely the distinct permutations of
λ, giving d = k and N = Nλ . The displayed factorization and the rank lower bound give κ = k.
The memory formula proves the remaining assertion; it also shows Nλ ≥ k. The two constant cases
are immediate from (S1.6).                                                                              □

   This is an exact prepared quantum experiment in every dimension k: take ρ = diag(λ), permuta-
tion unitaries for the commands, and terminal effect E = diag(t). The construction already lies
in the diagonal, classical subalgebra. It demonstrates a stochastic versus deterministic memory
separation, and does not assert a quantum advantage for this family. It also proves sharpness of
Corollary S1.3: the point-mass family has M = k and |G| = k!, while pairwise distinct coordinates
have M = k + 1 < N = k! for k ≥ 3.
   For a concrete rational example, take k = 3, λ = (1/2, 1/3, 1/6), and t = (0, 1/4, 1)T . The six
first-outcome values are
                                       1 3 7 13 11 7
                                                        
                                         , , , , ,         .
                                       4 8 24 24 24 12
It has d = 3, N = 6, κ = 3, and m = 4.

S1.2. The decoder constraint can remove the entire saving. The representation-theoretic
lower bound in (S1.5) need not attain the positive factorization minimum, even for the same group,
rank, and residual count as this example.

Proposition S1.6 (A strict bounded-decoder obstruction). Let u = (1, −1, 0) and let S3 act by its
natural permutation matrices. The binary response with first coordinate
                                               1 1
                                         β(g) = + uPg uT                                   (S1.7)
                                               2 4
has d = 3, N = 6, and νS3 (Vb ) = 3, but
                                          κS3 (b) = m(b) = 6.
It is realized by a prepared qubit with
                                                     √ !
                                               1 1
                                
                        1 0                      √    3
                   U0 =      ,            U1 =           ,         ρ = E = |+⟩⟨+|.
                        0 −1                   2   3 −1
Thus the least stochastic memory can differ for responses having the same observable group, module,
and residual cardinality.
Proof. The orbit of u consists of its six distinct permutations and spans the two-dimensional zero-sum
subspace. Pairing with all permutations of u is nondegenerate on that subspace. Including the
constant outcome sum gives dimension three and six distinct residuals. This representation is the
natural permutation module R3 , and the rank bound gives ν = 3.
  Write s = (1 2) and r = (1 2 3). Directly from (S1.7),
                                               1             3
    β(1) = 1,    β(s) = 0,    β(r) = β(r2 ) = , β(τ ) =         for the other two transpositions τ.
                                               4             4
Suppose a probability factorization exists on at most five points. Its action is faithful, by the same
residual argument as in Theorem S1.1. Transitive S3 -sets have sizes 1, 2, 3, 6, since their stabilizers
have orders 6, 3, 2, 1; the subgroups of order two are conjugate and the subgroup of order three is
unique. Thus our faithful action has one natural three-point orbit and, at most, one two-point sign
orbit together with fixed points. Two three-point orbits already exceed the budget, and actions on
only fixed and sign orbits annihilate the alternating subgroup.
   Let a be any point of positive initial mass and write q for the first-coordinate decoder. The
endpoint values force q(a) = 1 and q(a · s) = 0. No fixed point can carry mass. Within the natural
orbit, the point fixed by s cannot carry mass, and both of the points exchanged by s cannot carry
positive mass simultaneously. Thus the initial mass in that orbit is concentrated at one moved point
a; denote its total by A. If a sign orbit is present its mass is concentrated at a single point; write
this total as B. We have A + B = 1. Every even permutation has decoder one on the mass in the
sign orbit, and every odd permutation has decoder zero there.
   The images a · r and a · r2 are the other two points in the natural orbit. One is a · s, with decoder
zero, and the other’s decoder is some t ∈ [0, 1]. The two equal values β(r) = β(r2 ) = 1/4 give
B = 1/4 and At = 0. Hence A = 3/4 and t = 0. Of the two transpositions different from s, one
fixes a and the other sends it to the third point. Their first-outcome values would therefore be A
and zero, whereas both must be 3/4. This contradiction rules out all factorizations on at most five
points. The regular six-point residual machine gives κ ≤ 6, so κ = 6 and Theorem S1.2 gives m = 6.
   For the physical realization, the adjoint actions of U0 and U1 on the real Bloch plane are two
reflections whose product is a rotation by 2π/3. The initial and terminal Bloch vector is the x-axis
vector. The first reflection sends it to its negative, the other two reflections have inner product 1/2
with it, and the two nonidentity rotations have inner product −1/2. The Born probability is one
half of one plus that inner product. It has exactly the displayed six group values, so it is (S1.7)
after the specified generator assignment.                                                             □
   The cyclic partition formula of the preceding revision is recovered as a special case in Proposi-
tion S16.2. For the general finite group, however, both the start charge and the bounded-decoder
constraint belong in the exact minimization problem. Neither can be removed by a rank calculation.

                  S2. Exact memory under terminal readout contrast
   The normal form separates the cost of a permutation module from the positivity of its preparation
and decoder. We now determine when that linear lower bound is attained, for every finite observable
group, and describe the exact memory transitions as the terminal contrast varies. This is a change
of terminal readout. It differs from the length-dependent background damping of Section S11.
   Let b be a nonconstant compactly reversible response with finite observable group G, residual
cardinality N , and real observable module Vb . Set
                          1 X
                   π=            b(g),    bt (g) = π + t{b(g) − π},  0 ≤ t ≤ 1.               (S2.1)
                         |G| g∈G
If πc = 0, nonnegativity implies b(g, c) = 0 for every g; such an output can be removed and later
restored as a zero decoder column. Thus positivity of all remaining πc imposes no additional
hypothesis. For prepared unitary commands, (S2.1) is realized by the terminal measurement
Ec (t) = tEc + (1 − t)πc I, with the preparation and commands unchanged.
Theorem S2.1 (Attainment of the module bound at small contrast). For every finite observable
group G there is an explicitly positive t0 such that
                κG (bt ) = νG (Vb ),    m(bt ) = min{N, 1 + νG (Vb )}       (0 < t ≤ t0 ).      (S2.2)
For every t > 0, the observable group, module and residual cardinality agree with those of b. At
t = 0 the minimum is one label. The assertion allows arbitrary finite groups and arbitrary finite
terminal outcome sets.
Proof. Every f ∈ Vb has a suffix-independent total mass ℓ(f ) = c f (z, c), because this holds for its
                                                                  P

spanning residuals. Group averaging sends every residual to the same constant response b(z) = π.
Its image on Vb is therefore Rb, and
                                                                       
                                                            1 X 
                              Vb = Rb ⊕ W,       W = ker          Lg .
                                                           |G| g∈G

The module W has no invariant vector, and ℓ(W ) = 0.
   Choose a G-set X of minimum size k = νG (Vb ) and an equivariant injection j : W → RX .
The permutation inner product is invariant. Since group averaging commutes with j, its image
is orthogonal to the whole invariant subspace, including the uniform row p = (1/k, . . . , 1/k). In
particular its vectors have coordinate sum zero. Let F : RX → W be orthogonal projection onto
j(W ) followed by j −1 . It is equivariant, F j = IW , and F (p) = 0.
   Put v = b − b, a = j(v), and
                                         T (x, c) = F (ex )(∅, c).
The rows of T sum to zero, pT = 0, and equivariance gives aPg T = b(g) − π. Since a ̸= 0, we may
take
                           1                     |T (x, c)|
                  ϵ=             ,    B = max               > 0,     t0 = min{1, ϵ/B}.
                       2k∥a∥∞               x,c      πc
The row λ = p + ϵa is a probability row with every entry at least 1/(2k). Since (t/ϵ)B ≤ 1, for
0 ≤ t ≤ t0 the matrix
                                      Dt (x, c) = πc + (t/ϵ)T (x, c)
is row-stochastic. Direct multiplication yields
                              λPg Dt = π + (t/ϵ){pPg T + ϵaPg T } = bt (g).
This proves the upper κG (bt ) ≤ k with a bounded decoder.
  The linear map f 7→ tf + (1 − t)ℓ(f )b is equivariant and invertible for t > 0: it is the identity on
Rb and multiplication by t on W . It sends each residual of b to the corresponding residual of bt .
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                          7


It preserves the group kernel and the equality pattern among residuals. The module lower bound
(S1.5) now gives equality, and Theorem S1.2 charges the pure start. At zero contrast the decoder is
constant.                                                                                        □
   The construction is an equivariant positivity argument. Symmetric cone factorizations and
coordinate-symmetric lifts have an established theory [67, 68]; ordinary nonnegative rank also has
classical barycentric perturbation results [69]. The assertion here is the exact value νG (Vb ) while
preserving one common action for every command word and the prescribed-start charge. It is not a
claim that positivity under mixing or equivariant factorization was previously unknown.
   There is a useful distinction at an infinite compact observable group. The same invertible change
of residuals preserves that group for every t > 0, so Theorem 3.1 still excludes every finite exact
stochastic realization. Thus making the terminal probabilities strictly interior need not make exact
memory finite. Terminal readout contrast, repeated channel damping, and perturbations of an
entire instrument are different operations.
Theorem S2.2 (Attained exact contrast thresholds). For each integer k ≥ 1 there is τk ∈ [0, 1]
such that
                                 κG (bt ) ≤ k ⇐⇒ 0 ≤ t ≤ τk .                          (S2.3)
These thresholds are nondecreasing in k, satisfy τN = 1, and obey
                               k < νG (Vb ) =⇒ τk = 0,       τνG (Vb ) > 0.
The lower memory value is retained at every threshold itself. There are only finitely many positive-
contrast transitions. If all b(g, c) are real algebraic, all thresholds are effectively computable real
algebraic numbers. When all subgroups of G are normal, they are obtained by finitely many linear
programs; rational tables then have rational thresholds and rational optimal controllers.
Proof. For a fixed action on at most k points, the variables λ, D, t lie in a compact product of
probability simplexes and [0, 1]. The equations λPg D = bt (g) define a closed set, so its feasible
contrasts form a compact set. If s > 0 is feasible, the same λ and the decoder (t/s)D + (1 − t/s)1π
make every 0 ≤ t ≤ s feasible. There are only finitely many actions on at most k labelled points;
their feasible sets therefore form a closed initial interval. A single fixed point realizes b0 , including
when no positive contrast is feasible.
   The residual machine gives κG (bt ) ≤ N ; the remaining inequalities follow from Theorem S2.1
and its module lower bound. The exact memory is recovered from m(bt ) = min{N, 1 + κG (bt )}
for t > 0. For algebraic data the equations for each action are finite polynomial constraints
over the real algebraic numbers. Classical real quantifier elimination computes their projected
interval and endpoint, as in Section S10. This is a proved finite procedure, not a reported general
quantifier-elimination execution. The final assertion is the linear reduction and rationality proved
in Theorems S4.2 and S4.3 below.                                                                        □
S2.1. A sharp transition for the retained prepared-qubit experiment. The bounded-decoder
obstruction of Proposition S1.6 has a complete contrast law. Let u = (1, −1, 0) and

                                             2 4
and let bt = (βt , 1 − βt ). This is the same prepared-qubit experiment with terminal effect t|+⟩⟨+| +
(1 − t)I/2.
Proposition S2.3 (An exact qubit readout threshold). For 0 < t ≤ 1, this response has d = 3,
N = 6, and                    (                         (

                  κS3 (bt ) =                  m(bt ) =
                               6, 2/3 < t ≤ 1,           6, 2/3 < t ≤ 1.
At t = 0 its memory is one.

Proof. The group, rank and residual count are unchanged for t > 0 by Theorem S2.1. A faithful
S3 -set of size at most five has exactly one natural three-point orbit and otherwise only fixed points
or one two-point sign orbit, as in the proof of Proposition S1.6. Let A be the initial mass on its
natural orbit. Write its unnormalized initial row and scalar decoder as
                      p = (A/3)1T + r,        D = q1 + s,                 r1 = 0,   1T s = 0.
The contribution is Aq + rPg s. All remaining orbits contribute a linear combination of the constant
and sign functions. The functions rPg s and uPg uT are orthogonal to both: their group sums vanish
because |S3 |−1 g Pg = 11T /3, and g sgn(g)Pg = 0 entrywise. Consequently equality of the full
               P                     P

responses implies
                                 rPg s = (t/4)uPg uT      (g ∈ S3 ).                          (S2.4)
   Here no unproved matrix-coefficient injectivity is needed. On the row plane 1⊥ , use the basis
u, (1, 1, −2). The matrices of the identity, (12), (23) and the chronological cycle (123) are respectively
                                  −1 0          1 1 1                     1 −1 1
                                                                               
                           I2 ,        ,               ,                          .
                                   0 1          2 3 −1                    2 −3 −1
They span all real two-by-two matrices: the first two span the diagonal ones, and the latter two
have independent off-diagonal parts. Therefore (S2.4) holds with every endomorphism of this plane
in place of Pg . Equality of the associated rank-one bilinear functionals, with t > 0, forces r = au,
s = buT and ab = t/4. Nonnegativity of p gives |a| ≤ A/3, while the bounded decoder gives
|b| ≤ min(q, 1 − q) ≤ 1/2. Hence
                                          t/4 = |ab| ≤ A/6 ≤ 1/6.
No core of size at most five is possible above t = 2/3.
   For 0 < t ≤ 2/3 use the natural three-point action with λ = (2/3, 0, 1/3) and scalar decoder
D = 12 1 + (3t/4)uT . Its entries lie in [0, 1] and direct multiplication gives βt ; the rank bound proves
κ = 3. Above the threshold the six-residual machine supplies the matching upper. The pure-start
formula gives the stated minima and the constant case is immediate.                                     □

        S3. Orbit multiplicity, positive synthesis, and the convexity boundary
   The quotient reduction of Section S4 has a precise boundary. A single transitive orbit admits
convexification without enlarging the orbit exactly when its stabilizer is normal. For general
finite groups, irreducible multiplicities measure the obstruction and determine the exact number of
prescribed orbit copies at sufficiently small positive contrast. All representations below are finite
dimensional. We use classical complete reducibility, matrix-coefficient orthogonality, and Frobenius
reciprocity [75]; their representation-theoretic content is not claimed as new.
   Let G be a finite group, not necessarily the faithful observable group of the particular table under
discussion. For a nonempty finite right G-set X, let RX (C) denote the probability tables
                           b(g) = λPg D,        λ ∈ ∆(X),             D(x, ·) ∈ ∆(C),
where Pg Ph = Pgh . Thus the same preparation and decoder are used for every group element.
Taking an observable quotient does not change the table; keeping G explicit lets us compare different
allowed actions. For this ambient-group formulation, κG (b) denotes the minimum |X| over all such
factorizations; an action need not be faithful unless G is the observable group. Write G   b for the
complex irreducible representations, choose a unitary representative ρ of degree dρ , and define
                                                                     
                                                          bc (ρ)
                                                          b
                                                           1
                1 X                                            ..
                    b(g, c)ρ(g −1 ),
                                                                     

Lemma S3.1 (Fourier multiplicity and real descent). Let Vb be the real span of the translates
bh (g) = b(hg). The multiplicity of the right irreducible ρ in Vb ⊗R C is rρ (b). In particular r1 (b) = 1
and rρ (b) ≤ dρ . If U and V are real G-modules, then U embeds equivariantly in V if and only if
every complex irreducible multiplicity in U ⊗ C is at most the corresponding one in V ⊗ C.
Proof. The Fourier transform
                       L
                                  sends bh to the blocks Aρ (b)ρ(h). Matrix-coefficient orthogonality
identifies C[G] with ρ∈Gb Mdρ (C): the group algebra can independently prescribe an arbitrary
matrix on each irreducible block. Consequently the ρ-part of the cyclic module generated by b
identifies with
                                        {Aρ (b)M : M ∈ Mdρ (C)}.
If Aρ has rank r, a choice of a basis of its column space identifies this right module with r independent
rows of length dρ . It therefore consists of r copies of ρ. For the trivial representation the stacked
block is the nonzero probability vector π = |G|−1 g b(g), so its rank is one.
                                                      P

   Necessity of the second assertion follows by complexification. For sufficiency, complete reducibility
over C and the multiplicity inequalities give a complex equivariant injection F : U ⊗ C → V ⊗ C. In
real bases write F = A + iB. Both A and B are real intertwiners. A maximal nonzero minor of
F is the value at z = i of a real polynomial minor of A + zB. That polynomial is not identically
zero, so some real z makes this minor nonzero. The real map A + zB is the required injection. One
may choose z among 0, 1, . . . , dim U , since the nonzero polynomial has degree at most dim U . This
proves the assertion without excluding complex or quaternionic representation types.                   □
  For H ≤ G put                                                                
                                                            1 X
                                 mρ (H) = dimC ρH = rank          ρ(h) .
                                                           |H| h∈H
By Frobenius reciprocity these are the multiplicities of C[H\G]. Let qH (b) be the least positive
integer q for which b belongs to Rq(H\G) (C), where q(H\G) is the disjoint union of q copies;
set qH (b) = ∞ if no such factorization exists. This is an action-restricted resource. It is not
κG (b)/[G : H] in general, since an unrestricted optimal core may use other orbit types.

Proof. A factorization through a finite G-set Y makes Vbt a quotient of a submodule of RY . Averaging

Theorem S3.3 (Normality is the exact single-orbit convexity boundary). For H ≤ G and the
transitive right action X = H\G, the following conditions are equivalent:
     (1) H is normal in G;
     (2) RX ({0, 1}) is convex;
     (3) RX (C) is convex for every finite outcome set C.
If H is normal, this response set is exactly the polytope of probability tables constant on the left
cosets of H. If H is not normal, nonconvexity occurs in every neighborhood of the interior constant
binary table (1/d, 1 − 1/d), where d = [G : H].
   An explicit witness is supplied by the return tables
                                                                 1 X            χX (g)
                  fx (g) = 1{xg=x} ,     x ∈ X,       FX (g) =         fx (g) =        .
                                                                 d x∈X            d

Every (fx , 1 − fx ) lies in RX ({0, 1}), whereas (FX , 1 − FX ) does not when H is not normal. The
same conclusion holds after replacing every scalar f by d−1 + t(f − d−1 ), for every 0 < t ≤ 1. More
quantitatively, for any irreducible ρ with 0 < mρ (H) < dρ , put FX,t = d−1 + t(FX − d−1 ). Then
                                                                        t mρ (H)
                              inf        max |FX,t (g) − a(g)| ≥                 > 0.             (S3.4)
                       (a,1−a)∈RX ({0,1}) g∈G                             d dρ
This is a certified separation margin; optimality of its constant is not asserted.

and (i)⇒(iii)⇒(ii).

                                            0 < mρ (H) < dρ .

Lemma S3.4 (Full-contrast return-law rigidity). Let X be a transitive d-point G-set. If a scalar
response is a convex combination of responses on copies of X, has value one at e, and has group
average 1/d, every positive-weight component uses a point preparation and its point indicator decoder.
In particular every component is a return law fx .
Proof. For a component write λ ∈ ∆(X) and D ∈ [0, 1]X . The total value one at e forces λD = 1
for every component of positive weight, hence D(x) = 1 on the nonempty support of λ. Transitivity
gives its group average d−1 x D(x)P ≥ 1/d. Equality of the weighted mean with 1/d forces equality
                           P

for each component. Consequently x D(x) = 1, so the support of λ consists of exactly one point
and D is its indicator.                                                                        □
12                                             QIAN QI


   These statements separate three matters: the classical multiplicities of a permutation repre-
sentation, the positivity of its preparation and decoder, and the cost of an externally prescribed
pure start. The integer formula is exact at small positive contrast; it does not replace the general
full-contrast bilinear synthesis problem by a linear program. The convexity theorem pinpoints why
the retained normal-stabilizer absorption cannot simply be extended to arbitrary subgroups.
S3.1. An unbounded nonabelian family with a strict positivity cost. We apply the multi-
plicity theory to all symmetric groups Sn with n ≥ 5. The natural permutation character χn (g)
counts the fixed points of g on [n]. Consider the binary response whose first coordinate is
                             1−t     t

                              n      n
Its group average is 1/n, and at the identity it equals [1 + (n − 1)t]/n. This supplies a family with
growing group, observable rank, and exact memory, rather than a fixed-dimensional illustration.
   The positivity threshold is controlled by the following matrix extremum. No symmetry or
positive-semidefiniteness hypothesis is imposed on its coefficient matrix.

Theorem S3.6 (The exact two-level nonabelian memory law). For n ≥ 5 and t > 0, the response
(S3.6) has observable group Sn , residual cardinality n!, and observable dimension 1 + (n − 1)2 . Its
permutation-module bound is
                                        νSn (Vbt ) = n(n − 1).
                             √         √
At the sharp threshold τn = ( n − 1)/( n + 1), the unrestricted positive core and pure-start memory
satisfy
                                  contrast    κSn (bt )      m(bt )
                                0 < t ≤ τn n(n − 1) n(n − 1) + 1
                                τn < t ≤ 1        n2        n2 + 1.
The smaller memory is attained at the threshold itself. At t = 0 both minima are one.
14                                               QIAN QI


   Every minimum permutation core at t = τn is the disjoint union of n − 1 natural orbits. Its
probability preparation and binary decoder, after relabeling the orbit copies and simultaneously
relabeling their natural coordinates, have exactly the form

where ℓi is defined in (S3.8), wi is the preparation mass on orbit i, and Di is its first-output decoder
written as a row. The second-output decoder is 1 − Di . Conversely every displayed weight vector
gives an optimal factorization. This is a classification of minimum permutation-core factorizations
under the inherited normal form, not a classification of all stochastic transition realizations before
that reduction.
Proof. The real natural permutation module is the direct sum of the invariant line and the (n − 1)-
dimensional standard representation Stdn . Its complexification is irreducible on that zero-sum
subspace. One direct proof uses the transpositions: from a nonconstant vector in an invariant
subspace, a vector v − (ij)v produces a nonzero multiple of ei − ej , whose orbit spans the whole zero-
sum space. The standard representation is faithful, because a permutation fixing every difference
ei − ej fixes all points. Matrix-coefficient orthogonality gives the nontrivial block
                                                        t
                                       βbt (Stdn ) =          In−1 ,
                                                     n(n − 1)
and all other nontrivial irreducible blocks vanish. Therefore the observable module is 1 ⊕ Std⊕(n−1)
                                                                                               n      ,
of dimension 1 + (n − 1)2 . Equality of two residuals forces equality of their standard representation
matrices because this Fourier block is invertible; faithfulness then makes the group elements equal.
This proves the claims about the group and residual count.
   We next record the subgroup index estimate needed for an unrestricted memory converse. For
K ≤ Sn , let r be the number of its orbits on [n]. The multiplicity of Stdn in C[K\Sn ] is
                                       m(K) = dim StdK

For r ≥ 3, this is at least n!/(n − r + 1)!: transferring one unit from the smaller of two nonunit
parts to the larger increases their factorial product until only one nonunit part remains. The last
lower bound is at least nr for n ≥ 5. Indeed at r = 3 it is n(n − 1) ≥ 3n, and increasing r multiplies
the bound by at least two while r increases by one. If r = 2 and one part is a singleton, K lies in

uses the natural K-orbits and requires no classification of low-index subgroups. In particular, an
exceptional subgroup of S6 that is transitive in the natural action has m(K) = 0 and does not
supply a standard carrier.
   A G-set containing Vbt must have total standard multiplicity at least n − 1. Summing (S3.12)

   At full contrast, n natural orbits realize β1 : give each mass 1/n, start orbit i at i, and use the
indicator decoder of i. Hence κSn (b1 ) ≤ n2 . Suppose, to the contrary, that a factorization exists on
fewer than n2 points. The total standard multiplicity is at least n − 1. If any standard-carrying
orbit were not natural, (S3.13) and the ordinary estimates on the others would force at least
n(n − 1) + n = n2 points. Thus all standard-carrying orbits are natural. There must be exactly
n − 1 of them: each supplies multiplicity one and costs n points. Every other orbit has total size
less than n.
   Write the factorization as a mixture over its positive-mass transitive orbits. Since β1 (e) = 1, each
component’s decoder equals one on its initial support. On a transitive orbit of degree d its mean
is therefore at least 1/d. The natural components have mean at least 1/n; any additional orbit
here has d < n and would have mean strictly greater than 1/n. The mean of β1 is exactly 1/n,
so all additional orbits have zero initial mass and every active natural component has mean 1/n.
Lemma S3.4 now makes each component a deterministic return law. Let wi be the aggregate mass
of the components starting at natural point i. Evaluating at a transposition (ij) gives
                                              n−2                    2

needed, contradicting the availability of only n − 1 natural orbits. This proves κSn (b1 ) = n2 .
   We next exclude every intermediate positive-core value. Fix t > 0 and suppose a factorization has
fewer than n2 points. The same index argument gives exactly n − 1 natural standard-carrying orbits;
the remaining orbits have standard multiplicity zero. Write bt = BN + BE for their unnormalized
response contributions. The Fourier support of BN lies in the trivial and standard types, because
these are the only types in a natural permutation module. The target has the same support. On the
other hand, BE has zero standard block. Subtracting BN from bt shows that all nontrivial

Theorem S1.2, namely m = min{n!, 1 + κ}, gives the displayed pure-start values since n2 + 1 < n!
for n ≥ 5. The explicit quadratic threshold is also consistent with the retained algebraic-threshold
theorem; no quantifier-elimination calculation is needed here.

and no others, and the equality
                            √      part of Lemma

relabeling. Set a = 1 − 1/ n and b = 1/ n, so a > b > 0 for n ≥ 5. The leading (n − 1) × (n − 1)
block of A∗ is the positive diagonal matrix a2 I/(n−1). Each nonnegative rank-one summand wi λT i Di
can cover at most one of these diagonal positions: covering two would give a positive off-diagonal
entry. All n − 1 positions need coverage and there are exactly n − 1 summands. Therefore each
is covered by a different positive-weight summand. Relabel that summand by its position i. The
zero entries in row and column i then force both λi and Di to be supported on {i, n}. Only this
summand contributes to the entries (i, i), (i, n), and (n, i). Their ratios in A∗ are

these weights make every decoder valid and give wi λT           T

Prepared quantum realization. The family has a finite-dimensional prepared unitary realization, so

                              n                                             n

                      ρ=         |ii⟩⟨ii|,       Ug = PgT ⊗ I,        E=          |ii⟩⟨ii|.

                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                           17


Here Pg is the row-transition permutation matrix, so Ug |x, y⟩ = |xg, y⟩, consistently with chronolog-
ical command multiplication. Then tr(EUg ρUg∗ ) = χn (g)/n. The binary effect Et = tE + (1 − t)I/n
produces (S3.6). Two command types suffice uniformly in n: an n-cycle and a transposition of
adjacent points generate Sn , since conjugation by the cycle supplies the adjacent transpositions.
They give one common experiment valid for every word in that fixed-size commandPalphabet, with
a binary terminal outcome set. The pure maximally entangled preparation n−1/2 i |ii⟩ gives the
same terminal probabilities with this effect, if a pure preparation is desired. The statement is a
physical realization of the response, not a claim that this Hilbert-space dimension is minimal or
that it establishes a quantum-versus-classical separation under a common resource metric.
   The gap n between the positive core at full contrast and its module minimum is unbounded. The
relative ratio n/(n − 1) tends to one; no growing relative separation is asserted. The exact transition
tends to one as n grows. Its proof combines a general orbit-multiplicity converse, a sharp positivity
inequality with equality rigidity, and explicit optimal implementation; none of these counts requires
the completion of the wider Theta pipeline.

          S4. Quotient decompositions, linear duality and sharp transitions
  For groups whose subgroups are normal, the probability optimization in the normal form has an
exact linear reduction. This includes every finite abelian group and allows arbitrary probability tables
and terminal outcome sets. Neither a special phase choice nor a positive-amplitude trigonometric
ansatz is imposed. The reduction uses normal stabilizers; it is not an unrestricted claim that
equivariant nonnegative factorization is linear programming.
Lemma S4.1 (Absorption of within-orbit preparation). Suppose the stabilizers in a finite G-set
are normal. Every probability factorization on it can be replaced, without increasing its size, by one
having at most one orbit H\G for each normal subgroup H and whose initial row is concentrated at
the identity coset within each orbit. Only the mixture between different orbits is required.
Proof. On an orbit of positive initial mass, normalize its local preparation and write its response as
                                              X
                                 q(g, c) =            λ(Hx)D(Hxg, c).
                                             Hx∈H\G

For h ∈ H, normality gives Hxhg = Hxg, so q(hg, c) = q(g, c). Thus q itself is a probability
decoder on H\G. Replacing the preparation by the identity coset and the decoder by q leaves every
command-word response unchanged. Orbits with the same H can be merged by averaging their
decoders with their initial masses as weights. Delete zero-mass orbits. These operations weakly
decrease the size.                                                                             □
  Assume henceforth, until the Fourier specialization, that every subgroup of G is normal. Such
groups
P
       are called Dedekind groups. For a nonempty set H of distinct subgroups, put c(H) =
  H∈H [G : H].

Theorem S4.2 (Exact quotient linear programs). The invariant κG (b) is the minimum c(H) for
which there are pH ≥ 0 and functions fH : H\G × C → R≥0 satisfying
   X                                     X                 X
        fH (q, c) = pH   (q ∈ H\G),           pH = 1,           fH (Hg, c) = b(g, c)   (g ∈ G, c ∈ C).
    c                                     H                 H
                                                                                             (S4.1)
The residual cardinality is N = |G|, and m(b) = min{|G|, 1 + κG (b)}. For a rational response table
a minimum controller can be chosen rational. More generally, if the table lies in a specified real
algebraic number field, the controller can be chosen in that same field.
Proof. Apply Lemma S4.1 to a minimum probability factorization. Its orbit contributions give
(S4.1). Conversely, if pH > 0, put DH = fH /pH , place mass pH at its identity coset, and use the
18                                                  QIAN QI


right quotient action. If pH = 0, all its entries vanish and the orbit is discarded. This constructs
the whole response with at most c(H) points. The regular orbit H = {1} is always available, and
there are finitely many subgroup families.
   The stabilizer H0 of the initial residual is normal. If h ∈ H0 and g ∈ G, moving h past g using
normality shows that h fixes the residual after g. It therefore fixes their span and is the identity in
the minimal observable group. Thus H0 = {1} and orbit-stabilizer gives N = |G|. The pure-start
assertion follows from Theorem S1.2.
   For each family the displayed feasible set is a bounded closed polyhedron: 0 ≤ fH (q, c) ≤ pH ≤ 1.
A feasible vertex is obtained by solving a linear system over the coefficient field. It is rational for
rational data and remains in the given real algebraic field otherwise. The construction uses only
these entries and division by positive pH . If the residual machine is smaller than the initializer
construction, its decoder is the given table and has the same arithmetic property.                   □
  This theorem optimizes the common permutation action supplied by the inherited normal form.
The relation between symmetry and positive factorizations is classical [67, 68], and positivity can
cost more than a factorization inside the target linear space [70]. The additional step here is the
absorption of preparation on normal quotient orbits, which removes the bilinear unknowns without
changing the counted resource.

Theorem S4.3 (Payoff certificates and exact contrast endpoints). The family H is feasible in (S4.1)
if and only if
                                 ⟨y, b⟩ ≤ hH (y) for every real table y.                     (S4.3)
If infeasible, it has a strict rational witness y. A witness of margin ∆ = ⟨y, b⟩ − hH (y) > 0 still
excludes that family for any table eb with ∥eb − b∥∞ < ∆/∥y∥1 .
   For the contrast line (S2.1), put v = b − π. Its endpoint for this family is
                                          (                             )

Both minima are attained. Equivalently, maximize t in [0, 1] subject to (S4.1) with right side π + tv.
The endpoint for budget k is the maximum of these linear-program values over c(H) ≤ k. Rational
tables give rational endpoints and optimal controllers; no polynomial bound on the number of families
is claimed.
Proof. The feasible response set for a fixed family is exactly PH = conv H PH . Each PH is a
                                                                                S

product of probability simplexes. Maximizing a payoff independently on each coset gives its support
function hH ; taking a convex hull of the union gives hH . Membership proves (S4.3). If b is outside
this compact convex set, let q be a closest point. The first variation along the segment from q to any
point of the set gives ⟨b − q, x − q⟩ ≤ 0. With y = b − q this yields a strictly positive gap of at least
∥b − q∥22 . Approximate y by a rational table sufficiently closely to preserve that gap; the support
function is a finite maximum of linear functions and is continuous. The perturbation assertion is
the inequality |⟨y, eb − b⟩| ≤ ∥y∥1 ∥eb − b∥∞ .
   The constant table π belongs to every PH . Along its ray π + tv, a support inequality with
⟨y, v⟩ ≤ 0 imposes no restriction for t ≥ 0; every other inequality can be normalized by ⟨y, v⟩ = 1.
This gives (S4.4). Attainment requires no strict-feasibility assumption. If v lies outside the linear
space parallel to the affine hull of PH , an affine-hull equation gives an attained inner minimum
zero. Otherwise the bounded polytope has a finite facet description in that affine hull; the first
                                       CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                              19


boundary reached along the ray activates a facet with positive directional coefficient. Its normalized
supporting functional attains the inner minimum. The polytope is bounded and v =       ̸ 0, so the ray
endpoint is finite.
  One explicit linear formulation of the inner dual introduces zHq and s with
                       X                                       X
               zHq ≥         y(g, c)      (H, q, c),      s≥        zHq   (H ∈ H),          ⟨y, v⟩ = 1,

and minimizes s − ⟨y, π⟩. The primal formulation is obtained by replacing the right side of (S4.1)
by π + tv. It is bounded and contains t = 0. Vertex arithmetic proves the endpoint and controller
assertions, and the finite maximum over admissible families gives the budget endpoint. These are
exact linear certificates in the specified finite problem, not a formal proof-assistant verification. □
S4.2. Fourier support and a sharp positivity transition. For an abelian G, let S be the
complex characters whose vector Fourier coefficient in b is nonzero. It contains the trivial character
and is closed under conjugation.
                                                                                                          P
Corollary S4.4 (The abelian module cover). The number νG (Vb ) is the minimum H∈H [G : H]
over distinct subgroup families such that each character in S is trivial on at least one H ∈ H. This
lower bound is the exact value of κG (bt ) for all sufficiently small positive contrasts. It need not be
attained at full contrast.
Proof. Character averaging projects the cyclic vector b onto each of its nonzero Fourier modes. Thus
its complex cyclic module contains each active character once; conjugate pairs give the corresponding
real two-dimensional summands. The permutation module on H\G contains exactly the characters
trivial on H, each once. Containment of Vb is therefore exactly the stated cover. Repeated subgroups
add no new characters and may be deleted. Apply Theorem S2.1.                                      □
  The following example computes a complete rational threshold and an explicit dual obstruction.
Let G = (F2 )4 , with the four coordinate flips as commands, and let χ1 , . . . , χ4 be its coordinate sign
characters. Put χ5 = χ1 χ2 χ3 χ4 and

Proposition S4.5 (A sharp transition invisible to the observable module). For every t > 0, (S4.5)
has observable group (F2 )4 , dimension six, residual cardinality sixteen, and νG (Vb ) = 10. Its exact
positive core and pure-start memory are
                                (                                           (
                                    10,     0 < t ≤ 3/5,                        11,   0 < t ≤ 3/5,
                   κG (bt ) =                                    m(bt ) =
                                    12,     3/5 < t ≤ 1,                        13,   3/5 < t ≤ 1.
At zero contrast the minimum is one. In particular a twelve-label minimum does not occur in this
family.
Proof. The five signs have product one, so their sum is 5, 1, or −3. Consequently β1 takes the values
1, 1/2, 0, and every βt is a valid convex combination with the constant 3/8. Any four of the five
characters are independent over F2 . For t > 0 all five character coefficients and the constant mode
are nonzero, giving dimension six and trivial joint kernel. Thus G is the observable group and
N = 16.
   For a quotient orbit write W = H ⊥ and r = dim W ; its size is 2r . A proper W contains at most r
of the five active nontrivial characters, and 2r ≥ 2r for r = 1, 2, 3. An orbit with r = 4 costs sixteen.
Covering the five modes therefore costs at least ten, and the five two-point quotients attain this
module bound.
   Suppose that a positive core has at most eleven points. A size-eight orbit covers at most three
active characters; the remaining budget at most three covers at most one more. Size sixteen is
20                                                  QIAN QI


unavailable. The nontrivial orbits thus have sizes two or four. Their total cost must be ten and
their active-character blocks partition the five modes into singletons or pairs: each dimension must
carry an active character and no active character can be covered twice. At most one fixed point can
remain. Products belonging to different pairs are distinct and inactive, because equality would give
a nontrivial relation involving at most four of the five circuit characters.
  For this family of blocks let B2 denote its pairs and define

over a fixed orbit it gives 1. The absence of any relation of length at most four ensures that no
other term survives these averages. Each resulting function is nonnegative. Hence any nonnegative

Proof. Write η for the left side of (S4.6). Every normalized Fourier coefficient changes by at most η.

   For an exact physical realization one may use the sixteen-dimensional diagonal model: prepare
the identity basis state, use regular permutation unitaries for the four generators, and take the
diagonal terminal effect with entries βt (g). This is already classical; no quantum advantage is
asserted for this example. The general low-contrast theorem, the normal quotient linear programs,
and this exact transition have different scopes.

          S5. The retained instrument realization theory and its boundary
   The group results concern terminal responses. For an outcome at every call, one must instead
preserve each command–outcome history and every subsequent decision made from that visible
history. This stronger interface is already covered, under a sufficient uniform posterior condition,
by the earlier instrument theory of this paper. We recall its exact scope before proving the new
conditional-memory classification.
   For fixed known completely positive branches Ia,y whose command sums are trace preserving,
the hypothesis is
                                      η            
                           Ia,y (X) ⪰ tr Ia,y (X) I (X ⪰ 0),         η > 0.
                                      h
It is a condition on every recorded branch, including the zero-branch convention, and follows when
depolarizing noise of weight η is applied after each branch. In every finite dimension h ≥ 2, and for
k prescribed preparations, Theorem S22.2 gives at most
                                              l           m
                                          k + (4h/η)h−1

hidden labels, with at most h2 successors per branch row. All commands use the same physical
dictionary. The complete proof in Section S22 gives the operator identity for every history; it
preserves all adaptive stopped laws, including nontermination, and nonnegative costs. The hidden
label is not exposed to the observer. Corollary S22.3 consequently preserves the risk of every
common report-based observer, including constrained boundary budgets; it does not identify hidden
simulator size with optimal observer memory.
   The same retained theory includes a terminating algebraic construction (Proposition S22.5),
interiority after a finite block with the clock and intermediate reports counted (Theorem S22.6),
and matching powers of η −1 for universal state-positive representation (Theorem S22.4). The latter
lower bound is for generators which are actual density matrices. It is not imported as a lower bound
against arbitrary nonnegative hidden-state representations. The family in Section S14 will have a
lower bound against that unrestricted class.
22                                                 QIAN QI


   Invariant positive cones and their stochastic realizations are classical [48, 47]. Okay’s quantum
simulation framework explicitly includes invariant-polytope updates and adaptive composition [71].
Pure-state nets and approximation of the density-matrix body have an established convex-geometric
theory [72]; extension complexity is a different resource [73]. These ingredients, and the paper’s
already proved interior-instrument theorem, are not asserted as new discoveries of this revision.
Their role is to fix the sufficient condition and the resource convention against which the new sharp
examples are compared. The next common-contraction construction gives an explicit certificate in
supplied linear-response coordinates; it is also an application of positive-realization geometry, not a
replacement for the retained instrument theorem.

            S6. Sharp conditional memory with damping and adaptive commands
   The one-dependent family of Section S14 also admits a sharp positive theory under conditional
damping. The extension below realizes the complete observed instrument, with one physical
dictionary shared by every command. The classical sector inequality and the scalar thresholds
of Section S19 are inherited ingredients, not new spectral-region results. Earlier noise-dependent
process lower bounds, including an inverse-square-root worst-phase dependence, are due to Fanizza,
Lumbreras and Winter [50, Theorem 2 and Section 4.2]. Here the lower and upper meet for an
explicit uniformly positive, exactly depolarizing instrument, and the upper works simultaneously
under adaptive rotation commands.
   For a qubit set

The initial state is I/2. An allowed command specifies θ; the report is s ∈ {0, 1, 2}. A command
may be selected from the visible history. A finite known command alphabet is enough for every finite
lower witness below. The same construction also gives Borel transition tables for the entire angle
circle; this last formulation counts labels, not the encoding or exact sampling cost of a real-valued
command.
   This family does not satisfy the retained uniform-interior hypothesis on every posterior, even
when α < 1: report 1 always prepares the pure state τ . Repeated reports 1 keep that pure state,
so passing to a fixed block length does not restore that hypothesis. The finite construction below
instead uses the common geometry of the damped rotation, the reset vertex and the remaining
branch.
Lemma S6.1 (Physical margins and predictive rank). For every α ∈ [0, 1] and every angle, the
maps (S6.1) form a quantum instrument, their sum is exactly D, and their report probabilities lie in
{1/8}, [1/16, 3/16] and [11/16, 13/16], respectively. The prepared response is predictively stationary
under each unobserved command. At a fixed angle its output process is stationary and one-dependent.
When α > 0 and sin θ ̸= 0, its word Hankel rank is three, and
                                        8−n    αn
                                                                
                          p(1 0n 1) =       1+    cos(nθ)               (n ≥ 0).                (S6.2)
                                         64     2
At α = 0 the complete command response has rank two and is independent of the angles. For all α,
it has no one-label realization.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         23


Proof. The first two Choi matrices are positive. Their operator norms are at most 1/4 and 3/16,
while J(D) = I4 /2. Hence J(Ψα,θ 2 ) ⪰ I4 /16. The asserted trace bounds are unchanged by damping.
Since D(I/2) = I/2, averaging any unobserved command fixes the prepared predictive response,
including arbitrary subsequent commands and terminal POVMs. At a fixed angle, summing an
intervening report applies D and therefore factorizes the probabilities of the blocks on either side,
exactly as in Theorem S14.1.
   Report 1 has initial probability 1/8 and prepares τ . Conditional on n zero reports its Bloch
vector is αn (cos(nθ), sin(nθ)), giving (S6.2). All reached subnormalized states are real symmetric,
so the Hankel rank is at most three. For α > 0 and nonreal phase, the three modes 1/8, αeiθ /8
and αe−iθ /8 are distinct with nonzero coefficients. The 3 × 3 submatrix with entries p(1 0i+j 1) is
therefore a product of two nonsingular Vandermonde matrices and a nonsingular diagonal matrix.
This proves rank three. At α = 0 every reached state is diagonal, giving rank at most two; the 2 × 2
cylinder minor has determinant 2−19 > 0. Finally p(1) = 1/8 and p(11) = 3/128 ̸= p(1)2 for every
parameter, excluding a one-label realization.                                                      □
  Write Nmix (α, θ) for the least label count reproducing every report word at one fixed angle, and
define Npure with a counted pure start. The same conclusions below hold if every terminal qubit
POVM is included: the uppers realize the subnormalized quantum states themselves and the lowers
use report words alone. Write KM for the set of complex eigenvalues of M × M row-stochastic
matrices, as in Section S19.
Lemma S6.2 (Relative cylinder obstruction and polygon realization). For α > 0 and sin θ ̸= 0,
every M -label mixed-start realization satisfies
                                              αeiθ ∈ KM .                                          (S6.3)
Conversely, let Pq be the regular q-gon with vertices e2πij/q , q ≥ 3, one of which represents τ .
If αeiθ ∈ Pq , its q physical pure states form one dictionary invariant under all three normalized
branches, and give a q-label mixed-start realization of the complete instrument. A single dictionary
works for any collection of angles satisfying this condition.
Proof. For a candidate HMM put v = µT1 and d = T1 1, so that the cylinder in (S6.2) is vT0n d.
Restrict T0 to labels reachable from v and from which d is eventually positive, obtaining S. For
every pair i, j in this productive set, fixed positive entry and exit paths give constants cij > 0 and
integers ai , bj ≥ 0 with
                                 cij (S n )ij ≤ p(1 0ai +n+bj 1) ≤ Cij 8−n .
Thus ρ(S) ≤ 1/8. Cayley–Hamilton and the three distinct modes in (S6.2) force αeiθ /8 to be an

Perron normalization proved before Proposition S20.2 gives a stochastic matrix Q with eigenvalue
αeiθ /(8R) and eigenvector u. For a stationary row π of Q, πu = 0 because this eigenvalue is nonreal.
Consequently
                                      Q′ = 8RQ + (1 − 8R)1π
is stochastic and has eigenvalue αeiθ . Its size is at most M ; padding by self-loop labels proves (S6.3).
This normalizes only the irreducible block carrying the mode, and does not assume a positive Perron
vector for a reducible productive core.

   For a fixed phase the lower in Lemma S6.2 and its regular-polygon upper also transfer the
already proved arithmetic envelope of Theorem S18.1, with the same constants, to the complete
observed instrument. Consequently its two logarithmic exponents and its badly-approximable
criterion transfer as well. This is a change from a scalar response to an entire instrument, not a
new number-theoretic assertion. No equality between the full-instrument minimum and the scalar
spectral minimum is asserted outside the proved sectors and boundary cases.

          S7. The complete mean–contrast law and optimal-factor rigidity
   The mean of a response and the size of its nonconstant part impose distinct positivity constraints.
The return family of Theorem S3.6 fixes the mean at 1/n. We now allow every feasible mean and
both signs of contrast, for every n ≥ 5. A second positivity inequality changes the sharp boundary:
simply rescaling the old threshold gives the wrong answer on an interval of means. The proof yields
the complete boundary factorization on that interval. Throughout, the mean is the average terminal
probability over the group; it is not a prior on an unknown parameter.
   Bounded simplex-structured factorization and stacking complementary nonnegative slacks with a
common factor are established techniques [77, Section IV-C]. Threshold attainment and boundary
identifiability also occur in the geometric theory of nonnegative rank [78, Section 4.3]. Our additional
conclusion is the sharp simultaneous coefficient extremum below, its three-piece parameter region,
and the resulting unrestricted group-memory and boundary-factor laws.
   The following elementary extremum treats two nonnegative coefficient matrices sharing a left null
vector. Both outcomes must be represented through the same preparation; this common-null-vector
requirement is stronger than separate rank bounds on the two outcome matrices.

Corollary S7.3 (Exact memory throughout the mean–contrast plane). Let n ≥ 5 and let (p, a)
satisfy (S7.7). At a = 0, both mixed-core and pure-start minima are one. If a ̸= 0, put q = p when
a > 0 and q = 1 − p when a < 0. Then
                                 (
                                     n(n − 1),     0 < |a| ≤ Fn (q),
                 κSn (bp,a ) =                                            m(bp,a ) = 1 + κSn (bp,a ).         (S7.12)
                                     n2 ,          |a| > Fn (q),
Every nonconstant table has observable group Sn , residual cardinality n!, and observable dimension
1 + (n − 1)2 . For a > 0 and p ≥ 2/n, the smaller memory realizes every feasible contrast. Thus
positivity causes no additional core cost anywhere in that part of the parameter plane.
   At the nontrivial boundary a = Fn (p) with 0 < p ≤ 2/n, all minimum permutation-core factor-
izations are exactly those in (S7.9) and (S7.10). For negative contrast, exchange the two output
labels. This classifies minimum permutation cores, without asserting uniqueness of every unreduced
stochastic machine.
Proof. It suffices to treat a > 0, because exchanging the two outputs replaces (p, a) by (1 − p, −a).
The trivial block of the Fourier transform is (p, 1 − p), and the nontrivial standard block is
aIn−1 /(n − 1) in the first coordinate. The faithful-standard-module argument in Theorem S3.6
therefore gives the claimed observable group, residual count and dimension for every a > 0. That
theorem’s subgroup-index estimate depends only on this module. It implies the lower bound n(n − 1)
and proves that every core of size less than n2 compresses to exactly n − 1 natural orbits: extra
orbits have no standard component, so their nonnegative constant contribution is absorbed into the
natural-orbit decoders. This absorption preserves both output probabilities and does not use the
value p = 1/n.
   On n natural orbits, put weight 1/n on each, prepare point i on orbit i, and use
                                               Di (j) = p − a + na 1{i=j} .
These decoders lie in [0, 1] exactly under (S7.7). Their response is (S7.6). Thus the core is always
at most n2 , and there are no possible intermediate values.
   Suppose a realization on n − 1 natural orbits exists. Its preparation rows λi , weights wi and
decoder rows Di give
                              X                           X

                              i<n                         i<n

factor. Their permutation sums give the two outcomes.PThe permutation-switch argument in the
retained proof implies P AP = aP , and averaging gives ij Aij = np. The preparation matrix has
30                                               QIAN QI


at most n − 1 rows, so rank[A u] < n. The matrix criterion in Theorem S7.2 is therefore necessary.
Conversely its width-n − 1 positive factorizations give exactly n − 1 natural-orbit preparations and
valid binary decoders. Their coefficient identities imply the required response for every permutation.
Hence a core below n2 exists exactly when a ≤ Fn (p), and then the module lower bound makes its
size n(n − 1). The retained pure-start formula gives m = min{n!, 1 + κ}, which equals 1 + κ because
n2 + 1 < n! for n ≥ 5. At a = 0 the constant decoder uses one state. Every minimum core on the
nontrivial boundary consists of n − 1 natural orbits, so the factor classification follows from the
matrix theorem.                                                                                     □
Physical realization andPscope. Use the prepared unitary experiment of Theorem S3.6, with ρ =

                                        Ep,a = (p − a)I + naE
produces (S7.6); its two eigenvalues are p − a and p + (n − 1)a, so the exact validity range is
(S7.7), including negative contrast. The same two command generators realize every word. The
theorem solves the whole displayed two-parameter family, including its constant boundary and its
minimum-core factorization on the nontrivial transition curve. It is not an unrestricted high-contrast
formula for every finite group, an identification result for an unknown model, or a solution of the
original arbitrary-prior decision-risk problem.

             S8. Approximation by truncating rare conditional excursions
   Exact memory can be infinite although one finite simulator approximates every stopped experiment
with a prescribed expected cost. The damping construction of Theorem S9.1 gives such an upper
bound for the entire angle circle. For a fixed finite command alphabet we now obtain a smaller
bound by preserving a long conditional excursion exactly and modifying only its overflow. The
general mechanism depends on the growth of the continuation semigroup, and applies beyond the
qubit family.
   The ingredients have classical antecedents. Polyhedral positive realizations are part of the theory
of Heller and of Monràs and Winter [48, 47, 76]. Martingale calculations for pattern waiting times
are classical [80]. The use made here is a uniform approximation of complete adaptive stopped
laws, with an explicit memory bound from a finite return polytope and the number of distinct
continuation states. It is separate from the exact noisy realization problem of [50].
S8.1. A sharp cost bound for a long conditional excursion. Consider sequential calls, each
producing a report declared either continuing or returning. Conditional on the entire past, the
chosen command and any private randomization, suppose the probability of a continuing report is
at most r, where 0 < r < 1. Let ηL be the first call completing L consecutive continuing reports,
with L ≥ 1. A policy can stop initially or after any call; its number of calls is τ .
Lemma S8.1 (Sharp expected-cost excursion bound). For every such policy with Eτ ≤ B < ∞,
                                                      (1 − r)B
                                                                   
                                   P{ηL ≤ τ } ≤ min 1,           .                           (S8.1)
                                                       r−L − 1
The coefficient is optimal under the stated assumptions. For independent continuing indicators of
probability r, the supremum over randomized stopping policies of expected cost at most B is exactly
the right side.
Proof. Before the first completed run, let Kt ∈ {0, . . . , L − 1} be the current run length. It increases
by one on a continuing report and returns to zero otherwise. Absorb it at L after the first completed
run, and put
                                           r−k − 1
                                   u(k) =               (0 ≤ k ≤ L).
                                            1−r
                                      CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                           31


For k < L and any conditional continuation probability p ≤ r,
                         pu(k + 1) + (1 − p)u(0) − u(k) ≤ ru(k + 1) − u(k) = 1.
Stop this calculation at σH = min{τ, ηL , H}. Summing the conditional inequalities, including the
indicator that the next call is actually made, gives
                            u(L)P{ηL ≤ τ, ηL ≤ H} ≤ Eu(KσH ) ≤ EσH ≤ B.
Let H → ∞. Increasing events give (S8.1); no assumption about the expected duration of another
process is used.
   For sharpness, take independent Bernoulli indicators of probability r. The first completed run
has finite expectation: disjoint blocks of length L succeed with probability rL , so its expectation
is at most L/rL . In this case the displayed drift equals one. Stopping at ηL ∧ H and then using
bounded convergence for u(K) and monotone convergence for the duration proves EηL = u(L).
With probability min{1, B/u(L)}, selected before making any call, run this experiment until ηL ;
otherwise stop immediately. Its expected cost is at most B and its success probability equals the
right side of (S8.1).                                                                              □
  Sharpness in this lemma concerns the probability of the exceptional run. It will not imply that
every simulator must incur the approximation error estimated by that probability.
