Proof. Use the states of DL as hidden labels. At a label ρ, the probability of report y on command a
is pa,y (ρ) = tr Ia,y (ρ). Zero probabilities require no successor choice. For a returning report, choose
any convex decomposition of its normalized output in the seed states. For a continuing report whose
normalized output belongs to DL , use that output label deterministically. In the remaining case,
send the state to any one fixed seed. Multiply each successor distribution by pa,y (ρ) to obtain the
report transition row. All rows are nonnegative and the sums over the reports are stochastic. Use
a seed decomposition of ρ0 for the initial distribution. At label ρ, the terminal decoder for any
POVM (Ez )z is tr(Ez ρ).
   To verify the error, first use all finite continuation words, without the length restriction in (S8.3).
This countable refinement reproduces the target exactly. Every returning branch is expanded in
the seeds; every continuing branch uses its exact normalized image. On each individual hidden
label the weighted state identity is exact, so induction gives the target subnormalized quantum
state for every command–report history. Initial mixing, terminal POVMs and common randomized
policies preserve these identities. This is only a proof device, not an uncounted finite implementation
resource.
    Couple this exact refinement to the finite model by identical initial seed choices and by identical
reports and successor choices while the states agree. The coupling first fails only when a continuing
image leaves DL . After a return the exact label is a seed, so a product of fewer than L continuing
branches is in DL by definition. The same is true initially. Thus a failure requires a run of at least L
consecutive continuing reports. Identifying duplicate states does not change this implication: their
next normalized image depends only on the state itself, and every shorter word image is still present.
    Before a failure the two visible histories, quantum states and policy choices can be identical.
After a failure use arbitrary valid tails. For a fixed finite horizon, the stopped classical–quantum
states have a common positive contribution from all paths with no failure. If the remaining mass is
e, their trace distance is at most e, by positivity and the triangle inequality. The same assertion
applies to the complete record after any terminal POVM. The target’s probability of a failure before
stopping is at most Pπtarget {ηL ≤ τ }, which is bounded by Lemma S8.1. Its conditional continuation
bound follows from (S8.2), also after conditioning on the virtual seed choices.
    For unbounded stopping, retain every stopped history up to horizon H and combine the unfinished
histories into one symbol. The sum of trace norms on the finite stopped blocks increases to its full
sum; the two unfinished masses decrease to their respective nontermination masses. Their absolute
difference therefore converges to the nontermination term. The uniform finite-horizon bound passes
to the limit, giving (S8.4). The budget is always evaluated under the target. The argument does
not require the approximate model to have expected duration at most B.
    For a pure start append one initializer. On the first actual command it uses the initial mixture
times the corresponding report matrix and enters the core. Its zero-call terminal decoder is that of
ρ0 . It is never entered again. This proves the upper G(L) + 1 without an uncounted preliminary
                                     CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                              33


randomization. If a separate finite policy uses s labels, the same construction gives at most sG(L) + 1
labels for the closed product implementation with a prescribed initial label.
  There are bj words of length j, proving (S8.5). For commuting maps the product depends only
on a multi-index m ∈ Nb with |m| < L; there are L+b−1
                                                              
                                                           b    such multi-indices. Normalization and
deletion of zero images cannot increase this number. Finally it suffices to take
                                               log(1 + (1 − r)B/δ)
                                                                   
                        L ≥ max 1,                                         (B > 0, δ > 0).
                                                     log(1/r)
The asserted consequence of polynomial orbit growth follows.                                                   □
   The construction preserves every finite history containing no run of L continuing reports, including
its unnormalized terminal state. It does not claim that its approximate response is stationary or
that the extra pure-start label is necessary. When all continuation maps have finite normalized
orbit on the seeds, the full finite orbit instead gives an exact model. These are upper constructions,
not a converse classification of general positive realizations.

S8.3. A polylogarithmic simulator for finitely many commanded angles. Apply the theorem
to (S6.1) with α = 1. Let the finite known angle alphabet be Θ = {θ1 , . . . , θa }, where a ≥ 1. The
continuation report is 0, of probability 1/8. In the real Bloch plane put
                                                √                         √
                  v0 = (1, 0),     v1 = (−1/2, 3/2),         v2 = (−1/2, − 3/2).
Let σj be the corresponding pure states. Their triangle contains the disk of radius 1/2, and I/2 is
their equal mixture. Report 1 returns to σ0 = τ . By Lemma S6.2, every normalized report-2 image
has Bloch norm at most 5/11 < 1/2, so it returns to this same triangle, at every angle and from
every real-plane state. The real-plane density matrices form an invariant closed convex set: the
unitaries are real, and the other two branches preserve real symmetric matrices. Thus they supply
the set C in the general theorem.

when a periodic phase makes that next physical state coincide with an earlier one. The first possible

assertion.                                                                                         □
   If B = 0, a single initial decoder suffices, with no call and no error. The damping upper in
Theorem S9.1 remains available and can be smaller for particular B, δ, a; one may choose the smaller
of the two proved upper counts. Its simultaneous all-angle statement also remains valid. In contrast,
the finite dictionary in (S8.10) is for a fixed finite angle alphabet; the bound is not asserted for a
continuum of possible commands.
   For every non-root phase, exact finite classical realization is still impossible by Theorem S14.1.
At that same fixed physical instrument the present bound is logarithmic in B/δ for a single angle.
Thus an inverse-square-root exact noise law is not a lower bound on approximation of bounded-cost
stopped experiments. Section S8.4 proves a matching logarithmic law within the explicitly restricted
class of physical dictionaries; a matching lower for arbitrary classical terminal decoder tables remains
open. The stopped-law bound also does not control the approximate model’s expected duration:
unbounded costs require a further tail or uniform-integrability hypothesis. External policy storage,
table bits, arithmetic precision and exact sampling remain separate resources.
S8.4. A matching logarithmic law for physical dictionaries. The preceding uppers use genuine
density matrices as hidden dictionary states. Within that representation class a matching lower,
including the leading logarithmic constant, is possible. This restriction is substantive: an arbitrary
classical hidden-state model can have terminal decoder tables that are not Born probabilities of
fixed density matrices. The conclusion below does not impose that restriction on such a model
without proof.
   Fix one commanded angle θ = 2πω in the undamped instrument. A physical-dictionary simulator
has finitely many labels with fixed qubit density matrices σi , arbitrary stochastic report transition
tables, and terminal decoder dE (i) = tr(Eσi ) for every requested effect E. The causal transition
tables are fixed independently of any later terminal-measurement request. The transitions themselves
need not implement the quantum instrument on each dictionary state. Denote by Nphys     m (δ; B) and

to error at most δ, every common visible stopping and terminal-POVM policy whose target expected
calls are at most B.

Such a finite K exists for every irrational ω. Here is a direct argument sufficient for this use.
Pigeonholing finitely many fractional multiples of ω gives an integer q > 0 with 0 < d = ∥qω∥ < 1/4.
The points with indices 0, q, 2q, . . . , ⌊1/d⌋q have successive circular gaps at most d, in one orientation
or the other. Taking a prefix containing all these indices,  √ its inscribed polygon has every central
gap less than π/2, and hence inradius greater than 1/ 2 > 5/11. The origin is in this polygon. It
is a valid seed polytope: report 1 returns to v0 , and every report-2 output is in its contained disk.
   For one angle, all continuation images of these K seeds through depth L − 1 belong to
{v0 , . . . , vK+L−2 }, a set of K + L − 1 states. Returning branches are expanded in the original
K seeds. No overflow can occur before L successive zero reports, even when a return chooses the
last seed. Theorem S8.2 therefore proves (S8.15). The finite barycentric coefficients can be chosen
from any triangulation of PK . All labels, including the optional initializer representing I/2, are
physical states.

For arbitrary classical decoder tables, the general finite-alphabet upper remains valid, while the

error in (S8.8). All those orbit coordinates are rational. Triangulating their rational polygon gives

rational. This is an explicit upper for that algebraic instrument, not a claim about its unresolved
Diophantine lower certificate.

        S9. Finite-memory approximation uniform over bounded-cost policies
   Exact finite memory and approximation of an experiment at bounded expected cost have different
quantifiers. The noisy construction gives an explicit approximation of the undamped conditional
process, using one finite model for an entire class of adaptive stopping policies. The following
statement is an upper bound; it does not claim an optimal approximation exponent among arbitrary
classical models.
Theorem S9.1 (Stopped-law approximation from conditional damping). Fix a finite angle command
alphabet, initial qubit I/2, and the undamped instruments (S6.1) with α = 1. Let PB be any class of
common randomized policies for the next command, stopping and a terminal qubit POVM, such that
the number τ of physical calls satisfies Eπ1 τ ≤ B < ∞ for every π ∈ PB . The policy has access to
the visible record and its own classical randomness; there is no additional quantum register entangled
with the qubit.
   For every 0 < ϵ < 1, replace the instrument by the one with α = 1 − ϵ and use its common finite
realization from Theorem S6.3. This one model, independently of the chosen policy, obeys
                                     sup Lπ1 − Lπ1−ϵ TV ≤ ϵB/8.                                  (S9.1)
                                    π∈PB
Here the complete stopped record includes the stopping time, commands, reports, terminal mea-
surement result and a nontermination symbol. The stronger bound holds for the block-diagonal
classical–quantum terminal states before applying the terminal POVM; its nontermination block is
one-dimensional and records only the nontermination mass. The simulator uses at most K(1 − ϵ)
labels with a free mixed start, or K(1 − ϵ) + 1 with a counted pure start.
   Thus for 0 < δ < B/8, one may take ϵ = 8δ/B and use
                                              π              πq
                                                      
                        1 + max 3,                         =     B/δ + O(1)               (S9.2)
                                      arccos(1 − 8δ/B)       4
pure-start labels as δ/B ↓ 0. For δ ≥ B/8 the α = 0 model uses three pure-start labels and satisfies
the same error requirement. Policy memory is separate: a policy with L counted labels and a
prescribed initial label can be composed with the mixed core using at most LK(1 − ϵ) + 1 labels, with
its first actual call processed during entry into the product core.
Proof. For every positive qubit operator X and every angle,
                                     ∥D(X) − Uθ XUθ∗ ∥1 ≤ tr X.
Indeed, after division by tr X > 0, the difference from I/2 has eigenvalues ±|r|/2 for a Bloch
vector of length at most one; the zero-trace case is immediate. The perturbation of branch zero is
ϵ[D − AdUθ ]/8, that of branch one is zero, and that of branch two is the negative of branch zero.
Hence the one-call flagged output satisfies
                                   2
                                1X                            ϵ
                                      ∥[Ψs1−ϵ,θ − Ψ1,θ
                                                   s ](X)∥1 ≤   tr X.                            (S9.3)
                                2 s=0                         8
This bound is uniform in the command. Averaging it over a randomized command choice preserves
it.
    First cap the experiment at a deterministic horizon H. Retain the classical history and the
subnormalized qubit state in every live or stopped branch. A subsequent common policy step,
including a terminal measurement, is a positive trace-preserving map on this direct sum, and
38                                               QIAN QI


therefore contracts the trace norm of Hermitian differences. To see this directly, write such a
difference as Z+ − Z− , with Z± ≥ 0. Positivity, trace preservation and the triangle inequality give
∥Φ(Z)∥1 ≤ tr Z+ + tr Z− = ∥Z∥1 .
   Telescope between the undamped and damped H-step experiments, replacing one physical call at
a time. In the term for call t + 1, keep the first t calls undamped and the remaining tail damped.
The input to the changed call is then the target positive live state of total trace Pπ1 {τ > t}. Apply
(S9.3) there and contract the difference through its common tail. The resulting terminal trace
distance is at most
                                 ϵ H−1
                                   X                  ϵ
                                       Pπ {τ > t} = Eπ1 min{τ, H}.                               (S9.4)
                                 8 t=0 1              8
The reference distribution in this bound is the undamped target, not an uncontrolled distribution of
a hybrid or approximate process.
   To pass to the uncapped experiment, first retain separately every stopped history of length at most
H and merge the unfinished branches into one symbol. Coarse graining cannot increase the distance.
As H increases, the sum of the trace norms of the differences on the finite stopped blocks increases to
its full sum, while the unfinished probabilities decrease to the respective nontermination probabilities.
Thus the left side converges to the stated stopped classical–quantum distance, including the absolute
difference at the nontermination symbol. The right side increases to ϵEπ1 τ /8. A terminal POVM
and further classical processing contract this distance, proving (S9.1). The argument permits all
the policy randomization because that randomness is simply part of the common classical transition
at each step.
   The noisy dictionary reproduces these complete subnormalized word states exactly, so replacing the
noisy quantum system by its classical realization introduces no further error. The count √ follows from
the preceding theorem. Substitution of ϵ = 8δ/B proves (S9.2); use arccos(1 − x) = 2x + O(x3/2 )
as x ↓ 0. The same perturbation bound is valid at ϵ = 1, where the exact two-state core was given
explicitly. Finally a product with the L-label policy has LK core labels. Its separate initial label
has the initial terminal decoder and, on the first call, the appropriate policy-weighted transition
into that product core. This proves the claimed count without omitting the first physical call. □

   The bound is uniform over a class defined by the target’s expected number of calls. It does
not imply that the approximate experiment has expected cost at most B: total variation controls
bounded losses, whereas an unbounded stopping time requires a separate uniform integrability
or tail hypothesis. In particular the possible nontermination mass of the approximate process is
bounded by ϵB/8 but need not vanish. If a deterministic horizon is part of the policy class, both
processes terminate within that horizon and (S9.4) applies directly. No extra quantum register,
quantum input encoding, precision budget, or external policy memory is included in the stated
simulator label count. If B = 0, the target makes no call almost surely. The same policy then also
makes no call in the simulator, and a single label with the initial terminal decoder suffices with zero
error.

S9.1. Status of the inherited arithmetic consequences. For completeness, the claim after
Theorem S6.3 uses only the two implications already proved in Lemma S6.2. With α = 1 − ϵ,
x = θ/(2π) and
                             Ax (ϵ) = min max{3, q, ∥qx∥/(qϵ)},
                                           q≥1

the lower proof of Theorem S18.1 applies because αeiθ ∈ KM is necessary for a full-instrument M -
state realization. Its upper proof constructs a regular polygon containing αeiθ , so it also constructs
the complete instrument, rather than just its scalar decoder. Therefore
                              1                                   2
                              2 Ax (ϵ) ≤ Nmix (1 − ϵ, θ) ≤ (1 + 4π )Ax (ϵ).
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                          39


The exact +1 pure-start increment does not change logarithmic exponents, divergence, or the
constant-factor cubic-root criterion. All subsequent number-theoretic reasoning is inherited from
Section S18; no new irrationality exponent is assigned to a particular algebraic rotation. In particular
the full Karpelevič region alone has not been shown sufficient for this complete instrument away
from the sectors treated above.

                   S10. Exact synthesis from algebraic observable data
   The preceding normal form concerns existence over the real numbers. We now give a terminating
exact procedure on algebraic input, including a certificate that no finite stochastic realization exists.
The input is a supplied finite-dimensional probability response, rather than a finite sample from an
unknown process. All algebraic numbers are specified by defining polynomials and isolating data,
with a common real number field and its chosen embedding. The algorithms below concern exact
label count and exact controller tables. They make no polynomial running-time or bounded-bit
sampling claim.
   Finite matrix-group recognition is classical, and substantially faster algorithms than the elementary
enumeration below are known [64, 63]. Reduction of probabilistic-automaton minimization to the
existential theory of the reals is also established [65]. Together with the inherited reversible criterion,
these ingredients already yield decidability and bounded-size minimization on the input class
considered here. The new normal form supplies the more structured conclusion: a smallest controller
can be returned as a finite permutation core with a precisely counted initializer and a bounded
multi-output decoder. We give a complete terminating construction, including finite certificates of
the negative answer. Neither finite-group recognition nor real-algebraic automaton minimization is
asserted to be a new algorithm.
S10.1. Observable reduction and a finite stopping bound. Write the supplied representation
in chronological row convention as
                                    b(w) = βTw B,        Tuv = Tu Tv ,                             (S10.1)
where the matrices, row and decoder have entries in a real number field K of degree e over Q. The
displayed representation is linear; its matrices and intermediate coordinates need not be stochastic
or nonnegative. Probability validity means that the supplied b(w) belongs to ∆(C) for every word.
Compact reversibility is imposed on its minimal observable response space, as in Section 3.
   For clarity, minimal reduction itself is finite. Starting with β, close its row span under right
multiplication by each Ta . Starting with the columns of B, close their span under left multiplication
by the Ta . Either iteration stabilizes once its dimension fails to increase, and neither dimension
exceeds the supplied dimension. A reachable row is invisible precisely when it annihilates the
second span. Quotienting the reachable span by this kernel gives the minimal representation, with
dimension d = dim Vb and coefficients still in K. This is the familiar reachable/observable linear
reduction; it does not identify arbitrary hidden stochastic rows with observable states. Since the
response is normalized and nonempty, d ≥ 1.
   Set
                                                                h−1
                                                                Y
                             h = ed,      Bh = | GLh (F3 )| =         (3h − 3j ).                  (S10.2)
                                                                j=0
This deliberately conservative bound has the advantage of a short complete proof. Restriction of
scalars may use a larger field than necessary; doing so changes the computational bound, not the
resulting exact label minimum.
Lemma S10.1 (Classical finite-group bound). A finite subgroup of GLd (K) has order at most Bed .
Consequently a monoid generated by invertible d-dimensional matrices over K is finite if and only
if breadth-first enumeration of its distinct positive-word products closes before producing Bed + 1
elements.
40                                              QIAN QI


Proof. Restriction of scalars embeds the group faithfully in GLed (Q). For a finite subgroup H of
GLh (Q), let Λ = g∈H gZh . There is a common denominator q with Zh ⊆ Λ ⊆ q −1 Zh . Thus Λ is a
                 P

free abelian group of rank h, and it is invariant under every g and g −1 . In a lattice basis, H is a
subgroup of GLh (Z).
   The kernel of reduction modulo 3 has no nonidentity element of finite order. Indeed, such an
element would have a power A of prime order ℓ. Write A = I + 3s Z, with s ≥ 1 and with an integer
matrix Z not zero modulo 3. The binomial expansion of Aℓ = I, divided by 3s , gives ℓZ = 0 modulo
3, so ℓ = 3. The expansion for this prime, divided by 3s+1 , instead gives
                                       Z + 3s Z 2 + 32s−1 Z 3 = 0,
again contradicting Z ̸≡ 0 (mod 3). Reduction is therefore injective on H. Counting independent
ordered columns over F3 proves the bound in (S10.2). This is the standard Minkowski argument
[63]; the proof is included to make the termination certificate explicit.
   A finite monoid of invertible matrices is a group: for every element, two positive powers agree,
and invertibility yields a positive power equal to the identity and another equal to its inverse. A
finite generated monoid therefore has at most Bed elements. In a breadth-first search, process each
newly encountered product and multiply it by all generators. If the queue empties, the finite set
found is closed under all generators and is the entire monoid. If the monoid is infinite, the search
eventually finds Bed + 1 distinct products. These are the two asserted stopping events. All equality
tests are exact tests in K.                                                                       □
Theorem S10.2 (Decidability and minimal algebraic synthesis). For a supplied compactly reversible
probability response with algebraic linear representation (S10.1), there is a terminating algorithm
that decides whether an exact finite stochastic realization exists. If the answer is positive, the
algorithm returns its least number m(b) of live labels and an exact realization with algebraic entries,
a prescribed initial label and a row-stochastic terminal decoder. It can return a permutation core
with at most one transient initializer, as in Theorem S1.1.
   A negative answer has a finite certificate consisting of Bed + 1 distinct observable command
products. A positive answer has an exact controller, the finite observable-group multiplication table,
and finite real-algebraic feasibility or infeasibility certificates for every smaller candidate size. The
negative certificate concerns the promised compactly reversible input class; it is not a nonrealizability
test for arbitrary dissipative responses.
Proof. Perform the observable reduction and apply Lemma S10.1 to the minimal command matrices.
If enumeration exceeds its bound, the positive-word monoid and its compact group closure are
infinite. Theorem 3.1 rules out every finite stochastic realization. The list of distinct products, each
supplied with its generating word, is a finite independently checkable certificate of this conclusion.
   Otherwise enumeration closes to a finite group G. Compute the distinct reachable rows βTg in
the minimal representation; their count n is the residual cardinality. The deterministic residual
machine proves m(b) ≤ n. Theorem S1.2 expresses the answer as a minimum over finite permutation
actions, a probability row λ, and a stochastic decoder D. Only the following finite possibilities
can improve or attain the upper n: a core of size k ≤ n with a point initial row, or a core of size
k ≤ n − 1 with a possibly mixed initial row and one additional initializer.
   For each such k, enumerate assignments of permutations to the elements of G and retain those
satisfying P1 = I and Pg Ph = Pgh . This is a finite search through at most (k!)|G| assignments, and
it may of course be reduced using generators and relations. For a retained action impose
                  X                           X
        λi ≥ 0,         λi = 1,    Dic ≥ 0,        Dic = 1,     λPg D = b(g)    (g ∈ G).         (S10.3)
                    i                          c

For the point case impose λ = eTi and enumerate i. For the other case it is harmless to permit point
rows as well: that candidate then pays a redundant initializer, and the separately enumerated point
case can only improve its cost. Equations (S10.3) have algebraic coefficients and degree at most two
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        41


in their real unknowns. Exact real-algebraic elimination decides feasibility, and a nonempty such set
has an algebraic sample point. One may see the latter directly from a sign-invariant algebraic cell
decomposition: choose isolating algebraic sample coordinates successively in its nonempty sections
and sectors. Algebraic elimination is the classical decision theorem used here [15]; no numerical
optimizer is being substituted for it.
   Search in increasing total label cost and stop at the first feasible candidate. Termination follows
from the deterministic upper. Theorem S1.2 proves that no stochastic competitor outside the
enumerated normal forms can be smaller. For a point initial row use the permutation matrices
directly. For a mixed initial row add the counted initial state, give it decoder b(∅) = λD, and on
command a send it to λPga in the core. All later transitions are permutations. Every returned entry
is algebraic, and all response identities follow from the group action and (S10.3).
   The finite word criterion of Theorem S11.1 supplies an alternative bounded-size formulation
using arbitrary stochastic matrices, independently of the normal form. This is useful for checking
a returned implementation against the original linear representation. Its existence does not give
an efficient algorithm for minimizing probabilistic automata, a problem with established hardness
results [65].                                                                                       □

Corollary S10.3 (Algebraic prepared unitary experiments). Let the prepared density matrix, a
finite family of unitary commands and a finite terminal positive-operator-valued measurement have
algebraic real and imaginary entries. Whether their Born probabilities for all command words admit
an exact finite classical stochastic realization is decidable. In the positive case a smallest exact
classical realization and its bounded decoder can be synthesized with algebraic entries. For a protected
Hilbert space of dimension dphys , the observable linear dimension used in the algorithm is at most
d2phys .

Proof. Hermitian matrices have a real coordinate basis of size d2phys consisting of diagonal matrix
units and real and imaginary symmetric matrix-unit pairs. In this basis conjugation by each supplied
unitary, preparation, and terminal evaluation have real algebraic coefficients. This constructs (S10.1).
Positivity and normalization of Born probabilities follow from the declared density matrix and
measurement, not from a finite test. The observable reduction is finite linear algebra over a common
real number field. Corollary 3.2 proves compact reversibility of this quotient, so Theorem S10.2
applies.                                                                                             □

  This corollary allows noncommuting commands, nonfaithful physical actions, mixed preparations
and arbitrary finite terminal measurements. In particular, a physically infinite group with constant
observable response is correctly reduced to the one-label case before group enumeration. The result
concerns exact simulation of the supplied experiment. Choosing an optimal feedback experiment,
deciding validity of an arbitrary algebraic series, approximate program size, exact sampling and
workspace lower bounds are different questions. The complete retained causal and boundary theory
keeps its own quantifiers and resource accounts.



                     S11. Finite certificates and stable exact memory
   The normal form gives exact realizations at reversible responses. We next prove that their
memory obstructions admit finite tests and persist under a specified class of physical perturbations.
Finite-word equivalence for linear machines is classical [22]; the finite bound below is its direct
difference-space argument. The additional conclusions concern the positive separation from all
smaller stochastic machines, its effective algebraic certificate, and a matching realization under
damping toward a common background response.
42                                                   QIAN QI

supplied real linear representation
                      b(w) = βTw B,          β ∈ R1×d ,      Ta ∈ Rd×d ,       B ∈ Rd×|C| .

Theorem S11.1 (Finite obstruction and effective separation). An M -label stochastic machine
realizes b on every word if and only if it does so on the words of length at most d+M −1. Consequently
δM (b) > 0 if and only if no exact M -label realization exists. If another supplied response eb satisfies
                                        max        |eb(w, c) − b(w, c)| ≤ η,
                                      |w|≤L, c∈C

then every M -label machine has error at least δM (b) − η on these same probes. This last assertion
imposes no finite-rank hypothesis on eb.
   If the given entries of β, Ta , B are real algebraic numbers, δM (b) is an effectively computable real
algebraic number, and a minimizing machine with real algebraic entries can be computed. When it
is positive, a positive rational number strictly below it and a real-algebraic quantifier-elimination
certificate of that strict lower bound can be computed. No polynomial-time or practical-size bound is
asserted.
Proof. For a fixed candidate machine use the block-diagonal matrices Sa = diag(Ta , Qa ), the row
u = (β, −eT                                             T
            1 ), and the output columns vc = (Bc , Dc ) . The difference on w is uSw vc . Let Rj be the
span of uSw for |w| ≤ j. These spaces increase; if Rj = Rj+1 , the common space is invariant under
every Sa and all later spaces coincide. Since u ̸= 0, the sequence starts in dimension one inside a
space of dimension d + M and must have stabilized by j = d + M − 1. Vanishing on the indicated
finite set therefore implies vanishing on all words. This is the same linear-equivalence principle as
the inherited finite-realization test.
   The maximum in (S11.1) is a continuous function on the compact set PM , so its minimum is
attained. It is zero precisely when one machine passes every finite probe, hence, by the first assertion,
realizes every word. The perturbation claim follows pointwise from the triangle inequality and then
from the minimum defining δM (b).
   For algebraic input, every probe value is algebraic. The constraints defining row-stochasticity
and the inequalities between the finitely many polynomial errors and a variable t are polynomial
constraints over the real algebraic numbers. Real quantifier elimination decides their feasibility
and computes the least feasible t together with an algebraic witness. A singleton semialgebraic set
over the real algebraic numbers consists of an algebraic number. If δM (b) > 0, effective isolation
supplies a positive rational σ < δM (b); infeasibility with all errors at most σ is a finite real-algebraic
certificate. This uses the classical decision procedure [15]; it does not assert that this procedure was
executed by the finite diagnostics accompanying this article.                                            □
   For the prepared quantum response, these finite probes have an explicit physical perturbation
bound. Suppose two density matrices differ in trace norm by at most hρ , corresponding channels
differ in diamond norm by at most hΦ , and corresponding terminal effects differ in operator norm by
at most hE . All channels are trace preserving and completely positive, and both lists of effects are
valid measurements. Contractivity and telescoping give ∥Φe w (ρe) − Φw (ρ)∥1 ≤ hρ + |w|hΦ . The state
difference has trace zero; subtracting I/2 from a terminal effect, whose spectrum lies in [0, 1], gives
                             max |eb(w, c) − b(w, c)| ≤ hE + 12 (hρ + LhΦ ).                       (S11.2)

Thus a strict smaller-memory obstruction survives a specified open neighbourhood of physical
primitives. This supplies a lower bound there; it does not assert that an arbitrary perturbation has
a realization of the same size.

For algebraic input satisfying the hypotheses, a positive algebraic cutoff, or a smaller positive

Proof. Take a minimal realization in the normal form proved above. On its recurrent labels each

                                            πQa = π,        πD = b.
Indeed, the group average is invariant under each permutation. The initializer has no incoming
transition, and its first-input row is the original initial distribution followed by the commanded

this is smaller than δm−1 (b). Machines with fewer labels can be padded by unreachable rows, so
they too are excluded. This and the upper prove equality. A nonconstant response has m ≥ 2 and
d ≥ 2, hence L > 0. The effective assertion follows from the finite group enumeration and algebraic
synthesis theorem, together with Theorem S11.1. Finite maxima and averages over the computed
group preserve algebraicity.                                                                        □
  There is a physical realization of the common background for prepared unitary commands.
Let K be the compact closure of their physical conjugation group. The compact convex hull of
{Φ(ρ) : Φ ∈ K} has a unique element ρ∗ of least Hilbert–Schmidt norm: strict convexity gives
uniqueness. Every conjugation in K preserves that set and norm, so ρ∗ is invariant. Its observable
image belongs to the convex hull of the finite orbit of b and is invariant. Averaging any element of
44                                                 QIAN QI


that orbit over G gives b; applying this averaging projection to an invariant element in its convex
hull shows that its observable image is exactly b. Therefore the physical channels

produce (S11.3) under every command word. In general ρ∗ need not be maximally mixed. The
theorem concerns damping toward this common invariant background, not every depolarizing or
noisy channel.

  The certificate uses a stochastic-matrix inequality rather than a sample search over three-label

constructed all-word damping identities on explicit finite models, and exact finite recurrence
consequences. They do not replace the proofs, compute all algebraic gaps, or give an independent
human or editorial assessment.

                   S12. Relation to the retained exact resource laws
   The finite-group normal form has a cyclic specialization, but it does not replace the independent
spectral and arithmetic arguments for responses outside the reversible class. We record the precise
relation.
   For the probability-valid periodic mixture of Theorem S17.2, with distinct nontrivial frequency
orders q1 , . . . , qr , let                X
                                   K = min       lcm{qj : j ∈ B},
                                            P
                                                B∈P
where P ranges over partitions of the frequency indices. The exact pure-start count is the minimum
of the full period lcm(q1 , . . . , qr ) and K + 1, with the constant and single-frequency endpoints
as stated in the retained theorem. A cyclic permutation representation is a disjoint union of
cycles; a frequency of order q occurs on a cycle precisely when q divides its length. Assigning
required modes to cycles therefore gives the same partition expression. The bounded decoders and
positive-amplitude preparation in the retained theorem attain that lower bound. This agreement
identifies a model-specific closed form inside the general factorization problem. Arbitrary bounded
decoders do not have this same closed formula, as both the sharp S3 and the five-character contrast
transitions show. The quotient theorem applies to arbitrary finite abelian tables; it does not assume
the positive-amplitude mixture of the periodic closed form.
   The leakage theorem, Theorem S17.5, adds its separately proved inactive live label. Its lower
bound uses a decay-normalized observable core and the no-critical-chain argument; the common-
background plateau of Section S11 is a different perturbation,
                                                        √         as is terminal readout contrast. The
exact four-period damping threshold was already 1/ 3 by Theorem S19.3; the current supplement
makes this inherited closure explicit. The exact spectral construction of Theorem S19.1, the relative-
spectrum obstruction and the arbitrary-phase envelope of Theorem S18.1 remain proved with their
own hypotheses. In particular a strictly damped irrational mode need not have compactly reversible
observable dynamics.
   The following actual-history transfer theorem remains a general statement about causal experiment
comparison. It compares complete procedures, charges the common paid acquisition construction,
and forms its lower witness on actual competing histories. None of its hypotheses is inferred from
a finite observable group. The complete paired supplement retains the associated performed-law
minimax and dual theory, finite-width transfer, reset control, optimal discrimination, predictive
quotients and geometric results. The original ordered-measurement and two-stage-reset realizations
remain independently identifiable by source commit and path.

               S13. Joint contraction and bounded positive realizations
   The reversible classification has a complementary dissipative regime. The condition below is
imposed on all command products through one supplied norm, and the construction gives one
machine for all words. A separate realization for each command would not establish that conclusion.
Invariant-polytope positive realization is classical [48, 47]; the point of the statement is an explicit
sufficient certificate with bounded probability decoders, a prescribed pure start, and a stated number
of live labels.

with all ℓc and adding p gives the entire response. This proves the count, including the pure initial
label and every first command.                                                                       □
  The assumption may also be supplied as a uniform product bound. If a known norm ∥ · ∥0 and

The actual net and barycentric rows provide finite implementation data; their exact algebraic or
sampling encodings remain distinct resources.

         S14. Average mixing does not control conditional response memory
   Positive realization via a common invariant polytope, including composition of adaptive instru-
ments, is established machinery [47, 71]. A hypothesis forcing every normalized branch into an
interior region is stronger than fast convergence of the channel obtained by ignoring its outcomes.
The following exact example separates these statements even when every outcome has a probability
bounded away from zero. The infinite-memory conclusion is a response-level consequence of the
inherited relative spectral obstruction; it is not a claim that ordinary mixing theory has failed.
   The phase obstruction itself has classical predecessors. The FRDN processes discussed in [50,
Section 2.3] already exhibit irrational-phase obstructions to finite classical realization and phase-
dependent finite minima in commensurate cases. Our assertion concerns the simultaneous qubit,
rank-three, uniform-positivity and exact depolarization properties below, and the sharp additional
charge for a counted pure start; it does not claim priority for the phase mechanism.
   Fix sin θ ̸= 0. For a qubit put
        cos(θ/2) − sin(θ/2)

Started at I/2, its stationary outcome process is one-dependent: two finite blocks separated by at
least one unobserved outcome are independent. Its word-probability Hankel matrix has rank exactly
three. Write z = eiθ . If z has finite order q, the least classical hidden-state count reproducing every
word probability is exactly q when a mixed initial row is free, and exactly q + 1 when the initial label
must be pure and counted. If z is not a root of unity, no finite classical model exists, even with a
freely mixed start and arbitrary real stochastic entries. All primitive superoperators are algebraic
whenever cos θ and sin θ are algebraic.

All reachable subnormalized states are real symmetric matrices, a space of real dimension three, so

  Suppose a finite classical model exists. Write its nonnegative outcome matrices as T0 , T1 , T2 , with

                              un = vT0n d,     v = µT1 ≥ 0,        d = T1 1 ≥ 0.
Here T0 is row-substochastic. Delete states unreachable from the support of v and states from which
no positive terminal value of d is reachable. The remaining principal submatrix S still realizes un .
For every remaining i, j, choose a positive path from v to i, of length ai and weight pi > 0, and a
path from j to a positive terminal value, of length bj and weight rj > 0. Nonnegativity gives
                                    pi rj (S n )ij ≤ uai +n+bj ≤ Cij 8−n .
Thus (8S)n is entrywise bounded, and every eigenvalue of S has modulus at most 1/8.
   The three exponential modes of (S14.2) are 1/8, z/8, and z/8, all distinct and with nonzero
coefficients. Cayley–Hamilton and the Vandermonde independence of these sequences force z/8 to
be an eigenvalue of S. In an upper block triangular decomposition of S, it belongs to an irreducible
nonnegative diagonal block B. Its spectral radius is exactly 1/8. Perron normalization by a strictly
positive eigenvector therefore makes 8B similar to a stochastic matrix with eigenvalue z. This is the
elementary normalization proved before Proposition S20.2. At a largest-modulus coordinate of a
corresponding complex eigenvector, equality in the triangle inequality forces each positive successor
to have the same modulus and value multiplied by z. Irreducibility propagates this equality to every
coordinate, so the relation holds on every positive edge and every directed cycle. Following positive
successors reaches a directed cycle; its length l ≥ 1 gives z l = 1. This identity proves that z must
be a root of unity. If its order is q, every such directed cycle has length divisible by q, so every
realization has at least q labels.
   For the matching upper, suppose z has order q ≥ 3. In the real Bloch plane let P be the regular
q-gon with vertices
                             rj = (cos(2πj/q), sin(2πj/q)),      0 ≤ j < q,
and let σj be the corresponding pure qubit states. The polygon contains the disk of radius
cos(π/q) ≥ 1/2. The normalized branch 0 permutes its vertices and branch 1 resets to σ0 = τ . For
an arbitrary state with Bloch vector r in the unit disk, write h = tr Ψ1 (σ) ∈ [1/16, 3/16]. The
normalized Bloch vector after branch 2 is

above supplies a directed q-cycle using every state. An additional positive edge would close a
directed cycle of length less than q, which is impossible. Thus, before normalization, T0 is a weighted
directed q-cycle. Its successive row weights are positive. From the prescribed starting point,

                                      p(0n ) = 8−n      (n ≥ 0).

For n = 1, . . . , q, the left side is the product of the first n row weights encountered on this cycle.

  In particular, the fully algebraic choice

                                           2 −1
                                                
                                    −1/2
                             Uθ = 5             ,        eiθ = (3 + 4i)/5,
                                           1 2

has no finite classical realization. Its superoperator coefficients are rational. The phase is not a
root of unity, since otherwise z + z −1 = 6/5 would be a rational algebraic integer and hence an
integer. Every finite-order choice, on the other hand, gives the exact counts q and q + 1 in a fixed
physical qubit with the same uniform outcome probability bounds. The size of an exact hidden-state
realization cannot be inferred from predictive rank, unconditional mixing time, or finite dependence
range.
   Stationary one-dependent processes without a finite hidden Markov model were established
earlier, notably by the finitely dependent colorings of Holroyd and Liggett [74]. We make no
first-existence claim for that phenomenon. Here the simultaneous properties are an explicit qubit
instrument, rank-three linear prediction, uniformly positive outcome probabilities, and an exactly
depolarizing unconditioned channel, together with a sharp finite-order memory law and a separate
pure-initialization charge.
   This example satisfies a stronger average-mixing property than exponential convergence: one
unobserved step erases all prior state information. The infinite obstruction survives in a specified
observed word, whose conditional maps retain an irrational phase. It therefore does not contradict
a finite-realization theorem requiring uniform mixing of each normalized branch. It also explains
why positive single-outcome probabilities cannot replace that hypothesis. The example supplies a
precise boundary for the constructive theorem.

                       S15. An inherited sharp four-period transition
   The four-period certificate in Proposition S11.3 provides an explicit finite-probe separation. Its
sufficient interval was not the sharp exact-memory interval. The latter is already a consequence of
the inherited first-sector theorem, and is recorded here to correct the research-status distinction
rather than as a new spectral result.

                S16. The recurrent simplex and the cyclic specialization
Lemma S16.1 (A lazy finite-chain projection). Let T be a finite row-stochastic matrix with
strictly positive diagonal. Let C1 , . . . , Ck be its closed communicating classes. There is a stochastic
idempotent E = limn→∞ T n whose image on the probability simplex is the simplex spanned by the
stationary rows µi supported on the respective Ci . If a row-stochastic matrix Q preserves every Ci ,
then EQE = E. If T is a convex combination of matrices from a multiplicative semigroup S that
contains the identity, then E belongs to the closed convex hull of S.
Proof. Consider first one closed class C. Irreducibility and the positive diagonal imply that some
power K of the restricted matrix has every entry positive: choose a path between each ordered
pair, and pad the finitely many path lengths to their maximum using diagonal self-loops. Put
η = mini,j Kij > 0 and nC = |C|. Then
                                      K = nC ηJ + (1 − nC η)K0 ,
where all rows of J are uniform and K0 is row-stochastic, with the second term omitted if nC η = 1.
For probability rows p, q,
                                ∥pK − qK∥1 ≤ (1 − nC η)∥p − q∥1 .
A stationary row for T |C exists by taking an accumulation point of the Cesàro averages n−1 n−1     j
                                                                                                P

in the compact simplex: their difference from their right translates by T tends to zero. Call it µC .
The contraction shows it is unique and that all rows converge geometrically to µC along the chosen
power. The remaining finitely many residues of the exponent converge to the same row because
µC T = µC . This proves convergence on each closed class.
   Let H be the complement of their union. If H is empty, the preceding closed-class argument
already gives the asserted convergence. Otherwise, every vertex in H has a directed path to a closed
class. Since the graph is finite, there are an integer q and a number ζ > 0 such that, starting from
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         51


any vertex in H, the probability of leaving H by time q is at least ζ. Paths can again be padded
using the positive diagonal. In a permutation of coordinates, write

   If Q preserves Ci , the row µi Q is a probability law on Ci , so (µi Q)E = µi . Extend over the convex
combinations constituting the rows of E to obtain EQE = E. This does not require µi Q = µi .

gives the last assertion.                                                                              □
S16.1. Cyclic modules and the retained positive-amplitude model. The next statement

Proof. A transitive CQ -set is a cycle of length ℓ dividing Q. Its real permutation module contains

and diagonalize cyclic translation. Given any collection of cycles whose module contains V , assign

is at least Ψ(P) for the induced partition. Conversely, cycles of the lengths in any Ψ(P) contain

                 S17. Exact periodic memory, initialization and leakage
   At zero internal damping a finite mixture of rational frequencies is periodic. Its period, linear
response rank and least stochastic memory are different quantities. We determine the last one
exactly, with a prescribed initial label, and give a matching sparse implementation. This completes
the rational multi-frequency endpoint left as bounds by Corollary S18.5. The spectral cycle method is
classical Perron–Frobenius theory; periodic stochastic orbits and least-common-multiple constraints
are established subjects [59, 54]. We prove the precise partition and initialization rule needed for
the present bounded probability response rather than claim those ingredients as new.
   Choose distinct nonreal roots    ζj = e2πiaj /qj , 1 ≤ j ≤ r, no two conjugate, with gcd(aj , qj ) = 1
and qj ≥ 3. Let wj > 0, Γ = j wj ≤ 1, and φj ∈ R. The target is

All listed complex modes have nonzero coefficients. For a nonempty index set B, set ℓ(B) = lcm{qj :
j ∈ B}. If P is a partition of {1, . . . , r}, define
                                   X

Equal orders are allowed when their nonconjugate primitive modes differ. They impose compatible
divisibility constraints, not distinct mandatory recurrent classes.
Lemma S17.1 (Unit-circle modes of a reachable stochastic matrix). Every nonzero coefficient mode
ζ in a finite exponential response eT    n
                                      1 Q d is an eigenvalue of the restriction of Q to its reachable
states. If |ζ| = 1, it occurs in a reachable closed irreducible class. If that class contains primitive
root modes of orders in a set J, its number of states is at least lcm J.
Proof. Cayley–Hamilton gives a characteristic recurrence for the response. Distinct exponential
sequences are linearly independent by the Vandermonde determinant. Substituting the recurrence
therefore forces its characteristic polynomial to vanish at each mode with nonzero coefficient.
   Order the strongly connected components of the reachable transition graph to make Q block
triangular. An eigenvalue belongs to an irreducible diagonal block B, whose row sums are at most
one. For Bv = ζv, choose a coordinate with maximal nonzero modulus. If |ζ| = 1, equality in the
row-sum and triangle inequalities forces that row sum to be one and every positive successor to
have the same modulus, with value ζ times the preceding value. Irreducibility propagates this to
every coordinate and every row. Thus the block is closed in Q. Along each directed cycle of length
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         53


h, one has ζ h = 1. Applying this to every required mode in the block shows that every directed
cycle length is a multiple of their least common multiple. A simple directed cycle exists and has
length no greater than the number of states, proving the lower. This argument allows reducible Q
and arbitrary real stochastic entries.                                                         □
Theorem S17.2 (Exact partition law for periodic stochastic memory). The least exact stochastic
live-label count for (S17.1), with prescribed initial label and decoder in [0, 1], is exactly M∗ . It is
independent of the nonzero weights and preparation phases within the displayed probability-valid
family.
   The least count when transitions must be deterministic but the terminal decoder may still be
randomized is
                                        P = lcm(q1 , . . . , qr ).
                      q ≥ 3 with r ≥ 2, the stochastic count is 1 + j qj , whereas the deterministic-

                   Q j
transition count is j qj . An invariant finite residual orbit therefore need not have minimal stochastic
cardinality.
Proof. Consider an exact realizing machine after removing unreachable labels. Lemma S17.1 assigns
every required root to a reachable closed irreducible class. Assign each index to one such class and
omit classes
        P
              assigned no index. These assignments partition the indices, and their class sizes total
at least B∈P ℓ(B). If at least two assigned closed classes are reachable, the prescribed initial state
cannot belong to any one of them: a closed class cannot reach another. At least one additional
transient initial label is therefore necessary. This proves the lower M∗ . It would be incorrect to
omit this label by allowing a free mixed initial distribution.
   For the upper fix a partition. With one block, a deterministic cycle of length P and decoder
dk = bk is sufficient. With g ≥P
                                 2 blocks, take disjoint deterministic cycles of lengths ℓ(B) and one
new initial label. Put WB = j∈B wj , λB = WB /Γ, and on phase k of block B set
                                                                           
                                            Γ X
                           dB,k = 12 1 +            wj cos(2πkaj /qj + φj ) .

The total absolute oscillatory amplitude is at most Γ ≤ 1, so all decoder values lie in [0, 1]. Give
the initial label decoder b0 . Its first transition selects block B with probability λB and enters
phase oneP  modulo ℓ(B). All later transitions simply advance that cycle. Thus at time n ≥ 1 the
output is B λB dB,n = bn , and equality at zero holds separately. There is no one-step phase error
or uncounted block-selection state. This constructs exactly (S17.2) labels; minimizing proves the
stochastic formula.
   The exact period of b is P . Indeed any period annihilates every nonzero exponential mode under
shift minus identity, hence is divisible by each qj . A deterministic finite machine has an eventual
cycle. Its length must be an eventual period of b, and for a sequence already periodic from zero
an eventual period is also a period from zero: shift any given identity far enough by multiples of
P . That cycle length is therefore at least P . The P -cycle gives the matching upper. Finally, for
pairwise coprime orders at least three, merging two or more singleton blocks replaces their sum by
their product, strictly increasing cost even if a single initialization label is saved. Repeated splitting
proves the stated special formula.                                                                      □

   For example, orders 3, 5, 7 give exact stochastic memory 16 and deterministic-transition memory
105. Orders 4, 6 give min{12, 4 + 6 + 1} = 11. Divisibility alone, e.g. orders 3, 6, gives 6 rather than
10. These contrasting cases are covered by the same all-real-stochastic lower, not a search restricted
to proposed cycles. If a listed coefficient vanishes or conjugate modes cancel, one must first remove
that mode; the formula is not continuous across deletion of spectral support.

Corollary S17.3 (Constructive minimal synthesis and a zero-noise plateau). The optimum partition
is computable by at most O(3P   r ) subset comparisons and least-common-multiple calculations on

integers of bit length at most j ⌈log2 qj ⌉. After its partition is found, a minimal transition table
has only deterministic cycle rows and at most one randomized initialization row. Producing all
decoder entries requires at most O(rM∗ ) evaluations and arithmetic operations in their specified
exact number representation. This is an output-sensitive count, not a uniform polynomial bit bound
for arbitrary real phases or exact sampling.
   Let mα be the least count for the internally damped response
                                                                  
                                       X
                                n
                     b(α) 1
                      n = 2 1+α             wj cos(2πnaj /qj + φj ) ,   0 < α ≤ 1.
                                        j

Then mα ≤ M∗ for every α, and mα = M∗ for all α sufficiently close to one. This is an exact
multi-frequency endpoint plateau, not merely a limiting lower bound for each separate pole.
Proof. For a subset S put D(∅) = 0 and D(S) = min∅̸=B⊆S {ℓ(B) + D(S \ B)}. Compute these in
increasing subset size, using a canonical distinguished index in B to remove duplicate partitions
if desired. The total number of subset pairs is at most 3r . For r ≥ 2 compare ℓ({1, . . . , r}) with
1 + min∅̸=B⊊{1,...,r} [ℓ(B) + D(B c )]. This separately charges the one-block and multiblock cases.
The construction in the theorem proves the output counts. Real-algebraic weights and eiφj give
algebraic controller entries, since the rational-angle roots are algebraic; arbitrary real phases retain
their separate description requirement.
   In that construction every cycle has mean decoder 1/2, because each nonconstant root averages
to zero around its cycle. Choose a stationary row π uniform on any one cycle, with zero mass at
the transient initial label when present. Then πd = 1/2. Replacing Q by Qα = αQ + (1 − α)1π
preserves stochasticity on the same labels and gives
                                     Qnα = αn Qn + (1 − αn )1π.
This is the damped response, proving the upper. If sizes below M∗ occurred along α ↑ 1, pad them
to M∗ − 1 labels and take a convergent subsequence of their compact stochastic and decoder rows.
Every fixed response coefficient passes to the limit, producing an undamped realization smaller than
M∗ . This contradicts the theorem and proves the plateau. No numerical cutoff for its first plateau
point is claimed.                                                                                  □

S17.1. The exact leakage increment for the entire periodic mixture. The preceding count
also survives decay-normalization. This requires more than applying the single-pole bound to
each frequency separately, since those bounds would lose both the recurrent-class partition and its
initialization charge.
Lemma S17.4 (No chains of critical classes in a power-bounded core). Let A ≥ 0 have bounded
powers. No directed path connects two distinct irreducible diagonal blocks of spectral radius one.
Proof. Suppose such blocks B, C are connected. Choose a positive-weight path from a vertex of B
to a vertex of C whose interior avoids both blocks, and let W be the matrix with that path weight
in the corresponding entry and zero elsewhere. Let its length be h. Perron vectors give a strictly
positive left row uB = u and a strictly positive column Cv = v. For each 0 ≤ i, j ≤ n, concatenate
a length-i path inside B, the fixed connecting path, and a length-j path inside C. Their first exit
from B distinguishes different i, so they are distinct contributions to the powers. Therefore the B, C
block of 2n+h
          P        k            Pn        i    j                                            2
            k=0 A dominates        i,j=0 B W C . Pairing with u, v gives the lower (n + 1) uW v > 0.
Bounded powers give an upper growing only linearly in n, a contradiction. The elementary Perron
normalization used here is proved in Section S20.                                                    □
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        55


Theorem S17.5 (Periodic-mixture leakage law). For every fixed 0 < λ < 1, the least stochastic live
memory of un = λn bn , with b as in (S17.1), is exactly
                                           s(u) = M∗ + 1.
The extra label is live until the actual commitment event. In the fixed squared-task embedding, these
are exact optimal-observer counts at the original least cost 1/p, with event-stopped and state-stopped
total-label upper counts max{M∗ + 1, 2} and M∗ + 3.
Proof. The construction of Lemma S20.1 applied to a minimal realization of b gives the upper
M∗ + 1. For the reverse, take an arbitrary stochastic realization of u with M reachable labels.
Its output-visible core S has at most M − 1 labels, includes the prescribed start, and realizes u.
The path bound (S20.2), together with 0 ≤ un ≤ λn , shows entrywise that (S/λ)n is bounded.
Its required modes are the distinct roots 1, ζj , ζ j after scaling by λ, each with nonzero coefficient.
Every required nonreal root lies in an irreducible block of radius one. Perron scaling of that block
makes it stochastic without changing its graph or eigenvalues. Lemma S17.1 thus imposes the same
least-common-multiple size for every group of assigned modes.
   By Lemma S17.4, distinct assigned critical blocks cannot reach one another. If at least two are
reachable from the prescribed initial label, that label lies outside all of them and adds a further
core state. Hence the core has at least B ℓ(B) + 1{|P|≥2} ≥ M∗ labels. Including the necessarily
                                        P

inactive label gives M ≥ M∗ + 1. The argument does not impose a nonexistent globally positive
Perron vector on a reducible core. The squared-task and stopping interpretation follows from the
unchanged physical embedding and established role packing, not from interpreting a leakage event
as an observed halt.                                                                                 □

   A protected target for (S17.1) is obtained by the block-qubit preparation and terminal effect
of Theorem S19.8; leakage appends its actual dark physical state. These are sufficient physical
realizations, not minimal physical-dimension claims. The standard square-loss flag embedding is
distinct from a probability decoder supplied as an oracle. For a prescribed smaller observer size,
the finite rational-resolvent tests of Theorem S20.6 can also witness the new exact counts, using
response order 2r + 1. Their number of models grows with the tested memory. The partition law
does not solve the different general-M squared-sign risk curve, nor declare arbitrary controlled
boundary stopping classified.

            S18. An arithmetic envelope for exact memory at every phase
   The spectral criterion of Theorem S19.1 determines exact stochastic memory by membership
in the classical stochastic eigenvalue regions. A worst-phase bound and a matching estimate at a
badly approximable fixed phase do not describe all fixed phases. In particular, excellent rational
approximations can produce both unusually small memory and long intervening ranges of larger
memory. We determine a single arithmetic envelope that controls the entire memory function, and
derive its exact lower and upper logarithmic growth exponents for every irrational phase. The
arithmetic is classical continued-fraction theory; the conclusions here concern its conversion into
the least exact stochastic observer memory. We retain the distinction from the noise-dependent
classical process bounds in [50] and from the classical irrationality-exponent identity in [58].
   Throughout this section, 0 < ϵ < 1, α = 1 − ϵ, and
                  bn = 21 [1 + γαn cos(2πnx + φ)],      0 < γ ≤ 1,    sin(2πx) ̸= 0.
The phase and nonzero contrast describe one prepared target, not information revealed to its
observer. Write mx (ϵ) = m(1 − ϵ, 2πx) for its least live-label count, with a prescribed initial label,
stochastic transitions and a decoder in [0, 1]. Theorem S19.1 makes this independent of γ, φ. The
actual squared-loss embedding of Theorem S24.1 forces exactly this probability response at the
56                                                QIAN QI


least physical cost 1/p. Thus the conclusions below are about exact optimal implementation, not
approximation of the target sequence or a new definition of loss.
   For a positive integer q, put
                          ∥qx∥R/Z
               ex (q) =           = min |x − a/q|,        Ax (ϵ) = min max{3, q, ex (q)/ϵ}.
                             q      a∈Z                             q≥1

The minimum is attained: its summand is at least q, while q = 1 gives a finite upper. No global
Diophantine lower hypothesis is imposed.
Theorem S18.1 (Universal arithmetic envelope). For every nonreal phase and every 0 < ϵ < 1,
                             1
                             2 Ax (ϵ) ≤ mx (ϵ) ≤ C0 Ax (ϵ),     C0 = 1 + 4π 2 .                 (S18.1)
The constants are independent of the phase, contrast, preparation phase and damping. Each upper
bound comes from an explicit stochastic circulant realization with a prescribed initial label and
bounded decoder. The lower holds over all stochastic matrices and decoders, not only those circulants.
The estimate is a constant-factor law for the full arithmetic envelope; it is not an assertion of an
exact leading constant or a new classification of the stochastic spectral regions.
Proof. We use the polygon construction and the power-sector inequality in Lemma S19.4, whose
positive-matrix proofs are included in Section S19. Let M = mx (ϵ) ≥ 3. Among the 2M + 1
fractional parts of 0, x, . . . , 2M x, two lie in the same one of 2M intervals of length 1/(2M ). Their
difference supplies 1 ≤ k ≤ 2M with ∥kx∥ ≤ 1/(2M ). If this norm is zero, using q = k gives
Ax (ϵ) ≤ 2M directly.
   Otherwise put δ = 2π∥kx∥ and h = π/M . Then 0 < δ ≤ h ≤ π/3. For F = cos δ + sin δ tan h,
one has F ≤ sec h ≤ 2 and
                                          F − 1 ≥ 34 hδ − 12 δ 2 ≥ hδ/4.
Here sin δ ≥ 3δ/4, tan h ≥ h and 1 − cos δ ≤ δ 2 /2 on the stated interval. The eigenvalue of the
stochastic power Qk gives (1 − ϵ)k F ≤ 1. Bernoulli’s inequality therefore yields
                                  kϵ ≥ 1 − (1 − ϵ)k ≥ 1 − 1/F ≥ hδ/8.
Consequently ex (k)/ϵ = δ/(2πkϵ) ≤ 4M/π 2 . Since also k ≤ 2M , the envelope is at most 2M . This
proves the lower.
  For the upper fix any denominator q, choose its closest integer numerator a, and choose the
smallest positive multiple l of q satisfying
                                        l ≥ 3,     l ≥ 4π 2 ex (q)/ϵ.
Then l < q + max{3, 4π 2 ex (q)/ϵ} ≤ C0 max{3, q, ex (q)/ϵ}. The rational angle 2πa/q is an lth-root
angle. Thus the distance δl from 2πx to the nearest such root is at most 2πex (q). The directional
polygon bound gives
                              1 − Rl (2πx) ≤ 2(π/l)δl ≤ 4π 2 ex (q)/l ≤ ϵ.
Hence αe2πix lies in the regular l-gon. The positive combination of its two adjacent roots and zero
gives the stochastic matrix described in Lemma S19.4; its normalized eigenvector gives the bounded
decoder from Theorem S19.1. The original initial label suffices. Minimize over q to conclude. All
constructions act on the observer’s private memory and do not add a physical observation or a
call.                                                                                            □
   For irrational x, Ax (ϵ) → ∞ as ϵ ↓ 0: a bounded envelope would restrict q to finitely many
integers whose errors have a positive minimum. For a rational nonreal phase of reduced denominator
q, the exact memory is eventually q as ϵ ↓ 0. Indeed αCq + (1 − α)J gives the upper q, while a
bounded sequence of smaller stochastic realizations would converge, by compactness, to one with
the forbidden unit eigenvalue of order q. This rational boundary is different from assigning an
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                            57


irrationality exponent to a rational number. Zero contrast and real phases remain governed by their
separately stated degeneracies.

S18.1. The arithmetic input, with proof. For irrational x, changing it by an integer does not
change any quantity above. Write its fractional part as [0; a1 , a2 , . . .], with convergents pn /qn . We use
the standard initial values p−1 = 1, p0 = 0, q−1 = 0, q0 = 1 and the recursion pn+1 = an+1 pn + pn−1 ,
qn+1 = an+1 qn + qn−1 . The possible initial repeated denominator is irrelevant below; all asymptotic
statements use the eventually strictly increasing sequence.
Lemma S18.2 (Convergent errors and the irrationality exponent). Set En = qn x − pn , with x
reduced modulo one. Then
                   1                 1
                         < |En | <      ,        |qx − a| ≥ |En |       (0 < q < qn+1 , a ∈ Z).      (S18.2)
               qn+1 + qn           qn+1
For all sufficiently large n, ex (qn ) = |En |/qn . Define
                          µ(x) = sup{t : ex (q) < q −t for infinitely many q}.
Then
                                                         log qn+1
                                  µ(x) = 1 + lim sup              ∈ [2, ∞].                          (S18.3)
                                                n→∞       log qn
Moreover x is badly approximable, in the sense that ex (q) ≥ c/q 2 for some c > 0 and all q, if and
only if the ratios qn+1 /qn are bounded.
Proof. The recursions give pn qn−1 − pn−1 qn = (−1)n−1 by induction. If tn+1 = [an+1 ; an+2 , . . .],
repeated substitution in the continued fraction gives
                       pn tn+1 + pn−1                       1
                  x=                  ,    |En | =                  ,    En−1 = −tn+1 En .
                       qn tn+1 + qn−1                qn tn+1 + qn−1
Since an+1 < tn+1 < an+1 + 1, the denominator lies strictly between qn+1 and qn+1 + qn , proving
the first pair of bounds. These errors eventually have modulus below 1/2, so the numerator is the
nearest integer.
   For the second assertion, suppose |qx − a| < |En |. The determinant identity makes the two
consecutive convergent vectors an integral basis. Write (a, q) = A(pn , qn ) + B(pn−1 , qn−1 ) with
integers A, B. Then |A − Btn+1 | < 1. If B = 0, this forces A = 0 and q = 0. If B ≥ 1, it forces
A ≥ Ban+1 and hence q ≥ Bqn+1 ≥ qn+1 . If B = −b ≤ −1, it forces A ≤ −ban+1 and hence
q ≤ −bqn+1 < 0. These exhaust the possibilities and prove the asserted best-error inequality. No
best-approximation assertion for arbitrary fitted rational data has been used.
   Put Λ = lim sup log qn+1 / log qn ≥ 1. The convergent upper ex (qn ) < 1/(qn qn+1 ) shows µ ≥ 1 + Λ,
including the infinite case. If s > Λ is finite, eventually qn+1 ≤ qns . For qn ≤ q < qn+1 , the
second-kind inequality gives
                                                  1           1
                                    ex (q) >               ≥ s+1 .
                                             q(qn+1 + qn )  2q
For every positive η, this excludes infinitely many errors below q −(s+1+η) . Let s ↓ Λ and η ↓ 0 to
prove (S18.3).
   If qn+1 /qn ≤ B eventually, the same interval argument gives ex (q) > 1/[(B + 1)q 2 ] there; reducing
the positive constant handles the finitely many earlier denominators. Conversely ex (qn ) < 1/(qn qn+1 )
is incompatible with one positive lower constant when the ratios are unbounded. This proves the
last equivalence. The identity (S18.3) is classical; it is also stated in the cited arithmetic literature
[58], and is proved here to make the memory argument independent of that reference.                    □
58                                                 QIAN QI


S18.2. The complete logarithmic growth spectrum. The envelope law retains more information
than a single exponent. Its two logarithmic exponents nevertheless have a closed form for every
irrational phase, including phases not known to satisfy a uniform approximation bound.
Theorem S18.3 (Exact lower and upper memory exponents). For every irrational x, the exact
stochastic memory obeys
                                           log mx (ϵ)       1
                                    lim inf           =          ,                             (S18.4)
                                      ϵ↓0   log(1/ϵ)    µ(x) + 1
                                           log mx (ϵ)    µ(x) − 1
                                   lim sup            =            .                           (S18.5)
                                      ϵ↓0   log(1/ϵ)    2µ(x) − 1
For µ(x) = ∞, the right sides mean 0 and 1/2. In particular the logarithmic exponent exists exactly
when µ(x) = 2, and then it equals 1/3. This does not imply a constant-factor cubic-root law for every
such phase. The assertions describe one fixed phase while the noise tends to zero, not a worst-phase
choice depending on the noise.
Proof. By Theorem S18.1, multiplicative constants do not affect either logarithmic limit, so work
with Ax .
  First suppose µ < ∞ and choose t > µ. For all sufficiently large q, ex (q) ≥ q −t . Therefore
                           max{q, ex (q)/ϵ} ≥ max{q, q −t /ϵ} ≥ ϵ−1/(t+1) .
Every one of the finitely many remaining denominators has positive error, and its error term grows
as ϵ−1 , so those denominators do not invalidate this lower for sufficiently small ϵ. This gives a
lower limit at least 1/(µ + 1). Conversely, for 1 < t < µ, infinitely many q satisfy ex (q) < q −t . At
ϵ = q −(t+1) , the envelope is at most q once q ≥ 3. Taking t ↑ µ proves (S18.4); for µ = ∞ the same
argument with arbitrarily large t, together with nonnegativity, gives zero.
   For the upper limit with finite µ, again choose t > µ. Pigeonhole approximation at denominator
bound Q gives 1 ≤ k ≤ Q with ex (k) ≤ 1/(kQ). Such k tend to infinity along Q → ∞, because the
phase is irrational. The eventual lower ex (k) ≥ k −t therefore forces k ≥ Q1/(t−1) . Hence
                                  Ax (ϵ) ≤ max{3, Q, ϵ−1 Q−t/(t−1) }.
Choose Q = ⌈ϵ−(t−1)/(2t−1) ⌉ and then let t ↓ µ. This bounds the upper limit by the right side of
(S18.5). If µ = ∞, use only k ≥ 1 and Q = ⌈ϵ−1/2 ⌉ in the same pigeonhole argument to obtain the
universal upper limit 1/2.
  To prove the reverse upper-limit bound, take consecutive large convergent denominators Q = qn ,
Q′ = qn+1 and set
                                                    1
                                           ϵn =          .                                     (S18.6)
                                                 Q(Q′ )2
For every rational a/q with 0 < q < Q′ /2, either a/q = pn /Q, in which case its error is greater than
1/[Q(Q′ + Q)] ≥ 1/(2QQ′ ), or the determinant of the two integer pairs is nonzero and
                                     1                     1     1       1
                        |x − a/q| ≥     − |x − pn /Q| >      −     ′
                                                                     >      .
                                    qQ                    qQ QQ        QQ′
Thus all these denominators have ex (q)/ϵn ≥ Q′ /2. All other denominators have q ≥ Q′ /2. It
follows that Ax (ϵn ) ≥ Q′ /2. On the other hand, denominator Q′ has ex (Q′ ) < 1/(Q′ qn+2 ), so
ex (Q′ )/ϵn < QQ′ /qn+2 ≤ Q ≤ Q′ . Consequently
                                            Q′ /2 ≤ Ax (ϵn ) ≤ Q′ .                            (S18.7)
The corresponding logarithmic ratio is, up to a term tending to zero,
                                    log Q′           log Q′ / log Q
                                                =                      .
