The function on the right is increasing, continuous and tends to 1/2 at infinity. Lemma S18.2 proves
precisely the remaining lower bound in (S18.5). Finally the two displayed exponents are equal
exactly when µ = 2: solving the equality gives µ(µ − 2) = 0, and µ ≥ 2.                           □
Theorem S18.4 (Rigidity of the uniform cubic-root law). For an irrational fixed phase, the uniform
estimate
                                    mx (ϵ) = Θ(ϵ−1/3 )      (ϵ ↓ 0)
holds if and only if x is badly approximable. If it is not, the stronger oscillation statements hold:
                        lim inf ϵ1/3 mx (ϵ) = 0,     lim sup ϵ1/3 mx (ϵ) = +∞.                   (S18.8)
                          ϵ↓0                             ϵ↓0

Thus even an existing logarithmic exponent 1/3 need not give uniform cubic-root constants.
Proof. For badly approximable x, Theorem S19.6 already proves both uniform bounds, and that
result is inherited. Otherwise Lemma S18.2 supplies consecutive denominators Q, Q′ with Q′ /Q → ∞
along a subsequence. At ϵ′n = ex (Q)/Q, the envelope is at most Q, and the upper in (S18.1) gives
                      (ϵ′n )1/3 mx (ϵ′n ) ≤ C0 (Q2 ex (Q))1/3 < C0 (Q/Q′ )1/3 −→ 0.
At the crossover noise (S18.6), the lower in (S18.1) and (S18.7) instead give
                                                   ′
                                 ϵ1/3          1
                                  n mx (ϵn ) ≥ 4 (Q /Q)
                                                        1/3
                                                            −→ +∞.
Both noise sequences tend to zero. Nonnegativity proves the first equality in (S18.8), and the second
rules out a uniform upper constant. No convergence of sampled continued-fraction coefficients has
been substituted for a global arithmetic property.                                                  □
   These conclusions distinguish three questions: the complete envelope at a given noise, the
logarithmic exponents determined by µ, and uniform power-law constants governed by bounded
convergent ratios. A quadratic irrational phase has bounded partial quotients and retains the
inherited cubic-root law. For an explicit phase with exponent two but without uniform cubic
constants, take x = [0; 1, 2, 3, 4, . . .]. Its denominator ratios are unbounded, while the denominator
recursion implies at least Fibonacci growth and log(qn+1 /qn )/ log qn ≤ log(n + 2)/ log qn → 0. Hence
µ = 2 but (S18.8) holds. For µ = ∞, the two exponents are 0 and 1/2. The stronger universal
theorem does not determine the numerical value of µ for the distinct algebraic unit phase (3 + 4i)/5;
no cubic-root claim for that phase is introduced.

with the same infinite-exponent convention. We do not replace the lower limit of a maximum by the
maximum of lower limits; good approximation scales for different phases need not coincide. For a
single leaking frequency, the exact identity s = 1 + mx transfers both exponents and the uniform-law
rigidity unchanged, independently of λ.
   These are actual boundary-optimal squared-task observer counts under the retained embedding.
The protected target can be realized on dimension at most 2r + 1 before leakage and at most
2r + 2 after leakage; the flag embedding doubles these sufficient physical dimensions. No optimal
physical-dimension claim or uniform controller for unknown target phases is made.
Proof. The distinct modes 1, (1 − ϵ)e±2πixj have nonzero coefficients. Their rank and individual
spectral obstructions, together with the counted block-mixture construction, are exactly those of
Theorem S19.8. Apply (S18.1) to each component to obtain (S18.9).
   For leakage, the 2r + 1 decaying scalar modes gain the independent constant mode in the
complementary output, giving rank 2r + 2. The relative-pole obstruction of Proposition S20.2
requires 1 + m(1 − ϵ, 2πxj ) labels for each j. Conversely append one live zero-output state to the
entire unattenuated stochastic realization and scale its continuing matrix by λ. This realizes the
decaying mixture from the same initial label. These arguments prove (S18.10).
   For a finite list, the upper logarithmic limit of its maximum is the maximum of its upper
logarithmic limits. Rational components have bounded exact size and do not change this maximum
when an irrational component is present. Theorem S18.3 proves the formula. The single-frequency
assertion follows from the exact leakage increment of Theorem S20.3. Finally the block-diagonal
protected realization in Theorem S19.8, with one additional leakage state when needed, supplies
the stated dimensions; the actual squared-loss and stopping-cost assertions follow from the same
declared flag embedding. A private randomized block choice is stored in the counted labels and
never reveals the protected block.                                                               □
   The conclusions do not supply a sharp leading constant, a general exact intermediate-memory risk
curve, or a minimal controller for an unspecified noncomputable phase. For a supplied computable
phase and positive noise, interval bounds on ex (q) and the explicit polygon construction give finite
certified upper constructions; exact minimality still uses the separate spectral-membership problem.
Ideal finite labels, descriptions of transition probabilities, exact sampling and working storage remain
distinct resources. The complete arithmetic envelope closes the fixed-phase constant-factor question
for this response family, not the general multi-command foundations programme.

             S19. Spectral and arithmetic laws of exact response memory
   The instrument realization theorem preserves every terminal effect, whereas a prescribed task
can require substantially less memory. We now determine the exact memory problem for a whole
family of single-frequency protected responses, not just one damping factor or the previously solved
three-label case. The criterion reduces the problem to classical stochastic eigenvalue regions. A
self-contained sector argument and elementary rational approximation then give an exact worst-phase
law and matching growth at every fixed badly approximable phase. None of the spectral-region,
positive-realization, or sector machinery is claimed as newly invented [51, 52, 53]. The contributions
considered here are their exact response-level equivalence with a prescribed initial label, their
consequences for the physical squared task, and the phase-sensitive matched resource law.
S19.1. A physical family and the exact spectral criterion. Let 0 < α ≤ 1, 0 < γ ≤ 1, and
ϑ, φ ∈ R, with sin ϑ ̸= 0. Consider the binary response
                                   1
                              bn = 1 + γαn cos(nϑ + φ) ,
                                                        
                                                             n ≥ 0.                     (S19.1)
                                   2
The rotation, contrast and preparation phase specify one prepared instance; independence of the
minimum size does not assert one fixed decoder for unknown phases or contrasts. It is physically
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       61


prepared on a qubit: take the Bloch vector γ(sin φ, 0, cos φ), the channel
                                                                    cos(ϑ/2) − sin(ϑ/2)
                                                                                       
             Φ(X) = αUϑ XUϑ∗ + (1 − α) tr(X)I/2,         Uϑ =                           ,
                                                                    sin(ϑ/2) cos(ϑ/2)
and terminal effect E = (I + σz )/2. Its Born probabilities are exactly (S19.1). Theorem S24.1
embeds this response into the same three-outcome squared-loss experiment, with protected dimension
four, fixed decoder dictionary {−1, 1}, and least expected cost 1/p. It forces the probability of
output +1 after n failures to equal bn . Thus the mathematical realization problem below is the
exact optimal-observer problem of an actual bounded squared task, not a sequence supplied by a
loss oracle.
   Write m(α, ϑ; γ, φ) for the least M for which
                     bn = eT  n
                           1 Q d (n ≥ 0),       Q ≥ 0,    Q1 = 1,       d ∈ [0, 1]M .
The initial label is prescribed and counted. Let
            KM = {z ∈ C : z is an eigenvalue of some M × M row-stochastic matrix}.
These are the classical Karpelevič regions; their full boundary classification is prior work [52]. We
will not use that classification as an unproved step in any bound below.
Theorem S19.1 (Exact spectral realization, with no extra initial label). For the nondegenerate
family (S19.1),
                         m(α, ϑ; γ, φ) = min{M : αeiϑ ∈ KM }.                          (S19.2)
The value is independent of every nonzero contrast γ and of the preparation phase φ; denote it by
m(α, ϑ). The criterion is necessary and sufficient over all stochastic matrices, including reducible
matrices and arbitrary randomized decoders. For 0 < α < 1 it is finite. For α = 1 and ϑ/(2π) = a/q
in lowest terms it is exactly q; for irrational ϑ/(2π) it is infinite.
   In the squared-task embedding, these are exact live-label counts at cost 1/p. At each fixed smaller
memory bound, the attained risk is strictly above the full-history value 2. Event-stopped and
state-stopped total-label upper counts remain respectively max{M, 2} and M + 2.
Proof. Put z = αeiϑ . The response has the three distinct exponential modes 1, z, z̄, with nonzero
coefficients. Cayley–Hamilton for any realizing Q makes its characteristic polynomial an annihilating
shift polynomial of bn . Substitution and Vandermonde independence force that polynomial to
vanish at z. This proves necessity without assuming a minimal linear representation has positive
coordinates.
  Conversely let Qv = zv with v ̸= 0. Normalize a largest-modulus coordinate to one by multiplying
v by one complex scalar, and permute that coordinate into position one. Then v1 = 1 and |vj | ≤ 1.
The column
                                      dj = 12 [1 + γ Re(eiφ vj )]
lies in [0, 1]M . Row normalization and Qn v = z n v give eT  n
                                                           1 Q d = bn for every n. The same M labels
suffice: there is no preliminary random initialization or extra anchor. Unreachable labels can be
pruned. Finiteness for α < 1 follows from the regular-polygon construction proved below, or from
the inherited polygon upper in Theorem S27.6.
   At |z| = 1, equality in the triangle inequality at a largest-modulus eigenvector coordinate forces
each positive successor to have the same modulus and value z times the current value. Following
positive successors reaches a directed cycle of length l ≤ M , so z l = 1. An irrational phase is
therefore impossible. If z has order q, every such cycle has length divisible by q, giving M ≥ q; a
q-cycle realizes it. Finally the squared embedding forces the whole response. Compact attainment
at fixed M was proved in Proposition S23.8; equality of its risk with 2 would give the excluded
exact response. The existing packing lemma gives the terminal counts.                              □
62                                                QIAN QI


   The nondegeneracy assumptions cannot be suppressed. Zero contrast gives one constant label. At
α = 0, b0 = (1 + γ cos φ)/2 and bn = 1/2 for n ≥ 1; one label suffices if γ cos φ = 0, and otherwise
two are necessary and sufficient. Real phases and constant cases are classified in the supplement.
In particular a vanishing oscillation amplitude is not the limit of an exact-memory lower valid for
nonzero amplitude.
                                    ̸ 0, spectral membership has the compact polynomial certificate
   For algebraic x = Re z, y = Im z =
                         Q ≥ 0, Q1 = 1, Qa = xa − yc, Qc = ya + xc,
                                                                                             (S19.3)
                            a1 = 1, c1 = 0, a2j + c2j ≤ 1 (1 ≤ j ≤ M ).
It has M 2 + 2M real variables and polynomial degree at most two. Normalization of an eigenvector
proves equivalence in both directions. Real-algebraic elimination decides it at each fixed M , and
the finite polygon upper terminates a minimal-size search for |z| < 1. This improves the form
of the certificate, not the general complexity of real-algebraic elimination. A chosen algebraic
preparation and contrast then yield algebraic decoder entries. State labels, encodings of those
entries, exact-sampling resources and runtime remain separate accounts.
S19.2. The sector bound, its equality case, and exact finite phases. We include the classical
stochastic sector argument to expose every load-bearing inequality. The equality structure is
consistent with the Type-0 classification of Kirkland and Šmigoc [53]; it is not a new classification
of stochastic spectral arcs.
Lemma S19.2 (Stochastic sector inequality and rigidity). If z = x + iy, y ̸= 0, is an eigenvalue of
an M × M stochastic matrix, then M ≥ 3 and
                                          |y| ≤ (1 − x) cot(π/M ).                               (S19.4)
If equality holds, then, up to simultaneous relabelling and reversing the orientation when needed,
that matrix is
                                 Q = (1 − t)I + tCM ,     0 < t ≤ 1,                         (S19.5)
where CM is the directed M -cycle. Its eigenvalue on the equality ray is 1 − t + te   2πi/M , or its
conjugate.
Proof. Conjugate the eigenvector when necessary       so y > 0. Let P be the convex hull of its complex
coordinates. The matrix es(Q−I) = e−s n≥0 sn Qn /n! is stochastic for s ≥ 0, so multiplication by
                                           P

es(z−1) maps P into itself. Since z is nonreal, 1 − x > 0, and the spiral of any nonzero coordinate
visits every angle, a finite set of points of this spiral surrounds zero. Hence zero lies in the interior
of P .
   List its l ≤ M vertices counterclockwise as rj eiθj , with rj > 0. Their consecutive angular gaps ∆j
belong to (0, π) and sum to 2π. Put u = 1 − x > 0 and v = y > 0. The initial velocity (−u + iv)rj eiθj
must point into the half-plane to the left of the outgoing edge. Taking its signed two-dimensional
cross product with that edge gives
                                     rj /rj+1 ≤ cos ∆j + (u/v) sin ∆j .
All right sides are positive. Their product p is at least one. Write ϕ = arctan(u/v) ∈ (0, π/2). The
function log(cos ∆ + (u/v) sin ∆) = log 1 + (u/v)2 + log cos(∆ − ϕ) is strictly concave on the
interval of positive factors in (0, π). Jensen’s inequality therefore gives
                        Y

                         j

As l ≥ 3, this implies u/v ≥ tan(π/l) ≥ tan(π/M ), proving (S19.4).
   At equality, l = M , every gap is 2π/M , and all radius ratios equal one: the polygon is regular
and every coordinate  is one distinct vertex. The velocity at a vertex is along its outgoing edge. But
(z − 1)vj = k Qjk (vk − vj ) is a nonnegative combination of chords. Strict convexity of this polygon
            P
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                          63


makes all chords other than the self-chord and outgoing edge point strictly to the interior side of
that edge. Their coefficients must therefore vanish. The remaining coefficient t = Qj,j+1 satisfies
z − 1 = t(e2πi/M − 1) and is independent of j. Row normalization gives (S19.5) and t ≤ 1.        □

   For the physical realization, take a direct sum of r qubits, prepare the phase-rotated pure state
in block j with weight wj , and use the damped rotation channel in that block. Drop off-diagonal
block coherences. If w0 > 0, add one scalar block of mass w0 with effect 1/2 and identity evolution.
Each diagonal branch is completely positive and their direct sum is trace preserving. The terminal
effect is the direct sum of the qubit Z effects and the scalar half effect. Its probability is exactly
(S19.14). Theorem S24.1 therefore gives the same fixed squared loss and cost 1/p, and forces this
response at the optimum. The observer’s independently randomized block selection does not reveal
or copy the protected block.                                                                           □
   The three memory statements have different quantifiers: the exact worst-phase law permits the
phase to vary with the noise, the cubic-root law fixes any phase with the displayed arithmetic
certificate, and the last corollary fixes the older algebraic rotation without such a certificate. Noise-
dependent classical memory bounds for quantum processes were already established by Fanizza,
Lumbreras and Winter [50]; their worst-phase process bound is not a fixed-phase matched law
for this response task. The stochastic regions and their Type-0 rigidity are also classical. The
present proofs make the task-level reduction and the new arithmetic comparison explicit, rather
than claiming earliest priority from a literature search. No statement in this section solves the
different squared-sign prediction frontier or the phase-type timing frontier.

               S20. Transient output faces and leakage-sensitive memory
   Uniform decay of a physical state and decay of its observable probabilities do not measure
the same obstruction to exact stochastic realization. In this section leakage removes population
from a protected subspace, whereas internal depolarization changes the spectrum relative to that
surviving population. We prove that the first operation costs exactly one additional live label in
the nondegenerate single-frequency family, independently of how rapid the leakage is. We then
obtain finite-model witnesses on a fixed six-dimensional squared-prediction experiment, and an
exact finite-realization result when a positive background is added to the terminal effect.
   Nonnegative realizations, dominant-pole obstructions and transient-state reductions have classical
antecedents [54, 56, 57, 47]. We do not claim to originate these methods. The statements below
specify the constrained stochastic decoder, prescribed initial label, sharp label increment, physical
leakage channel and finite squared-task tests. All necessary positive-matrix arguments are included.
S20.1. The output-visible transient core. For a nonzero sequence u = (un )n≥0 in [0, 1] tending
to zero, let s(u) be its least stochastic realization size:
                          un = eT  n
                                1 Q d,       Q ≥ 0,    Q1 = 1,    0 ≤ d ≤ 1.
The initial label is fixed, not a freely randomized initial distribution. Define t(u) by the same
formula with a nonnegative row-substochastic matrix S, S1 ≤ 1, whose spectral radius is strictly
below one. Both minima are infinite if no finite realization exists.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        67


Lemma S20.1 (Exact transient-core reduction). For every such sequence,
                                           s(u) = t(u) + 1.                                     (S20.1)
More precisely, any M -label stochastic realization has a nonempty set of at most M − 1 reachable
labels that can eventually give positive output. Its restriction S realizes u, is transient and uses the
same initial label. If also un ≤ Cλn for 0 < λ < 1, then the spectral radius of this restriction is at
most λ.
Proof. Remove labels not reachable from label one. Call a remaining label active if a positive-
probability path from it reaches some label j with dj > 0. The initial label is active because u is
nonzero: there is some n with eT    n
                                 1 Q d > 0, so a positive-weight path from label one reaches a label
with positive decoder output. This remains true when u0 = 0. A path that leaves the active set
cannot return: a return would make every intervening reachable label active. Thus, with S the
active principal submatrix and dA its decoder, un = eT         n
                                                            1 S dA .
   For each active i, choose a path of length ai from the start to i of weight pi > 0. For each active
j, choose a path of length bj from j with positive terminal expected output rj > 0. Positivity gives,
for every n,
                                        pi rj (S n )ij ≤ uai +n+bj .                           (S20.2)
                                n
Consequently every entry of S tends to zero. In finite dimension this forces every eigenvalue to
have modulus less than one. In the exponential case it gives (S n )ij ≤ Cij λn , and applying this
bound to an eigenvector proves the stronger spectral-radius bound. There must be a reachable
inactive label: otherwise S would be stochastic and have eigenvalue one. Hence the active size is at
most M − 1, proving t(u) + 1 ≤ s(u).
   Conversely a k-label transient realization extends to the stochastic matrix and decoder
                                                                    !
                                 b = S 1 − S1 ,                   dA
                                                  
                                 Q                         db =      .                          (S20.3)
                                     0    1                        0
They realize the same sequence from the same initial label with k + 1 labels. This proves the reverse
inequality, including the infinite case. The added label is a live zero-output label until the actual
herald; it is not permission to stop at an unobserved physical leakage event.                      □
   We recall a small positive-matrix fact with its proof. If B ≥ 0 is irreducible and has a nonzero
eigenvalue, it has a vector h > 0 with Bh = Rh, where R is its spectral radius. Indeed, for
B + ϵ11T the map sending a simplex vector to its normalized image has a fixed point by the
finite-dimensional simplex fixed-point theorem.PThe fixed point is strictly positive. Along ϵ ↓ 0 a
convergent subsequence gives Bh = Rh, h ≥ 0, i hi = 1. If a coordinate vanished, the eigenvector
equation and irreducibility would propagate that zero to every coordinate. Thus h > 0, and R > 0.
The similarity R−1 diag(h)−1 B diag(h) is stochastic, so its maximum-row-sum bound shows that
R is indeed the spectral radius. This is the Perron normalization used below, not a new Perron
theorem.
Proposition S20.2 (A relative spectral obstruction for decaying outputs). Suppose an M -label
stochastic response satisfies 0 ≤ un ≤ Cλn with 0 < λ < 1. If its rational generating function has a
nonreal pole at ζ −1 , then
                                            ζ/λ ∈ KM −1 .                                    (S20.4)
Here Kj is the stochastic eigenvalue region defined in Section S19. It is the decay-normalized mode,
not merely the absolute mode ζ, that constrains the active state count.
Proof. The core in Lemma S20.1 gives n un z n = eT               −1
                                                       1 (I − zS) dA . Hence ζ is an eigenvalue of
                                          P

S. Order its strongly connected components to obtain a block triangular matrix. One irreducible
diagonal block B has that eigenvalue. Its spectral radius R obeys |ζ| ≤ R ≤ λ. Perron normalization
gives a stochastic matrix T of size l ≤ M − 1 with eigenvalue ζ/R and eigenvector v.
68                                                QIAN QI


  A stationary probability row π for T exists: take a convergent subsequence of the averages of any
probability row and its first n − 1 iterates. Since ζ/R ̸= 1, one has πv = 0. Put a = R/λ ∈ (0, 1].
The stochastic matrix T ′ = aT + (1 − a)1π satisfies T ′ v = (ζ/λ)v. Padding by unreachable self-loop
labels, if needed, proves membership in KM −1 . No global Perron scaling of the reducible core has
been assumed.                                                                                       □
                                                                                    
                                                                           1/2 1/4
  The last qualification matters. A reducible matrix such as S = 0 1/4 has spectral radius 1/2,
but no strictly positive h satisfies Sh ≤ h/2. The proof normalizes the irreducible block carrying
the observed pole, rather than silently imposing that false global inequality.

S20.2. An exact leakage law for every damping and phase. Fix 0 < λ < 1, 0 < α ≤ 1,
0 < γ ≤ 1, and sin ϑ ̸= 0. Let
                                         λn
                                      un =  [1 + γαn cos(nϑ + φ)].                            (S20.5)
                                          2
The scalar λ is survival against leakage; α is coherence retention within the surviving qubit. These
parameters are known properties of the prepared target channel, not extra observations available to
the observer.
Theorem S20.3 (Exact leakage increment). For the nondegenerate family (S20.5),
                             s(u) = 1 + m(α, ϑ) = 1 + min{k : αeiϑ ∈ Kk }.                          (S20.6)
This holds over all stochastic matrices and randomized decoder rows, with a prescribed initial label. In
particular the value is independent of every 0 < λ < 1, every nonzero contrast and every preparation
phase. For α < 1 it is finite. At α = 1 it is h + 1 if eiϑ has finite order h, and it is infinite otherwise.
   The full two-output Hankel response has rank four, regardless of this possibly much larger or
infinite exact memory. In the fixed squared-task embedding it is the least live memory at the original
cost 1/p, with event-stopped and state-stopped total-label upper counts max{s(u), 2} and s(u) + 2
when finite.
Proof. The three modes λ, λαeiϑ and λαe−iϑ in u are distinct and have nonzero coefficients because
λ > 0, α > 0, γ > 0 and sin ϑ ̸= 0. The phase factor eiφ never vanishes, including at initial-output
zeros. Thus its generating function has a pole at (λαeiϑ )−1 . Proposition S20.2 makes every finite
realizing size M satisfy αeiϑ ∈ KM −1 . Theorem S19.1 proves the required lower and the infinite
case.
   For the upper take a minimal realization (Q, d) of the unattenuated response bn = [1+γαn cos(nϑ+
φ)]/2. Replace its continuing block by λQ and append a live zero-output label, as in (S20.3). Its
output is exactly λn bn from time zero. This proves equality without an extra initialization operation.
The unit-circle alternatives follow from the already proved root-of-unity characterization.
   The scalar response spans its three exponential modes. Its complement adds the independent
constant mode one, because λ < 1. Vandermonde independence therefore gives full response
rank four. Theorem S24.1 forces this entire probability response in the actual squared task; the
role-packing statement is then inherited without identifying protected physical states with live
observer labels.                                                                                     □
Corollary S20.4 (Noise laws depend on relative damping). For fixed 0 < λ < 1 and 0 < α < 1,
the worst-phase exact count is
                                 max s(u) = 1 + max{3, ⌈π/ arccos α⌉}.
                                sin ϑ̸=0

allowed to vary simultaneously with α. At the original algebraic phase, the first nontrivial threshold
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        69
                                                √

Proof. Substitute (S20.6) into the exact worst-phase, fixed-phase and three-label results. All

been subtracted from an event-stopped dictionary.                                               □
S20.3. A strictly contractive quantum channel with infinite exact memory. Let K =
C2 ⊕ C|⊥⟩, let P project onto the qubit and let Ω = |⊥⟩⟨⊥|. With the qubit channel Φα and
preparation from Section S19, define
                    Ψλ,α (X) = λΦα (P XP ) + [tr((I − P )X) + (1 − λ) tr(P X)]Ω.                (S20.7)
The qubit output is embedded in its original block and cross-block coherences are discarded. Prepare
the qubit and audit the qubit effect E = (I + σz )/2, extended by zero at ⊥.
Theorem S20.5 (Uniform physical mixing versus exact response memory). Equation (S20.7) is a
completely positive trace-preserving channel with unique stationary state Ω. For all density matrices
ρ, σ and all n ≥ 0,
                  ∥Ψλ,α (ρ) − Ψλ,α (σ)∥1 ≤ λ∥ρ − σ∥1 ,       ∥Ψnλ,α (ρ) − Ω∥1 ≤ 2λn .
Its prepared audit response is exactly (S20.5), so its exact stochastic observer memory is (S20.6).
In particular, at α = 1 and the algebraic rotation (3 + 4i)/5, every 0 < λ < 1 gives infinite exact
memory despite strict trace-norm contraction and exponential convergence to one stationary state.
For algebraic λ and algebraic preparation data, all primitive superoperator entries are algebraic.
   The squared-prediction embedding has protected dimension six, fixed dictionary {−1, 1}, three
actual audit outcomes and least expected cost 1/p. In the specified irrational, internally undamped
case, every finite live-memory class has an attained strictly larger risk than the full-history value 2.
A prefix with T exact-output labels and one live zero-output tail label has the same cost and excess
risk at most
                                2εp(q t̄λ)T
                                            ,   q = 1 − p, ε = 1/4.                             (S20.8)
                                 1 − q t̄λ
Proof. Write Ξ(X) = Φα (P XP ) + tr((I − P )X)Ω and E(X) = tr(X)Ω. Both are channels: com-
pression, the declared qubit channel and scalar replacement are completely positive, and their
output traces sum to the input trace. Then Ψλ,α = λΞ + (1 − λ)E. On a traceless Hermitian
difference the second term vanishes. Trace-norm contraction of a channel follows directly by applying
positivity and trace preservation to the positive and negative parts of that difference. This proves
the contraction inequality. Since Ω is fixed, iteration gives the displayed bound and uniqueness.
   Starting in the qubit, its surviving block after n calls is λn Φnα (ρ); the complementary mass is at
⊥. Taking the effect gives (S20.5). The preceding theorem proves the memory claim. At (3 + 4i)/5
the unit phase is not a root of unity, by the rational algebraic-integer argument already proved. The
spectral obstruction is in the decaying output-visible block, not at the peripheral spectrum of the
entire trace-preserving channel.
   The embedding of Theorem S24.1 doubles the protected dimension and retains its real squared
audit. Its unique optimal probability kernel must reproduce u. Compactness of each fixed-memory
controller class and the uniform geometric word tail give attainment; equality at a finite size would
contradict the exact-memory theorem. For the approximation, store u0 , . . . , uT −1 and thereafter
output probability zero. Since un ≤ λn , summing the exact risk identity (S24.4) yields (S20.8).
Nothing in this construction observes leakage or changes first-herald stopping.                      □
   This is not the earlier unique-sign counterexample under depolarization. Here the required
response is probability valued and its absolute nonconstant eigenvalues all lie strictly inside the unit
disk. The impossibility comes from their phases relative to the visible decay rate. The stationary
70                                              QIAN QI


state is on a face, so the interior hypothesis of the instrument-wide realization theorem is genuinely
unavailable. With γ = 1 and φ = π, moreover, u0 = 0 and 0 ≤ un ≤ λ for all n ≥ 1. The one-label
zero response is uniformly within λ of this law while exact memory remains infinite for every positive
λ. Exact realization is not a robust approximate-memory assertion.

S20.4. Finitely many physical models detect the obstruction. The continuum of herald-flag
parameters used to force an arbitrary full response can be replaced, against a prescribed finite-
memory bound, by a finite set whose cardinality depends only on that bound and the target’s linear
order.
Theorem S20.6 (Finite rational-resolvent tests and fixed-dimensional witnesses). Suppose a target
channel response has generating function U (z) = P (z)/D(z) with deg P ≤ r − 1, deg D ≤ r, and
D(z) ̸= 0 on [0, q t̄]. Fix M ≥ 1 and any M + r distinct parameters tj ∈ [0, t̄]. In the squared
embedding, an observer with at most M live labels has risk exactly 2 on the finite family (tj , σ),
σ ∈ {−1, 1}, if and only if it realizes the entire target response at every failure count.
   Consequently for the algebraic leaking qubit with λ = 1/2, α = γ = 1, eiϑ = (3 + 4i)/5 and
φ = 0, the following holds for every M : a six-dimensional prepared squared experiment with only
2(M + 3) rationally specified models, one unit-cost command and the fixed two-value dictionary has
boundary value 2, but every at-most-M -label observer has risk at least 2 + δM , where δM > 0 is an
attained, computable real algebraic number. One may take p = 1/2, t̄ = 1/2 and tj = j/[2(M + 2)],
0 ≤ j ≤ M + 2. Neither the physical dimension nor the target response order increases with M .
Proof. Boundary feasibility still forces first-herald commitment in this finite model family, since its
physical herald probability is always p. An M -label rule has B(z) = eT          −1
                                                                      1 (I − zQ) d = PQ (z)/DQ (z),
where deg PQ ≤ M − 1, deg DQ ≤ M and DQ (z) ̸= 0 for 0 ≤ z < 1. Smaller matrices may be
padded by unreachable rows.
   The two signs and (S24.4) make risk 2 equivalent to B(qtj ) = U (qtj ) at every chosen parameter.
The polynomial PQ D − P DQ has degree at most M + r − 1 and vanishes at M + r distinct points.
It is therefore zero. Equality of the power series about zero gives every response coefficient. The
converse follows immediately from the same risk identity. This is rational interpolation with known
degree, not extrapolation from arbitrary fitted samples.
   For the stated target,
                                     1          1 − λcz
                       U (z) =            +                      ,      c = 3/5,
                                 2(1 − λz) 2(1 − 2λcz + λ2 z 2 )
so r = 3 suffices and all coefficients are rational. The exact leakage theorem excludes every finite
realization. Hence the finite tests exclude equality for the specified M . The full-history target kernel
still attains 2 in every selected model. Compact controller simplexes and the geometric tail imply
the fixed-M minimum is attained, so its gap δM is strictly positive.
   Finally introduce real variables Q, d and vectors vj satisfying (I − qtj Q)vj = d. These matrices
are invertible for every stochastic Q, and the achieved risk is 2 + 2εp maxj |(vj )1 − U (qtj )|. All
defining constraints are polynomial over the rationals. Real-algebraic elimination computes the
attained minimum and an algebraic minimizing controller; in particular the positive gap is algebraic
and computable. This is an exact finite procedure, not a polynomial-time claim or a numerical
certificate inferred from local optimization. The general procedure is proved here; a full elimination
at every M has not been executed in the finite diagnostics.                                            □

  Thus finite linear rank four does not bound the classical memory forced by a finite family of actual
squared-loss tests, even on a fixed protected dimension. The number of models in this conclusion
grows with the tested memory bound. It is not a claim that one fixed finite model list excludes
every finite observer.

This changes a real terminal measurement, not the protected channel or the observer’s information.
Theorem S20.7 (Exact finite realization after background regularization). For all parameters of

                                          (1 − η)λT ≤ η/2,                                      (S20.9)

   For fixed λ ∈ (0, 1), α = 1 and irrational phase, the least exact size tends to infinity as η ↓ 0,
while the displayed upper is Oλ (log(1/η)). No matching lower rate in η is asserted. For algebraic
nondegenerate inputs this finite upper and the inherited fixed-size response test give a terminating
search for a minimal exact controller.
Proof. Put a = (1 − η)λT /2 and ψ = T ϑ + φ. The suffix starting at T is the average of the two
probability responses
                      fk = η/2 + 2aλk ,        gk = η/2 + 2aγαT (λα)k cos(kϑ + ψ).
The first has a two-label transient/absorbing realization with decoder values η/2 + 2a and η/2. For
the second, use the normalized eigenvector construction of Theorem S19.1 at eigenvalue λαeiϑ , with

[0, η] ⊂ [0, 1].
                                     (η)         (η)
   Store a deterministic chain for u0 , . . . , uT −1 . At its final failure transition enter the two disjoint
suffix machines with weights 1/2, 1/2. No additional initialization label is needed: the last prefix
row makes and stores the choice within the counted blocks. At every later failure count the output
                   (η)
is (fk + gk )/2 = uT +k . This proves the exact count and preserves the physical stopping rule.
   If exact sizes stayed bounded along η ↓ 0, pad their stochastic matrices and decoders to one
size and take a convergent subsequence in the compact row simplexes. Every finite coefficient is
continuous in those variables. The limit would realize u at η = 0, contradicting Theorem S20.3.
The logarithmic upper follows by solving (S20.9) with fixed λ. For algebraic input, the target has
a supplied linear representation of dimension at most four. The finite word-equality criterion of
Proposition S27.7, together with this size upper, terminates a minimal exact-size search. Algebraic
controller encodings and exact sampling resources remain distinct from label count.                         □

             S21. The finite Hankel determinant used by the new hierarchy
    For completeness we verify the nonsingularity used by Theorem S24.2 without appealing to a
positive-realization theorem. Put h = k − 1 and un = an P (n), where P has degree h − 1 and
nonzero leading coefficient, and 0 < a < 1. The h polynomials P (n), P (n + 1), . . . , P (n + h − 1) are
linearly independent. Indeed their successive forward differences have strictly decreasing degrees
h − 1, h − 2, . . . , 0 and nonzero leading coefficients; the change from translates to differences is
triangular and invertible. Thus the columns ui , ui+1 , . . . , ui+h−1 span the same evaluation space as
ai , iai , . . . , ih−1 ai .
    On rows i = 0, . . . , h, the matrix with columns 1, ai , iai , . . . , ih−1 ai is nonsingular. To see this
directly, begin with the Vandermonde matrix on distinct nodes 1, x1 , . . . , xh , whose columns are
their powers on these rows. Replace the lasth columns by successive divided differences and let
all xj tend to a. The limiting columns are ji ai−j for j = 0, . . . , h − 1, with determinant (a − 1)h
72                                                   QIAN QI


times the nonzero within-group confluent factor (equal to one for these divided-difference columns).
Multiplication by nonzero powers of a and an invertible triangular change from falling powers of i to
ordinary powers gives the claimed matrix. Equivalently its determinant is, up to the fixed ordering
sign, (a − 1)h ah(h−1)/2 h−1
                          Q
                            j=0 j!, which is nonzero. The distinct-node Vandermonde formula itself
follows by divisibility by every pairwise difference and comparison of its leading    monomial.
                                                                                n
   For the Erlang survival sequence in the main theorem, P (n) = h−1                   − a)/a)j , whose
                                                                         P
                                                                           j=0 j   ((1
leading coefficient is ((1 − a)/a)h−1 /(h − 1)! ̸= 0. Appending the constant column to its h translates
therefore gives the nonsingular matrix Au used in the finite physical witness. This also proves
the exact full two-output Hankel rank: the displayed k columns give the lower, while the k-state
stochastic chain gives the upper. No assertion that finite rank alone implies positive stochastic
realization is involved.

                              Independent current developments
  The following theorem bodies are retained in full, with their individual hypotheses. The new
core does not depend on all of them. Cross-references distinguish the new main article from these
independent complementary consequences; the source preservation map additionally points to the
unchanged predecessor packet.

               S22. Finite realization of uniformly interior instruments
   The boundary decision theorems identify the observer memory needed for an optimal task. We
now consider a distinct, stronger form of physical-model reduction: one finite hidden classical model
must preserve every controlled report word, every stopping rule and every terminal measurement
of a declared instrument family. Its hidden labels are not made available to the observer. The
distinction is essential: a finite hidden-state realization does not in general make the posterior, or
an optimal observer, finite-state.
   Invariant positive cones are classical realization tools [48, 47]. Our purpose here is not another
abstract cone criterion. We derive a finite invariant set from a quantitative condition on every actual
report branch, give its dimension dependence and an explicit sparse implementation, and prove
exact stopped-law and cost transfer. The commands need not commute, need not reset the input,
and need not share a stationary state. The condition is sufficient, not necessary for every finite
hidden realization. A matching noise exponent below concerns the expressly defined state-positive
class, not every possible classical response representation.

S22.1. A finite hull containing all uniformly mixed states. Let Dd be the density matrices
on Cd , d ≥ 2, and put
                 Dd (η) = {ρ ∈ Dd : ρ ⪰ ηI/d} = (1 − η)Dd + ηI/d,                  0 < η ≤ 1.
For unit vectors modulo phase use the metric
                                     q
                  dist([u], [v]) =       1 − |⟨u, v⟩|2 = 21 ∥uu∗ − vv ∗ ∥1 = ∥uu∗ − vv ∗ ∥op .
In particular it satisfies the triangle inequality. All convex hulls below are hulls of actual positive
density matrices, not signed coordinates.
Lemma S22.1 (An explicit interior hull). There are pure states P1 , . . . , PK such that
                                                                           l           m
                      Dd (η) ⊆ conv{P1 , . . . , PK } ⊆ Dd ,         K ≤ (4d/η)d−1 .             (S22.1)

Any additional k prescribed initial states may be included as k further generators. The generators
are shared by all maps satisfying the same interior margin.
                                       CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                     73


Proof. For the unitarily invariant probability measure on complex projective space, a ball of radius
r ∈ [0, 1] has mass r2d−2 . To verify the formula, normalize d independent standard complex Gaussian
coordinates. Their squared moduli before normalization are independent exponential variables.
Changing variables to their sum and normalized proportions gives constant density on the probability
simplex. Thus |u1 |2 has density (d − 1)(1 − x)d−2 on [0, 1], and the probability of |u1 |2 > 1 − r2 is
r2d−2 . Thispalso proves the formula at the endpoints by continuity.
   Set ε = η/d. A maximal set of rays with pairwise distances greater than ε is an ε-net. The
radius-ε/2 open balls about its points are disjoint. Their equal positive masses bound the cardinality
by (4/ε2 )d−1 . Such a finite maximal set exists by successively adding uncovered points; the same
mass bound forces this process to stop.
   Let A be any Hermitian matrix, with greatest and least eigenvalues a, b. Choose a net vector v
within ε of a greatest-eigenvalue vector. Expanding in that eigenbasis gives
                                            tr(Avv ∗ ) ≥ a − (a − b)ε2 .
On the other hand,
                                                                 
                          max tr(Aρ) = a − η a − tr A/d ≤ a − η(a − b)/d.
                        ρ∈Dd (η)

The net hull therefore has support function at least that of Dd (η) in every Hermitian direction.
Finite-dimensional separation proves containment. The other inclusion and the additional-generator
assertion follow from positivity and convexity.                                                 □
S22.2. Report-resolved positive interpolation. There is a finite set of globally legal commands
A and, for each command, a finite report set Ya . Write Ia,y for its completely positive report
map and assume y Ia,y is trace preserving. A finite list ρ(1) , . . . , ρ(k) of allowed prepared states
                   P

is specified. The initial preparation index is fixed before execution; it is not an unknown state
revealed to the observer. Assume the following unnormalized inequality for every command, report
and positive input:
                                           η           
                                Ia,y (X) ⪰ tr Ia,y (X) I,     X ⪰ 0.                            (S22.2)
                                           d
A zero branch is allowed. No probability is divided out at zero. The hypothesis is on each recorded
branch, not merely on the sum of the maps. It is satisfied when a depolarizing channel with mixing
weight at least η is physically applied after each report branch. Merely assuming positive definite
outputs at each nonzero input is not used to infer a uniform ratio at a vanishing branch.
   A state-positive realization consists of density matrices τ1 , . . . , τM , including the prescribed
preparations as designated initial labels, and nonnegative matrices Qa,y satisfying
                                  X                                        X
                   Ia,y (τi ) =        Qa,y (i, j)τj ,                           Qa,y (i, j) = 1.   (S22.3)
                                   j                                       y,j

The second equation includes all reports of a fixed command. The simulator in state i, on receiving
command a, draws the pair (y, j) with these probabilities, reports only y, and retains j privately.
For a terminal positive-operator-valued measurement {Ez }z , it reports z with probability tr(Ez τj ).
A fixed finite decoder dictionary is handled by the unchanged external observer.
Theorem S22.2 (Uniform finite controlled realization). Under (S22.2), a state-positive realization
exists with                                  l          m
                                  M ≤ k + (4d/η)d−1 .                                      (S22.4)
Each row of each Qa,y may be chosen with at most d2 nonzero entries. The count is independent of
the horizon, number of commands, number of reports, and choice of terminal measurement; those
quantities still affect the transition-table size.
   For every adaptive randomized command policy using only past declared reports and its own
randomness, this one realization preserves all finite report laws and all adaptively chosen terminal
74                                              QIAN QI


measurement laws. It also preserves the complete stopped law, including the event of never stopping.
Every nonnegative cost that is a function of the executed command/report path has the same
expectation, finite or infinite. If calls instead have specified unobserved nonnegative mean observables
Ga , the assignment ca (i) = tr(Ga τi ) preserves their total expected cost at every stopping rule. This
latter assertion is an expectation statement, not a joint-law assertion about an unspecified random
cost observation.
Proof. Take the hull of Lemma S22.1 and append the prepared states. For any generator τi , let
s = tr Ia,y (τi ). If s = 0, positivity makes the branch matrix zero, and set that whole row of Qa,y to
zero. If s > 0, its normalized output belongs to Dd (η) and hence to the pure-state core hull. Choose
convex coefficients there and multiply them by s. This proves the first identity. Taking its trace
and summing in y proves row normalization.
   A convex combination in the trace-one Hermitian affine space needs at most d2 generators. Indeed
any larger supported list is affinely dependent; subtract a scalar multiple of an affine dependence
until one positive coefficient becomes zero, preserving nonnegativity, total mass and represented
matrix, and iterate. Applying this finite reduction separately to each branch gives the sparsity
claim.
   For a fixed command/report history h, let Xh be its unnormalized physical state and ph the
simulator’s unnormalized hidden-state row. Induction using (S22.3) gives the matrix identity
                                                  X
                                           Xh =       ph (i)τi .                                (S22.5)
                                                  i
It holds initially because the initial label is the prescribed preparation. Multiplying both sides by
a report branch gives the next identity. Traces give the report probability, and pairing with any
terminal effect gives its joint audit probability.
   For an adaptive observer the command probabilities and commitment probabilities conditional on
a declared history are the same in the two experiments. Condition on its private tape, apply the same
identity at each finite history, and integrate over that tape. Equivalently keep one unnormalized
identity for every observer-memory state. The simulator’s hidden state is not an extra input to this
policy. Thus all finite stopped cylinders agree. The events of survival through n calls decrease to
nontermination; their equal probabilities give equality there as well. Bounded terminal measures
follow by summing disjoint finite stopping events. Nonnegative path costs follow from equality of
the path law and monotone convergence.
P
   For mean observables, the expected cost contribution at a begun history is tr(Ga Xh ) =
   i ph (i)ca (i). Sum these identities first to a finite horizon and then monotonically to the stopping
time. No total-variation bound on bounded terminal scores is used to infer an unbounded
waiting-cost bound.                                                                                   □
   This theorem realizes the prepared experiment, not an arbitrary entangled quantum input channel.
It gives no affine encoding of every density matrix into one simplex and does not grant the hidden
simulator label to the original observer. For a single prescribed initial state one anchor suffices. If
d = 1, the original scalar instrument already has one state. If η = 1, every nonzero output is I/d,
so one core state plus the preparation anchors suffices without a projective net.
Corollary S22.3 (Same-policy resource and risk invariance). The original instrument and its
hidden classical realization have identical feasible expected-cost budgets and identical risks for each
common report-based observer. Consequently their optimal risk curves agree at every fixed observer
memory bound and every fixed decoder dictionary, including feasible boundary budgets. The assertion
also holds modelwise for an unknown family satisfying one uniform margin, with model-dependent
hidden transition tables and the same report interface; the parameter is not supplied to the observer.
If the prepared state varies with the model, it is represented by one designated model-dependent
anchor and a common pure-state core.
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        75


   An observer with ℓ live labels coupled to an M -state simulator has at most ℓM joint live states.
These are joint system states, not a claim that M is the observer’s optimal memory. Giving the
hidden label to the observer changes the statistical experiment and invalidates the claimed same-policy
comparison.
Proof. Theorem S22.2 equates the cost and risk of every admissible observer without changing
its transition rows, dictionary or report information. Taking the same constrained infimum gives
the assertion, even when the infimum is not attained in that memory class. For a model family
use the same core hull in every model and solve its branch identities separately; each modelwise
equality holds for the same observer. Taking the supremum over the unknown model therefore
preserves the robust objective and constraints. The product count records the two distinct retained
coordinates.                                                                                     □
S22.3. A matching exponent for state-positive universal realization. Let vd (η) be the
least number of arbitrary density matrices whose convex hull contains Dd (η). Mixed vertices are
permitted. This number measures uniform state-positive representation, not the smallest hidden
model for one selected scalar response.
Theorem S22.4 (Sharp noise exponent at fixed physical dimension). For d ≥ 2 and 0 < η ≤ 1/2,
put s = η(1 − 1/d). Then                       l          m
                                   s−(d−1) ≤ vd (η) ≤ (4d/η)d−1 .                               (S22.6)
Thus the exponent d − 1 as η ↓ 0 is necessary and sufficient at each fixed d. Constants in this
comparison depend on d.
   For every 0 < η ≤ 1/4 there is also a finite-command, unit-call-cost prepared instrument satisfying
(S22.2) for which every state-positive exact realization preserving all terminal effects needs at least
                                                     d−1
                                              4/(5η)                                            (S22.7)
hidden states. Its command count may grow as the margin decreases. No lower bound of this
exponent for unrestricted, possibly non-state-positive, response realizations is asserted.
Proof. The upper is Lemma S22.1. For the lower, for each unit u consider the state (1−η)uu∗ +ηI/d.
Pairing a convex representation in density vertices τj with uu∗ shows that at least one vertex satisfies
⟨u, τj u⟩ ≥ 1 − s.
   For any fixed density matrix τ , this inequality holds on a set of projective measure at most sd−1 .
If its largest eigenvalue a is below 1 − s, the set is empty. Otherwise a ≥ 1 − s > 1/2 and its top
eigenvector v is unique. With x = |⟨u, v⟩|2 , all other eigenvalues are at most 1 − a, so
                                    ⟨u, τ u⟩ ≤ ax + (1 − a)(1 − x).
To reach 1 − s one needs x ≥ (a − s)/(2a − 1) ≥ 1 − s; the last inequality is equivalent to
(1 − a)(1 − 2s) ≥ 0. The projective ball formula from the lemma now bounds the measure by sd−1 .
The caps associated with all vertices cover every ray, so their measures sum to at least one. This
proves the first lower.
   For the finite-command assertion take a finite projective η/4-net of rays uj and a command for
each replacement channel
                                Φj (X) = tr(X) (1 − η)uj u∗j + ηI/d .
                                                                     

These are valid completely positive, trace-preserving maps with the required margin, one deterministic
report and unit cost. Exactness of every terminal effect after that command forces its output matrix
to be a convex combination of the simulator’s density vertices. Thus at each net point some vertex
has expectation at least 1 − s. For an arbitrary ray u choose a net point within η/4. Since the
operator norm distance of the two projectors is at most η/4, pairing with any density matrix
changes the expectation by at most η/4. The caps at level 1 − s − η/4 therefore cover all rays. Here
76                                                QIAN QI


s + η/4 ≤ 5η/4 < 1/2, and the preceding cap bound gives at least (s + η/4)−(d−1) ≥ (4/(5η))d−1
vertices. This is a finite physical family, not an uncountable list disguised as a finite input. □
   For unrestricted classical hidden models a single scalar response may admit non-quantum inter-
mediate response vectors. The state-positive lower does not exclude that possibility. Conversely, a
hidden decoder defined as a positive normalized affine functional of every terminal effect is necessarily
of the form E 7→ tr(Eτ ) for a density matrix τ : extend the affine functional linearly from the effect
interval to the Hermitian space, use the trace pairing, and test rank-one positive effects. This
explains the positivity requirement in the count rather than hiding it in an abstract factorization.

S22.4. Sparse algebraic construction and several-step interiority.
Proposition S22.5 (Finite algebraic construction with sparse branch rows). For a finite instrument
family and finite preparation list with real-algebraic entries and a supplied algebraic margin η > 0,
a state-positive realization can be constructed by a terminating real-algebraic procedure. One may
use the size bound (S22.4), and all generator matrices and transition probabilities can be chosen
algebraic. At most d2 successors per command/report row are needed. The original margin condition
is itself a finite quantified real-algebraic condition on pure inputs; a positive rational margin, if one
exists, can be found by enumeration and such tests.
   This is a finite synthesis assertion, not a polynomial-time bound. The retained-state count, the
number and algebraic encoding lengths of table entries, and the resources of exact random sampling
remain different quantities.
Proof. The existence proof uses at most K = ⌈(4d/η)d−1 ⌉ pure core projectors and the stated
anchors. Treat their real Hermitian coordinates and all branch coefficients as variables. Impose
Pj ⪰ 0, tr Pj = 1, Pj2 = Pj , the fixed anchor identities, nonnegative normalized rows, and the
finitely many matrix equations (S22.3). These are polynomial conditions over the real algebraic
field; positivity can be expressed by principal minors. The preceding theorem proves feasibility.
Real-closed-field elimination and sample-point construction give an algebraic solution [15]. Row
sparsity can be obtained afterwards by elimination of an affine dependence over the same algebraic
field, or by a finite disjunction of supports of size at most d2 .
   For margin testing, it suffices by linearity to quantify over pure inputs uu∗ with ∥u∥ = 1; the
output inequality is again a principal-minor condition in real and imaginary coordinates. If some
real positive margin works, a smaller positive rational margin works. Enumeration of positive
rational candidates with an exact test therefore eventually succeedsPon the strict-interiority class. No
termination is asserted when no positive margin exists. For J = a |Ya |, a sparse implementation
stores at most JM d2 nonzero branch probabilities, besides M d2 density coordinates and any chosen
audit table. These are storage counts of mathematical entries, not bit bounds for their encodings. □
Theorem S22.6 (Interiority after a finite block, with its clock counted). Suppose instead that
for some integer L ≥ 1, every length-L command/report branch   product satisfies (S22.2) with the
same margin. Individual branches need not do so. Put J = a |Ya |. Then an exact state-positive
                                                          P

realization exists with at most
                                             l            m L−1
                                                              X
                                           k + (4d/η)d−1            Jj                           (S22.8)
                                                             j=0
hidden states. It preserves intermediate reports and stopping within the block; it does not replace L
calls by one unpriced call.
Proof. Start with the core projectors and preparation anchors. For each, list its normalized nonzero
images under all command/report products of length less than L, retaining the product as part
of the hidden label. There are at most the displayed number. At a depth below L − 1, an actual
branch goes to its listed child with its actual conditional probability. At depth L − 1, the next
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                          77


product output is either zero or belongs to Dd (η); decompose it into the core and restart at depth
zero. Zero branches have zero rows. These transitions satisfy the same matrix interpolation identity
at every single call. Theorem S22.2’s history induction therefore applies without suppressing any
intermediate observation, audit or charge. The depth is encoded in the enlarged state label, not in
an external clock.                                                                                □
S22.5. Why forgetting the report invalidates the premise. The average channel can be
completely depolarizing even though every recorded branch sends pure states to pure states. The
Pauli instrument IP (X) = P XP ∗ /4 on a qubit is an example. Thus an average-state calculation
cannot verify (S22.2).
   A stronger example has, in addition, a recorded irrational rotation. Mix half of that Pauli
instrument with the single recorded branch Iu (X) = U XU ∗ /2, where
                                                              2 −1
                                                                 
                                                   −1/2
                                           U =5                    .
                                                              1 2
Its average sends every density matrix to at least I/4, but the report word un retains the pure state
U n |0⟩⟨0|U ∗n . These states are all distinct: eiϑ = (3 + 4i)/5 is not a root of unity, since 2 cos ϑ = 6/5
would otherwise be a rational algebraic integer. A finite state-positive simulator preserving all
terminal effects would represent every one of these pure states as a convex combination of its finite
density vertices. An extreme point of D2 can only be represented by identical density matrices,
so every distinct orbit state would have to occur as a vertex. This is impossible. Thus average
interiority does not even guarantee finite state-positive realization, while all finite report words here
have well-defined nonzero probabilities.
   The example is a boundary qualification of the positive theorem. It is not used in place of that
theorem, and no unrestricted classical hidden-state impossibility is inferred from this particular
extreme-state proof alone.
S22.6. An explicit unrestricted memory obstruction at weak damping. The preceding
matching exponent is for state-positive realization. A different argument gives a quantitative, albeit
non-sharp, obstruction for every stochastic realization of the fixed-angle scalar qubit response. It
sharpens the qualitative divergence in Theorem S27.6 without changing its exact three-label result.
Lemma S22.7 (A finite near-peripheral spectral witness). If an M × M row-stochastic matrix has
a nonzero eigenvalue z = αeiθ , 0 < α ≤ 1, then either 1 − α > 1/(2M ) or some 1 ≤ l ≤ M satisfies
                                              √             √

The assertion does not require irreducibility, reversible rows or a lower bound on all positive transition

                              E|xXn − z n |2 = E|xXn |2 − |z|2n ≤ 1 − α2n .

   Noise-dependent classical-memory obstructions for quantum processes precede this result. In
particular Fanizza, Lumbreras and Winter prove a worst-angle lower bound for noisy qubit processes,
conditional on positive realizations for the whole phase family [50]. Their statement is not a lower
rate for the one fixed angle in this corollary, nor does our weaker fixed-angle estimate replace it.
The new general contribution of this section is the branch-resolved constructive realization and its
stopped-resource identity, together with the state-positive dimension exponent. The established
cone and spectral mechanisms are credited rather than renamed.

                   S23. Boundary stopping and finite stochastic realization
   The weighted potential identity and the cost-active support criterion of Theorem S27.1 already
allow model- and state-dependent reports. We now replace that sufficient coverage condition by a
necessary-and-sufficient stopping criterion. We first characterize rigidity for prepared instruments
with model- and state-dependent report probabilities. On the resulting boundary face, ties lead to a
stochastic realization problem, not in general a deterministic residual quotient. The optimal-face
and anchored convex realization theorems are inherited in full as Theorems S27.2 and S27.3. Their
pointed-polytope formulation below extends them to the larger rigidity class; neither the convex
realization method nor the original robust-tie classification is claimed as new in this continuation.
S23.1. A primitive class and an exact boundary stopping criterion. Let Θ be compact
metric and let the protected physical space have finite dimension d. There is one globally legal
command, a finite failure alphabet Y , and one productive herald s. Model θ has a prepared density
matrix ρθ and completely positive maps Iθ,y and Iθ,s whose sum is trace preserving. All these
data are continuous in θ. A nonnegative call cost has conditional mean tr(Gθ ρ), with continuous
Hermitian Gθ satisfying
                                     0 < c∗ I ≼ Gθ ≼ c∗ I < ∞.
A failure forbids commitment. A herald permits commitment to a member of one fixed finite
dictionary C, or rejection and continuation from the actual post-herald state. The full report is the
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                              79


only physical observation available to the controller. No extra cost observation, reset, state inspection
or free clock is supplied. After commitment an unobserved prospective audit has continuous loss
observables 0 ≼ Fθ,r ≼ LI, r ∈ C. A separately executed fixed final-audit cost is added to all
budgets.
   Put Tθ = y Iθ,y and impose the primitive transience test
             P

                                       I − (Tθ∗ )d I ≻ 0    (θ ∈ Θ).                                    (S23.1)
This condition does not require scalar herald effects. Define
               δ = min λmin I − (Tθ∗ )d I > 0,         κ = c∗ d/δ,                       (Tθ∗ )n Gθ .
                                                                                  X
                                                                            Hθ =
                     θ
                                                                                   n≥0

For T ≥ 0, set βT = (1 − δ)⌊T /d⌋ , interpreting the zeroth power as one even when δ = 1. For a
failure word w = y1 · · · yn , let Iθ,w = Iθ,yn · · · Iθ,y1 , with the identity map at the empty word. Its
first-herald mass and terminal score are
                                                                  
                                      λθ (w) = tr Iθ,s Iθ,w (ρθ ) ,                                     (S23.2)
                                                                       

In particular 0 ≤ gθ (w, r) ≤ Lλθ (w). These are unnormalized quantities; null reports are never
divided out.
Lemma S23.1 (Uniform first-herald control from the instrument). First-herald commitment is
proper from every physical state in every model, has conditional remaining cost at most κ, and
has survival mass after T failures at most βT . Its expected cost from the declared preparation is
tθ = tr(Hθ ρθ ), continuous in θ. The least robust feasible cost is
                                               N0 = max tθ .
                                                      θ∈Θ

Moreover, (S23.1) is equivalent, modelwise, to absence of a nonzero subspace invariant under every
failure Kraus operator and annihilated by the herald effect.
Proof. For one model write B = I − T ∗ I = Is∗ I and On = I − (T ∗ )n I. Then On+1 = B + T ∗ On .
For Kraus operators Lj of T , positivity gives ker On+1 = ker B ∩ j L−1
                                                                     T
                                                                         j (ker On ). The decreasing
kernels stabilize at their first equality and can strictly decrease at most d times. Their stabilized
space is exactly the failure-invariant dark subspace in the statement. This proves the equivalence
without choosing a normalized posterior.
   Continuity and compactness give δ > 0. The inequality (Tθ∗ )d I ≼ (1 − δ)I iterates, uniformly
over θ, to the displayed survival bound. Summing in blocks of d calls bounds expected cost by
c∗ d/δ. The cost series converges uniformly in operator norm; hence Hθ and tθ are continuous. Every
legal committed trajectory contains all calls through its first herald. Nonnegative additional costs
therefore make its expectation at least tθ in that model. First-herald commitment attains all these
modelwise costs simultaneously, proving the formula for N0 and its attainability.                   □
  Let W = {w : λθ (w) > 0 for some θ}. Define the normalized unused-budget margin
                                                    N0 − tθ
                                 a(w) =       inf                (w ∈ W).                               (S23.4)
                                          θ:λθ (w)>0 λθ (w)

This is a criterion on the primitive first-herald experiment, not a condition on an unknown optimal
decision policy.
Theorem S23.2 (Necessary and sufficient boundary stopping rigidity). Every robust policy of
expected cost at most N0 commits at the first herald in every model, up to null histories, if and only
if a(w) = 0 for every w ∈ W.
80                                                 QIAN QI


     More precisely, if rw is its conditional probability of rejecting its first herald after w, then
                                                c∗ rw ≤ a(w).                                       (S23.5)
If a(w) > 0 for some word, there is a finite-label boundary-feasible policy that rejects that first herald
with positive probability and then commits at the next herald. Thus failure of the criterion has an
actual legal, paid implementation.
Proof. Before the first herald the sole command is fixed. Conditional on its full word, the private
controller randomness is independent of the physical state and of the model: it changes no executed
instrument. Consequently the rejection probability rw is the same in all models. On that event at
least one further call is required, whose conditional expected cost is at least c∗ . Decomposing the
nonnegative cost at the first herald gives
                                                          X
                                        Eθ Cτ ≥ tθ + c∗       λθ (w)rw .
                                                          w
Keeping one word and using feasibility proves (S23.5). If every a(w) vanishes, rejection occurs with
probability zero in each model; countability of words suffices.
   Conversely choose a word with a(w) > 0 and set e = min{1, a(w)/(2κ)} > 0. Accept every first
herald except that, after precisely this word, reject with probability e and then accept the next
herald. The extra conditional cost after rejection is at most κ, from the actual post-herald state, by
Lemma S23.1. Thus total cost in each model is at most tθ + eκλθ (w) ≤ N0 . A finite word matcher,
a default accepting label and one post-rejection label implement this rule. Its only persistent
information is those counted labels. This proves necessity and the realization assertion.           □
Corollary S23.3 (Active preparation coverage). The rigidity condition holds if every w ∈ W
has positive mass in at least one cost-active model θ with tθ = N0 . In particular it holds if one
cost-active model has positive mass at every possible word. It also holds when the first-herald costs
are equal across models. A positive definite herald effect and failure maps that send every nonzero
positive input to a nonzero output give positive mass to every word, and therefore imply rigidity
under (S23.1).
Proof. An active positive-mass model makes the ratio in (S23.4) zero. For the last assertion, a finite
failure composition has nonzero positive output, and pairing it with a positive definite herald effect
gives positive trace. Lemma S23.1 supplies an active model.                                         □
   The infimum in (S23.4) matters. Rigidity may also follow from models approaching the active
set with both unused budget and word mass tending to zero, even if no active model gives that
word positive mass. Conversely a word supported only where there is enough relative budget slack
can permit boundary rejection. The theorem classifies stopping rigidity in this single-command
uniformly transient class; it does not claim that a residual decision rule classifies multi-command
boundary control.
Example S23.4 (Active coverage is sufficient but not necessary). In dimension one take θ ∈ [0, 1/2],
h(0) = 0, h(θ) = e−1/θ for θ > 0, and herald probability pθ = 1/(4 − h(θ)). Give failures 1, 0
probabilities (1 − pθ )θ and (1 − pθ )(1 − θ), with unit calls. Then N0 = 4 and the only cost-active
model is 0, which gives no mass to words containing 1. Nevertheless for a word of length n containing
j occurrences of 1,
                              N0 − tθ                e−1/θ
                                       =                              −→ 0.
                               λθ (w)     pθ (1 − pθ )n θj (1 − θ)n−j
Indeed the factors other than θj have positive limits, and e−1/θ /θj ≤ (j + 1)!θ by the exponential
series. Words with no 1 already have positive active-model mass. The exact criterion therefore gives
boundary rigidity even though active support coverage fails. All primitive data are continuous and
the herald probability is uniformly positive. No algebraic-data claim is made for this example.
                                      CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         81


Example S23.5 (A paid rejection remains feasible at the robust least budget). Take two scalar
models with unit costs. Model A has herald probability 1/4 and only failure 0; model B has
herald probability 1/2 and only failure 1. The robust least budget is 4. For the word consisting of
one 1, its mass is 1/4 in B and zero in A, so a(1) = 8. Accept the first herald except that after
precisely this word reject with probability 1/2, then accept the next herald. Its cost is 4 in A and
2 + (1/4)(1/2)2 = 9/4 in B. A four-label matcher implements it. Thus a robust minimum-cost
boundary need not eliminate stopping freedom merely because first-herald operation is modelwise
cheapest.
S23.2. The entire robust optimal face, including ties. Assume now that a(w) = 0 on W. At
N0 every feasible policy is a kernel b : Y ∗ → ∆(C) with risk
                                                   X
                                        Rθ (b) =         b(w, r)gθ (w, r).
                                                   w,r

Its values on globally null words can be completed
                                             R     arbitrarily. Define V = minb supθ Rθ (b). For a
probability measure µ on Θ, write gµ (w, r) = gθ (w, r) dµ(θ) and Aµ (w) = argminr∈C gµ (w, r).
Theorem S23.6 (Exact robust optimal face). Primal and dual optima are attained, and
                                                       X
                                        V = max            min gµ (w, r).                              (S23.6)
                                            µ∈P(Θ) w r∈C

For any attaining µ∗ , the complete set of robust optimal kernels is
                  B∗ = {b : b(w, ·) ∈ ∆(Aµ∗ (w)) (w ∈ Y ∗ ),           Rθ (b) ≤ V (θ ∈ Θ)} .           (S23.7)
The global inequalities in (S23.7) cannot in general be omitted. Wordwise Bayes ties do not authorize
an arbitrary robust decoder.
Proof. The product of finite decision simplexes is compact convex. Every risk tail beyond length
T is at most LβT , uniformly in the kernel and model. Finite sums are jointly continuous, so the
full payoff is continuous and bilinear in the kernel and model measure. Compact minimax, or
finite-prefix separation followed by compact limits with this uniform tail, gives (S23.6) and both
attainments [40]. The fixed-measure minimization separates wordwise. For an optimal kernel,
                      X                                                Z
                                                                 
                 0≤         b(w, r) gµ∗ (w, r) − min gµ∗ (w, u) =            Rθ (b) dµ∗ − V ≤ 0.
                                                   u
                      w,r

Every summand is nonnegative, so all positive decoder weights are on minimizing actions. No
strictly positive common word law is needed: zero-mass words simply impose no nontrivial local
restriction. The all-model inequalities are necessary; together they imply optimality by the definition
of V . This proves the characterization. The physical constructions in Theorem S24.1 below show
why local ties alone are insufficient.                                                                □
