S23.3. What replaces a finite deterministic residual quotient. Let F = (∆(C))Y . Elements
are full decoder-response functions, not hidden physical states. Define the affine shift Sy f (z) = f (yz).
A finite-dimensional polytope P ⊂ F is called invariant here when Sy P ⊂ P for every failure letter.
It is the convex hull of finitely many response functions; its extreme points are consequently finite.
For b ∈ P put
                                    h(P, b) = | ext P | + 1{b∈ext
                                                             /    P }.
The extra term records a prescribed pure initial label rather than an uncharged random initial state.
Theorem S23.7 (Exact finite stochastic-memory criterion with robust ties). The least live-label
count attaining V at budget N0 is exactly
                   min{h(P, b) : b ∈ B∗ , b ∈ P ⊂ F, P a finite invariant polytope},                   (S23.8)
82                                               QIAN QI


with value infinity when no such pair exists. This equivalence includes stochastic transitions,
randomized terminal decisions, impossible words, and all robust ties. It requires one common
realization, not a different machine in each model.
   For a deterministic kernel forced uniquely on every word, the criterion reduces to the number of
distinct right residuals z 7→ b(wz), recovering the classical minimal output-machine count. Without
uniqueness, a finite number of deterministic residual classes is neither the right requirement on an
optimal randomized kernel nor a substitute for (S23.8).
   If a realizing observer has M live labels and m distinct actual decoder values, event stopping
uses at most max{M, m} total labels and state stopping at most M + m. These are the established
packing counts, not extra live memory or a hidden clock.
Proof. An M -label first-herald observer has row-stochastic matrices Qy and decoder rows Di ∈ ∆(C),
from one prescribed initial label. Let fi (z) be its decision distribution on a future herald after failure
word z, when initialized in label i. Then
                                                             X
                                 fi (∅) = Di ,     Sy fi =       Qy (i, j)fj .
                                                             j

Thus P = conv{f1 , . . . , fM } is invariant and contains its initial response b as one listed point. It has
at most M extreme points. If b is not extreme, it is itself a listed nonextreme point, so h(P, b) ≤ M
still holds. Its optimality puts b in B∗ . This proves necessity and the lower count.
   Conversely list the vertices of an admissible polytope, adding b as an initial label only when
it is not already a vertex. Every shift of each listed function belongs to P , and hence has fixed
convex coefficients in those vertices. Choose these coefficients as its row of Qy . Decode at a herald
using that function’s value at the empty word. Induction on word length proves that the machine’s
complete response from each label is the listed function. In particular its initial response is b. This
produces exactly h(P, b) labels, commits at the first herald and attains V . No physical state or
posterior is read. Pruning unreachable labels cannot invalidate either implication or a minimal
count.
   When b is deterministic, each distinct residual is an extreme point of the entire product F: every
coordinate is a vertex of ∆(C). Invariance puts every residual in P , so each must be one of its
vertices. Conversely a finite set of distinct residuals is closed under the shifts and realizes the
usual deterministic output machine. Its convex hull has precisely these vertices. This proves the
reduction. The terminal counts follow by independently packing continuing and final roles as in
Lemma 11.4.                                                                                               □
   Theorem S23.7 is an exact geometric existence criterion in response-function space. It is not a
finite numerical test for arbitrary data, and it does not assume that a minimal stochastic realization
lies in the affine span of the reached residuals. Such an assumption could discard valid hidden-state
realizations. Positive realization by invariant cones or convex sets, and the distinction between
linear rank and positive realization, have a long history [48, 49]. The new mathematical use here is
the explicit least-budget condition, the robust tied optimal face, the pure-initial-label accounting,
and the physical realization and obstruction results which follow.
Proposition S23.8 (Fixed-memory attainment and boundary approximation). For every prescribed
M , the least boundary risk among stochastic observers with at most M live labels is attained. If
(S23.8) exceeds M , this risk is strictly larger than V . Nevertheless, a finite prefix of an optimal
kernel, followed by one arbitrary decoder label, Pcosts exactly N0 in the robust sense and has excess
                                                     −1
risk at most LβT . A sufficient live count is 1 + Tn=0  |Y |n . Modelwise cost equals tθ , not necessarily
N0 in every model.
Proof. Transition matrices and decoder rows at fixed M range over compact simplexes. Finite-prefix
risks are continuous and their uniform tail is at most LβT . Thus worst-model risk is continuous and
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         83


takes its minimum. Equality with V is equivalent to an optimal realization by Theorem S23.7. A
prefix tree storing words shorter than T , followed by a common tail label, changes only terminal mass
bounded by βT in every model. It still accepts the first herald and hence has the same modelwise
costs tθ . This proves all assertions without taking an interior-budget completion theorem to zero
slack.                                                                                              □

          S24. Squared-prediction realizations of robust stochastic memory
   A local Bayes tie can conceal global constraints on the entire randomized decision law. We
construct such constraints using a fixed finite-dimensional instrument and a genuine squared terminal
audit, not a time-indexed loss oracle. The construction gives exact finite-memory thresholds of every
integer order, finite physical witnesses for strict smaller-memory gaps, and a finite-rank quantum
law which has no finite stochastic realization. It also permits an exact algebraic fixed-memory
optimization on a declared compact-model class.
S24.1. A physical embedding of a target channel response. Fix a channel Ψ on a d0 -
dimensional protected system, a preparation ρ, and an effect 0 ≼ E ≼ I. Its target response
is
                                 un = tr(EΨn (ρ)) ∈ [0, 1],       n ≥ 0.
Choose 0 < p < 1, q = 1 − p, 0 < t̄ < 1, and ε = 1/4. The unknown fixed model is (t, σ) ∈
[0, t̄] × {−1, 1}. Add one protected classical two-state register, initially alive. Its channel Dt discards
coherences and maps the alive population to alive with probability t, otherwise to dead, while dead
remains dead. Thus the real matrix entries of Dt depend polynomially on t. A failure applies
Dt ⊗ Ψ with probability q and a herald applies the identity with probability p. There is one unit-cost
command, one failure letter, and the fixed dictionary C = {−1, 1}.
   Let A be the alive projection and set T = 2E − I. Define two commuting Hermitian audit
moments on the combined protected space by
                             vσ = I − σεA ⊗ T,         mσ = −σε(A ⊗ I)/2.
For h = 3/2, use audit outcomes z ∈ {−h, 0, h} with effects
                        vσ + hmσ               vσ − hmσ
                Bσ,+ =        2
                                 ,     Bσ,− =             ,    Bσ,0 = I − vσ /h2 .        (S24.1)
                           2h                     2h2
The observer commits before this audit is performed and pays loss (z − r)2 . Its conditional loss
observable is
                                  Fσ,r = 2I + σεA ⊗ (rI − T ).                            (S24.2)
Theorem S24.1 (A robust tied squared-task embedding). The displayed data define a legal prepared
experiment with protected dimension 2d0 , bounded squared loss, and least cost 1/p. At that budget
its robust risk is exactly 2. Its unique optimal decision kernel is
                                  b(n, +1) = un ,     b(n, −1) = 1 − un .                          (S24.3)
For every fixed t, the equally weighted pair (t, +1), (t, −1) is a least-favorable measure under which
both actions are tied at every word. Nevertheless the all-model constraints force (S24.3), including
its fractional values.
   For an arbitrary first-herald kernel bn = b(n, +1), its exact worst-model excess is
                                                          X
                           sup Rt,σ (b) − 2 = 2εp sup         (qt)n (bn − un ) .                   (S24.4)
                            t,σ                     0≤t≤t̄ n≥0

Thus its exact finite stochastic-memory problem is the positive realization problem for the physically
generated response un . Algebraic input channel, state, effect and constants give algebraic-polynomial
primitive data.
84                                                QIAN QI


Proof. One has 3I/4 ≼ vσ ≼ 5I/4 and ∥hmσ ∥ ≤ 3/16. Consequently all three effects in (S24.1) are
positive and sum to I. Their first two moments are exactly mσ , vσ . Since r2 = 1, the squared-loss
observable is vσ − 2rmσ + I, which is (S24.2). The realized loss is bounded by (h + 1)2 = 25/4; its
expectation is not an arbitrary signed score.
   After n failures the alive mass is tn , independently of the target state Ψn ρ. The expected squared
loss at decision r is therefore 2 + σεtn [r − (2un − 1)]. First-herald commitment is forced at 1/p,
either by the indicator identity of Lemma S28.1 or by Theorem S23.2. Averaging the decision and
summing its terminal mass pq n gives (S24.4). The kernel (S24.3) makes every model’s risk exactly
2, while each symmetric pair of models averages to 2 for every kernel. This proves the value and the
stated least-favorable measures.
   If a robust optimum differs at some coefficient, let k be its first index with bk − uk ̸= 0. The
power series in (S24.4) vanishes on the interval by optimality. Divide it by (qt)k and let t ↓ 0. The
remaining tail tends to zero because its coefficients are bounded, forcing bk − uk = 0, a contradiction.
This proves uniqueness without assuming an analytic inverse oracle. The target physical channel is
not accessible to the observer except through the final audit. Its response must be reproduced by
the observer’s actual retained labels.                                                                □
   This theorem explains why the local simplexes in (S23.7) must be coupled by global risk inequalities.
In this family every local simplex is the whole ∆(C) under the stated least-favorable measure, but
there is only one robust optimal kernel.
   The classical Erlang-chain rank mechanism is inherited from Theorem S27.5. The additions here
are its realization by the same physical squared loss in every model and the finite physical witness
theorem, which no longer needs continuum model uncertainty for the exact memory lower.
S24.2. Every finite exact memory order occurs. For an integer k ≥ 2 and a rational 0 < a < 1,
take a classical target chain with k − 1 transient stages and one absorbing dead state. At each
transient stage it stays with probability a and advances to the next stage with probability 1 − a;
advancing from the last transient stage reaches dead. It starts at the first stage. Let E indicate the
transient states. Its survival response is
                                            k−2
                                                    !
                                            X     n
                                     un =           (1 − a)j an−j ,                             (S24.5)
                                            j=0
                                                  j
where a term is zero for j > n.
Theorem S24.2 (A squared-loss realization of the exact memory hierarchy). The squared-task
family of Theorem S24.1 built from this target has robust boundary value 2 and least exact live-label
count k. It has protected physical dimension 2k. A k-label stochastic observer realizes the optimum
at exactly 1/p cost. No observer with fewer live labels, even with arbitrary real stochastic transitions
and randomized readouts, attains it. No deterministic full-history decision rule attains it either.
   The full vector-valued Hankel response has rank exactly k, supplying the matching lower. Moreover,
a finite collection of rational physical model parameters gives an explicit positive risk gap for every
observer with fewer than k live labels, as specified in Proposition S24.3.
Proof. Use the target chain itself as the observer’s failure-transition matrix; at a herald, decode +1
in transient stages and −1 in dead. Its probability of +1 after n failures is (S24.5), so it attains the
value with k labels from a prescribed first label.
   For any M -label stochastic observer with initial row e1 , failure matrix Q and probability-of-+1
column d, its response is bn = e1 Qn d. The matrix with rows indexed by i and columns by suffixes
j and decoder outcomes has rank at most M , since each entry factors through e1 Qi . Include in
particular the constant column 1 and the columns ui+j for 0 ≤ j ≤ k − 2, on rows 0 ≤ i ≤ k − 1.
   These k columns are independent. Indeed un = an P (n) for a polynomial P of degree k − 2 with
nonzero leading coefficient. Its minimal shift annihilator is (X − a)k−1 : applying shift minus a lowers
                                    CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                      85


the polynomial degree by exactly one, until zero. Equivalently, the finite confluent Vandermonde
matrix with columns 1, ai , iai , . . . , ik−2 ai , 0 ≤ i < k, is nonsingular because 1 ̸= a. A direct
determinant proof is given in the supplement. The columns ui+j span the last k − 1 columns because
their translated degree-k − 2 polynomials are independent. Hence the displayed Hankel block is
nonsingular and every exact observer has at least k labels. The construction supplies the upper.
  At n = k − 1, the survival probability lies strictly between zero and one: both no advances and all
advances have positive probability. The unique optimal kernel is therefore fractional. A deterministic
decision rule, even one with unlimited history, cannot equal it. The remaining assertion follows from
the next proposition.                                                                               □
Proposition S24.3 (Finite physical witnesses for a smaller-memory gap). Let un ∈ [0, 1] be any
target response for which the k × k matrix
                 Au (i, 0) = 1,      Au (i, j + 1) = ui+j ,   0 ≤ i < k,         0≤j <k−1
has nonzero determinant, where k ≥ 2. Put K = 2k −3 and ∆ = | det Au |/[k!(k −1)] > 0. Let Wjn =
(j/K)n for 0 ≤ j, n ≤ K, taking 00 = 1, and C0 = ∥W −1 ∥∞ . Choose 0 < r ≤ min{q t̄, 1/2, ∆/(4C0 )},
put Zjn = (rj/K)n , and Cr = ∥Z −1 ∥∞ . Then every observer with fewer than k live labels has
worst-model risk at least
                                             2 + εp∆/Cr .                                      (S24.6)
It suffices to take the finite models tj = rj/(Kq) and both signs σ. For rational target data and
rational p, t̄, all these constants and witnesses can be chosen rational. This is an explicit positive
certificate, not an asserted sharp asymptotic memory rate.
Proof. The corresponding matrix Ab for an M < k label observer has rank at most M and
determinant zero. All entries lie in [0, 1]. The Leibniz formula and telescoping a product of k − 1
nonconstant entries give | det Au − det Ab | ≤ k!(k − 1) max0≤n≤K |un − bn |. Thus one coefficient
difference en =Pbn − un has magnitude at least ∆.
   Let h(z) = n≥0 en z n . At zj = rj/K, its tail after degree K is at most rK+1 /(1 − r). Inverting
the finite Vandermonde system gives
                                                                         !
                                                                  rK+1
                                  max |en | ≤ Cr   max |h(zj )| +            .
                                  n≤K               j             1−r
Since Z = W diag(1, r, . . . , rK ), one has Cr ≤ C0 r−K for r ≤ 1. Our choice implies Cr rK+1 /(1−r) ≤
2C0 r ≤ ∆/2. Consequently maxj |h(zj )| ≥ ∆/(2Cr ). Substitute this in the exact risk identity
(S24.4), with both signs present at each node. The proof keeps the full infinite tail, not merely a
fitted finite sample.                                                                                 □
Corollary S24.4 (Finite-model rational exact-memory hierarchy). For every integer k ≥ 2, there
is a finite family of algebraic, in fact rational-superoperator, prepared classical instruments with the
same fixed two-value dictionary and a genuine squared terminal audit, whose least-budget optimum
is 2 and whose exact least live-label count is k. A finite list of physical models gives an explicit
strictly positive risk gap for every smaller stochastic observer. The first compulsory call and all
subsequent calls remain charged. This finite-family statement does not assert uniqueness of the
full-history optimal kernel.
Proof. Take rational p, t̄, a in the preceding hierarchy, and retain just the finite parameter list of
Proposition S24.3, with both signs at every node. Symmetry lower-bounds every policy’s worst
risk by 2. The k-label target chain attains 2 at every retained model. The finite-witness lower
excludes every smaller memory. All maps and the finite audit effects have rational entries in the
classical diagonal representation. First-herald commitment still has the common cost 1/p, and the
same predictable identity forces it at the boundary. The finite subfamily need not identify every
coefficient of an arbitrary infinite decision kernel, which explains the final qualification.       □
86                                              QIAN QI


S24.3. The inherited rank obstruction under a common squared audit. Theorem S27.6
already establishes the rank-three obstruction for the signed calibration task. Here its same stochastic-
realization obstruction is transported, with full proof retained, to one common squared-loss audit.
The obstruction itself is not a newly discovered fact.
Theorem S24.5 (A rank-three protected quantum response with no finite realization). Take the
target qubit with
                                            1 2 −1
                                                           
                    ρ = E = |0⟩⟨0|,       U=√       ,               Ψ(A) = U AU ∗ .
                                              5 1 2
Its response is un = (1 + cos(nϑ))/2, where eiϑ = (3 + 4i)/5. The full two-outcome Hankel response
has rank three, but no finite stochastic observer realizes it exactly.
   The embedding theorem therefore gives a compact uncertain-model squared-prediction family
on protected dimension four whose unique full-history boundary optimum is randomized, whose
least-favorable wordwise actions are all tied, and whose exact live-memory count is infinite. At each
finite memory bound its optimal risk is strictly above 2. A prefix of length T , followed by one tail
label, has excess risk at most
                                                2εp(q t̄)T
                                                                                              (S24.7)
                                                 1 − q t̄
and costs exactly 1/p.
Proof. The response formula follows by the direct qubit rotation. Its three distinct exponential modes
are 1, ζ n , ζ̄ n , where ζ = (3 + 4i)/5. Their nonzero coefficients and the Vandermonde determinant
give Hankel rank three. The number ζ is not a root of unity: otherwise ζ + ζ̄ = 6/5 would be a
rational algebraic integer and hence an integer.
   If a finite stochastic matrix Q realized un = e1 Qn d, its generating function would be e1 (I −zQ)−1 d.
But
                                X                1         1            1
                                    un z n =         +           +            .
                                n≥0
                                             2(1 − z) 4(1 − ζz) 4(1 − ζ̄z)
The pole at z = ζ −1 forces ζ to be an eigenvalue of Q. Every unit-modulus eigenvalue of a finite
row-stochastic matrix is a root of unity. To verify the needed fact, choose a complex eigenvector
and a coordinate of maximal nonzero modulus. Equality in the stochastic triangle inequality forces
all positive successors to have the same modulus and phase multiplied by that eigenvalue. Following
positive edges reaches a directed cycle; the phase must return after its finite length. This gives a
root of unity, a contradiction.
   The embedding gives uniqueness of the optimal randomized kernel, and Proposition S23.8 gives
strict attained gaps at fixed finite memory. Matching un for n < T and assigning any tail probability
changes each coefficient by at most one. Summing the tail in (S24.4) gives (S24.7). There is no
change to first-herald stopping or its paid cost.                                                  □
   Irrational quantum rotations and the separation of linear from positive stochastic realizations are
established mechanisms, not new priority claims [48, 49]. The assertion here concerns their exact
robust squared-prediction embedding, all-tied least-favorable actions, fixed-dictionary boundary
optimality and real-valued stochastic-controller exclusion. It does not solve the sharp intermediate-
memory risk curve for the different noisy-qubit sign task in Theorem S28.10.
S24.4. Exact optimization at any prescribed finite memory. The invariant-polytope criterion
is not asserted effectively decidable in general. For a specified memory bound, however, the physical
instrument gives a finite algebraic optimization without an unproved exact posterior algorithm.
Theorem S24.6 (Algebraic fixed-memory boundary optimization). Suppose Θ is a nonempty
compact semialgebraic set over the real algebraic numbers, and the real Hermitian-coordinate entries
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        87


of all prepared states, instruments, costs and loss observables are algebraic-polynomial functions of
its parameters. Assume (S23.1) and boundary rigidity. For each prescribed live bound M , the exact
robust boundary optimum VM is an attained real algebraic number and is computable by real-algebraic
elimination. An attaining stochastic controller can be chosen with real algebraic entries.
                                                                           ∗ I ≻ 0 and I ∗ I ≻ 0 for
   A completely primitive, decidable sufficient subclass for rigidity is Iθ,s            θ,y
every θ, y; compactness then also gives uniform first-step transience. These conditions allow model-
and state-dependent report probabilities. Both squared-task embeddings above belong to this subclass.
   For the same algebraic data, V is a computable real via finite-prefix games with the explicit LβT
tail. Whenever VM > V , finite computations eventually certify that strict inequality. No general
terminating test for the equality VM = V , no test that some finite M attains V , and no polynomial
complexity for quantifier elimination is inferred.
Proof. Write the controller variables as row-stochastic Qy and decoder probabilities Di (r), with
initial label 1. For each model its exact score potentials are the unique Hermitian solution of

Indeed the associated positive continuation map on block observables sends the identity tuple, at its
nth power, to the tuple with common entry (Tθ∗ )n I. Its norm on Hermitian tuples is therefore at
most βn . Its Neumann series converges, giving uniqueness and 0 ≼ Ki ≼ LI. The risk is exactly
tr(ρθ K1 ). This proves the equation’s meaning and boundedness from the raw instrument, not by

                ∀θ ∈ Θ ∃(K1 , . . . , KM ) :         (S24.8),   0 ≼ Ki ≼ LI,     tr(ρθ K1 ) ≤ v.
In real coordinates these are polynomial equations and inequalities; positive semidefiniteness is
expressed by principal minors. Projection and quantifier elimination over the real algebraic field
produce the exact feasible value set [15]. Compact controller space and the uniform tail prove its
lower endpoint is attained. A finite semialgebraic endpoint over that field is algebraic, and the
nonempty optimal-controller fiber contains an algebraic point. This gives the value and a controller
by a finite elimination procedure. The M d2 modelwise potential coordinates are analytic variables
in this computation, not runtime observer memory.
   Positive definiteness of the primitive effects is a finite quantified algebraic condition. It implies
positive mass at every word, and compactness bounds the herald effect below by a common positive
multiple of I. Corollary S23.3 therefore gives rigidity. The general theorem’s rigidity premise is not
claimed algorithmically checked merely by writing the infinite-word criterion.
   Finally, truncate the decision kernel to words shorter than T . The finite-prefix robust minimum
is an algebraic quantified problem of the same kind, now with one decision row per word. Its value
is below V and within LβT of it because any prefix can be completed by a first-herald tail decoder.
A rational positive lower certificate for δ can be found by increasing-precision algebraic tests, so
these are effective converging intervals. Once the exact algebraic value VM is strictly above V , some
finite upper interval endpoint for V is strictly below VM . This certifies nonattainment at that fixed
bound. In the equality case that search need not terminate.                                           □

       S25. A constructive finite-budget frontier from primitive instruments
  The geometric transfer theorem identifies a geometric curve when its converse and construction
certificates are available. We now give a different, intrinsic route for finite time-homogeneous
experiments: derive a proper fallback from the raw instruments and compute converging bounds on
the optimal task risk itself. Neither a reset state, a balancing group, a supplied reference member
nor a posterior stored by the controller is assumed. The finite and all-initial-state qualifications
88                                                      QIAN QI


are essential. The qualitative invariant-subspace obstruction has a direct antecedent in quantum
reachability [28, Theorem 3.6]; it is proved below to obtain the quantitative fallback required by the
new budget-preserving approximation.
S25.1. Primitive interface and universal termination. The unknown model belongs to a
nonempty finite set Θ. Model θ has physical space Hθ of dimension dθ . The nonempty finite
command set A is globally legal. A command has completely positive report maps Iθ,a,y with
normalized sum. Reports range over finite sets, with a declared productive subset Ya+ . After a
productive report a controller may commit a decision from a fixed dictionary C or reject; after
any rejected or nonproductive report the post-report state is the next state. No additional return
or reset is available. An interface requiring extra physical return calls must first represent and
charge those calls explicitly; the theorem is not applied by hiding them in an unpriced transition.
Commitment occurs exactly once, before a future audit is observed. The audit loss for decision
r ∈ C is represented on the post-report state by
                                     0 ≼ Fθ,a,y,r ≼ LI,             0 < L < ∞.
Nonnegative call costs have observables c∗ I ≼ Gθ,a ≼ c∗ I, with 0 < c∗ ≤ c∗ < ∞. These are the
precommit core costs. A final audit with one fixed separately charged cost is added to every total
budget after the theorem; an action-dependent unmodelled terminal charge is not included by
assertion. Policies share one set of command and decision rules across models and unknown initial
density matrices. Let RC (N ) be their least worst-model, worst-initial-state expected terminal loss
at uniform expected cost at most N . Their precommit memory is unrestricted for this value; a
finite-label implementation is derived, not assumed.
   Fix any command distribution πa > 0. The fallback chooses that row independently at every call
and commits at the first productive report, using one fixed r0 ∈ C. Its killed physical map is
                                                       Bθ = I − Tθ∗ I,         Oθ,n = I − (Tθ∗ )n I.
                          X         X
                   Tθ =       πa            Iθ,a,y ,
                          a          / a+
                                   y ∈Y

A common dark subspace in one model is a nonzero subspace annihilated by every productive effect
and invariant under every Kraus operator of every nonproductive report map. This condition is
independent of the Kraus representation: it says that every positive state on the subspace remains
there on failure and has zero productive probability for every command.
Theorem S25.1 (Primitive fallback and its sharp depth test). The following are equivalent: there
is one common policy proper at finite uniform expected cost from every model and initial density
matrix; no model has a common dark subspace; and Oθ,dθ is positive definite for every θ. In that
case the displayed one-row fallback is proper. With
                              δθ = λmin (Oθ,dθ ) > 0,               κ = max c∗ dθ /δθ ,
                                                                           θ
it costs at most κ from every current physical state in every model. One may replace this bound by
the sharper certified value
                                     κ = max (I − Tθ∗ )−1
                                                                    X
                                                                         πa Gθ,a .
                                                θ
                                                                     a
Only one live label and one output value are needed; these share a label in event stopping and
require two labels in state stopping. Depth d cannot be replaced uniformly by d − 1. There is no
dimension-only upper bound on expected cost.
Proof. Work in one model. Put Kn = ker On , so K0 = H and On+1 = B + T ∗ On . For a Kraus
representation T (A) = v Lv AL∗v , positivity gives
                      P

                                                               \
                                            Kn+1 = ker B ∩            v Kn .
                                                                v
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        89


The spaces decrease. Equality at two consecutive levels makes that space invariant under every Lv
and annihilated by B, hence makes all later levels equal. There are at most d strict dimension drops,
so Kd is stable. Since every πa is positive, ker B is the intersection of the productive-effect kernels,
and the Lv include nonzero multiples of every nonproductive Kraus operator. Thus a nonzero Kd is
a common dark subspace. Conversely every such subspace lies in all Kn . Starting in it, no history
policy can encounter a productive report: induction holds commandwise and reportwise. Such a
policy cannot commit legally.
   If Kd = 0, positivity yields (T ∗ )d I ≼ (1 − δ)I. Iteration bounds survival after kd fallback calls
by (1 − δ)k for every normalized input. Summing blocks gives expected calls at most d/δ and
expected cost at most c∗ d/δ. The finite model set makes the maximum bound finite. The same
estimate implies convergence of the positive cost series, whose sum is the stated resolvent; its largest
eigenvalue is the exact uniform cost for the fixed row. The fallback does not read the model or
physical state. Its single row and fixed decoder give the label counts.
   For sharp depth, on basis e1 , . . . , ed take one nonproductive Kraus operator L = dj=2 |ej−1 ⟩⟨ej |
                                                                                         P

and a productive effect |e1 ⟩⟨e1 |. Then On is the projection onto the first min(n, d) basis vectors. The
input ed requires exactly d unit-cost calls. For the lack of a cost bound, already in dimension one a
productive probability ϵ > 0 with unit costs has expected time 1/ϵ. This proves both limitations. □

   The qualitative obstruction is inherited mathematical machinery, not a new discovery of invariant
subspaces. Its purpose here is to supply an actual common fallback and its paid constant. Absence
of an invariant dark subspace on a restricted preparation is a different problem; the all-initial-state
hypothesis cannot be silently removed. Finiteness of Θ likewise cannot be replaced by modelwise
termination in an arbitrary family without a uniform bound.

S25.2. Finite problems and restoration of the exact budget. For a horizon T ≥ 1, use one
common realization plan on the action–report tree, as in Lemma S30.1. At every productive report
include either commitment to r ∈ C or continuation. Let Cθ,T , Sθ,T and Vθ,T be, respectively, its
expected cost through the first T calls, its open effect after the depth-T decision, and its accumulated
terminal-loss operator. They are affine functions of the common sequence weights, computed by
composing the primitive maps. In particular 0 ≼ Sθ,T ≼ I and 0 ≼ Vθ,T ≼ L(I − Sθ,T ).
   Define the finite semidefinite value
                 qC,T (N ) = min {q : Cθ,T + c∗ Sθ,T ≼ N I,         Vθ,T ≼ qI   (θ ∈ Θ)} .       (S25.1)
The minimum is over the common plan and 0 ≤ q ≤ L. The reserve c∗ S is not a cutoff declaration
of success: every open branch owes another paid call. For N ≥ κ, prefixes of the actual fallback
show feasibility. Compactness gives attainment. Set
                     DT (N ) = min{1, (N/c∗ − 1)/T },             eT = (κ − c∗ )DT (N ).
Theorem S25.2 (Same-budget constructive risk frontier). Under the preceding primitive conditions
with no common dark subspace, the finite values are nondecreasing in T and satisfy qC,T (N ) ≤ RC (N ).
For every N > κ,

   At N = κ the completed finite plan still gives cost at most N + eT and loss at most qC,T (N ) +

Proof. A genuine cost-N policy restricted to depth T satisfies the reserved cost inequality, because
after every open path it must make at least one further call. Its truncated terminal loss is no greater
than its full nonnegative loss. This proves the lower. A feasible plan at T + 1 restricts to one at T :
the new calls cost at least c∗ ST , new terminations reduce open mass, and their loss contributions are
nonnegative. The reserve and risk inequalities therefore survive restriction, proving monotonicity.

           C comp ≼ CT + κST ≼ (N + eT )I,            V comp ≼ VT + LST ≼ (qC,T + LDT )I.
It is proper because the finite prefix is followed by a proper fallback. There is no assertion that the
original policy’s expected remaining cost is uniformly small.
   For N > κ, choose the completed policy with probability p = (N − κ)/(N + eT − κ) and the
fallback with probability 1 − p, storing the branch in the ensuing live role. Its cost is at most
p(N +eT )+(1−p)κ = N . Its risk is at most p(qC,T +LDT )+(1−p)L ≤ qC,T +LDT +LeT /(N −κ+eT ).
If eT = 0 choose the completed policy directly. This proves the upper and convergence. The boundary
statements follow from the unmixed completion.
   A prefix stores its current action–report node, at most t<T (ay)t live roles; its current report is
                                                            P

within-call input. One fallback role and one initial mixture role suffice. At that initial call, sample
the branch and its command jointly; after the report either commit or enter the selected branch’s
counted next role. If both branches chose the same command, their conditional branch weights are
proportional to their command probabilities, independent of the physical state, since the executed
report instrument is identical. Equivalently use the joint within-call coin directly. No branch bit
survives outside the retained label. Commitment uses the same fixed dictionary in both branches.
Lemma 11.4 gives the two total counts.                                                               □
Corollary S25.3 (Compact decisions at a fixed output width). Suppose the decision carrier D is
compact metric and the raw audit losses obey the common decision modulus ω of (S32.1). Fix width
m. For a finite η-net Dη ⊂ D, let qm,T,η (N ) be the least value in (S25.1) among dictionaries in Dη
of size at most m. Then, for N > κ,
                                                                              LeT
              [qm,T,η (N ) − ω(η)]+ ≤ Rm (N ) ≤ qm,T,η (N ) + LDT (N ) +              .
                                                                           N − κ + eT
The constructed observer uses at most m terminal values. As T → ∞ and η ↓ 0 this gives a
two-parameter constructive characterization of the unrestricted-history finite-output risk, not just
a verifier for a supplied balancing program. With algebraic primitive data and polynomial score
dependence on algebraic net points, every finite test is a finite real-algebraic problem; exact real
samplers and program arithmetic remain separate implementation resources.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       91


Proof. Round each point of any competing dictionary to the same chosen nearest net point at every
history. Neither stopping nor cost changes, and at most m values remain. Its expected loss changes
by at most ω(η), because terminal events have total mass one. Restrict this rounded program to
a finite prefix to obtain the lower. For the upper, choose an attaining net dictionary and apply
Theorem S25.2; the fallback uses a point of this same dictionary. There are finitely many such
dictionaries, so the optimization is finite. With the stated algebraic inputs all coefficients of the
finite plan are algebraic, and semidefinite inequalities can be expressed by principal minors. This
gives decidability via the already credited real-algebraic method, not an efficient uniform algorithm
for arbitrary real input data.                                                                      □
S25.3. Removing the crude fallback threshold on every interior budget. The cost of an
arbitrary positive command row may greatly exceed the least feasible cost. It is not an intrinsic
resource threshold. The following preliminary optimization removes that restriction without assuming
the optimal policy is already known.
   Let κ0 be the uniform cost bound of the one-row fallback and let Hθ be its exact cost operator, so
0 ≼ Hθ ≼ κ0 I. Ignore terminal loss for this preliminary step and commit one fixed dummy decision.
Write Nmin for the infimum of uniform expected cost among proper common policies. For a finite
prefix define
                                                               ∗
                                              X
                               Wθ,T (Hθ ) =         γopen (h) Iθ,h (Hθ ).
                                             h: |h|=T
Here γopen includes the decision to continue after the depth-T report, and Iθ,h is its raw path map.
This is the exact cost of executing the fallback at the open leaves. Both W and the earlier CT , ST
are affine in the one common realization plan. Set
                          aT = min{a : Cθ,T + c∗ Sθ,T ≼ aI          (θ ∈ Θ)},
                          uT = min{u : Cθ,T + Wθ,T (Hθ ) ≼ uI          (θ ∈ Θ)}.
Theorem S25.4 (Constructive feasibility threshold and all interior budgets). Both finite problems
attain their values, and
                                c∗ ≤ aT ↑ Nmin , κ0 ≥ uT ↓ Nmin ,
with the quantitative enclosure
                          0 ≤ uT − aT ≤ (κ0 − c∗ ) min{1, (κ0 /c∗ − 1)/T }.                     (S25.3)
The least common proper cost Nmin is attained, though no finite-memory attainment is concluded at
that boundary.
   For every N > Nmin a finite search through these upper problems eventually exhibits uT0 < N .
Its optimizing prefix completed by the one-row fallback is an actual finite-label fallback of cost
κ = uT0 < N , valid from every initial state and model. Using it in Theorem S25.2 gives the
same-budget risk enclosure for every strictly interior feasible budget, not only budgets above the
original one-row cost. If this improved fallback has B live labels, the sufficient live bound becomes
                                                     −1
                                                    TX
                                          1+B+            (ay)t .
                                                    t=0
All decisions may still use one prescribed dictionary; the cost search is independent of its values.
Algebraic primitive data give finite real-algebraic upper tests, but no uniform efficient bit-complexity
claim is made.
Proof. The prefix of a genuine proper policy satisfies the one-call reserve, so aT ≤ Nmin . Restriction
to shorter prefixes proves aT is nondecreasing. Every prefix completed by the fallback is proper and
has exactly the cost CT + WT (H); hence uT ≥ Nmin . Prefixes of the fallback itself give uT ≤ κ0 .
Extending an optimizing upper prefix by one fallback call reproduces its exact completed cost, by
92                                             QIAN QI


the identity Hθ = gθ + Tθ∗ Hθ and first-productive commitment. Therefore uT +1 ≤ uT . Compactness
of the finite plan space and continuity of its cost operators prove both attainments.
   For a lower optimizer, the reserve proof gives Sθ,T ≼ min{1, (aT /c∗ − 1)/T }I. Positivity and
Hθ ≼ κ0 I give Wθ,T (Hθ ) ≼ κ0 Sθ,T . Complete that very prefix. Its cost is at most
                               aT + (κ0 − c∗ ) min{1, (aT /c∗ − 1)/T }.
Since aT ≤ Nmin ≤ κ0 , this proves (S25.3). The squeeze proves both limits. For boundary
attainment, the compact product of all finite nodewise decision simplexes has the finite-intersection
property for the closed prefix cost constraints at budget Nmin : feasibility at the largest of finitely
many horizons follows from aT ≤ Nmin . A common infinite policy therefore obeys all reserved
constraints. Their survival bound tends to zero, and monotone convergence gives properness and
cost at most Nmin . This is the same compactness mechanism as the inherited synthesis theorem,
not a finite-memory assertion.
   If N > Nmin , convergence of uT implies that the strict inequality uT0 < N holds at some finite
level. An upper optimizer is itself the finite prefix and a physically executable fallback, not a
model-dependent analytic certificate without a common policy. Its start-state uniformity permits
it at every later open leaf of a time-homogeneous experiment. Substitute its cost and its counted
live labels in the completion and initial-mixture proof; every inequality remains unchanged, except
that its B live roles replace the one fallback row. A fixed dummy decision may be replaced by any
point of the eventual task dictionary without changing precommit calls or cost. Exact algebraic
tests decide a strictly feasible upper threshold when the coefficients and proposed budget have the
stated algebraic representation. General real data still require an exact oracle or further numerical
certification.                                                                                       □
  For example, two orthogonal nonzero failure projections have one-row fair fallback cost κ0 = 2,
but fair-first alternation has uniform cost 3/2 and stops by call two. The cost upper problem at
depth two therefore supplies a fallback below every budget N > 3/2, including 3/2 < N < 2. Thus
the preliminary optimization removes a genuine restriction of the crude fallback, rather than only
renaming its constant.
S25.4. Robustness and scope of the construction. If two fallback killed maps on the same
space satisfy ∥T ∗ − Te ∗ ∥∞→∞ ≤ ϵ on Hermitian matrices, telescoping powers gives ∥Od − O   e d ∥ ≤ dϵ:
each positive subunital adjoint is a contraction in this norm. Hence δ − dϵ > 0 certifies a perturbed
proper fallback and cost at most c∗ d/(δ − dϵ), when the perturbed calls obey the same cost upper
bound. This is a quantitative stability certificate for the fallback, not a claim that a terminal-law
distance controls unbounded cost. The sharp ladder concerns depth; the scalar rare-success example
shows why a spectral margin must enter any numerical certificate.
   This section treats finite known candidate models, not statistical learning of an unrestricted
unknown kernel. It constructs a common policy without a lower witness, but does not prove that
its risk is always the geometric profile Q. The geometric transfer and this primitive synthesis
are complementary routes: one gives interpretable sharp resource laws under its certificates, the
other supplies a convergent constrained optimum and finite implementation on its finite interface.
Quantum reachability, sequence form, potential arguments and semidefinite separation are established
ingredients; the present assertion is their explicit paid, same-budget, fixed-output synthesis with a
derived fallback and all-model/all-initial-state quantifiers.

               S26. Boundary attainment and finite optimality witnesses
  An upper construction and a lower inequality do not by themselves characterize which strategies
are optimal. We now close the constrained risk frontier at its feasible boundary, derive its exact
variational dual at a fixed dictionary, and identify finite witnesses for each compact-model prefix
problem. Cost and task witnesses are allowed to be different physical preparations in different
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         93


models. Their common-program Bellman certificate is necessary and sufficient at the finite level,
and, together with the preceding paid completion, certifies arbitrarily accurate strategies at every
interior budget.
   The convex-analytic ingredients are classical: finite-support semi-infinite duality is developed in
[33, 34], and compactness of strategic and occupation measures has an extensive control-theoretic
history [11, 35]. We prove the needed statements in this experiment’s coordinates. We do not claim
a new general separation or Carathéodory theorem. The distinction between a fixed dictionary and
a hard bound on the number of freely chosen decoder values is essential: the latter can destroy
convexity even in physical dimension one.

S26.1. The exact frontier, including its left endpoint. For this subsection only, the model
family need not be compact, time homogeneous, or finite-dimensional. It suffices that all models
share a countable, finitely branching action–report tree with nonempty finite legal action sets at
open nodes. Costs have finite conditional means at each finite history, are nonnegative, and have
the same positive conditional mean floor c∗ . Terminal losses lie in [0, L]. Initial preparations may
be absorbed into the model index. One common observer must commit exactly once and satisfy its
expected-cost bound in every model. The decision set D is compact metric and the terminal losses
have a common decision modulus. These conditions are satisfied by the primitive interfaces above;
they specify the wider scope used here without assuming a free posterior or a common physical
reset.
   Use the common sequence weights of Lemma S30.1. A fixed ordered decoder tuple C may have
repeated entries. Let PN (C) denote the common infinite plans of uniform expected cost at most
N . Let qC,T (N ) be the infimum of worst truncated terminal loss over common depth-T plans with
the one-call reserve Cθ,T + c∗ Sθ,T ≤ N in every model and preparation. For an operator interface
this inequality means the corresponding Loewner inequality. Define qm,T by optimizing the decoder
tuple in Dm , and likewise define Rm . A finite infeasible problem has value +∞.
Theorem S26.1 (Closed constrained frontier and boundary convergence). Every nonempty PN (C)
is compact in common-plan coordinates. Terminal-risk functions converge uniformly to their depth-T
truncations on this set, with error at most
                                 L min{1, (N/c∗ − 1)/T }       (T ≥ 1).
Thus the optimal risk is attained at every feasible finite budget, including the least feasible budget
when one exists. The same assertion holds with a compact decoder tuple of prescribed width m.
  A budget is feasible if and only if all its finite reserved problems are feasible. At every feasible N ,
                              qC,T (N ) ↑ RC (N ),     qm,T (N ) ↑ Rm (N ).                       (S26.1)
Both risk functions are nonincreasing and right-continuous on their feasible budget ray. For a fixed
dictionary, RC is convex and hence continuous at every interior feasible budget. No convexity of Rm
is asserted. Boundary convergence gives neither a computable rate nor a finite-memory optimum at
the least budget.
Proof. The infinite common-plan space is a closed subset of the countable product of the intervals
[0, 1], specified by the common flow equalities. It is compact and metrizable. Every prefix cost,
survival mass and truncated terminal loss is continuous in its finitely many sequence coordinates for
each model. Intersecting all prefix reserve constraints over every model and every depth gives a
closed set.
   On that intersection, the cost floor and monotonicity of survival give CT + c∗ ST ≥ c∗ [1 + T ST ].
Hence the displayed uniform survival bound holds, and survival tends to zero. Nonnegative prefix
costs increase to the actual cost; their bounds imply a proper common plan of cost at most N .
Conversely a proper cost-N plan satisfies every reserve because each open path owes a further paid
94                                               QIAN QI


call. This identifies the closed intersection with PN (C), without assuming continuity of total cost or
a uniform expected remaining-cost tail.
   The nonnegative omitted terminal loss is at most L times survival. A finite prefix loss is uniformly
continuous in its finite plan coordinates, uniformly over models: its coefficients are bounded by L,
and there are finitely many terminal paths. For a decoder tuple, the common decision modulus
supplies the additional uniform continuity. Thus the full worst-model risk is a uniform limit of
continuous functions on the compact cost-bounded set. It attains its minimum. The product with
Dm is compact as well; output indices, not unknown model-dependent values, are the terminal
choices.
   A later feasible prefix restricts to an earlier one while preserving its reserve and decreasing its
accumulated nonnegative loss. Thus the finite values are nondecreasing and bounded above by the
actual value. Conversely choose optimizers at depths tending to infinity, extend them by arbitrary
legal rows, and take a coordinatewise convergent subsequence, also in Dm when needed. At each
fixed earlier depth the limiting reserve and terminal-loss constraints hold in every model. The limit
is proper by the preceding argument, and its terminal risk is at most the increasing limit of the
finite values. This proves (S26.1). The same compact finite-intersection argument without the loss
constraints proves the feasibility equivalence. Choosing a sequence of feasible budgets decreasing to
their infimum also proves attainment of the least finite feasible cost.
   For right-continuity, use optimizers at budgets Nj ↓ N , all bounded by N + 1, and take a
convergent subsequence in that compact cost-bounded set. Its limit is feasible at N , and uniform
terminal-tail control identifies the limiting risk. Monotonicity supplies the reverse inequality. Finally,
two common plans with the same dictionary may be mixed in sequence coordinates. Their costs
and losses mix linearly before taking worst models, so the worst cost and risk mix sublinearly.
This proves convexity of RC . Such mixing is a common behavioral policy by Lemma S30.1; a
finite implementation must count its live branch roles as in the supplement. Mixing two optimized
width-m dictionaries can use 2m values, so this argument does not prove convexity of Rm .              □

   For a fixed dictionary, write C(P ) and V(P ) for a plan’s worst expected cost and worst terminal
risk, and let Pfin contain the proper common plans of finite uniform cost. The following is a scalar
resource dual, not an interchange of a physical model and an observer.

Corollary S26.2 (Exact resource-price dual). If some finite budget is feasible, then for every
N ≥ Nmin ,
                             RC (N ) = sup inf {V(P ) + λ[C(P ) − N ]}.                           (S26.2)
                                        λ≥0 P ∈Pfin

At N > Nmin the supremum is attained. If a common plan of cost at most κ < N is known,
an attaining multiplier satisfies 0 ≤ λ ≤ L/(N − κ). Every primal optimum then minimizes the
penalized expression and obeys λ[C(P ) − N ] = 0. At N = Nmin the supremum remains exact but
need not be attained by a finite multiplier. These statements concern one fixed dictionary, not its
nonconvex width-constrained union.

Proof. Weak duality follows by testing any feasible plan. On the interior the finite convex non-
increasing function RC has a supporting line of slope −λ ≤ 0 at N . For every finite-cost plan
of cost n, its risk is at least RC (n), which is at least RC (N ) − λ(n − N ). A primal optimum
gives the reverse bound and complementary slackness. Applying the supporting inequality at κ
gives λ(N − κ) ≤ RC (κ) − RC (N ) ≤ L. Supporting lines exist because the left and right secant
slopes of a finite convex function are ordered and locally bounded. At the left endpoint choose
Nj ↓ Nmin from the interior. The attaining interior multiplier gives a dual value at Nmin at least
RC (Nj ). Right-continuity and weak duality prove endpoint equality without a bounded-multiplier
assumption.                                                                                     □
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                            95


S26.2. A complete decision comparison of performed stopped laws. Fix the actual decoder
dictionary C, rather than only its cardinality. Let Ω be the measurable disjoint union of terminal
action–report words, committed values in C, and a prospective audit outcome in a common standard
Borel space. The audit kernel after each such word and commitment is fixed in each model. Write
PPθ for the complete performed stopped law of a legal plan P . It includes the stopping call and the
raw visible word, not just the final audit marginal. For a specified target family of probability laws
Tθ on this same space define
              δN,C (T) =     inf      sup TV(PPθ , Tθ ),         TV(µ, ν) = sup |µ(A) − ν(A)|.
                           P ∈PN (C) θ                                            A
The target is data, not an extra executable oracle. Its feasibility in the source interface is the
question being tested.
Theorem S26.3 (Paid complete-score randomization criterion). Under the common finite-branching
and positive-cost hypotheses of Theorem S26.1, at any feasible finite N , the set of families {PPθ }θ
is compact and convex in the uniform total-variation metric. Hence the infimum defining δN,C is
attained, and                                                                
                                                     X                        X
                   δN,C (T) = sup  min                       πθ EPP gθ −              πθ ETθ gθ  .   (S26.3)
                                π,g    P ∈PN (C)                   θ
                                                   θ∈supp π                 θ∈supp π
Here π ranges over finitely supported model probabilities and each gθ : Ω → [0, 1] is a finite-valued
measurable score. The inner policies remain budget-feasible in every model, not only the support of
π. In particular exact common realization of the target at the prescribed budget and dictionary is
equivalent to its dominating the optimal source score in every such finite decision test.
   On the compact primitive interface, let one common finite-label fallback have conditional cost at
most κ < N . For every T ≥ 1 and every P ∈ PN (C) there is a common finite-label PT ∈ PN (C)
satisfying
                                     eT
   sup TV(PPθ T , PPθ ) ≤ DT +              ,     DT = min{1, (N/c∗ − 1)/T }, eT = (κ − c∗ )DT .
     θ                           N − κ + eT
                                                                                                (S26.4)
The prior finite-prefix and fallback live-label bounds apply. Therefore finite-label laws are dense
at the same budget at every strictly interior feasible budget, and finite-score lower witnesses and
actual finite-label realizations approximate the exact deficiency. This is a comparison of the declared
performed laws, not of every quantum channel with arbitrary ancillary experiments.
Proof. At depth at most T there are finitely many terminal action–report words and output indices.
Their contributions to a stopped law are fixed physical audit measures multiplied by common
sequence weights. For two plans, the total-variation difference of these finite contributions is at
most the sum of the absolute differences of those weights, uniformly over models, since each physical
measure has mass at most one. The two omitted positive tails have mass at most DT each on
the budget set. First choose T large and then make the finitely many coordinates close. This
proves continuity of the law-family map into uniform total variation. Compactness of PN (C) gives
compactness of its image; linearity in sequence weights gives convexity. Distance to any fixed target
family is a continuous function in this metric, so its minimum is attained. This argument does not
need continuity of total expected cost or uniform integrability of its tail.
   For equal-mass probability measures, TV(µ, ν) = supA [µ(A) − ν(A)], since complements reverse
the sign. Thus each test functional Fθ,A (P ) = PPθ (A)−Tθ (A) is continuous and affine on the compact
convex plan set. The right side of (S26.3) is at most the deficiency by the elementary minimax
inequality. If r < δN,C , the closed sets {P : Fθ,A (P ) ≤ r} have empty total intersection. Compactness
gives a finite subcollection with empty intersection. Its attainable test vectors form a compact
convex set disjoint from the lower orthant (−∞, r]q . Strict separation gives nonnegative weights
summing to one, with the weighted test value greater than r for every feasible plan. Nonnegativity
96                                                    QIAN QI


follows because a negative coefficient would make the supremum on that lower orthant infinite.
Grouping tests with the same model yields a finitely supported π and a convex combination of
finitely many indicators as gθ . The latter is finite-valued. This proves the reverse inequality by
letting r increase to the deficiency. Zero deficiency implies exact realization by attainment, not only
approximation.
   For (S26.4), keep P unchanged through the depth-T commitment decision, and execute the
real fallback on its open paths. Coupling the original and completed experiments with the same
calls and current coins until then makes their complete stopped laws identical unless the original
path is still open. The total-variation discrepancy is therefore at most DT , including all raw
history and audit coordinates. The completed cost is at most N + eT , because its reserved prefix
already paid c∗ for each open path. Mix this completion with the common fallback with probability
p = (N − κ)/(N − κ + eT ). The expected cost is at most N in every model. Mixing changes a
probability law by at most 1 − p in total variation. The triangle inequality proves the displayed
bound. Its finite implementation counts the actual finite tree, the fallback and the initial branch
role; the same dictionary is used throughout. The compact-family cost theorem provides such a
fallback at every N > Nmin . Applying this approximation to an attaining deficiency minimizer
proves the final assertion.                                                                          □

   The decision-separation principle belongs to the Blackwell–Le Cam tradition [1, 37]. The additional
work here is the compactness of the performed law family under a uniform paid stopping constraint,
together with finite-label approximation without increasing that constraint. It requires all bounded
measurable scores of that performed outcome; localized affine-square tests alone do not suffice. The
conclusion does not grant a free stateful simulator, an uncharged audit on rejected calls, or control
of unbounded expected duration by total variation. The latter is controlled by the separate budget
throughout. If the dictionary itself varies, laws recording different numerical readouts need not vary
continuously in total variation; the general-loss width theorem and the fixed-dictionary comparison
are distinct statements.

S26.3. Sparse spectral certificates on compact uncertainty. Return to the continuous compact
finite-dimensional interface of Section S31. Fix a dictionary and horizon. Let XT be the common
finite sequence-plan polytope and let kT be its affine dimension, after eliminating flow equalities.
The same statement applies to a common tree with compulsory terminal nodes, or to a prescribed
face of its plan polytope; kT then denotes the affine dimension of that polytope. For z = (θ, u) with
θ ∈ Θ and ∥u∥ = 1, put
                    cz (x) = ⟨u, (Cθ,T (x) + c∗ Sθ,T (x))u⟩,         vz (x) = ⟨u, Vθ,T (x)u⟩.
These are continuous families of affine functions of x; 0 ≤ vz (x) ≤ L. Testing all such z is equivalent
to the operator constraints. Write qT (N ) for the resulting compact-model finite optimum.
Theorem S26.4 (Exact finite witness and complementary slackness). Suppose some x0 ∈ XT
satisfies cz (x0 ) ≤ κ < N for every z. Then there are risk atoms zj , cost atoms wi , and nonnegative
weights βj , αi such that
     X                        X            L
          βj = 1,      λ :=       αi ≤        ,        #{j : βj > 0} + #{i : αi > 0} ≤ kT + 1,   (S26.5)
      j                       i
                                         N −κ
                                                               
                     X                    X                    
     qT (N ) = min          βj vzj (x) +       αi [cwi (x) − N ] .                               (S26.6)
               x∈XT                                            
                        j                  i

For any fixed optimal plan x∗ these atoms can be chosen active there:
                        βj > 0 ⇒ vzj (x∗ ) = qT (N ),           αi > 0 ⇒ cwi (x∗ ) = N.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       97


Their preparations are pure; active vectors are respectively eigenvectors of the loss operator at qT and
the reserved-cost operator at N . Conversely, a feasible plan, such active atoms and minimization
of the displayed affine expression certify optimality. The total atom bound is sharp for each affine
dimension kT . It concerns dual witnesses, not the number of controller labels or a fixed horizon-
independent adversarial prior.
Proof. Set v(x) = maxz vz (x) and c(x) = maxz cz (x). Compactness gives attained maxima. The
finite value as a function of its budget is convex and nonincreasing. A supporting line at N , which
is interior because κ < N , yields a multiplier λ ≥ 0 for which x∗ minimizes v + λ(c − N ) and
λ(c(x∗ ) − N ) = 0, exactly as in Corollary S26.2.
   For completeness, the finite polytope argument underlying its stationarity is proved here. The
directional derivative of a compact maximum of affine functions is the maximum of their active
gradients: take a maximizing index along a sequence of positive steps, pass to a convergent
subsequence, and compare with every active index. Let A be the compact convex sum of the active
risk-gradient hull and λ times the active cost-gradient hull; omit the second term if λ = 0. If A did
not intersect the negative normal cone of XT at x∗ , separation of a compact convex set and a closed
convex cone would give a tangent direction h with maxa∈A a · h < 0. For a polytope, every vector
in this tangent cone gives a feasible sufficiently short step: impose its finitely many active linear
inequalities, then choose the step short enough for the inactive ones. The directional derivative
would be negative, a contradiction. Thus there are active finite convex combinations whose sum g
satisfies g · (x − x∗ ) ≥ 0 for all x ∈ XT . Convex combinations in a finite-dimensional compact hull
can be chosen finite by eliminating affine dependencies.
   In the kT -dimensional affine coordinates, append one coordinate to the active gradients. Represent
(g, 1) as a conic sum of (∇vz , 1) for risk atoms and (∇cw , 0) for cost atoms. If more than kT + 1
generators have positive coefficients, a linear dependence permits subtracting a suitable multiple of
that dependence until one coefficient vanishes and none becomes negative. Repeat. This preserves g
and risk mass one, and all remaining atoms stay active. Their affine combination is minimized at
x∗ . Its value there is qT , proving (S26.6). Evaluating that minimum at x0 gives qT ≤ L − λ(N − κ),
hence the bound in (S26.5), even if compression changed the original multiplier.
   For sufficiency, at any feasible x the expression in braces is at most v(x), while at the specified
active minimizing plan it equals its risk. This sandwiches the optimum. If a positive semidefinite
matrix N I − C has zero quadratic form at a unit vector, it annihilates that vector; the spectral
claims follow, and similarly for risk. Finally take a compulsory one-call scalar experiment with k + 1
actions and k + 1 models, unit cost, one decoder and loss 1 exactly when action and model indices
coincide. With commitment required after that call, its plan polytope is the k-simplex and its risk is
maxi xi . At any N > 1, cost atoms cannot be active. The value 1/(k + 1) requires the risk weights
all equal 1/(k + 1), since the dual minimum is their smallest weight. Every one of the k + 1 atoms
is necessary. This proves sharpness within physical experiments, not just an abstract cone.          □
  Finite-support duality and the conic reduction are classical [34, Theorem 4.4]. The operational
content is their use with the actual common plan, the open-path reserve, distinct spectral cost
and score preparations, and a completion at the same resource budget. An arbitrary coarse grid is
not a replacement for this certificate: active models may lie in the interior of a parameter set, as
Example S31.10 shows.
Proposition S26.5 (Common Bellman evaluation and equality of used rows). For any finite atom
lists with risk mass one, the right side of (S26.6) is evaluated exactly by a backward recursion on
the single action–report tree. At each productive report it minimizes between a decision in the fixed
dictionary and continuation; nonproductive reports must continue. The open depth-T cost is the
actual one-call reserve. There is a deterministic finite plan minimizing this dual expression, but it
need not satisfy the original robust budget and risk constraints. A randomized plan minimizes it if
and only if every choice of positive sequence weight has zero backward slack at its reached node.

   Each minimization admits a deterministic choice because its alternatives are finite. Keeping the
chosen current command or report never reveals the model: the propagated matrices are offline
analytic coefficients for a common tree. At every row, its randomized value minus its minimum is
a nonnegative slack. Backward substitution expresses the root excess as the sum of those slacks
times the corresponding nonnegative sequence weights. It vanishes precisely when every positively
used alternative has zero slack. A dual-minimizing plan alone may violate the individual model
constraints; Theorem S26.4 requires their separate primal verification.                                     □
Corollary S26.6 (Finite certificates for the full stopped optimum). Fix N > Nmin and a dictionary
C on the compact primitive interface. For every η > 0 there is a finite horizon, at most kT + 1
spectral witness atoms, and a common proper finite-label observer at cost at most N such that
the Bellman-evaluated dual lower and that observer’s worst risk differ by at most η. The same
actual dictionary is used at every history and by the fallback. With compact optimized output width,
attainment still holds, but this dual is conditional on the dictionary; global certification requires the
separate dictionary optimization or a certified decision net. No minimax swap over dictionaries is
authorized.
Proof. The compact-family cost hierarchy supplies a finite fallback of uniform cost κ < N . Its prefix
is strictly feasible for every reserved finite problem. Apply Theorem S26.4. The compact-model
problem imposes all model constraints, so completion and initial mixing introduce no model-
cover error. With DT = min{1, (N/c∗ − 1)/T } and eT = (κ − c∗ )DT , its realized risk is at most
qT + LDT + LeT /(N − κ + eT ). This difference tends to zero, while every dual value lower-bounds
every feasible full-history strategy. Choose T to make the difference at most η. The prior completion
proof counts the finite prefix, fallback and initial-mixture roles. For variable dictionaries use the
preceding decision-net or algebraic optimization, not convexification of their union.               □

Proof. For the finite problem set cℓ (x) = maxθ,u ⟨u, (Cθ,T + cℓ Sθ,T )u⟩ and v(x) = maxθ,u ⟨u, Vθ,T u⟩.

   Repeat the active-gradient and normal-cone argument with one cost-gradient hull for each
positive multiplier. All cost generators still have appended coordinate zero, and risk generators have
appended coordinate one. The conic reduction in dimension kT + 1 therefore proves the same total
support bound. Evaluating the resulting affine minimum at the jointly strict fallback prefix gives
the displayed weighted margin bound. At each node the immediate scalar cost is simply the sum
over resource-indexed atoms, which proves the recursion claim.
   Resource 0 bounds survival by DT . Completing an optimizer by the one common fallback costs at
most Nℓ +eℓ,T in resource ℓ and adds at most LDT risk. Choose this completed plan with probability
pT and the fallback otherwise. For every ℓ its cost is at most pT (Nℓ + eℓ,T ) + (1 − pT )κℓ ≤ Nℓ . The
risk bound follows exactly as for one resource. Strict positive margins make pT → 1. Common-tree
and fallback labels are counted as before. On a general feasible vector, intersect all prefix reserve
constraints; the single coercive resource 0 forces properness and uniform bounded-loss tails. The
compactness and finite-intersection proof of Theorem S26.1 then applies with all the additional
closed constraints. Separate fallbacks safe for separate resources would not justify this proof: joint
feasibility of one common fallback is essential.                                                              □

S26.4. Distinct witnesses and the price of hard output width.
Example S26.8 (A shared adversarial prior can give a strict dual gap). There are two commands
A, B, one productive report, one decoder, and two scalar models. Costs are charged but are not
extra observations; the visible action–report history has the same law in both models. Model 1 has
costs (1, 2) and losses (0, 0); model 2 has costs (1, 1) and losses (1, 0). Commitment may occur only
after a call. At budget N = 3/2, writing x for the probability that the final command is B, every
plan costs at least 1 + x in model 1 and has risk 1 − x in model 2. One-call mixing at x = 1/2
attains the exact value 1/2. The dual certificate has one unit cost atom at model 1 and one unit
risk atom at model 2.
   If one incorrectly forces both atom lists to be proportional to the same probability (t, 1 − t), the
one-call dual becomes
                                                                             √
                        sup     min {(1 − t)(1 − x) + λ(tx − 1/2)} = 23 − 2 < 21 .
                     0≤t≤1,λ≥0 0≤x≤1

For t ≤ 1/2 its best value
                     √     is zero; for t > 1/2 the best multiplier is (1 − t)/t, giving 3/2 − t − 1/(2t),
maximized at t = 1/ 2. All quantities come from real cost and loss tables. A single least-favorable
prior for one scalarized task is not a substitute for independently imposed robust cost and risk
constraints.
100                                            QIAN QI


Proposition S26.9 (An exact hard-width convexification gap). There is a one-model, one-
dimensional physical experiment with two commands, compact decision set D = {0, 1} and bounded
nonnegative loss, such that for 1 ≤ N ≤ 2,
                        R1 (N ) = min{1/2, 2 − N },      R2 (N ) = (2 − N )/2.
Thus R1 is attained and right-continuous but not convex. Its scalar Lagrange relaxation over all
width-one programs equals R2 ; at N = 3/2 it has a gap 1/4. Randomizing two different one-value
dictionaries pays for a two-value dictionary, not for an uncharged initial seed.
Proof. Both commands always give one productive report, with costs 1, 2. Decision 0 has loss 1/2
after either command. Decision 1 has loss 1 after the cheap command and zero after the costly one.
These are permissible prospective audit losses: the future audit can be the deterministic command
symbol with the displayed loss table. This is a bounded general-loss example, not a claim about a
squared-loss realization.
   For the sole decoder 0 every proper program has loss 1/2. For the sole decoder 1, if its final
command is costly with probability x, expected total cost is at least 1 + x and risk is 1 − x. One-call
mixing realizes all these pairs, so optimization gives R1 . With both values, the smallest loss is 1/2
after a cheap last command and zero after a costly one. The same cost lower and one-call realization
give R2 .
   Over width-one programs the penalized infimum is min{1/2 + λ(1 − N ), λ(2 − N )}: the cheap
decoder-1 endpoint is dominated. Maximizing over λ ≥ 0 gives (2 − N )/2, with equality of the two
lines at λ = 1/2. At N = 3/2 the primal width-one value is 1/2 and the relaxation is 1/4. In the
randomized implementation attaining the latter, a cheap call uses decoder 0 and a costly call uses
decoder 1, so two distinct final values are genuinely required.                                      □
   The preceding results supply an exact variational and finite-witness completion of the primitive
risk route. They do not establish that every causal family admits the geometric reference certificates,
a common finite unknown-model predictive quotient, or optimal computational resources. Boundary
primal attainment is compatible with the failure of uniform expected cost-tail convergence and
with lack of a finite boundary implementation. Those independent questions are not resolved by a
success certificate for a finite optimization problem.

        S27. Weighted boundary decisions and positive stochastic realization
   The deterministic residual quotient of Section S28 does not classify a robust problem with tied
Bayes actions. In that case the object to realize is a probability-valued decision function, and
its residuals need not take finitely many values. We first derive that object without assuming
model-independent report probabilities. We then characterize its finite stochastic implementation by
an invariant convex family, and give exact memory classifications for a random-horizon calibration
task. The convex realization machinery is classical in origin: linear representations and positive
realizations are different notions [45, 46, 47]. The new assertions concern the physical minimum-cost
face, its robust optimal decision face, and their counted stochastic implementation.
S27.1. State-dependent reports and a minimum-cost potential. Fix a compact metric
model space Θ, a finite-dimensional physical space K, a nonempty finite failure alphabet Y , and
a fixed nonempty finite decoder dictionary C. All primitive matrices below depend continuously
on θ. The prepared density is ρθ . There is one globally legal command, with completely positive
branches Sθ for a productive herald and Kθ,y for failure y, whose sum is trace preserving. A rejected
herald retains the actual post-Sθ state; a failure retains the actual post-Kθ,y state. No replacement
preparation, inspection of the protected state, or additional unpriced operation is available. Calls
have nonnegative cost with mean observable
                               c∗ I ≼ Gθ ≼ c∗ I,      0 < c∗ ≤ c∗ < ∞.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       101


Commitment is allowed only on a herald and precedes the prospective audit. Its loss at decoder
r has observable 0 ≼ Fθ,r ≼ LI on the post-herald state. An independently executed final audit
cost must be added separately. The declared reports are the whole observer input; charged costs
do not supply an additional observation channel. These are single-command hypotheses, not a
finite-memory criterion for a general controlled POMDP.
   Put Tθ = y Kθ,y and d = dim K. Assume

This is the primitive depth test already proved in Theorem S25.1, now applied to the one command.
Compactness gives a common δ > 0 with the left side at least δI. Thus first-herald survival after n

                                          bn = (1 − δ)⌊n/d⌋ .

The empty word is included. Words may have zero probability. We use the following support

                 λθ (w) > 0 for some θ =⇒ λθ′ (w) > 0 for some θ′ ∈ Θ0 .           (S27.2)
This is a sufficient coverage condition for robust boundary rigidity, not a claimed necessary condition
for every such rigidity. In particular it holds if one cost-maximizing model has S ∗ I ≻ 0 and Ky∗ I ≻ 0
for every y. These inequalities ensure that every finite word has positive mass in that model; neither
its posterior nor the model label is supplied to the observer.
Theorem S27.1 (Weighted herald rigidity). For every proper finite-cost observer in a fixed model,
                                                         X
                             Eθ Cτ = hθ + Eθ                             tr(Hθ ρ+
