where ρ+i is its normalized post-herald physical state. Consequently the least robust budget is N0 .
Under (S27.2), every budget-N0 observer commits at the first herald in every model. Model- and
state-dependent failure and herald probabilities are permitted.
Proof. At a reached pre-call state ρ, the expected cost plus the potential after continuing failures
is tr[(Gθ + Tθ∗ Hθ )ρ] = tr(Hθ ρ). Continuing after a herald adds exactly its post-herald potential,
weighted by the actual rejection decision. Conditional summation to a deterministic horizon gives
the displayed identity with cost and rejected-herald sums truncated, minus the remaining live
potential. That remainder is bounded by K0 Prθ (τ > n) and tends to zero. Finite cost and the
positive cost floor imply proper survival decay; monotone convergence applies to the two nonnegative
sums. This proves (S27.3). First-herald commitment realizes hθ simultaneously, so its largest value
is the least robust budget.
   At every θ′ ∈ Θ0 , a boundary observer has zero residual. Since Hθ′ ⪰ c∗ I, it rejects no herald
with positive probability. Before the first herald, the distribution of its private memory conditional
on the failure word depends only on that word and its own coins: there is one command, and the
physical report maps do not depend on those coins. In particular its rejection probability at the
first herald after w is model independent. If w is possible anywhere, (S27.2) makes it possible at a
cost-maximizing model, where this probability is zero. Thus the probability is zero in every model.
The sufficient full-support test follows by successively taking traces of positive, nonzero branch
outputs.                                                                                            □
102                                                 QIAN QI


   The coverage qualification cannot be dropped. With unit calls, let one model have geometric
herald rate 1/3 and only failure letter a. Let another have a prepared two-state system whose first
call fails with letter b, enters a state with deterministic heralds, and thereafter remains there. Both
pass (S27.1) (use a two-dimensional identity extension in the first model). Their first-herald costs
are three and two. A policy accepting all a-histories but rejecting the first herald after b still has
robust cost three. The word b is absent from the expensive model. This failure of coverage licenses
an actual extra stopping decision; it is not a zero-probability technicality.

Every summand vanishes. This gives the support restriction even without positive model-independent

This proves the full face, not sufficiency of Bayes ties alone. For example two constant loss vectors
(0, 1) and (1, 0) have fair robust optimum 1/2, while both pure actions are tied under the equal
model mixture and each has worst risk one.                                                         □
S27.3. What replaces a deterministic residual congruence. View each g ∈ D as a probability-
valued response function on future words. Its left shift is (Ly g)(z) = g(yz). An anchored finite
convex realization of b consists of g1 = b, g2 , . . . , gM ∈ D with
                          Ly gs ∈ conv{g1 , . . . , gM }   (s = 1, . . . , M, y ∈ Y ).              (S27.6)
The distinguished initial response g1 is counted even if it is not an extreme point of this convex hull.
This anchor reflects a prescribed initial live label; it must not be replaced by a free random initial
state. The auxiliary gs need not themselves be robust optimal from a new preparation.
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                          103


Theorem S27.3 (Exact stochastic boundary memory with ties). Under the weighted rigidity
hypotheses, an exact robust boundary optimum with at most M live labels exists if and only if some
b ∈ B∗ admits an anchored convex realization with at most M generators. The least such generator
count is the least exact live-label count. This criterion permits all robust ties, state-dependent word
masses and stochastic transitions.
   For a specified response b, the block Hankel matrix
                                             Hb (w, (z, r)) = bwz (r)
has rank at most the generator count. Finite Hankel rank alone is not sufficient. Event-stopped
implementations use at most max(M, |C|) total labels, and state-stopped implementations at most
M + |C|; unused decoder values can be removed from these upper counts.
Proof. From (S27.6) choose stochastic coefficients Qy (s, t) representing each shifted generator. Set
the decoder row Ds = gs (∅). Induction on word length gives

                                        gs (y1 · · · yn ) = eT
                                                             s Qy1 · · · Qyn D.

The machine starts in label one, updates on failures with these matrices, and on the first herald
draws its decoder from Ds . It uses no physical-state input and realizes b exactly, so (S27.5) proves
optimality.
   Conversely, for each reached label of any stochastic machine define gs (z) to be its terminal decoder
distribution after
              P
                   processing z and then a herald. Its rows are probabilities. The Markov property
gives Ly gs = t Qy (s, t)gt , and the initial function is b. Unreachable labels may be removed; any
unused rows can be completed arbitrarily. Thus the same number of reached labels gives the required
generators, proving both directions and minimality. Factor the Hankel matrix as the row of reached
state probabilities after w times the column of future response probabilities from each state after
z to obtain the rank lower bound. The established event/state role packing gives the total-label
upper counts.                                                                                         □

   This is a positive realization criterion on the optimal decision face, not a claim to originate
residual automata or finite-dimensional linear realization. Rabin–Scott and the Moore/Myhill–
Nerode tradition characterize deterministic finite residual functions [44]; observable-operator and
multiplicity-automaton theories separate linear rank from stochastic realization [45, 46]. Here
exact unique Bayes actions recover the deterministic criterion of Section S28. With ties, a convex
family can have infinitely many different residual points, and selecting positive successors no longer
preserves the required output probabilities. The next application exhibits both phenomena exactly.
   For finite primitive models the criterion also has a direct operator verification form. Given
stochastic rows Qy , D, define the killed map on the direct sum of M physical blocks by

                                         ∗
                                  Zθ − Tθ,Q Z θ = Aθ ,           0 ≼ Zθ ≼ LI.                        (S27.7)
Its initial risk is tr((Zθ )1 ρθ ). Consequently, when an exact target value V is supplied, stochastic
rows satisfying this risk at most V in every model are a finite necessary-and-sufficient certificate for
exact M -label optimality. The equations follow by summing the absolutely convergent killed score
series; normalization bounds its sum by LI, and transience proves uniqueness. With finitely many
algebraic models and algebraic V , this is a finite real-algebraic existence test. It does not assume
that the unknown optimum of an arbitrary problem is itself algebraic.

S27.4. Robust Born-probability replication at an unknown random horizon. Consider a

one. On commitment output r ∈ C = {0, 1} before observing the Bernoulli audit Z with effect E.
There are two audit-loss models σ ∈ {−1, 1}, neither affecting report probabilities, with loss
                                   ℓσ (r, Z) = 21 [1 + σ(r − Z)] ∈ [0, 1].                          (S27.8)
The unknown sign enforces two-sided calibration rather than choosing one favorable signed discrep-
ancy. This is a replication task for the Born probability at a random horizon, not the squared-sign
prediction task of the previous qubit theorem. The observer has no access to the protected system
except the failure/herald reports.

Theorem S27.4 (Unique randomized optimum and exact realization memory). The full-history
boundary value is 1/2, and its unique response kernel is bn = βn for all n ≥ 0. An exact finite-memory
optimum exists precisely when
                                        βn = eT   n

Under a least-favorable measure putting equal weight on the two signs at any fixed t, every wordwise
action is Bayes-tied. Thus uniqueness of the robust randomized kernel does not imply uniqueness of
any wordwise Bayes P  action.
   Put g(t) = (1 − t) n≥0 tn βn . The exact one-live-label value is
                                    V1 = 12 + 14 (max g(t) − min g(t)).                           (S27.11)

For every fixed M the best risk is attained. Whenever (S27.10) is impossible at that size, its risk

1/2 + tT+ /2.
Proof. A direct expectation of (S27.8) gives (S27.9). The choice bn = βn proves         value 1/2, which is
also a lower bound from the two opposite signs. At equality the power series n (bn − βn )tn vanishes
                                                                                   P

on a nonempty interval inside its unit disk of convergence. The analytic identity theorem makes all
its coefficients zero. For completeness, a nonzero convergent power series is locally its first nonzero
power times a nonvanishing analytic factor at any interior zero; hence its zeros cannot accumulate
in its disk unless it is identically zero. This proves uniqueness.
   Theorem S27.3 in the unary alphabet gives exactly (S27.10). Averaging the two loss signs makes
every conditional loss 1/2, proving the least-favorable tie assertion. A one-label rule is a constant
output probability u. Its discrepancy is u − g(t); the midpoint of the minimum and maximum of g
minimizes the uniform absolute error, with radius half their difference. This proves (S27.11).
   For fixed M , the compact set of stochastic rows and decoder values has continuous risk: each
finite sum is continuous and the tail is uniformly at most tT+ . Attainment and unique optimality
prove the strict gap. Store labels 0, . . . , T − 1, advance until the final label and repeat it thereafter,
with outputs β0 , . . . , βT −1 . It is exact for n < T and differs by at most one later. Formula (S27.9)
bounds its risk excess by tT+ /2. Its stopping is unchanged, so this is the same least budget, not an
enlarged-budget approximation.                                                                           □

Theorem S27.6 (Exact qubit realization phases). For the robust calibration task, the following
hold.
   (a) At α = 1 no finite classical memory attains the boundary optimum, although the probability-
valued Hankel rank is three. At α = 0 the least exact live memory is two.
   (b) At every 0 < α < 1 an exact stochastic optimum exists with at most
                                        Mα = max{3, ⌈π/ arccos α⌉}

live labels. The least number
   (c) Put α3 = 5/(3 + 4 3). The least exact live memory is three precisely when 0 < α ≤ α3 . It
is at least four for α3 < α < 1. The three-label assertion is global over all stochastic transition
matrices and randomized decoder rows, not only circulant candidates.
Proof. For α > 0, the three distinct modes 1, αeiϑ and αe−iϑ have nonzero coefficients in (S27.13).
Vandermonde independence gives Hankel rank three. Cayley–Hamilton applied to any representation
(S27.10) shows that its transition matrix must have the two latter modes as eigenvalues: substituting
its characteristic recurrence and using independence of the three exponential sequences makes the
characteristic polynomial vanish at each mode.
106                                                QIAN QI


   Every unit-modulus eigenvalue z of a finite stochastic matrix is a root of unity. Indeed choose an
eigenvector coordinate of maximal modulus. Equality in the triangle inequality for its stochastic
row forces every positive-probability successor to have the same maximal modulus and its value to
equal z times the current value. Follow positive successors to a repeated state. The directed cycle
gives z j = 1 for some positive integer j. But eiϑ is not a root of unity: otherwise 2c = 6/5 would be
a rational algebraic integer and hence an integer. This proves (a) at α = 1. At α = 0 a starting
label with output one followed by an absorbing fair-output label realizes the sequence, whereas one
label cannot change its initial output.
   For (b), take the regular Mα -gon with vertices vj on the unit circle, including v0 = (1, 0). Its
inradius is at least α, so αRϑ maps every vertex into the polygon. Choose barycentric coefficients
Q(j, l) of each image in these vertices and let dj = (1 + (vj )1 )/2. Then Q is stochastic, all dj ∈ [0, 1],
and the expected vertex after n transitions from vertex zero is (αRϑ )n v0 . This proves (S27.13) exactly.
If least exact memories stayed bounded along α ↑ 1, compactness of their padded transition/decoder
rows would give, by taking a subsequence and each finite coefficient limit, a finite realization at
α = 1. This contradicts (a).
   For (c), define a circulant stochastic matrix with first row

Therefore 3αs ≤ 1−αc, exactly α ≤ α3 . No assumption of irreducibility, symmetry or deterministic

   The damped channel has no strict limiting preference between the two signed calibration losses,
and its exact optimal decoder law remains nonconstant at arbitrarily late times. Positive stochastic
realization nevertheless succeeds for every α < 1. This does not contradict the earlier impossibility
for unique squared-sign prediction: the fixed losses, the robust optimal face and the output law
differ. The unit-circle obstruction and invariant positive cones belong to classical positive-realization
theory [47]; the task-specific exact count, the global three-label threshold and the same-boundary
physical implementation are proved here. No sharp asymptotic memory rate is inferred from the
polygon upper.
Proposition S27.7 (Finite algebraic certificates and minimal synthesis). Suppose a probability-
valued response has a supplied real-algebraic linear representation bw = aT Tw B of dimension r on a
finite alphabet. Existence of an exact stochastic realization with M live labels is equivalent to the
finite conditions
         Qy ≥ 0, Qy 1 = 1,        D ≥ 0, D1 = 1,            eT         T
                                                             1 Qw D = a Tw B           (|w| ≤ M + r − 1).
These are real-algebraic feasibility conditions, hence decidable at each prescribed M , and a feasible
instance has algebraic controller entries. For the qubit family with algebraic 0 < α < 1, the finite
upper from Theorem S27.6 makes successive size tests a terminating synthesis of a minimal exact
stochastic observer. No efficient bit bound or general decision procedure for unbounded positive
realization is asserted.
                                     CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                                107


Proof. Use the block diagonal representation with transition diag(Qy , Ty ), initial row (eT          T
                                                                                                1 , −a )
                            T
and final columns (D, B) . Its reachable row spaces grow from dimension at most one in ambient
dimension M + r. Equality of successive spaces implies invariance under every letter, so the space
generated by words of length at most M + r − 1 contains all word rows. Annihilating the final
columns on these finitely many words therefore annihilates them on every word. The reverse
implication is immediate. The conditions are polynomial in the controller entries, with real-algebraic
coefficients, so real-closed-field elimination decides feasibility and yields an algebraic sample when
nonempty [15]. The polygon construction supplies a finite upper; one can obtain a certified integer
bound by testing α < cos(π/M ) with increasing integers, using algebraic comparisons. Thus finitely
many prescribed-size tests find the first feasible size. This is a complete terminating construction
for this family, not a claim about the complexity of general probabilistic automata.                  □

              S28. Finite-memory optimality at the least feasible budget
   Boundary attainment in Theorem S26.1 concerns the compact space of full-history plans. It does
not assert that the optimum belongs to any finite-label class. We resolve that distinction on a class
of prepared heralded channel experiments. The result is a necessary-and-sufficient memory criterion,
an explicit finite-dimensional quantum obstruction, and quantitative approximation at the same
boundary budget. It is not an additional assumption of the general compactness theorem.

S28.1. Prepared heralded channels and a boundary rigidity identity. Let 0 < p < 1, put
q = 1 − p, and fix a nonempty finite failure alphabet Y with probabilities πy > 0 summing to one.
There is one globally legal unit-cost command. Model θ has a prepared density matrix ρθ on a fixed
finite-dimensional space and channels Φθ,y . Its report instrument is
                          Iθ,s (A) = pA,         Iθ,y (A) = qπy Φθ,y (A)         (y ∈ Y ).                   (S28.1)
The herald s is productive; a failure y forbids commitment. Rejection of s leaves the physical state
unchanged. The observer cannot inspect the protected physical state. After commitment to r in one
fixed nonempty finite dictionary C, the future audit has conditional loss observable 0 ≼ Fθ,r ≼ LI.
Any separately charged fixed final-audit cost is added to all budgets below. No unpriced channel,
phase input or clock is supplied to the observer.
   For w = y1 · · · yn ∈ Y ∗ , including the empty word, set
                                                                |w|
                                               λ(w) = pq |w|
                                                                Y
           ρθ (w) = Φθ,yn · · · Φθ,y1 (ρθ ),                          πyi ,   ℓθ (w, r) = tr(Fθ,r ρθ (w)).
                                                                i=1

Every word has positive mass, w λ(w) = 1, and |w|≥T λ(w) = q T . Probabilities of visible failures
                                  P                       P

are independent of both the hidden model and the private controller coins. The channels themselves
may change the prepared state noncommutatively.
Lemma S28.1 (The least budget forces first-herald commitment). The least feasible precommit
expected-call budget is Nmin = 1/p. For every proper finite-cost policy, let J count productive reports
which it rejects. Then, in every model,
                                               pEθ τ = 1 + Eθ J.                                             (S28.2)
Consequently a policy is feasible at Nmin if and only if it commits at the first herald, up to null
histories. At that budget the only choice is its decision distribution bw ∈ ∆(C) after each failure
word w, and its risk is
                                               X          X
                                    Rθ (b) =       λ(w)         bw (r)ℓθ (w, r).                             (S28.3)
                                               w          r∈C
108                                                  QIAN QI


Proof. The event that call i is made is predictable, andP the conditional herald probability at that
call is p. Summing the nonnegative indicators gives Eθ i≤τ 1{si =s} = pEθ τ . Exactly one of these
heralds is accepted; all others are rejected. This proves the identity without an optional-stopping
integrability assumption. It implies the lower 1/p, attained by first-herald commitment. Equality
forces the nonnegative integer J to vanish almost surely. Conversely that rule has a geometric
call count of mean 1/p. Conditional on a failure word, the physical state is the displayed channel
product and is independent of the private tape. The independent, unobserved audit is performed
only after the decision. This gives the risk formula.                                             □

A singleton is permitted; an arbitrary prior is not part of the data. Write V = minb supθ Rθ (b) at

                                                                                       w

Theorem S28.2 (Boundary minimax and the regularity criterion). The primal optimum and a
least-favorable measure are attained, and
                                                V = max V (µ).                                           (S28.4)
                                                     µ∈P(Θ)

For an attaining µ∗ , every robust optimum has zero expected decision slack:
                  Z                             X          X
                      Rθ (b) dµ∗ (θ) − V =          λ(w)       bw (r)[ℓµ∗ (w, r) − aµ∗ (w)] ≥ 0.         (S28.5)

Suppose the minimizer a(w) ∈ C of ℓµ∗ (w, ·) is unique for every word. Then a is the unique robust
optimal decision rule, and a finite stochastic autonomous observer attains V if and only if the
relation
                        w ≡a v ⇐⇒ a(wz) = a(vz) for every z ∈ Y ∗                          (S28.6)
has finite index. The least number of live labels attaining V is exactly that index. Random transitions
and randomized readouts cannot reduce this exact count.
   At each prescribed finite live-label bound M , the best boundary risk is attained. If the index
is infinite, that best risk is strictly larger than V for every finite M , althoughPfinite-label policies
                                                                                      −1
converge to V at the same budget. A prefix implementation uses at most 1 + Tj=0          |Y |j live labels
and has excess risk at most Lq T . Its output dictionary remains C. For any implementation with M
live labels and at most m actual decoder values, event stopping uses at most max(M, m) total labels
and state stopping uses at most M + m, by Lemma 11.4.
Proof. The product B = w∈Y ∗ ∆(C) is compact and convex. The tail of every risk series is at
                               Q

most Lq T , uniformly in b and θ. Each truncated expression is jointly continuous and affine in the
appropriate variable. The full payoff on B × P(Θ) is therefore continuous and bilinear, with both
factors compact convex. The elementary compact minimax theorem applies [40]. For completeness,
it can also be obtained here from the finite-prefix games: truncate at T , separate the convex hull of
the finite-dimensional model loss vectors from a strict lower orthant, and take compact subsequential
limits of priors and plans. The uniform error Lq T passes both inequalities to the limit. Minimizing
for a fixed measure chooses a minimizing decision separately at every word and gives V (µ). This
proves equality and both attainments. Nonnegative summation proves the slack identity. At a
robust optimum its left side is at most zero and hence is zero. Since every λ(w) > 0, uniqueness
forces bw to be the point mass at a(w). Existence of a robust optimum then proves that this Bayes
rule is itself robustly optimal; a general Bayes minimizer need not have this property when ties
occur.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                      109


   At the boundary an M -label stochastic observer is represented by one stochastic transition matrix
Qy for each failure letter and one decoder distribution ds ∈ ∆(C) at each live label. Its initial
live label is fixed. Unreachable rows can be completed arbitrarily. Zero slack says that every
label reached with positive probability after w decodes a(w) with probability one. Choose one
positive-probability successor at each pair (s, y) and the uniquely prescribed output on each reached
label. This gives a deterministic observer with no more labels and the same output at every word:
its path always had positive probability in the stochastic observer. Thus exact random realization
implies deterministic realization.
   If a deterministic machine sends w, v to the same label, every common continuation sends them
through identical transitions and gives the same output. Hence they are equivalent in (S28.6).
Any exact machine has at least its number of equivalence classes. Conversely this relation is a
right congruence: w ≡a v implies wy ≡a vy. Its classes, with transition [w] 7→ [wy] and decoder
a(w), form an exact deterministic observer whenever the index is finite. This is the standard
residual-function construction for finite automata, applied to the optimal decision rule rather than
to the full predictive state.
   For fixed M , all Qy and ds lie in a finite product of compact simplexes. Each finite risk sum is
continuous in their entries. The same uniform geometric tail makes full risks, and their supremum
over Θ, continuous. A minimum is attained. It can equal V only if an exact machine exists, proving
the strict gap for infinite index. Finally follow an optimal full-history rule on words of length less
than T , storing that finite tree, and use one fallback decoder label thereafter. The policy still stops
at the first herald and costs exactly 1/p. Only the terminal mass q T can change loss, proving the
approximation and state bound. This argument uses no interior budget slack.                           □
   The quotient in (S28.6) is specific to this boundary task and this fixed dictionary. It is not a
common predictive quotient preserving every experiment or future audit. The minimax argument
is classical; the new content is its combination with exact minimum-cost commitment, zero-slack
determinization, and a necessary-and-sufficient autonomous memory count. Finite-index residual
characterizations and unary periodicity are established automata theory [43]; their use does not
establish a new priority claim for those tools.
Proposition S28.3 (Ties for one preparation, and why robust ties differ). For a single prepared
model, without uniqueness, an exact finite stochastic observer exists if and only if there is a finite-
index right congruence ∼ on Y ∗ such that
                       \
                            arg min ℓ(w, r) ̸= ∅   for every equivalence class A.
                               r∈C
                      w∈A
The least number of live labels is the least number of classes among such congruences. For several
unknown models the same zero-slack condition is necessary for an optimal stochastic observer under
a least-favorable measure, but deterministic selection from tied Bayes actions need not preserve
robust optimality.
Proof. For one preparation, every wordwise minimizing rule is globally optimal. Zero slack makes the
decoder support of any reached stochastic label a subset of every optimal-action set at its reaching
words. Choose one such output and a positive successor for each failure letter. Determinization
preserves optimality. The partition of words by its reached deterministic label is a right congruence
with the displayed nonempty intersections. Conversely choose an output in each intersection and
run the congruence-class observer. Minimizing the number of classes proves the count.
   For the distinction, take two models with identical channels and reports and losses (0, 1) and
(1, 0) on a two-point dictionary, independent of the word. The robust value is 1/2, attained with
one live label and a fair terminal decision. Under the equally weighted least-favorable measure both
actions are tied at loss 1/2. Either constant deterministic selection has worst loss one. Thus Bayes
zero slack alone cannot license robust determinization in the presence of ties.                    □

Proof. After n failures the prepared state is U n ρ0 U ∗n , whose audit mean is cn . The expected

integer, hence an integer, a contradiction. Thus ϑ/π is irrational; no cn is zero, and each residue
subsequence (n0 + jr)ϑ is dense modulo 2π for every positive integer r. The latter elementary
rotation fact follows, for example, by the pigeonhole approximation of a nonzero arbitrarily small
rotation and its successive multiples. Each residue subsequence therefore visits both open semicircles
on which cosine has opposite signs. The optimal sign sequence cannot be ultimately periodic. A
finite deterministic unary observer always follows a finite initial path and then a cycle, so it produces
an ultimately periodic decision sequence. Theorem S28.2 now excludes every finite stochastic
observer and proves the strict gap and its attainment at fixed M .

rule is exact for n < M and its excess loss is bounded by four on the tail of mass q M . Its cost is
still exactly 1/p. This proves the upper in (S28.10).

Corollary S28.6 (A compact uncertain-preparation family). Replace the known initial qubit by
ρθ = (I + θZ)/2, with unknown fixed θ ∈ [η, 1] and 0 < η ≤ 1. Keep the same instrument, dictionary
and audit. The robust boundary value is
                                                            X
                                       Vp,[η,1] = 2 − 2ηp         q n |cn |.
                                                            n≥0

A least-favorable preparation is ρη , and the unique robust optimal rule is again sgn cn . It is unattain-
able by every finite stochastic autonomous memory, while the same-budget prefix approximation
remains valid. Thus the obstruction is not tied to a chosen Bayesian prior or to one pure initial
preparation. It concerns this declared preparation family, not all qubit density matrices.
Proof. At preparation θ the conditional mean is θcn . The sign rule minimizes loss word by word
for every positive θ, and its total risk decreases with θ. Its worst value is therefore the displayed
one at η. Every common policy is lower-bounded by its risk in that same preparation, whose
unique minimizer is the sign rule. This proves the robust value, the least-favorable measure δη , and
uniqueness without an assumed prior. Apply Theorem S28.2. If η < 1, the family includes both
mixed and pure states; no inference is made at η = 0, where the least-favorable audit is a fair bit
and uniqueness fails.                                                                               □
Corollary S28.7 (Exact classification on unitary orbits). For any finite-dimensional unitary failure
channel, fixed prepared state, fixed finite dictionary and audit observables, suppose the minimizing
decision along the orbit is unique at every nonnegative integer time. A finite-label boundary optimum
exists if and only if the optimal decision sequence is periodic from time zero. Its least live-label count
is the least positive period.
Proof. The residual theorem first gives equivalence with ultimate periodicity. If the sequence has
eventual period r, there are integers kj → ∞ with U rkj → I in operator norm, by compactness of
112                                             QIAN QI


the finite-dimensional unitary torus. For each fixed n, the states at times n + rkj and n + r + rkj
converge to those at n and n + r. Uniqueness among finitely many decisions gives a strict gap
at each of these two limiting states, so their optimal decisions eventually agree with the limiting
decisions. Eventual r-periodicity makes these decisions equal. Therefore an = an+r for every n ≥ 0.
A cycle of length the least period realizes the rule, and every finite unary machine has an eventual
cycle at least that long.                                                                         □

S28.4. Dissipation and the strict decision-gap condition. Finite memory is not confined to
periodic unitary orbits. A stable decision at the limiting state gives a different positive realization
mechanism.

losses agree, explaining why the preceding positive-gap theorem does not apply.                  □

S28.6. A finite-precision synthesis bound for unary rational channels. There is also a
positive computational conclusion at the boundary, rather than only a nonattainment theorem.
This statement concerns one known prepared channel and a fixed finite dictionary; the full robust
synthesis problem is not asserted to have the same complexity.
Theorem S28.11 (Polynomial prefix synthesis at a bounded herald rate). Suppose Y has one
letter, the prepared density matrix and the channel superoperator in real Hermitian coordinates have
rational entries, and the m loss observables have rational entries and lie in [0, LI]. Let p and a
requested 0 < ϵ < L be rational. From these data one can construct an autonomous observer of
exact cost 1/p and risk at most V + ϵ, using
                                                       log(L/ϵ)
                                                                   
                                    T = max 1,
                                                      − log(1 − p)
live labels. The integer T may instead be chosen by exact successive rational tests of L(1 − p)T ≤ ϵ,
without evaluating a logarithm.
   After a common-denominator preprocessing, suppose the data entries have denominator dividing
D and numerators and D have at most B bits; include the rational herald rate, loss bound and
tolerance in the input size. The synthesis uses O(T (d4 + md2 )) rational coordinate operations on
integers with O(T B + log d) bits, in addition to the scalar stopping test. A direct implementation has
O(d2 (T B + log d)) bits of working storage, apart from its output program, and a transition/readout
table of O(T (log(T + 1) + log(m + 1))) bits. Thus, for a herald rate bounded away from zero, this is
a polynomial bit-time construction in the rational input size and log(1/ϵ), using standard integer
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         115


arithmetic. It is not a polynomial bound uniform as p ↓ 0, a matching lower bound, or a complexity
claim for continuum-model robust optimization.
Proof. Compute ρn = Φn (ρ0 ) for 0 ≤ n < T . At each step evaluate the m rational numbers tr(Fr ρn )
and choose a least one, resolving ties by the fixed dictionary order. Use a chain of T live labels; on
failure advance until the last label, which repeats. At a herald commit the decision stored at that
label. The rule is optimal on all terminal words of length less than T . Its excess loss is at most
L on the remaining terminal mass q T , while its call count is still geometric with mean 1/p. This
proves the guarantees.
   A channel application is a matrix–vector product in d2 real Hermitian coordinates, requiring O(d4 )
scalar operations. Evaluating all decisions costs O(md2 ) more. Use real and imaginary matrix-entry
coordinates, with off-diagonal entries paired, so all physical state coordinates have absolute value at
most one. After n applications their denominator divides Dn+1 . Keeping this common denominator
explicitly gives integer numerators of O((n + 1)B) bits; a loss inner product adds only O(B + log d)
bits. The same size bound follows directly during multiplication and summation, and comparison
of decisions uses integer comparisons at the shared denominator. Store two state vectors and the
current best decision; the stated working bound includes the growing state numerators. The input
itself is read-only and not included in working storage. The output table stores a successor and a
dictionary index per live label, with no stored floating phase or external clock. Standard integer
addition and multiplication have polynomial bit cost, giving the final claim. If the exact scalar test
is used, the denominator and numerator of q T also have O(T B) bits. The displayed bound on T is
logarithmic in precision only when p has the stated positive lower bound.                            □

   The qubit in Theorem S28.5 belongs to this rational-superoperator class: although U has algebraic
entries, its conjugation map has rational coefficients with denominator five. Its exact optimum
requires unbounded classical memory, but its boundary-optimal risk has efficiently computable
finite-memory approximants. The stored number of live-label bits is ⌈log2 T ⌉; this does not replace
the separate program and arithmetic storage accounts above.
   The algebraic rotating-qubit idea and nonperiodic quantum acceptance events have established
antecedents in quantum automata [43]. We do not claim priority for that mechanism. Here
it is compiled into a paid, single-command prediction experiment whose least feasible cost is
attained, whose full-history bounded-risk optimum is attained, and whose exact risk is nevertheless
unattainable with every fixed finite classical memory. The same boundary admits exponentially
accurate finite-label approximations. The transition-table account excludes the fixed numerical
dictionary and primitive description, whose encoding lengths must also be added when measuring
total program size. These facts distinguish optimum attainment, fixed-memory gaps, and density,
rather than inferring one from another. They do not settle the sharp intermediate-memory phase-type
timing curve of Theorem S29.1, the general boundary-density question, or a universal unknown-model
predictive quotient.

                 S29. A timing task with an exact attainment threshold
   The complete stopped law can distinguish controllers whose terminal outputs are identical. A
basic instance is a delayed-release protocol: a stateless physical device emits the same productive
report at every unit-cost call, and the observer is asked to commit at a prescribed call. There is
one legal command, a constant decoder and a constant audit. An autonomous observer has no free
clock, call counter or persistent random tape. Its retained label is its only intercall state. Thus finite
live-state implementations are discrete phase-type stopping mechanisms. Phase-type timing and its
optimal variability have classical antecedents; the continuous-time coefficient-of-variation theorem
of Aldous–Shepp [39] is a different objective from the complete-law, discrete-call approximation
considered here.
116                                             QIAN QI


   Fix an integer K ≥ 2 and let the target stopped law be commitment exactly at call K. For
a proper observer with stopping time τ ≥ 1, its total-variation discrepancy from this target is
1 − Pr(τ = K). Write ∆N,M (K) for the smallest discrepancy at expected-call budget N ≥ 1 with at
most M autonomous live labels. Event stopping may reuse one live label for the constant decoder.
State stopping requires an additional disjoint terminal label. Let ∆N,∞ (K) denote the full-history
optimum. This notation counts live labels, not total labels, in both conventions.
Theorem S29.1 (Delayed release: budget curve, exact state threshold and pumping gap). For
every K ≥ 2 and N ≥ 1,
                                                (K − N )+
                                    ∆N,∞ (K) =            .                        (S29.1)
                                                  K −1
For one live label, put n = min{N, K}. The exact optimum is
                                                    1       1 K−1
                                                             
                                   ∆N,1 (K) = 1 −      1−           .                          (S29.2)
                                                    n      n
At every N > 1, the least live-label count attaining (S29.1) is exactly K. Consequently its least total
count is K for event stopping and K + 1 for state stopping. At N = 1, immediate commitment with
one live label already attains the discrepancy one.
   More quantitatively, for M < K, every feasible autonomous observer with s = Pr(τ = K) obeys
                                s2                           Ks2
                         s+     2K−2
                                     ≤ 1,       (K − 1)s +          ≤ N − 1.               (S29.3)
                             M                             M 2K−2
These explicit bounds force a strict gap from the unrestricted optimum at every N > 1. For N ≥ K
they give the budget-independent bound ∆N,M (K) ≥ 1/(4M 2K−2 ). The one-label curve and K-label
attainment threshold are exact; no sharpness of the intermediate-M quantitative bound is asserted.
Proof. Put s = Pr(τ = K). Since τ ≥ 1, Eτ ≥ 1 + (K − 1)s. Thus s ≤ min{1, (N − 1)/(K − 1)}.
This is attained by stopping at call one with probability 1 − s and at call K with probability s. It is
an actual causal construction: at the first call either commit or enter a finite deterministic chain, and
then advance one counted label per call until commitment. It uses K live labels when s > 0, with
no external clock or surviving seed. For N ≥ K use the deterministic chain. This proves (S29.1)
and its upper state count. Equivalently, the bounded score 1{τ ̸=K} witnesses this complete-law
discrepancy in Theorem S26.3; the constant terminal-output marginal would not witness it.
   With one live label the post-report halt probability is one constant p > 0, so τ is geometric on
the positive integers. The budget says p ≥ 1/N , and its target mass is p(1 − p)K−1 . Its derivative
on (0, 1) has the sign of 1 − Kp. The maximum over [1/N, 1] is therefore at p = 1/ min(N, K). The
endpoints give the same formula, proving (S29.2).
   For the lower state count and the quantitative bound, let Q be the substochastic transition matrix
on the live labels and let h = (I − Q)1 be its halt vector. The initial live label is prescribed. A
path that halts at call K contains K live labels before its calls and at most M K−1 possible label
sequences. If s > 0, one such path has probability q ≥ s/M K−1 . When M < K, its label sequence
repeats a label. The intervening live cycle has length ℓ ≥ 1 and transition-product probability r.
Every other factor in the original path is at most one, so r ≥ q. Traverse that same cycle once
more and follow the original remainder to halt. This is a legal path of length K + ℓ, of probability
qr ≥ q 2 . It is disjoint from all length-K paths. Normalization yields 1 ≥ s + q 2 , proving the first
inequality. The identity Eτ = 1 + E(τ − 1) yields
                     Eτ ≥ 1 + (K − 1)s + (K + ℓ − 1)q 2 ≥ 1 + (K − 1)s + Kq 2 ,
which proves the second. Zero-probability paths need no conditioning, and the case s = 0 is
immediate.
  For 1 < N < K, the positive quadratic term makes the largest permitted s strictly smaller than
(N − 1)/(K − 1); for N ≥ K the first inequality makes it strictly smaller than one. Thus fewer
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         117


than K live labels cannot attain the unrestricted curve, even as an infimum. If s ≥ 1/2, the first
inequality gives 1 − s ≥ 1/(4M 2K−2 ); if s < 1/2, that bound is weaker than 1 − s > 1/2. This gives
the last assertion. The globally typed state-stopped interface has a separate terminal label, whereas
the final event permits reuse in event stopping, yielding the two total counts.                    □
   This temporal lower bound is not a command-domain incompatibility count: every live context has
the very same legal command. It comes from repeated autonomous state and the extra observable
stopping histories forced by a positive cycle. A clock supplied as a physical input, a nonconstant
report encoding elapsed time, or a persistent external seed changes the interface and invalidates that
lower. The result is an exact delayed-release consequence with matching budget and attainment
counts, not a solution of the entire intermediate-state phase-type optimization or a claim to settle a
previously recognized open timing problem.

                   S30. An intrinsic hierarchy for paid causal synthesis
   A comparison inequality does not create the acquisition program used by its upper bound. We
give an intrinsic criterion over all classical feedback programs on a finite-branching causal interface.
It applies to unknown-model classical experiments and finite-dimensional quantum instruments,
with one program shared by every model and initial state. Exact unrestricted-history synthesis and
exact fixed-memory synthesis remain different conclusions.
S30.1. Primitive data and the controller class. A visible history records executed commands
and reports. At each open decision node h, the legal next-command set A(h) is finite and nonempty.
After a productive report there may also be a finite set O(h) of legal terminal routes. There is no
terminal route at the root or after a nonproductive report. Each command has finitely many reports,
so the tree is locally finite and countable. Legal domains are common to the admitted models and
are part of the interface. A physical phase determined by visible history is not an additional free
input to a finite-memory observer.
   The unknown model θ belongs to an arbitrary nonempty set Θ, not necessarily finite, compact or
equipped with a prior. At each history the hidden quantum state lies in a specified finite-dimensional
matrix algebra. Command a has completely positive maps Iθ,h,a,y with
                                                  ∗
                                           X
                                                 Iθ,h,a,y (I) = I.
                                             y

Dimensions may depend on the model and finite history. The initial identity is Iθ . Nonnegative
classical transition matrices are the diagonal special case. There is no positive-likelihood or eigenvalue
floor.
   Costs are nonnegative. Their conditional expected-cost observables satisfy the uniform floor
                                         Gθ,h,a ⪰ cI,        c > 0.                               (S30.1)
A pathwise floor implies this assumption, but only the displayed conditional floor is needed here.
The cost Cτ is the core cost through commitment. A separately executed preparation or audit
is charged in addition or represented as a compulsory call; it is not silently included without its
physical operation.
   Fix finitely many bounded terminal scores j = 1, . . . , J. Route o at h has a Hermitian score
observable Fθ,h,o,j on the current state, with
                            αj I ≼ Fθ,h,o,j ≼ βj I,       −∞ < αj ≤ βj < ∞.                       (S30.2)
The route is chosen before observing its future audit. For example, F is the adjoint of the declared
audit instrument applied to a bounded score of its outcome and the chosen route. These are
primitive unnormalized observables, not assumed posteriors. Prescribed Hermitian targets on the
initial state are denoted by Aθ,j ; constant targets are scalar multiples of Iθ .
118                                                     QIAN QI


  A controller uses full visible history and independent private randomness, but neither the unknown
model nor an unknown initial density matrix. It must commit exactly once almost surely and satisfy
                sup Eθ,ρ Cτ ≤ N,            Eθ,ρ Φj = tr(ρAθ,j )   (θ ∈ Θ, ρ ⪰ 0, tr ρ = 1).     (S30.3)
                 θ,ρ

This is classical feedback over the specified quantum experiment, not an uncharged coherent controller
memory. Uniformity over initial states is distinct from uniformity over rooted continuations of a
history-dependent experiment; both are required before a program may be repeated after rejection.
Restricted preparation faces and deadlocks are treated explicitly in the current supplement.
S30.2. One realization plan, rather than modelwise policies. At a horizon T ≥ 1, include
decisions following the first T reports. Continuing at depth T leaves an open branch; its next
physical call has not occurred. The weight γ(h) is the product of controller choice probabilities
along h, excluding physical report probabilities. Write γ(h, d) after a command or terminal decision.
The common sequence-form polytope PT is given by
                                                   X

                                                    d
for every present report child. All variables lie in [0, 1], and the same variables are used for every
model. Sequence form itself is classical [10]; its role is to preserve the common-policy quantifier.
   Let Iθ,h be the composition of the primitive maps along h, without controller weights. Define
affine functions on this polytope by
                                            ∗

Every coefficient is a raw finite-path matrix. No report probability is divided out.
Lemma S30.1 (One plan for all models). The polytope is nonempty and compact. Its elements are
exactly the finite-prefix laws of common randomized feedback controllers, with expectations given by
traces against the displayed operators, simultaneously for every model and initial state. Arbitrary
private random memory does not enlarge these laws relative to visible-history policies using fresh
randomness. Moreover Bθ,T , Sθ,T ⪰ 0 and their sum is Iθ .
Proof. A behavioral policy gives the product weights. Conversely, at a node of positive weight choose
d with probability γ(h, d)/γ(h); at a zero-weight node choose any legal row. Induction recovers
every specified weight. The finite linear constraints form a closed bounded set, and any choice of
legal rows proves nonemptiness.
   To include arbitrary private memory, fix a visible action–report path and integrate over all private
random tapes producing those controller choices when supplied that report path. This probability is
independent of the physical model and initial state. The raw path instrument is the same for every
compatible tape, because the executed commands and reports have been fixed. Thus it factors from
the tape integral, and the integrated choice weights obey the same equations. The ratio construction
reproduces the laws in all models at once. It uses full visible-history storage; it does not make a
tape into free finite memory.
   Multiply the raw path effects by these weights. Summing report effects uses normalization of
each instrument, and summing controller choices uses the plan equation. Induction shows that
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        119


terminated and open effects sum to the incoming effect, starting from Iθ . Every such effect is
positive. The cost and score formulas follow by summing their actual finite-prefix contributions. □
S30.3. The necessary cost reserve and terminal-score intervals. For a finite F ⊂ Θ, let
HT (F, N ) be the feasibility problem in γ ∈ PT defined by
                     Cθ,T + cSθ,T ≼ N Iθ ,                                                         (S30.8)
                           αj Sθ,T ≼ Aθ,j − Vθ,j,T ≼ βj Sθ,T   (θ ∈ F, 1 ≤ j ≤ J).                 (S30.9)
These are linear inequalities in the classical case and linear matrix inequalities in the quantum case.
They are not implemented by pretending that open paths terminate at the cutoff. Those paths owe
at least one further paid call, and their remaining bounded scores lie in the displayed interval.
Lemma S30.2 (Restriction and the sharp budget tail). A feasible plan at level T + 1 restricts to a
feasible plan at level T . Every feasible level with N ≥ c satisfies
                           0 ≼ Sθ,T ≼ δT Iθ ,     δT = min{1, (N/c − 1)/T }.                     (S30.10)
If N < c, there is no feasible proper program.
Proof. Write DT = BT +1 − BT ⪰ 0 for the new terminal effect. Normalization gives ST = DT + ST +1 .
New score increments lie between αj DT and βj DT . Adding them to the next-level remainder
intervals proves the current-level intervals. Every path still open after depth T executes another call
before the next decision. Therefore CT +1 − CT ⪰ cST . Together with the next-level cost constraint
this implies the current one.
   Let St denote the open    effect after t calls, so S0 = I and St ⪰ ST for t < T . The conditional
                         PT −1
cost floor gives CT ⪰ c t=0      St ⪰ c[I + (T − 1)ST ]. Adding the reserve gives c[I + T ST ] ≼ N I.
Positivity and ST ≼ I prove the claimed bound. Every proper program must execute its first paid
call, proving the infeasible boundary below the floor.                                               □
Theorem S30.3 (Finite-obstruction characterization of paid causal synthesis). Under the stated
finite-branching, positive-cost and bounded-score hypotheses, the following are equivalent:
   (i) One common proper visible-history program satisfies (S30.3) for every model and every initial
state.
   (ii) HT (F, N ) is feasible for every finite model set F ⊂ Θ and every integer T ≥ 1.
   Consequently nonexistence is witnessed by a finite horizon and a finite set of operating models.
Feasibility at arbitrarily many tested levels alone is not a finite certificate for the infinite condition.
No fixed bound on controller memory is asserted for the limiting policy.
Proof. For necessity take the prefix of the actual program. Every open path must make at least one
additional call before commitment, so its conditional future expected cost is at least c. Nonnegative
conditional summation proves the reserved cost inequality. Scores on the disjoint future terminal
events lie between αj and βj times their total open mass. Almost-sure commitment makes that
mass exactly ST , proving the score intervals. These same prefix weights work in every model.
   For sufficiency consider the compact product of all nodewise probability simplexes on the countable
history tree. A finite-prefix plan can be extended to an element of this product by arbitrary legal
rows at later nodes. Its realization weights are finite products of coordinates, hence continuous.
Each finite collection of the cost and score constraints is closed. For such a collection take the union
of its finitely many model indices and its greatest horizon. Assumption (ii) and the restriction
lemma give one feasible plan for every constraint in the collection. Extend it to all nodes. The closed
constraints therefore have the finite-intersection property in a compact space, and one common
infinite policy satisfies them all.
   For this policy (S30.10) gives uniformly vanishing survival probabilities, including the exact
boundary N = c. Thus it commits almost surely in every model and initial state. Its partial costs
are nonnegative and at most N ; monotone convergence yields the actual total cost bound. The
120                                                    QIAN QI


norm of the score remainder is at most max(|αj |, |βj |)δT . It tends to zero, so the bounded terminal
scores have exactly the prescribed operator expectations. The program is common because the
product coordinates were common throughout. Finally, negating the equivalence proves existence of
a finite obstruction when no such program exists.                                                   □
Corollary S30.4 (Attainment of the least finite cost). If any common proper program satisfies the
prescribed targets at finite uniform cost, then the infimum of such cost budgets is attained.
Proof. Let feasible budgets decrease to their finite infimum N∗ . For each fixed finite model set
and horizon, the corresponding nonempty compact feasible sets decrease with the budget. Their
intersection is nonempty and is feasible at N∗ . Every finite problem at N∗ is therefore feasible; the
theorem gives a proper program at that budget. The first-call floor ensures N∗ ≥ c.                 □

S30.4. A strict dual certificate at an obstructing level. List all left-minus-right matrix
violations of (S30.8)–(S30.9) as Kl (γ), 1 ≤ l ≤ L0 . Thus feasibility is Kl ≼ 0 for every l. Define
                                v = min max{0, max λmax (Kl (γ))}.
                                      γ∈PT                   l

Compactness makes this minimum attained and v > 0 exactly when the finite problem is infeasible.
Theorem S30.5 (Strict finite dual obstruction). The exact violation value is
                                                                 X
                                 v=     max            min           tr[Wl Kl (γ)].          (S30.11)
                                      P Wl ⪰0          γ∈PT
                                                                 l
                                        l
                                            tr Wl ≤1

The maximum is attained. An infeasible level has a positive-matrix separating family with strictly
positive value. The inner minimization is a finite linear program in the common realization weights.
No Slater hypothesis or inverse covariance is required.
Proof. For a fixed collection of violations, maximizing the displayed pairing over positive matrices
with total trace at most one gives their largest positive eigenvalue, or zero by taking all matrices
zero. Taking a minimizer defining v gives the weak upper bound for every family W .
   For the converse when v > 0, fix 0 ≤ η < v. In the finite direct sum of Hermitian matrix spaces,
the point (ηIl )l does not belong to the closed convex set
                                                                     Y
                                 {(Kl (γ))l : γ ∈ PT } +                  {Dl ⪰ 0}.
                                                                      l

This set is closed because its first summand is compact. Finite-dimensional strict separation gives a
nonzero linear functional whose infimum on that set exceeds its value at the excluded point. The
functional must be nonnegative on the positive cone, or its infimum along a cone ray would be minus
infinity. Hence it has the form of pairing with positive Wl . Their total trace is positive; normalize
it to one. Separation yields an inner minimum greater than η. Let η ↑ v and take a convergent
subsequence in the compact normalized positive-matrix set. The inner minimum is continuous in
W , being the minimum of a continuous bilinear function over compact PT . Its limiting value is at
least v. If v = 0, a feasible plan gives the upper bound zero and W = 0 attains zero. This proves
equality and attainment in all cases.                                                               □

  The finite dual is a genuine obstruction, not an assertion that the first obstructing horizon is
uniformly bounded or effectively discoverable for arbitrary real primitive data. Existing quantum
decision-process and reachability theories already contain substantial control, duality and com-
putability results [12, 13, 14]. Generic quantum MDPs, sequence form and convex separation are
not claimed as new ingredients.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       121


S30.5. Paid finite-prefix completion. Suppose every open leaf reachable by a selected finite
prefix admits one common legal fallback that commits with conditional expected cost at most κ0 ≥ c,
uses at most B0 live roles, and has terminal scores in the same [αj , βj ] intervals. Its command and
physical phase compatibility must be certified at these leaves.
Corollary S30.6 (A paid finite-prefix completion). A feasible level-T plan completed by this fallback
has, for every model and initial state,
                                       EC ≤ N + (κ0 − c)δT ,                                   (S30.12)
                           −(βj − αj )δT I ≼ Aθ,j − E[Φj ] ≼ (βj − αj )δT I.                   (S30.13)
Here the score expectation is its operator on the initial state. If the finite prefix uses LT live node
roles and the completed terminal decoder has t actual values, a sufficient state-stopped label count is
LT + B0 + t and an event-stopped count is max(LT + B0 , t). Program constants, arithmetic and the
possible exponential growth of LT are separate costs.
Proof. The completed cost is at most CT + κ0 ST . Subtract the reserved cost inequality and use the
survival bound. Both the prescribed remaining score and the fallback’s remaining score lie between
αj ST and βj ST . Their difference lies between the stated opposite multiples of ST , proving the error
bound. A finite prefix is implemented by storing its current node; the fallback uses its declared live
roles. The exact role-packing lemma gives the two sufficient counts. These are existence counts, not
a claim that a real-valued finite plan has an exact finite-bit sampler.                              □
Proposition S30.7 (Sharpness of the uniform tail rate). For unit cost per call, every b ≥ 1 and
integer T ≥ 1 with b − 1 ≤ T admit a proper finite-branching program of mean cost b and survival
probability (b − 1)/T after T calls.
Proof. With probability p = (b − 1)/T commit on call T + 1, and otherwise on call one. Then
Eτ = 1 + T p = b and Pr(τ > T ) = p. Its prefix has cost 1 + (T − 1)p and reserve p. Thus the
reserve and the boundary b = 1 are exact. If b − 1 > T , the universal tail bound one is attained by
a deterministic sufficiently long path. This concerns the survival bound, not a uniform expected
cost-tail estimate.                                                                               □
S30.6. Synthesis of a finite marked squared-task reference. For a finite virtual mark set
X, fix masses νx , target means f (x) ∈ Rk and nonnegative residuals w(x) of a genuinely prepared
reference law. Assume bounded audit coordinates. Use as terminal scores
                          1{X=x} ,   1{X=x} Zi (1 ≤ i ≤ k),     1{X=x} ∥Z∥2 ,
with respective scalar-identity targets νx , νx fi (x) and νx [w(x) + ∥f (x)∥2 ]. They are computed from
the declared terminal audit instrument and route.
Corollary S30.8 (Intrinsic synthesis of finite-mark squared-task balance). There is one common
proper finite-branching acquisition program with these unnormalized marked moments and cost at
most N for all models and initial states if and only if every finite problem of the hierarchy for this
finite score list is feasible. Such a program satisfies
                                              X
                     E[q(X)∥Z − r(X)∥2 ] =         νx q(x)[w(x) + ∥f (x) − r(x)∥2 ]
                                               x
for every selected readout. If, in addition, the same return rule is legal and the same certificate
holds on every actual rooted continuation after rejection, it gives exact task balance for repeated
acquisition. The hierarchy alone does not supply a finite live-memory bound.
Proof. The first equivalence is the hierarchy theorem applied to the explicit bounded score list.
Expand the squared loss in each mark and substitute the three unnormalized moment identities.
This proves the task equation without division by νx , so zero-mass marks are harmless. Conversely
122                                                QIAN QI


the displayed marked moment requirement is exactly that finite target list. Applying this program
repeatedly requires its hypotheses on the actual later roots; no equality of initial-state matrices can
replace missing history-dependent continuation data. A finite prefix with a legal fallback, or the
separate finite-memory theorem, supplies an explicitly charged implementation when its additional
hypotheses hold.                                                                                      □

          S31. Compact uncertainty: certified risk at the unchanged budget
   The preceding primitive construction uses finitely many operating models. A finite grid of an
unknown continuum is not itself a robust model family: a policy that meets its budget on the grid
can overspend between grid points. We now control that discrepancy from the raw instruments,
including the unbounded waiting cost after completion. The resulting finite programs enclose the
optimal risk for a compact model family and produce a common finite-label observer at the original
budget. No model index is revealed to the observer. The proof replaces a worst-horizon accumulation
by the actual pre-call occupation of the tested policy.
   Robustness and approximation of partially observed control have substantial prior theory [29, 30,
14]. The particular assertion here concerns an undiscounted stopping task, a uniform expected-cost
constraint, finite output width, and a physically executable completion which restores that same
constraint. It is not priority for model approximation or perturbation bounds in general.

S31.1. A compact family of primitive instruments. Use the finite command/report interface
of Section S25, but let the unknown, fixed throughout a run, model θ range over a nonempty compact
metric space Θ. All models act on the same d-dimensional physical space. Productive report sets
and legal commands are common. Each normalized instrument is {Iθ,a,y }y , each nonnegative call
cost has observable Gθ,a , and the prospective terminal loss at r ∈ D has observable Fθ,a,y,r . Here D
is compact metric, and
                   c∗ I ≼ Gθ,a ≼ c∗ I,        0 ≼ Fθ,a,y,r ≼ LI,        0 < c∗ ≤ c∗ < ∞,
with L > 0. An observed cost or phase used by a policy must be included in the declared report; it
is not another free input. Additional preparation, return and terminal-audit charges use the same
explicit convention as before.
   For two models define symmetric primitive discrepancies
                          1              X
            ϵI (θ, ϕ) =     max sup         ∥Iθ,a,y (ρ) − Iϕ,a,y (ρ)∥1 ,                                  (S31.1)
                          2 a ρ⪰0, tr ρ=1 y
            ϵg (θ, ϕ) = max ∥Gθ,a − Gϕ,a ∥,        ϵF (θ, ϕ) =       sup        ∥Fθ,a,y,r − Fϕ,a,y,r ∥.
                           a
                                                                 a,y∈Ya+ ,r∈D

Assume these discrepancies tend uniformly to zero with the model distance. Assume also a common
modulus ω(η) ↓ 0 for terminal losses as decision distance tends to zero. Joint continuity on the
compact model and decision carriers is one sufficient primitive condition. The report-resolved trace
distance in (S31.1) is for classical feedback on the specified physical system; a coherent external
reference or quantum controller is not included by calling it a diamond distance.
   A finite set F ⊂ Θ is an (ϵI , ϵg , ϵF )-cover when each θ ∈ Θ has a ϕ ∈ F with all three discrepancies
bounded by these displayed numbers. The bounds, not just the grid points, are the certificate.
Compactness and the assumed continuity provide arbitrarily fine finite covers. An effective cover
requires supplied representations and certified moduli; compactness alone does not provide an
algorithm on unspecified real data.
Lemma S31.1 (A uniform common fallback from compact primitive data). There is a proper
common policy of finite uniform expected cost for every model and every initial density matrix if and
                                    CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       123


only if no model has a common dark subspace. In this case any one fixed full-support command row,
stopped at its first productive report, is a uniform fallback. With the notation of Theorem S25.1,
                            δ = min λmin [I − (Tθ∗ )d I] > 0,          κ0 = c∗ d/δ
                                 θ∈Θ
is a valid cost bound. If a finite cover has instrument error at most ϵI , then
                                     δ ≥ min λmin [I − (Tϕ∗ )d I] − dϵI .                          (S31.2)
                                          ϕ∈F
A positive right side certifies the common fallback on the entire family, not only on the cover.
Proof. A dark subspace in even one model prevents any all-initial-state policy from committing
there, by the commandwise argument of Theorem S25.1. If none exists, that theorem makes every
displayed depth-d observable positive definite. Its entries depend continuously on θ. Compactness
and continuity of the least eigenvalue therefore give a strictly positive minimum. The same block-
survival proof bounds every expected cost by c∗ d/δ, using the one fixed row and no model-dependent
choice.
   For the quantitative inequality, if 0 ≼ Ay ≼ HI, instrument normalization permits subtraction of
HI/2 in every report component. Trace duality gives, for every density matrix,
                               X
                                    tr [Ay (Iθ,a,y − Iϕ,a,y )(ρ)] ≤ HϵI (θ, ϕ).                    (S31.3)
                                y

Indeed the sum of the traces of the differences is zero, and the remaining terms are at most H/2 times
the sum of their trace norms. To compare killed maps on 0 ≼ A ≼ I, take Ay = A on nonproductive
reports and zero on productive ones, and average the common command row. Telescope the d
powers. Positive subunital maps preserve the interval [0, I] and contract the Hermitian operator
norm, so each of the d differences has norm at most ϵI . The least-eigenvalue inequality gives (S31.2).
This argument uses bounded effects, not a normalized posterior or a positive likelihood floor. □
S31.2. Perturbation controlled by actual occupation. For a common depth-T realization
plan, write Cθ,T , Sθ,T and Vθ,T as before. After the decision following report T , the open branches
have not yet executed another call. For a fixed initial density ρ, put
                                         nϕ,T (ρ) = Eϕ,ρ min(τ, T ).
This is the actual number of begun calls in the finite prefix under ϕ. The next lemma requires a
budget only in this source model, not in a hybrid execution.
Lemma S31.2 (Occupation-sensitive prefix comparison). For every common depth-T plan, every
initial density matrix and every κ ≥ 0,
                                | tr ρ(Sθ,T − Sϕ,T )| ≤ ϵI nϕ,T (ρ),                               (S31.4)
                                                   
           tr ρ (Vθ,T + LSθ,T ) − (Vϕ,T + LSϕ,T ) ≤ LϵI nϕ,T (ρ) + ϵF ,                            (S31.5)
                                                                           ∗
           tr ρ (Cθ,T + κSθ,T ) − (Cϕ,T + κSϕ,T ) ≤ nϕ,T (ρ)[ϵg + ϵI (c (T − 1) + κ)].             (S31.6)
Here the errors may be replaced by any certified bounds for this pair of models. In particular, a
source reserve Cϕ,T + c∗ Sϕ,T ≼ N I implies nϕ,T (ρ) ≤ N/c∗ for all ρ. Thus the loss error need not
grow as T ϵI on a fixed-cost prefix, whereas the cost comparison still has an explicit remaining-horizon
factor.
Proof. Use backward cost or score observables on the finite history tree. At a pre-call node h with
t < T calls already made, average its common command row. For the loss padded by L on open depth-
T leaves, the target-model post-report continuation observable is a convex combination of the terminal
score and the subsequent padded score; it lies in [0, LI]. For the survival indicator the corresponding
observable lies in [0, I]. For cost padded by κ on open leaves it lies in [0, (c∗ (T − t − 1) + κ)I]. These
are deterministic bounds for the finite prefix, valid before any comparison of its stopping laws.
124                                             QIAN QI


   Subtract the two backward recursions by writing
                            Iθ∗ Aθ − Iϕ∗ Aϕ = (Iθ∗ − Iϕ∗ )Aθ + Iϕ∗ (Aθ − Aϕ ).
At each node, (S31.3) bounds the first term by the corresponding continuation bound times ϵI .
Iterate the second term with the source path instruments and source controller weights. Pairing
at the initial state expresses the accumulated discrepancy as a sum over actual source pre-call
occupation. The total of these masses is nϕ,T (ρ), including all failures and all private random
choices.
   For cost, the immediate observable difference adds at most ϵg per source call. Replacing T − t − 1
by T − 1 gives (S31.6). For terminal loss, the differences of the actual terminal observables add at
most ϵF times source commitment mass through the cutoff, which is at most one. They are not
charged at every rejected report. The padded terminal value at an open cutoff is the same L in both
models. These facts prove (S31.5); survival has no terminal-observable discrepancy. The positive
source cost floor gives the last assertion.
   One can equivalently obtain the recursion by first fixing all private choices at each visible history
and then integrating them. The common behavioral realization is justified by Lemma S30.1. The
proof does not reveal ϕ to the controller, condition on a future audit, run a competitor on a fictitious
reference past, or divide by a possibly zero report mass.                                             □

S31.3. A finite certificate at precisely the required budget. Fix a nonempty finite dictionary
C ⊂ D and a common fallback which can start from every current physical state in every θ, commits
with a value in C, has uniform cost at most κ, and uses B live labels. Initially Lemma S31.1 supplies
one with B = 1; the cost hierarchy below supplies cheaper ones. Assume N > κ.
  Let qF,C,T (N ) be the finite semidefinite value (S25.1) with constraints only at the finitely many
cover models ϕ ∈ F , but with the same common realization plan. Define
                    DT = min{1, (N/c∗ − 1)/T },
                                       N
                   eF,T = (κ − c∗ )DT + [ϵg + ϵI (c∗ (T − 1) + κ)],                             (S31.7)

                                    N                               LeF,T

A proper fallback must make at least one call, so κ ≥ c∗ . These errors are nonnegative.
Theorem S31.3 (Same-budget risk enclosure for compact uncertainty). Under the primitive
hypotheses just stated, the finite problem is feasible and attains its value, and
                        qF,C,T (N ) ≤ RΘ,C (N ) ≤ min{L, qF,C,T (N ) + ΓF,T }.                  (S31.9)
The upper is realized by one proper finite-label controller of expected cost at most N for every θ ∈ Θ
and every initial density matrix. It uses one dictionary, not a modelwise family of dictionaries.
With a commands and at most y reports per command, sufficient live labels are
                                                     −1
