                                           1+B+            (ay)t .                             (S31.10)
                                                     t=0

Event-stopped total labels are the maximum of this bound and |C|; state-stopped total labels are their
sum.
   For nested covers and increasing horizons the finite lower values are nondecreasing. If T → ∞,
T ϵI → 0, and ϵg , ϵF → 0, their limit equals RΘ,C (N ). At a√fixed slack N − κ > 0, taking T ≍ ϵ−1/2
when all primitive errors are at most ϵ gives an explicit O( ϵ) enclosure. This is an upper modulus,
not a claim that its exponent is optimal.
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       125


Proof. The actual fallback has cost below N in all models, so its prefixes prove feasibility on F . A
finite plan lies in a compact sequence-form polytope, and the constraints are closed with 0 ≤ q ≤ L.
The minimum is attained. Restriction of any feasible full policy on Θ to the finite models and
horizon proves the lower. Adding cover models or extending the horizon cannot weaken that lower,
by the restriction lemma.
   Take a finite optimizer. At every ϕ ∈ F , the reserve gives Sϕ,T ≼ DT I and source call occupation
at most N/c∗ . For any θ choose a covering ϕ. Complete the same prefix on the actual experiment
with the declared fallback at every open leaf. Positivity bounds the completed cost by Cθ,T + κSθ,T
and the completed loss by Vθ,T + LSθ,T . At the covering model these are respectively at most
N + (κ − c∗ )DT and qF,C,T (N ) + LDT . Lemma S31.2 now gives completed cost at most N + eF,T
and loss at most qF,C,T (N ) + zF,T in every model and initial state. The fallback is applied on the
actual post-prefix state, not the state at the covering model. Its all-state guarantee is what makes
this completion legal and proper.
   Before the first physical call, choose the completed prefix with probability
                                                 N −κ
                                          p=
                                               N − κ + eF,T
and the fallback alone otherwise. The choice is independent of the unknown model and input. Its
robust expected cost is at most p(N + eF,T ) + (1 − p)κ = N . Its loss is at most
                      p(qF,C,T + zF,T ) + (1 − p)L ≤ qF,C,T + zF,T + (1 − p)L.
This is the claimed gap. The actual loss is always at most L, giving the truncation of the upper.
Both branches commit almost surely, so does their mixture. The sole initial choice is compiled
into one initial row whose within-call branch choice routes to prefix or fallback labels; it is not an
uncounted persistent seed. The finite tree has at most the displayed sum of live node roles, and the
fallback uses B. The role-packing lemma proves both total-label counts.
   The explicit errors tend to zero under the stated joint refinement. Compact continuity supplies
arbitrarily fine model covers, so such refinements exist. For the rate, substitute DT = O(T −1 ) and
bound the mixture denominator below by N − κ. The gap is at most a constant times T −1 + ϵT + ϵ,
yielding the asserted choice. None of these estimates assumes independence of the physical states
between calls or a mean-cost tail converging uniformly over feasible policies.                       □
Corollary S31.4 (Preserving an optimized finite output width). Let RΘ,m (N ) optimize over all
dictionaries of at most m points in D. For a finite η-net Dη , minimize qF,C,T (N ) over tuples
C ∈ Dηm with repetition, and call the result qF,m,T,η (N ). The fallback may use any one value of the
chosen dictionary without changing its costs. Then
                 [qF,m,T,η (N ) − ω(η)]+ ≤ RΘ,m (N ) ≤ min{L, qF,m,T,η (N ) + ΓF,T }.
The common observer has at most m outputs. In particular model and decision approximation do
not create a hidden continuum-sized decoder or a stored model estimate.
Proof. Round each point of any feasible competitor’s single dictionary to one net point, identically at
every history. Its costs and stopping law do not change. Bounded terminal-observable error changes
expected loss by at most ω(η) because the terminal events have total mass one. Restricting gives
the lower. A finite net has finitely many tuples, so choose a minimizer and apply Theorem S31.3 for
the upper. Repetitions can only reduce the actual width.                                             □

S31.4. The least feasible cost and every interior budget. The primitive fallback cost κ0 can
be far from optimal. It must not become an artificial admission threshold. For each finite cover and
horizon, let
                          aF,T = min{a : Cϕ,T + c∗ Sϕ,T ≼ aI (ϕ ∈ F )},
126                                             QIAN QI


ignoring task loss and committing a fixed dummy value. This is an attained finite semidefinite
problem with c∗ ≤ aF,T ≤ κ0 . Put DT0 = min{1, (κ0 /c∗ − 1)/T } and
                              κ0
       EF,T = (κ0 − c∗ )DT0 + [ϵg + ϵI (c∗ (T − 1) + κ0 )],   UF,T = aF,T + EF,T .   (S31.11)
                              c∗
Here a cover needs only instrument and cost errors. Let Nmin (Θ) be the least uniform expected cost
of a common proper policy.
Theorem S31.5 (Constructive compact-family feasibility threshold). One has
                                       aF,T ≤ Nmin (Θ) ≤ UF,T .
The upper is the certified cost bound of an actual common finite-label policy. Along nested covers
and horizons with T ϵI → 0 and ϵg → 0, the lower bounds increase to Nmin (Θ) and the running
minima of the upper bounds decrease to the same number. The least cost is attained by a full-history
common policy.
   For every N > Nmin (Θ), a sufficiently fine finite cover and sufficiently large horizon exhibit
UF,T < N . Their completed policy is a finite-label fallback with certified κ = UF,T < N . Consequently
Theorem S31.3 and Corollary S31.4 apply at every strictly interior feasible budget, with no supplied
balancing program or reference witness. For effective data and certified covers this is a terminating
search for a strictly feasible fallback, not a dimension-only efficient synthesis bound. Finite-memory
attainment at N = Nmin (Θ) is not asserted.
Proof. Every actual proper policy supplies a feasible reserved prefix on the cover, proving the lower.
Complete a lower optimizer with the original uniform fallback. At a covering model, its open mass
is at most DT (aF,T ) ≤ DT0 , its expected prefix calls at most aF,T /c∗ ≤ κ0 /c∗ , and its padded cost
at most aF,T + (κ0 − c∗ )DT0 . Apply (S31.6) with padding κ0 to every covered model. This proves
the upper and its operational realization. It bounds the cost on arbitrary current states, since the
prefix problem and perturbation bound were uniform in their initial density matrices.
   The error in (S31.11) tends to zero along the stated refinement. Restriction and addition of
models prove lower monotonicity; the running minimum construction gives upper monotonicity
without incorrectly claiming that the raw error bounds themselves decrease. The squeeze proves
both limits.
   For attainment, choose proper common policies with uniform costs decreasing to Nmin . The
countable product of finite nodewise probability simplexes is compact. Every finite-prefix reserved
cost constraint at each fixed model and state is closed in these coordinates. A finite collection of such
constraints at budget Nmin is feasible: take a convergent subnet of the selected policies’ coordinates
and use continuity of the finite-prefix operators and convergence of their budgets. Compactness then
gives one policy satisfying all these constraints simultaneously. Its survival is at most DT (Nmin )
after T calls, and the positive floor and monotone convergence prove properness and total cost at
most Nmin in every model. No uniform expected-cost tail estimate was used.
   At a strictly larger requested budget, the upper sequence eventually falls below it. The completed
policy attaining that certificate supplies exactly the fallback required for the risk theorem. Relabelling
its dummy output to a chosen task decision changes no call, so it works for any eventual dictionary.
Its finite live-state count is retained in (S31.10). Effective cover and primitive arithmetic hypotheses
are additional inputs for performing these tests; the proof does not infer them from topological
compactness.                                                                                            □
Corollary S31.6 (Effective synthesis from compact algebraic primitives). Suppose Θ ⊂ Rp and
D ⊂ Rk are nonempty compact semialgebraic sets defined over the real algebraic numbers. Suppose
the real and imaginary entries of the primitive instrument maps and costs are polynomial in θ, and
the terminal-loss entries are polynomial in (θ, r). Assume the common finite interface, positive
cost floor and absence of dark subspaces as above. Then Nmin (Θ) is a computable real. For every
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       127


algebraic N > Nmin (Θ), integer m ≥ 1 and positive algebraic accuracy ξ, a terminating finite
procedure produces a common finite-label observer of cost at most N whose risk differs from RΘ,m (N )
by at most ξ, with algebraic controller probabilities and decoder values.
    The procedure also produces rigorous lower and upper value bounds. It need not be computationally
efficient; the statement concerns a supplied finite polynomial description, not learning the experiment
from data. Exact bounded-bit sampling, minimal program size and finite-memory optimality at the
boundary are separate questions.
Proof. All assertions about positivity of the finite-dimensional Hermitian primitive matrices, nor-
malization, and Oθ,d ⪰ δI are first-order polynomial assertions over real algebraic coefficients.
Represent complex entries by their real and imaginary parts, and use principal minors for positive
semidefiniteness. Quantifier elimination over real closed fields [15] therefore verifies these assertions
and exhibits an algebraic δ > 0 valid for all θ when it exists. Compactness and the no-dark lemma
guarantee existence. The positive lower cost bound and finite upper cost/loss bounds may also be
chosen algebraic: compactness gives such bounds, and the same polynomial tests verify them. This
gives an algebraic bound κ0 for the common one-row fallback.
   At a fixed horizon, use common sequence weights and a tuple of m decoder values as variables.
Composing a finite number of polynomial primitive maps gives polynomial coefficients in θ. The
constraints Cθ,T + c∗ Sθ,T ≼ N I and Vθ,T ≼ qI for all θ ∈ Θ are thus a finite first-order real-algebraic
formula. Compactness of the finite plan and decoder space gives an attained minimum. Its value
and some minimizing variables are algebraic, by quantifier elimination and the existence of algebraic
points in nonempty semialgebraic sets defined over the real algebraic numbers. This is a robust
finite-horizon optimization, not necessarily an ordinary finite-list semidefinite program or a convex
optimization in the decoder variables.
   The same method computes the uniform reserved cost minimum aΘ,T . Complete its optimizer
with the known fallback. With no model-grid error, its cost is at most
                               aΘ,T + (κ0 − c∗ ) min{1, (κ0 /c∗ − 1)/T }.
This explicit interval converges to Nmin and can be made narrower than any rational tolerance,
proving computability. If N > Nmin , enumeration of these finite uniform problems eventually
certifies a completed fallback cost κ < N ; it has finite labels and algebraic probabilities. The
uniform risk problem just described and the completion-mixture proof then have gap at most
                                                L(κ − c∗ )DT (N )
                                LDT (N ) +                            .
                                             N − κ + (κ − c∗ )DT (N )
Choose a finite T making this less than ξ, compute an algebraic optimizer, and mix its completion
with the certified fallback. The mixing probability is algebraic and restores the original budget.
Its one common decoder tuple has at most m values. This proves every claimed termination and
approximation assertion without supposing a finite grid represents a continuum exactly. Real-
algebraic elimination may be extremely expensive, and equality at the limiting feasibility boundary
is not decided by an unbounded strict-threshold search.                                          □
S31.5. Two-sided robustness and boundaries of the theorem.
Corollary S31.7 (Robustness of the optimal constrained risk). Let Θ1 , Θ2 be two compact families
on the same interface. Suppose each member of either family is within (ϵI , ϵg , ϵF ) of some member
of the other. Suppose one common finite-label fallback costs at most κ < N on their union, with the
same cost and loss bounds. Then, for fixed C or optimized width m,
                                |RΘ1 (N ) − RΘ2 (N )| ≤ ΓF,T   (T ≥ 1),
where the right side is the explicit expression (S31.7)–(S31.8) evaluated at the pairwise error bounds;
no finite cover is needed in this corollary. Hence it is at most the infimum of these bounds over
128                                            QIAN QI


T . In particular
                √ it tends to zero with the primitive errors at a fixed positive budget slack. The
same-budget O( ϵ) upper modulus follows when all three errors are at most ϵ.
Proof. Take any feasible policy on Θ1 within ξ of its optimal risk and truncate it at depth T . Its
reserve and nonnegative loss bounds hold at every member of Θ1 . For each target in Θ2 , use its
close source member in the occupation comparison. Complete and mix with the common fallback
exactly as in Theorem S31.3. This gives a single policy feasible at N on Θ2 with risk at most
RΘ1 (N ) + ξ + ΓF,T . For optimized width, its one source dictionary is used on every target and by
the fallback. Let ξ ↓ 0, then reverse the roles. This compares the optimal risks through feasible
strategies and does not exchange a minimization and a maximization.                              □
Example S31.8 (Risk need not be continuous at the feasibility boundary). In physical dimension
one, both commands A, B always report a productive result. There is one retained decoder value.
Let its prospective losses be zero after A and one after B. At model θ = 0, both call costs are one;
at θ = ϵ > 0, they are respectively 1 + ϵ and one. There is no instrument or loss perturbation, only
cost error ϵg = ϵ, and there is no dark state. At budget N = 1, any positive probability of choosing
A or continuing after the first report costs strictly more than one in the perturbed model. Thus
R{0} (1) = 0 while R{ϵ} (1) = 1. Both instances remain feasible. Therefore the strict interior/slack
condition cannot simply be deleted from a general continuity theorem. The example can be realized
by command-dependent deterministic future audits and squared loss at the same decoder.
Example S31.9 (Why the remaining-horizon cost term is real). In dimension one, command
A costs one and is immediately productive at the source; at the target it fails with probability
ϵ ∈ (0, 1/2] and is productive otherwise. Command W costs one and is never productive. Use a
prefix that first calls A, commits on success, and after failure makes T − 1 calls to W before leaving
the cutoff open. Source prefix occupation is exactly one. Instrument discrepancy is ϵ, while costs
and task observables have not changed. The target’s cost padded by κ on the open leaf is exactly
                                          1 + ϵ(T − 1 + κ),
compared with source value one. Repeated A is a genuine fallback of cost at most two. This attains
the instrument term of (S31.6) when c∗ = 1. It proves the need for a remaining-horizon factor for
arbitrary tested prefixes; it does not prove sharpness of the optimized-risk square-root modulus.
Example S31.10 (Even affine primitive families cannot be certified at vertices). Let θ ∈ [0, 1],
physical dimension one, and one unit-cost command whose productive report is a Bernoulli(θ) bit.
At commitment a fresh independent Bernoulli(θ) bit is the future audit. Fix C = {0, 1} with squared
loss. Both the report instruments and the post-report loss observables are affine in θ. Nevertheless,
for every budget N ≥ 1,
                              R{0,1},C (N ) = 0,    R[0,1],C (N ) = 12 .
For the two endpoint models, committing the first report as prediction has zero loss. For the
full interval this rule has risk 2θ(1 − θ) ≤ 1/2 at cost one. Under θ = 1/2, the prospective audit
is fair conditional on any finite stopping history and any selected decision in C; every proper
policy therefore has loss exactly 1/2. This gives the matching lower against arbitrary history and
randomization. It is a continuum-uncertainty obstruction even with a uniform one-call fallback
and no hidden dynamical state. A convex parameter set and affine primitive entries do not justify
testing only its vertices: finite-path coefficients and adaptive terminal scores need not be affine in
the fixed unknown parameter.
Example S31.11 (Pointwise termination and model switching are different). The noncompact
scalar family with productive probability p ∈ (0, 1] terminates in every model but has no finite
uniform cost. Its compact closure adds the dark model p = 0. Compactness and a positive certified
margin are therefore substantive.
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                               129


   Nor is the model allowed to change adversarially during a run. With one command on a qubit,
let model 1 have failure Kraus operator |0⟩⟨1| and model 2 have |1⟩⟨0|, with the complementary
productive effects. Each fixed model terminates within two calls from every initial state. Alternating
models 1 and 2 from state |1⟩ causes failure forever. The compact finite union satisfies the present
fixed-model theorem but not a switching-adversary theorem. Such uncertainty requires a different
joint survival certificate.
   This compact-family result is an intrinsic constrained-risk approximation on its stated finite
physical interface, not an intrinsic characterization of when the geometric profile Q is exact. It
supplies no uncharged model-identification phase and requires no normalized-filter contraction. Its
cost threshold, risk certificate, finite output and finite observer implementation come from the
same primitive data. The separate exact geometric, spectral, reset-communication and predictive-
realization theorems retain their own hypotheses and research questions.

               S32. The exact task defect at a prescribed output width
   The raw matrix order of Section S38 is exact for localized affine-square tests. Ordinary squared
prediction tests form a smaller family. Requiring the stronger order can therefore discard valid
task comparisons, while taking a supremum over arbitrarily many readouts can overcharge a finite
decoder. We identify the least additive defect for the actual task and its output width.

S32.1. Finite dictionaries and an attained optimal defect. Let Y, Z be standard Borel spaces,
D a nonempty compact metric decision space, and ℓ : Z × D → [0, L] Borel. Assume a uniform
decision modulus
                        |ℓ(z, r) − ℓ(z, s)| ≤ ω(d(r, s)), lim ω(δ) = 0,              (S32.1)
                                                                          δ↓0
with ω nondecreasing. For finite positive measures P, Q on Y × Z, put λ = PY + QY . The
unnormalized conditional loss densities gP (y, r), gQ (y, r) relative to λ have jointly Borel versions
continuous in r. Indeed disintegrate P +Q over λ, multiply its conditional law by the Radon–Nikodym
densities of P, Q relative to P + Q, and integrate ℓ. Off one completed null set,
           |gP (y, r) − gP (y, s)| ≤ p(y)ω(d(r, s)),          |gQ (y, r) − gQ (y, s)| ≤ q(y)ω(d(r, s)),
where p = dPY /dλ, q = dQY /dλ and p + q = 1. Zero total mass causes no difficulty. Put
F (y, r) = gP (y, r) − gQ (y, r) and
                                                      Z
                               ∆ℓC (P | Q) =              max{0, max F (y, r)} dλ
                                                                   r∈C

for a nonempty finite dictionary C ⊂ D.
Theorem S32.1 (Width-resolved optimal task defect). For every integer m ≥ 1, the following
equal quantities are attained:
                                                  Z
               ∆ℓm (P | Q) =        max               max{0, F (y, r1 ), . . . , F (y, rm )} dλ,          (S32.2)
                               (r1 ,...,rm )∈Dm

                           =               sup                     a(y)ℓ(z, r(y)) (P − Q)(dy, dz).        (S32.3)
                                   a:Y →[0,1] mBorel
                               r:Y →D mBorel, |r(Y )|≤m

Thus this is the smallest additive error valid for every selected width-m task. An optimum uses a
deterministic report event and readout from one fixed dictionary; independent current randomization
cannot improve it. The value is independent of the dominating measure and satisfies
                                                                         ∆n   ∆m
               ∆1 ≤ ∆m ≤ m∆1 ,          ∆m+n ≤ ∆m + ∆n ,                    ≤          (1 ≤ m ≤ n).       (S32.4)
                                                                         n    m
130                                                QIAN QI


Moreover                                 Z
                       ∆ℓ∞ (P | Q) :=        [sup F (y, r)]+ dλ = lim ∆ℓm (P | Q).                  (S32.5)
                                             r∈D                  m→∞

If a δ-net of D has n points, then
                           0 ≤ ∆∞ − ∆n ≤ ω(δ)[P (Y × Z) + Q(Y × Z)].                                (S32.6)
In particular ∆1 = 0 if and only if ∆∞ = 0. The width hierarchy distinguishes positive defect
magnitudes, not different zero-defect orders.
Proof. Fix an ordered dictionary. At each report choose the first index maximizing its positive
loss-density difference, and accept exactly when that maximum is positive. These decisions are
Borel and give the displayed envelope. They pointwise dominate every other selection and every
randomized readout in that dictionary. Every finite-image rule uses some such dictionary, proving
equality of the suprema.
   Moving every tuple coordinate by at most δ changes the envelope by at most (p + q)ω(δ) almost
everywhere. Integration makes the objective continuous on compact Dm , with repetitions allowed.
It attains its maximum, and the selected deterministic test attains the second supremum. Averaging
over an independent current seed cannot increase that value. A seed choosing dictionaries whose
union exceeds m does not describe a width-m decoder.
   The test expression gives independence of the dominating measure. Inclusion gives monotonicity. A
nonnegative maximum is bounded by the sum of the single-center positive parts, proving ∆m ≤ m∆1 .
Partition a dictionary into two groups to obtain subadditivity. For m ≤ n, choose an optimal n-tuple
and average over its m-element index subsets. A selected maximizing positive index is retained with
probability m/n. Thus the average subdictionary envelope is at least m/n times the original one.
Integrate and take one subdictionary at least as good as its average. This proves the last inequality,
including repeated entries.
   Continuity makes a countable dense decision set determine the pointwise supremum. The positive
envelopes of its finite initial segments increase to that supremum; monotone convergence proves the
limit and measurability. A finite net gives the quantitative estimate by the same modulus calculation.
Finally, ∆1 = 0 makes F (y, r) ≤ 0 almost everywhere for each point in the countable dense set.
Intersect these full-measure sets and use continuity to obtain the inequality for all decisions on one
common set. Hence ∆∞ = 0; the converse is immediate.                                                □

   For fixed P0 , P1 , the map t 7→ ∆ℓm (tP0 | P1 ) is convex, nondecreasing and LP0 (Y × Z)-Lipschitz
on [0, ∞). It is a supremum of affine functions of t with slopes between zero and that bound.
The positive-variation and measurable-selection ingredients are classical. The decision-cardinality
restriction should not be confused with Blackwell k-decision orders [8]: the present localized fixed-loss
order at zero defect already follows from width one.

Proof. Expanding the square makes the directed loss-density difference for (tP0 , P1 ) equal to −ψ.
The preceding theorem proves the integral and common-null-set statements. Completing the square
proves the first formula; a = 0 gives a linear optimization. On a ball with a > 0, either the
unconstrained minimizer b/a lies inside or its radial projection minimizes. If a ≤ 0, the least value
at radius s is c + as2 − 2∥b∥s, nonincreasing for 0 ≤ s ≤ R. Its minimum is at R. These arguments
include ties, b = 0 and R = 0.                                                                     □

R Report mass is a separate resource test: its least selected-event defect in PY ≤ uQY + e is
 [p − uq]+ dλ. It is not implied by one arbitrary task inequality. Equality of the entire task family
has a sharper conclusion.
Proposition S32.3 (Task equality and its exact moment content). Suppose the affine hull of H has
dimension at least one. Localized squared-task equality for P, Q at every constant readout in H is
equivalent to equality of their report masses, their first-moment measuresRprojected onto the direction
space of that affine hull, and their full centered squared-norm measures ∥z − h0 ∥2 P (dy, dz) for an
origin h0 in the affine hull. Full second-moment matrix measures need not agree. For a singleton
carrier the mass must instead be tested separately.
Proof. Choose a countable dense set of readouts and intersect their full-measure equality sets. The
common modulus extends equality to all of H. Translate by h0 and restrict the quadratic difference
to the direction space V of the affine hull. A compact convex set with this affine hull has nonempty
relative interior. A polynomial vanishing there vanishes on V : restrict successively to line segments
in a relative open ball, where a one-variable polynomial with an interval of zeros is identically zero,
and identify its coefficients. The quadratic coefficient is the scalar mass difference times the identity
on V , so that difference is zero because dim V ≥ 1. The linear coefficient is the projected centered
first-moment difference, and the constant coefficient is the full centered squared-norm difference.
They vanish. Conversely these three equalities make every expanded squared-task difference zero.
When H is a singleton, only one constant loss measure is tested, so a zero-loss audit does not identify
mass.                                                                                                  □
Example S32.4 (Identical tasks with incompatible matrix supports). There is one report and the
decision carrier is the unit ball in R2 . Let P0 audit the two signs of e1 fairly and P1 the two signs of
e2 fairly. Every ordinary squared decision has loss 1 + ∥r∥2 under both laws. Their affine-square
moment matrices are respectively diag(1, 1, 0) and diag(1, 0, 1) in the coordinates (1, z1 , z2 ). No
two-sided positive relative matrix order can compare their different kernels. Thus exact squared-task
comparison is strictly broader than the sufficient positive-matrix criterion, without contradicting
that criterion’s exact statement for its larger test family.
Theorem S32.5 (Sharp width amplification in every finite dimension). For every integer k ≥ 1
there are probability report–audit laws with identical report masses and squared decisions in the unit
132                                              QIAN QI


ball of Rk such that
                                             min(m, k)
                                ∆sq
                                 m (P0 | P1 ) =          (m ≥ 1).                        (S32.10)
                                                 5k
Both the linear width growth and the discrepancy between fixed-width and unrestricted defects are
sharp in this family.
Proof. Let the report i be uniform on {1, . . . , k}. Under P0 the audit is zero. Under P1 , conditional
on i, it is ei with probability 4/5 and −ei with probability 1/5. The conditional loss difference is
(6/5)ri − 1. It is positive only when ri > 5/6. A unit-ball vector cannot meet this condition for two
different coordinates, since 2(5/6)2 > 1. Its positive contribution in its one possible coordinate is
at most 1/5. A dictionary of m vectors therefore contributes at most min(m, k)/(5k). Choosing
that many distinct basis vectors and accepting their corresponding reports attains the bound.
The construction has bounded audits, exact report-mass equality, and no asymptotic dimension
assumption.                                                                                           □

S32.3. The same dictionary across actual histories. Let u range over a standard Borel context
space with finite positive occupation measure µ. Let Pu , Qu be finite kernels with integrable
total masses; assume the same bounded decision-continuous loss. Form the finite measures P =
µ(du)Pu (dy, dz) and Q = µ(du)Qu (dy, dz), where the full report is (u, y).
Proposition S32.6 (Exact fixed-dictionary occupation defect). One has
                                      Z                            Z
                 ∆ℓm (P | Q) = maxm       ∆ℓC (Pu | Qu ) µ(du) ≤       ∆ℓm (Pu | Qu ) µ(du).   (S32.11)
                               C∈D

The maximum uses a single dictionary for every context. The inequality can be strict by an arbitrarily
large factor as the number of contexts grows. Neither side asserts that a prescribed occupation
measure is causally realizable.
Proof. For a fixed dictionary, take the positive envelope of its unnormalized loss densities on the
product context/report space. Disintegration and integration give exactly the integral of the rowwise
fixed-dictionary envelopes. This can also be checked without selecting contextwise densities: a finite
partition of reports and rational acceptance/readout choices approximate every bounded selected
task in the finite measure Pu + Qu . A countable algebra generating the standard Borel report space,
together with a countable dense decision set, suffices for these approximations. Hence the rowwise
suprema are measurable. The uniform decision modulus and integrable total masses make the
integrated fixed-dictionary envelope continuous on compact Dm , so it attains its maximum. Applying
the width-defect theorem to the product measures yields the equality. Moving the maximum inside
the integral gives the inequality.
   For strictness use the preceding sharp example with context u = i and no additional report.
Every row has one-center defect 1/5, whereas a single common width-m dictionary over uniform
contexts has defect min(m, k)/(5k). Thus even individually exact rowwise errors may overcharge a
common finite decoder. In a stopped experiment µ must be the actual pre-call occupation of the
competitor; this algebraic identity does not optimize or fabricate that occupation.                 □

                         S34. The exact zero-risk resource spectrum
   The mass of one favorable atom gives a sufficient resource threshold, not in general a necessary
one. Other zero-loss atoms may be available to the same decoder. We determine the complete
finite-width threshold, for the general acquired loss first and then for squared prediction. This
statement is about a genuinely acquired probability law; converting it to a physical budget still
requires the exact transfer hypotheses.
   Use the compact decision space and nonnegative conditional losses gr (x) of Section S33. For a
dictionary C, put dC (x) = minr∈C gr (x) and define
                                      zm =       max        ν{dC = 0}.
                                             C⊂D, 1≤|C|≤m

Theorem S34.1 (Exact finite-width zero-loss criterion). The maximum defining zm is attained.
For every finite m ≥ 1 and finite b ≥ 1,
                                      Qℓm (b) = 0     ⇐⇒     bzm ≥ 1.                              (S34.1)
When the inequality holds, an optimal procedure accepts only reports in a zero-loss set of an attaining
dictionary. When it fails, the attained profile is strictly positive. Neither implication requires positive
residual variance, a dimension assumption, a density, or an absence of atoms.
Proof. Represent a dictionary by a tuple in compact Dm , allowing repetitions. Conditional losses
are continuous in each decision on one common full-measure set. For a convergent sequence of
tuples, the nonnegative minima converge pointwise. The indicator of the closed condition dC (x) = 0
is upper semicontinuous in the tuple. Reverse Fatou for bounded functions therefore makes its
integral upper semicontinuous; compactness gives a maximizing tuple.
   If v = ν{dC = 0} ≥ 1/b, the density h = v −1 1{dC =0} has unit mass and cap at most b, with
objective zero. Conversely, take an attained optimal tuple and density for a zero profile, whose
existence was proved in Theorem S33.1. Nonnegativity implies h(x)dC (x) = 0 almost everywhere.
Thus its unit mass is supported on {dC = 0} and is at most bν{dC = 0} ≤ bzm . If bzm < 1, no
optimizer has zero value; nonnegativity and attainment then make the value strictly positive. □
136                                                   QIAN QI


  For squared prediction, form the finite measure on H
                                µ0 (A) = ν{x : w(x) = 0, f (x) ∈ A}.
List its positive atomic masses in nonincreasing order as a1 ≥ a2 ≥ · · · , padding with zeros if there
are finitely many, and set aj = 0 for every j if there are none. The total mass is at most one.
Repeated values in this list belong to distinct atom locations.
Theorem S34.2 (Atomic zero-risk spectrum and exact resource thresholds). For every m ≥ 1,
                                m
                                X                                                 m
                                                                                  X
                         zm =         aj ,        Qm (b) = 0    ⇐⇒            b         aj ≥ 1.   (S34.2)
                                j=1                                               j=1

The nonatomic part of µ0 contributes nothing at a fixed finite width, even when w = 0 everywhere.
All finite-width zero-risk acquisition thresholds are therefore determined by the ordered atomic masses
of the zero-residual predictive law, not by its matrix rank or its ambient dimension.
   Under the exact matched certificates with identity exports, the checkpoint threshold at width m is
N = κ/zm when zm > 0, and no finite budget yields zero risk when zm = 0. For the event-stopped
equality, replace m by the total width M ≥ B. For the state-stopped equality with a sharp mandatory
live count, use total labels B + m. These conclusions are necessary and sufficient within those
declared exact-transfer classes, not for arbitrary experimental interfaces.
Proof. For squared loss, dC = w + dist(f, C)2 is zero exactly when w = 0 and f ∈ C. Hence
ν{dC = 0} = µ0 (C). A finite set receives mass only from atoms. There are at most countably
many positive atoms: for each positive integer n, at most n atoms can have mass exceeding 1/n. A
nonempty collection of remaining positive masses has a largest member. Indeed if its supremum is
a > 0, only finitely many members can exceed a/2, and one of this finite set attains the supremum.
Successive removal therefore defines the displayed ordering. Choosing the locations of its first m
positive masses maximizes µ0 (C) among all dictionaries of size at most m. Missing locations may
be filled by arbitrary repeated points in H. This proves the first identity; Theorem S34.1 proves the
equivalence. Finally substitute b = N/κ into the respective exact transfer equalities, keeping their
different memory counts.                                                                           □
Example S34.3 (Why one atom does not determine the necessary threshold). Let the acquired
marked law put mass 1/4 at (0, 0) and mass 3/4 at (1, 0), in a carrier containing [0, 1]. Treating only
the first atom would suggest budget cap four. In fact z1 = 3/4 and z2 = 1. Thus Q1 (b) = 0 exactly
for b ≥ 4/3, while Q2 (b) = 0 already at b = 1. The remainder has zero residual variance, which is
precisely why the single-atom necessary threshold does not apply. Proposition S39.5 now explicitly
states the additional hypothesis needed for its one-atom necessary threshold; the remainder of that
proposition is unchanged.
S34.1. An exact variational form, including ties. The following fixed-dictionary formula
records the classical selection mechanism explicitly. It is a population trimming or bounded-density
variational principle, not a new invention of a trimming functional [2, 3]. Its role here is to keep
boundary mass and the physical acquisition cap in the same proof.
Proposition S34.4 (Selected-mass dual and optimal selection). For 0 ≤ d ≤ L measurable on a
probability space and b ≥ 1,
                                             Z                           Z                   
                             min
                              R                  hd dν = max        t−b       (t − d)+ dν .       (S34.3)
                       0≤h≤b,   h dν=1                  0≤t≤L

There is a threshold t satisfying ν(d < t) ≤ 1/b ≤ ν(d ≤ t). An optimizer has density b on {d < t},
zero on {d > t} and a constant in [0, b] on the tie set, chosen to give unit mass. More generally these
saturation rules characterize every optimum for an attaining threshold, up to null sets. Applying
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       137


this formula with d = dC and then minimizing over C ∈ Dm gives the exact acquired profile; no
interchange of the dictionary minimum and threshold maximum is asserted.

Proof. For every feasible h and every t, pointwise minimization of h(d − t) over [0, b] gives h(d − t) ≥
−b(t − d)+ . Integrating and using unit mass proves weak duality. Take a lower 1/b quantile of d in
[0, L]; elementary continuity of probability measures from below and above gives the two quantile
inequalities, including b = 1 and atoms at the endpoints. The prescribed density exists because the
missing mass lies between zero and bν(d = t). It attains equality in the pointwise inequality, proving
equality and both attainments. The nonnegative difference in that inequality is h(d − t) on d > t
and (b − h)(t − d) on d < t. Its integral vanishes exactly under the stated saturation rules. On d = t
it is zero for every allowed density. This also proves the claim about all optimizers and ties.        □


         S35. Exact finite-memory synthesis on reachable quantum supports
   The preceding hierarchy characterizes full-history strategies. We now fix the observer alphabet
and give a finite certificate that respects its persistent memory. The physical models in this section
are time-homogeneous and finite-dimensional, with finite command and report sets and globally
legal command rows. Restricted preparation faces and physical command domains have an explicit
extension in the supplement. A finite model family is required for the finite algebraic synthesis
conclusion. The analytic certificate itself can be imposed modelwise on any declared family, always
with the same controller variables.

S35.1. The actual continuing instrument. Fix ℓ live classical labels, a prescribed initial label
s0 , and t terminal decoder values ru in a compact carrier D. In a model θ, the hidden physical
algebra is Mdθ (C). A command a has completely positive report maps Iθ,a,y with normalized report
sum, and expected-cost observable Gθ,a ⪰ cI, c > 0. A terminal audit following a productive report
has bounded score observable Fθ,a,y,j (ru ) on the post-report state. Every terminal choice is made
before that audit.
    The observer uses common command probabilities ps,a . After command a and report y, it uses
                          0
common probabilities qs,a,y,v                                    1
                                to continue at live label v, or qs,a,y,u to commit to decoder value ru .
These nonnegative probabilities sum to one. Nonproductive reports have all q 1 = 0. Only the
current command, report and fresh randomness are available within that call; there is no external
clock, port, persistent seed or hidden controller state.
    In the event-stopped interface, live and terminal roles may share labels, and max(ℓ, t) total labels
suffice. In the state-stopped interface their types are disjoint and ℓ + t labels are required. Any
fixed overlap or halt partition can instead be declared explicitly. The physical quantum state is
part of the experiment, not a free coherent controller memory.

The remaining trace is bounded in magnitude by max(|αj |, |βj |) Pr(τ > n) and tends to zero. The
finite sum records the scores on disjoint terminal events. Bounded scores and proper operation
identify its limit with the actual terminal expectation. The initial block equation proves (a).
   For necessity fix a model, let X0 = ι(I), take the least reachable projection and write D = ℓd.

                                         X

The corresponding series starting at T n X0 is a nonnegative tail of this series. Sum these tails for

                                           k≥0
Since R is strictly positive on its support, every positive supported matrix is bounded by a finite
multiple of R. Positivity proves finite expected cost from every such matrix. This includes states
which need not occur as individual pure conditional states on any one path.
   The positive partial cost potentials increase, and their pairing with R is bounded by (S35.12).
Strict positivity of R on its finite-dimensional support bounds their norms. They converge to a
positive H min solving H min − TP∗ H min = P gP . Its initial block is precisely the cost operator and is
at most N I.
   The cost floor also makes k (TP∗ )k P converge in norm. In particular (TP∗ )n P → 0. Every
                                P

supported Hermitian matrix lies between −∥X∥P and ∥X∥P ; positivity makes its image under these
powers tend to zero. Decompose any complex matrix into Hermitian parts. In finite dimension,
convergence of all these powers forces every eigenvalue to have modulus less than one. Otherwise
an eigenvector of modulus at least one would not tend to zero. This proves (S35.10) and its
norm-convergent Neumann inverse.
   The score series converges absolutely in norm by (S35.4) and the summable survival series.
Normalization gives P bP = P − TP∗ P , whose series sums to P . Consequently the score series sums
to a Kj between αj P and βj P , solves its equation and has the prescribed initial block. This proves
(b) and both resolvent formulas.
   Conversely, spectral radius less than one gives finite cost through the same Neumann series and
proper operation through normalization. Budget and targets are then exactly the initial-block
conditions. Finally any positive cost superpotential dominates every partial cost potential by the
first telescoping inequality, and thus dominates their limit. More precisely, its nonnegative drift
slack DH = P (H − T ∗ H − g)P satisfies
                                   H − H min = (I − TP∗ )−1 DH ⪰ 0.
This proves both minimality and the exact excess-certificate formula.                                 □
140                                             QIAN QI


S35.3. An attained finite-memory algebraic frontier. For synthesis the common probabilities,
decoder values and modelwise certificate matrices become variables. The state-stopped interface is a
finite union over permitted halt partitions. The event-stopped interface permits declared live/output
reuse. Both keep a fixed initial live label.
Corollary S35.3 (Finite algebraic synthesis at an exact memory budget). Assume finitely many
physical models with real algebraic primitive coefficients, a compact semialgebraic decoder carrier,
and audit-score observables polynomial in decoder coordinates. Fix a total label budget M and
algebraic cost budget N . Existence of a common observer with the stated exact targets and cost at
most N is equivalent to feasibility of a finite system of polynomial equalities and positive-semidefinite
inequalities. It is decidable by real-algebraic quantifier elimination. A feasible instance has a common
controller and certificates with real algebraic entries.
   For bounded continuous nonnegative terminal loss, the worst-model, worst-initial-state loss at
budget N attains its minimum whenever the feasible fixed-memory class is nonempty. Under
the algebraic hypotheses its optimum is real algebraic. These are exact existence statements, not
polynomial-time bounds, rational-optimum claims or finite-bit exact implementation of all algebraic
samplers.
Proof. Use polynomial normalization and positivity for the probability rows. Encode Hermitian
matrices by their real diagonal and real and imaginary off-diagonal coordinates. Positive semidefi-
niteness is equivalent to nonnegativity of every principal minor. Encode P = P ∗ = P 2 , Ps0 = I,
invariance, support of H, Kj , and the cost and score conditions of the theorem. All expressions in
(S35.1)–(S35.3) and the certificate are polynomial in these variables. There are finitely many choices
of live and terminal role counts and halt partitions under M . Every model retains its own certificate
matrices but the same controller variables. For a loss upper bound R, replace its exact initial score
target by (Kloss )s0 ≼ RI. The theorem identifies this exactly with the required worst-state loss
bound. Classical real-algebraic elimination proves decidability and algebraic witnesses [15]; no new
elimination algorithm is claimed.
   Attainment needs a separate argument because the auxiliary potentials may be unbounded away
from their initial block. The probability rows and decoder tuples form a compact space. Every
finite-prefix cost and score is continuous in these parameters. A limit of observers with cost at most
N has every finite-prefix cost at most N . Monotone convergence and the positive floor give the
same total budget and proper operation for the limit. The uniform bound Pr(τ > T ) ≤ N/[c(T + 1)]
makes bounded terminal-score tails uniformly vanish on this budget class. Therefore full terminal
scores are continuous there, as are the finitely many largest-eigenvalue worst-model losses. Exact
score constraints are closed. The loss minimum on a nonempty compact feasible set is attained.
   The achievable loss-bound set is semialgebraic over the real algebraic numbers by the first part.
An attained finite endpoint of such a one-variable set is algebraic. Its nonempty fiber also has real
algebraic coordinates. This proves the last statement without imposing a false compactness bound
on the certificate matrices or claiming rational optimal probabilities.                             □
   Finite-state POMDP synthesis, potential equations, spectral transience, quantum decision pro-
cesses and real-algebraic methods have established antecedents [16, 17, 12, 13, 14]. The present
claim is the precise common-model, all-initial-state, paid certificate with exact event/state semantics,
its necessary reachable-support construction and its connection to acquired task balance. Those
classical ingredients separately are not presented as new.
Example S35.4 (An unreachable recurrent block is not an obstruction). There are two live labels
and one physical state. From the prescribed start 1, a productive cost-one call commits immediately.
Label 2 is never reached and has a cost-one self-loop. The full continuing matrix is diag(0, 1), with
spectral radius one. Nevertheless the actual observer is proper and has cost one. Its reachable
projection is diag(1, 0); on that support the continuing matrix is zero and H min = P . Thus an
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        141


unrestricted inverse of I − T or a global strict-transience requirement would incorrectly reject it.
Scalar blocks embed this example in any quantum dimension.
Corollary S35.5 (Same-task balance with a certified finite live count). Apply the finite-memory
certificate to the localized mass, first-moment and squared-norm scores in Corollary S30.8. A feasible
certificate gives one actual finite-label common balancing program at its certified cost. Conversely
every such observer on the declared time-homogeneous interface yields the certificate. If the return
rule and all actual rooted continuations are also certified, the program’s actual live roles and exported
decoder values may be used in the upper widths of the transfer theorem. Failure at a prescribed M is
an obstruction for that observer class, not merely failure of a searched single-call mixture.
Proof. The score list is finite and its quadratic expansion is exactly the unnormalized squared-task
identity. The potential theorem is necessary and sufficient for the finite observer producing it. The
common p, q, r variables are the observer itself, so no uncounted history tape or model index is used.
Its live labels and costs are those of its certified transitions. The rooted-continuation condition
supplies the required uniformity when attempts repeat. The supplement gives the post-report
compilation: prospective audits are not observed on rejected branches, and a logical subroutine
return is not mistaken for an irrevocable outer halt. The transfer theorem then uses these same
actual resource counts.                                                                             □

                   S36. A finite test for reusable causal task balance
   The synthesis theorems produce an acquisition routine, but its first invocation is not the whole
stopped experiment. A rejected report changes the physical state. Checking only the average
returned state loses the report history on which rejection depends. Requiring task equality on
every density matrix on the reachable Hilbert support, on the other hand, can be unnecessarily
strong. The correct object for exact linear task identities is the report-resolved real linear span of
the unnormalized returned states.
   Fix a time-homogeneous routine with a finite visible return alphabet X. At a routine start, the
physical state is a density matrix on a finite-dimensional carrier K. The routine is legal and proper
on that carrier, as certified for example by Theorem S35.2; all its physical operations and live roles
are counted. On return x, a specified legal rejection operation produces the unnormalized next-start
state
                                                 Rx (ρ).
The maps Rx are completely positive and x Rx is trace preserving. The rejection operation is
                                               P

trace preserving conditional on that return, so it does not change the return probability. It is
performed only when the caller rejects and is charged separately when it has a physical cost. Its
phase must be in the counted controller labels. There is no implicit physical reset.
   For finitely many bounded prospective audit scores, let Fx,j be the Hermitian start-state observable
of the unnormalized score on return x, before the caller’s commitment decision. The future audit
is not observed P on rejection. Put Fx,0 = R∗x (I) for report mass. Fix constants mx,j , including
mx,0 = νx , with x νx = 1. The initial preparation family S is a nonempty set of density matrices
on K; it need not contain every such matrix. All data may be indexed by an unknown model θ,
but the actual routine and caller rules are the same in every model. The following statements are
imposed modelwise without allowing the controller to read θ.
   Let d = dim K, let Herm(K) be the real vector space of Hermitian matrices, and define
                       W0 = spanR S,                                  r0 = dim W0 ,              (S36.1)
                                    X
                                                                             2
                    Wn+1 = Wn +           Rx (Wn ),                   n0 = d − r0 .              (S36.2)
                                    x∈X

The additions are sums of real linear subspaces, not sums of channels or support projections.
142                                               QIAN QI


Theorem S36.1 (Finite report-resolved continuation criterion). One has Wn0 = Wn0 +1 =: W∞ .
This is exactly the span of all positive, unnormalized routine-start states
                                  Rxn · · · Rx1 (ρ),    ρ ∈ S,   n ≥ 0.
The following conditions are equivalent.
   (i) After every finite positive-probability history of rejected returns, from every preparation in S,
the next routine has conditional marked audit moments mx,j , including its report masses νx .
   (ii) Every residual Fx,j − mx,j I annihilates W∞ under the trace pairing.
   (iii) Those residuals annihilate Wn0 .
   These conditions imply the same identities for every caller using the past returned marks and
private randomness to choose acceptance, provided rejection uses exactly the declared return maps.
They are also necessary when all finite sequences of rejections are legal caller choices. No unseen
future audit, fresh model-dependent program or stored physical posterior is used by the caller.
Proof. An equality Wn = Wn+1 makes Wn invariant under every Rx , so all subsequent subspaces
agree. Before equality each dimension increases by at least one, starting at r0 and never exceeding
d2 . Stabilization therefore occurs by n0 . Induction expands Wn as the span of word images of length
at most n of W0 ; taking the invariant limit proves the first assertion.
    For a word w = (x1 , . . . , xn ) and ρ ∈ S, write Aw = Rxn · · · Rx1 (ρ). Complete positivity gives
Aw ⪰ 0. If its trace is positive, the conditional next-start state is Aw / tr Aw . Its marked expectation
is tr(Fx,j Aw )/ tr Aw , so equality to mx,j is precisely tr[(Fx,j − mx,j I)Aw ] = 0. If its trace is zero,
positivity gives Aw = 0, and the same unnormalized equality is automatic. Thus (i) is equivalent to
annihilation of all word images, hence to (ii). Stabilization gives (iii).
   A caller’s randomized rejection decisions contribute scalar nonnegative weights to each fixed
visible word, because its choices use only that word and private randomness, not the hidden
matrix. Conditioning also on its private tape changes these weights but not the physical word
map. Integrating over compatible tapes preserves every displayed linear identity. Therefore the
identities hold at every actual surviving caller history. Conversely deterministic rejection of the
first n returns makes each positive-probability word of length n a possible start history, proving
necessity. These arguments use finite histories; conditional-hazard summation, rather than a claim
of renewal, supplies the infinite stopping conclusion below.                                             □
Corollary S36.2 (A finite-dimensional certificate for repeated finite-mark balance). Take the mass,
first-moment and squared-norm score targets of Corollary S30.8. Suppose their residuals annihilate
the report-resolved span in every model, and one invocation including its executed rejection return
costs conditionally at most κ on all actual start states. For any finite dictionary and any feasible
0 ≤ h ≤ b with x h(x)νx = 1, accept return x with probability h(x)/b. The resulting routine
                  P

commits almost surely, costs at most κb, and has exact terminal task value
                                  X
                                       h(x)νx [w(x) + ∥f (x) − r(x)∥2 ].
                                   x
The same finite observer implements every attempt, with no added intercall history register. A
rejection return performed within the productive call adds no live role; an extra physical return
subroutine must have its calls and live roles counted. This yields the upper construction needed by the
transfer theorem from a finite report-resolved continuation test, rather than leaving its history-uniform
task identity as an unverified premise.
Proof. The three marked moment identities and expansion of the square give the same unnormalized
task identity conditional on every actual start history by the theorem. Thus the conditional
acceptance probability is 1/b and the unnormalized loss is b−1 times the displayed value. Induction
gives survival (1 − 1/b)n through n invocations; summing begun invocations gives expected number
b. Summing their conditional costs and disjoint terminal losses proves both assertions. For a final
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        143


accepted invocation the rejection operation is not performed, and nonnegative cost only improves
its budget. The decision is made at the final productive report before the prospective audit. The
existing routine’s live labels are reused on rejection, while its finite terminal decoder is read only on
commitment. This proves the memory statement with the explicit return-operation qualification. □
   The cost assumption in this corollary is not silently converted into a linear-span equality. A
positive cost superpotential on the reachable support is a sufficient, checkable bound. If the exact
whole-invocation cost observable H is available and constant cost κ is required at every reachable
start, the condition is exactly (H − κI) ⊥ W∞ by the same proof. A one-sided inequality on only
the reachable positive states need not be equivalent to a Loewner inequality on their whole support;
no such equivalence is asserted.
Example S36.3 (Averaging is too weak; support equality is too strong). On a qubit let ρ0 = I/2
and
                          R0 (A) = 12 tr(A)|0⟩⟨0|,    R1 (A) = 12 tr(A)|1⟩⟨1|.
The average return sends ρ0 to itself, so iterating only that average generates the one-dimensional
span of I. But the report-resolved span is the entire diagonal Hermitian subspace. The observable
σz has zero expectation at the initial and averaged returned state, and expectations +1, −1 at the
two actual conditional starts. An initial or pooled calibration of that task therefore fails the required
repeated identity.
   Conversely σx annihilates the complete report-resolved diagonal span, though it is a nonzero
operator on its full two-dimensional Hilbert support. A test insisting on the operator identity σx = 0
on that support would reject an exact reachable-task identity. Both examples can be realized by a
fair visible coin, a prospective Pauli audit at commitment, and a charged conditional replacement
preparation on rejection. They distinguish a span of density matrices from a Hilbert-space support
and do not make replacement preparations free.
Proposition S36.4 (Finite exact arithmetic verification). For a fixed finite-dimensional routine
whose return maps, task observables and a finite spanning preparation list have algebraic real
and imaginary parts, the continuation criterion is decidable by finitely many exact linear-algebra
operations. At most d2 − r0 span enlargements are needed; each enlarges a space of dimension
at most d2 . For a finite family of physical models the same test is performed in every model on
the one fixed controller. This is a verification result for the supplied routine, not a polynomial-
time synthesis theorem for its controller parameters or a complexity bound for evaluating arbitrary
algebraic numbers.
Proof. Represent Hermitian matrices by d2 real coordinates and every return map by a real matrix on
those coordinates. Starting with a basis of the supplied preparation span, append all its images under
all return maps and retain a basis by exact elimination. The dimension argument in Theorem S36.1
bounds the number of enlargements. At stabilization check the finitely many trace pairings with
every residual. All operations and zero tests are exact in the finite algebraic field generated by the
input entries. This implements the equivalence of the theorem.                                      □
   Finite reachable linear spans and word-equivalence tests have classical probabilistic-automata
antecedents [22]; reachable Hilbert supports and quantum termination also have established theory
[23, 24]. Those algorithms and the dimension argument are not claimed as new. The assertion
used here is the exact report-resolved test for repeated marked tasks, its distinction from pooled
calibration and support-wide identities, and its paid post-report compilation into the same causal
resource theorem.
S36.1. Continuous reports and a sharp finite-depth obstruction. The finite-return restriction
can be removed from the exact continuation test without asserting general effective synthesis. Let
X now be standard Borel. A rejected return is described by a completely positive instrument
144                                             QIAN QI


D 7→ R(D), countably additive in the finite-dimensional space of linear maps, with R(X) trace
preserving. Let Fj (D) be finite Hermitian operator-valued prospective task measures and mj (D)
their prescribed finite scalar measures; include report mass as F0 (D) = R(D)∗ I and m0 = ν. Each
bounded score gives such a finite measure. Define
       Wn+1 = Wn + spanR {R(D)A : D ∈ B(X), A ∈ Wn },                Ej (D) = Fj (D) − mj (D)I.
The operational identity below is an almost-sure identity at actual reached histories, not a condition
imposed at individually null continuous reports.
Theorem S36.5 (Finite-depth criterion for standard Borel return instruments). With W0 = spanR S
of dimension r0 and physical dimension d, the span stabilizes by depth d2 − r0 . Exact marked task
identities after every finite report-dependent rejection history hold if and only if
                        tr[Ej (D)A] = 0    (A ∈ Wd2 −r0 , D ∈ B(X), every j).                     (S36.3)
If an identity fails, there is a witness using an allowed initial preparation, at most d2 − r0 rejected
returns, a rectangular cylinder of report events of positive probability, and one subsequent task event.
                                                                                2
   There exists a finite Borel partition Π of the return space with at most 2d −r0 cells such that the
maps {R(C) : C ∈ Π} generate exactly the same reachable span as all Borel events. This is an
existence statement about exact instrument measures. An arbitrary coarse partition or numerical
binning is not such a certificate, and testing task-measure equality still requires its exact measure
data.
Proof. Dimension growth and invariance give stabilization exactly as before. Induction identifies Wn
with the span of the matrices R(Dn ) · · · R(D1 )ρ for words of length at most n and ρ ∈ S. These are
positive unnormalized states for rectangular cylinders. If a residual does not annihilate their span,
it does not annihilate one such generator. That generator is positive and nonzero, so its trace is
positive. This proves the bounded-depth witness assertion and necessity of the displayed condition.
   For sufficiency, fix an initial preparation and a finite depth. Iterating the instrument gives a finite
positive operator-valued measure on X n . Pairing it with a fixed residual Ej (D) gives a finite signed
scalar measure. Its value on every rectangular cylinder is zero by the invariant-span condition.
Rectangles form a generating pi-system, so uniqueness of finite signed measures makes it zero on
every measurable history event. A bounded nonnegative report-dependent rejection weight can be
integrated against this zero measure; approximation by simple functions proves that the conditional
task discrepancy is zero at the actual surviving histories. To obtain one common almost-sure set for
all next task events, first use a countable generating algebra of the standard Borel report space,
intersect its full-measure sets for the finitely many scores, and then use uniqueness of finite measures
at each remaining history. Private caller randomness is integrated in the same way. This gives the
asserted operational identities, including measurable rejection depending on the full report history
rather than only on rectangles.
   For the finite partition, start with V = W0 . If V is not invariant under all event maps, choose
an event D and an A ∈ V such that R(D)A ∈          / V . Add this event to a list and replace V by the
invariant span generated by all listed event maps and W0 . Its dimension increases by at least
one. After at most d2 − r0 such choices, the resulting space is invariant under every event map
and hence equals W∞ . Take the common Boolean refinement of the listed events. Every selected
map is the sum of the maps of its partition cells. The invariant span under the partition maps
therefore contains W∞ , while each cell is a Borel event and so cannot generate a vector outside W∞ .
                                 2
Equality follows. At most 2d −r0 cells are needed. Nothing in this argument supplies an effective
representation of those selected Borel events for arbitrary input measures.                             □

paid invocation cost.

up to the asserted depth and the task identity then fails. No report of zero probability, infinitely
expensive routine, free physical reset or numerical approximation is used.                          □

146                                              QIAN QI


are the standard Borel report-event formulation for reusable marked tasks, its finite-coarsening
statement, and the matching physically realizable d2 − 1 witness depth connected to paid rejection.
Neither the finite witness bound nor its sharp example constitutes a general decision procedure for
arbitrary continuous instrument measures.

                S37. A spectral memory law for two quantum detectors
   We apply the finite-memory framework without a balancing group action or reset to a prescribed
reference state. The measurements disturb the state: this is first detection, not estimation of an
untouched pre-command parameter. Quantum first-detection laws, dark states and probing-time
optimization have substantial antecedents [19, 20]. Our question is the exact paid-call versus
classical-label frontier when the observer chooses between two specified detectors.
   Let P, Q be orthogonal projections on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space. Detector A has failure
map ρ 7→ P ρP and success map ρ 7→ (I − P )ρ(I − P ); detector B uses Q in the same way. Every call
costs one. There is no intervening evolution, additional measurement, reset or coherent controller
memory. A failure does not permit commitment. The first success requires immediate productive
commitment, with one constant terminal decoder value. The initial density matrix is arbitrary
and unknown. An autonomous controller has a prescribed initial live label, fixed command rows
and post-failure transitions, with no free clock or persistent external seed. Write Vj for the least
worst-initial-state expected call count using at most j live labels, and V∗ for the infimum over all
visible-history policies.
Theorem S37.1 (Exact two-detector resource law). Suppose P, Q ̸= 0 and c = ∥P Q∥ < 1. In every
finite dimension,
                                                          1
                                            V∗ = 1 +            .                                  (S37.1)
                                                       2(1 − c)
It is attained by choosing the first detector fairly and then switching after every failure. The
one-live-label value is
                                                     1
                                        V1 = 1 +         = 2V∗ − 1,                                (S37.2)
                                                   1−c
and a fair independent detector choice at every call attains it. With a fixed initial label, three live
labels are necessary and sufficient to attain V∗ . The two-live-label optimum is attained and satisfies
                                                             1
                                          V∗ < V2 ≤ 1 +           ;                                (S37.3)
                                                           1 − c2
its exact value is determined in Theorem S37.3 below.
   If an explicitly randomized initial distribution over counted labels is instead part of the interface,
two live labels are necessary and sufficient to attain V∗ . Under event stopping the stated live counts
are also total-label counts, since the constant terminal value may reuse a live label. State stopping
requires one additional disjoint terminal label.
   If either failure projection is zero, one call to that detector gives value one. If c = 1, their ranges
have a common nonzero dark subspace and no policy is proper for every initial state. The robust
cost is then infinite.
Proof. We give the required spectral reduction explicitly. Diagonalize P QP on ran P . For an
                                                      √                               √
eigenvector u with eigenvalue q ∈ (0, 1), put v = Qu/ q. Then ∥v∥ = 1 and ⟨u, v⟩ = q. Their span
is invariant under both projections, which restrict to the rank-one projections onto u, v. Distinct
orthonormal eigenvectors give orthogonal such two-dimensional blocks, including after resolving
multiplicities. The zero eigenspace gives ran P ∩ ker Q; the remaining part of ran Q is orthogonal to
P . The orthogonal complement of the two ranges lies in both kernels. Eigenvalue one is exactly a
common failure range. This is the classical two-subspace geometry [18], proved here in the form
used below. The largest nontrivial squared overlap is q = c2 when c > 0.
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                      147


   After a failure of A, a second immediate A fails with probability one and leaves the state
unchanged. The analogous statement holds for B. Such a repetition gives no information. In
a full-history policy it can be deleted, with its private coins and deterministic failure simulated
internally, leaving all subsequent physical states and detector choices coupled and weakly reducing
calls. Infinite repetitions give infinite cost and cannot improve the optimum. Thus, after its first
choice, a pointwise optimal policy switches after every failure.
   On a block of squared overlap q, the post-failure pure state switches between u and v, and
every further failure has probability q. Its expected remaining cost is 1/(1 − q). On the whole P
failure range this continuation cost is the positive operator HP = (IP − P QP )−1 , and similarly
HQ = (IQ − QP Q)−1 . These inverses exist because c < 1. A first choice of A with probability r
therefore has cost operator bounded below by
                                    I + rP HP P + (1 − r)QHQ Q,                                 (S37.4)
with equality for subsequent alternation. On a principal block its largest eigenvalue is

It increases with q and is minimized uniquely at r = 1/2. Unpaired parts have the corresponding

   With one live label, the same probability r of choosing A is used after every failure. A persistent

The larger eigenvalue is 1 + [a + b + q(a + b)2 + (1 − q)(a − b)2 ]/2. Since a + b ≥ 2/(1 − q), it is at
                √                          √
least 1 + (1 + q)/(1 − q) = 1 + 1/(1 − q), with equality at r = 1/2. The deterministic endpoints
have an undetectable failure state. The largest principal block proves (S37.2); the fair row realizes
that operator bound in every block.
   For the fixed-start unrestricted optimum, its first command row must be fair. On a largest
principal block, both possible first failures have positive probability from the top vector of the fair
cost operator. Every positive-probability repetition of the last failed detector strictly increases the
expected cost there. Consequently an optimum needs a pure B row after A fails and a pure A row
after B fails. These are two distinct rows, neither equal to the initial mixed row. Three live labels
are necessary; those three rows and their obvious switching transitions suffice. This is a rigidity
statement on the extremal block, not a claim about behavior on irrelevant physical subspaces.
   The two-label parameter space is compact. For a sequence of proper two-label observers with
uniformly bounded worst-state costs, every finite-prefix cost is continuous in the parameters and
remains bounded at the limit. Monotone convergence gives a finite full cost there; unit costs imply
proper operation. A minimizing sequence therefore has a proper minimizing limit. Such a limit
cannot attain V∗ by the preceding row argument, proving the strict lower in (S37.3). Deterministic
alternation from a fixed first detector uses two labels and has worst cost 1 + (1 − c2 )−1 , proving the
upper.
   If the interface permits a randomized initial distribution, start fairly in the two pure switching
labels. This stores the first choice in a counted label and attains V∗ . One live label cannot attain it
by (S37.2). The event/state counts follow from their exact halt semantics. Finally, a zero failure
148                                                      QIAN QI


projection always succeeds. In finite dimension ∥P Q∥ = 1 implies a nonzero vector in both failure
ranges by the equality case for orthogonal projections. It remains there under every permitted
command and is never detected. This proves the degenerate cases.                                □
Corollary S37.2 (A primitive least-favorable preparation). Choose unit failure vectors u, v in a
largest principal-angle block, with ⟨u, v⟩ = c, and put
                                                    q
                               ψ∗ = (u + v)/ 2(1 + c),               ρ∗ = |ψ∗ ⟩⟨ψ∗ |.
For c = 0 choose any unit vectors in the two nonzero orthogonal failure ranges. This one pure state
gives expected cost at least V∗ against every visible-history policy. Fair-first alternation has cost at
most V∗ against every initial state. Thus it is an explicit minimax saddle without a policy-dependent
preparation or a minimax exchange. The same preparation gives the one-live-label lower V1 against
every one-label row.
Proof. The first failure probability from ρ∗ is (1 + c)/2 for either detector. Its post-failure state is
respectively u or v, whose least remaining cost is 1/(1 − c2 ) by the switching argument. Every first
command mixture therefore costs at least 1 + (1 + c)/[2(1 − c2 )] = V∗ . The uniform upper was proved
above. For a one-label row, the cost at ρ∗ is 1 + (a + b)(1 + c)/2. The bound a + b ≥ 2/(1 − c2 )
gives V1 , attained by the fair row. This preparation is a witness for the stated detection task, not
an automatically recurrent reference row after arbitrary rejected experiments.                        □

These are lower potential inequalities for every allowed label and randomized label choice. Iteration

In a smaller principal block with squared overlap q ′ ≤ q, both nonnegative coefficients aq′ (r)/(1 − q ′2 )
and bq′ (r)/(1 − q ′2 ) increase with q ′ . The larger eigenvalue of aP + bQ is nondecreasing in either
nonnegative coefficient by positive matrix order, and in overlap by its square-root formula. Thus
the worst block is the largest. Unpaired ranges have no greater than the corresponding zero-overlap
bound, and the common kernel succeeds immediately. The same observer therefore has cost at most
Fq (r) for every initial state and attains it on the largest block. Its finite post-failure costs prove
proper operation. Equality in (S37.8) follows.

 2
r + r − 1 = 0, giving the first line of (S37.9).
   For 0 < q < 1, use one fixed principal block. Its numerator matrix is aq (r)P + bq (r)Q, with aq
affine and b′′q (r) = 2/r3 > 0. Its largest eigenvalue is strictly convex. Indeed the convex-combination
matrix at two different parameters exceeds the matrix at the combined parameter by a positive
multiple of Q. A top eigenvector of the latter has positive Q-expectation since q > 0 and aq > 0.
Evaluation at that vector proves strict convexity. The top eigenvalue is simple, so the function is
differentiable, and it diverges as r ↓ 0.
   At r = 1 the numerator is (1 + q)P . Its top vector is u, with Q-expectation q. Differentiation at
this simple eigenvector gives
                                         (1 − q 2 )Fq′ (1) = 1 − q − q 2 .
For q ≥ q∗ this is nonpositive, so strict convexity makes r = 1 the unique minimum. For q < q∗
it is positive; divergence at zero and strict convexity give a unique interior stationary point.
Differentiating the square root gives (S37.10). This proves every case. The already proved live-role
counts give the full integer frontier.                                                                   □
   The golden-ratio threshold concerns the squared principal-angle cosine and the optimal controller
row, not a universal physical constant. At small overlap one continuing row is pure and the initial
row is mixed. At and above the threshold the optimal two-label policy is fixed-start deterministic
alternation. A third live label restores a fair initial choice without contaminating either continuing
row and strictly improves the worst-state cost.
