S37.2. A sharp memory-constrained minimax gap. The exact finite-memory frontier also
identifies a limitation of replacing a uniform converse by an optimized single-prior calculation. Let
C2 be the proper fixed-start observers with at most two live labels in this detection interface.
Corollary S37.4 (Minimax gap at two live labels). For P, Q ̸= 0 and c < 1,
                                inf sup Eπρ τ = V2 (c) > V∗ (c) = sup inf Eπρ τ.                      (S37.12)
                                π∈C2   ρ                              ρ π∈C2

                    √ initial preparation supplies the sharp two-label robust lower V2 . At zero
In particular no single
overlap the gap is ( 5 − 2)/2. Permitting a randomized initial distribution over the two counted
pure labels removes this gap and gives robust value V∗ . No analogous gap occurs at one live label or
at three or more for this experiment.
Proof. The left equality and strict inequality are the memory theorems. For any fixed initial state,
choose the better of the two deterministic first detectors and then alternate. Both choices are
proper two-label observers. Their better cost is no larger than their average, which is the cost of
fair-first alternation and is at most V∗ . Hence the maximin is at most V∗ . The single state ρ∗ in
Corollary S37.2 gives
                   √     cost at least V∗ against every policy, proving the reverse inequality. When
c = 0, V2 = (1 + 5)/2 and V∗ = 3/2, giving the displayed gap. A randomized initial label realizes
fair-first alternation directly. For one label, the same least-favorable state and fair independent row
form a saddle at V1 ; for three or more the full-history saddle applies.                              □
   The policy in the inner maximin may depend on the fixed preparation; the robust policy on the
left may not. The fixed-start two-label class is not closed under the convexification that would mix
the two pure initial rows while remembering which continuing row to use. A free random initial
distribution changes precisely that resource. Thus a minimax exchange would erase a real memory
constraint. This is an exact result for the stated detector model, not a failure of the general sufficient
transfer theorem or a claim that every finite-memory problem has such a gap.

                     S38. Raw task order and changing-rank stability
   The central theorem is formulated in measures because selection acts before normalization. This
section gives a verification principle from primitive kernels and a singular extension of that principle.
It does not assume a covariance or information matrix in the experiment.
A measure determined by executable affine-square tests. For a report–audit law P (dy, dz)
on Y × H define the finite positive matrix measure
                                Z
                   JP (D) =            v(z)v(z)T P (dy, dz),        v(z) = (1, z1 , . . . , zk )T .    (S38.1)
                                 D×H
For finite positive matrix measures write A ≼ B when B(D) − A(D) is positive semidefinite for
every Borel D. This definition is independent of the choice of dominating scalar measure.
Proposition S38.1 (An exact order criterion for localized affine-square tests). For two joint laws
P, P 0 on the same full report space and 0 < l ≤ 1 ≤ u, the following assertions are equivalent:
                                              lJP 0 ≼ JP ≼ uJP 0 ;                                     (S38.2)
for every Borel report event D and every a ∈ Rk+1 ,
                          Z                       Z                       Z
                      l       |aT v(z)|2 dP 0 ≤       |aT v(z)|2 dP ≤ u       |aT v(z)|2 dP 0 .        (S38.3)
                          D                       D                       D
It suffices to test rational vectors a. These equivalent conditions imply, for all Borel q ∈ [0, 1] and
finite-image Borel r : Y → H, both the report-mass and the squared-task inequalities with zero
additive defect and these same factors. A violation of (S38.2) is witnessed by a single Borel report
event and a rational affine-square test.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        151


Proof. The integral in (S38.3) is aT JP (D)a. The definition of positive semidefiniteness gives the
equivalence. Continuity of the quadratic form gives sufficiency of rational vectors and a rational
witness for a strict violation. Taking a = (1, 0, . . . , 0) gives mass order. For a fixed readout r, sum
the k quadratic tests with vectors (−rj , ej ), obtaining the order of ∥z − r∥2 . Partition Y into the
finitely many cells where r is constant. For a simple nonnegative q, apply order on intersections
with these cells and add. Bounded monotone approximation extends to arbitrary q ∈ [0, 1]. All
measures are finite and all tested losses bounded.                                                      □
   Thus the criterion is necessary and sufficient for the declared task-rich affine-square family. It
is a sufficient condition for the narrower squared-prediction task of the central theorem. It is not
claimed necessary for that narrower task, or for the existence of an arbitrary causal balancing
strategy. The matrix is built from raw joint audit laws, not from an assumed parameterization. The
upper construction may use its virtual report space after averaging its current command; the lower
criterion must use the full report seen by a competitor, conditional on that competitor’s chosen
command.
Theorem S38.2 (Order on measurable singular ranges). Let J0 , J1 be finite positive matrix measures
of size k + 1. With λ = tr J0 + tr J1 , take measurable positive semidefinite densities A0 , A1 with
respect to λ. Then
                                           lJ0 ≼ J1 ≼ uJ0                                     (S38.4)
is equivalent, λ-almost everywhere, to equality of kernels ker A0 = ker A1 and to
                                               †/2     †/2
                                       lP0 ≼ A0 A1 A0        ≼ uP0 ,                             (S38.5)
where P0 is the orthogonal projection onto ran A0 and † denotes the Moore–Penrose inverse. On the
zero range both sides are zero. All these maps are measurable, even when rank changes. No positive
lower bound on a nonzero eigenvalue of A0 is required. In particular every zero affine-square task
on an acquired report event remains exactly zero under the order.
Proof. A finite signed matrix measure is positive if and only if its density is positive semidefinite
almost everywhere. To see the nontrivial direction, test the density against all rational vectors; a
negative set of positive measure for one such quadratic form contradicts positivity of its integral
on that set. The countable intersection of the resulting full-measure sets handles all rational
vectors, hence all real vectors by continuity. Thus (S38.4) is equivalent to lA0 ≼ A1 ≼ uA0 almost
everywhere.
   For two such matrices, a vector in ker A0 has zero quadratic form under A1 , and positivity
implies that it is in ker A1 . The lower inequality gives the converse. On their common range A0 is
positive definite; congruence by its inverse square root yields (S38.5). Conversely equality of kernels
                                                                     1/2
makes A1 supported on that range. Congruence of (S38.5) by A0 gives the original two matrix
inequalities, extended by zero on the common kernel.
   Measurability follows from finite-dimensional spectral functional calculus: P0 is the pointwise
limit of A0 (A0 + n−1 I)−1 , and the scalar function equal to x−1/2 on x > 0 and zero at zero is Borel.
Applying it spectrally gives a Borel matrix function on each rank stratum, hence on their finite
measurable union. We use these matrices pointwise; their unbounded inverse norms are never taken
as a uniform error constant. Finally a nonnegative task of zero integral under J0 has integral at
most u times zero under J1 , proving preservation of zero tasks.                                     □
   There is an important distinction between this assertion and a rank formula for risk. Rank is
allowed to change; the rate still comes from the acquired masses and coverings of Section S39. For
example a nonzero matrix density with arbitrarily small positive eigenvalues can obey the order
with fixed l, u, while an arbitrarily small absolute perturbation can create a new positive direction
in its kernel and fail the order. This is precisely the difference relevant to a zero-loss stratum.
152                                                QIAN QI


Proposition S38.3 (Positive-kernel verification and relative command calibration). Suppose raw
positive input states satisfy ls0 ≤ s ≤ us0 in an ordered vector space and a fixed positive normalized
report–audit instrument sends an input to a joint probability law. Its output laws and task matrices
satisfy the same order. More generally let Ij be positive instruments and let a nominal probability
vector (λ0j ) satisfy a desired joint-law identity after averaging. If
                      (1 − η)λ0j ≤ λj ≤ (1 + η)λ0j      for every j,      0 ≤ η < 1,
then the actual averaged joint law lies between 1 − η and 1 + η times the nominal law, for every input.
Successive relative input and instrument bounds multiply their lower factors and upper factors. They
do not add an acquisition-amplified defect.
Proof. Positivity sends the two positive differences s−ls0 and us0 −s to positive measures. Integration
of v(z)v(z)T against a positive measure preserves positive matrix order. The command assertion
follows by multiplying each nonnegative instrument measure by its nonnegative weight difference
and summing. Applying the two inequalities successively multiplies the factors. These arguments
do not divide by any report probability.                                                              □

Actual-mass-weighted predictive stability. The following is the trace/base-norm contraction
principle for positive normalized instruments; in the quantum case compare [31, Corollary 3.40].
We include its proof to retain the unnormalized and zero-mass conventions used here.
Proposition S38.4 (Continuity through likelihood zeros and pure states). A classical stochastic
instrument on signed measures, or a finite-dimensional completely positive normalized quantum
instrument, maps normalized inputs s, t to unnormalized post-report states Ay , By . Use any common
finite dominating measure for these two outputs and let py = u(Ay ), qy = u(By ) be their masses.
Then
                                             Z
                                                 ∥Ay − By ∥1 dy ≤ ∥s − t∥1 ,                   (S38.6)

                                                     ∥p − q∥TV ≤ 21 ∥s − t∥1 ,                 (S38.7)
                                                 Ay   By
                             Z
                                 min(py , qy )      −      dy ≤ 2∥s − t∥1 .                    (S38.8)
                                                 py   qy 1
Terms of zero mass are zero. The same unnormalized and report-law inequalities hold for a complete
stopped instrument under one common legal policy which terminates almost surely under both inputs.
Proof. Write s−t = D+R−D− with positive parts. Positivity, the triangle inequality and preservation
of integrated mass give ∥Iy (D+ ) − Iy (D− )∥1 dy ≤ u(D+ ) + u(D− ) = ∥s − t∥1 . Taking masses gives
(S38.7). If p ≤ q, then
                             p∥A/p − B/q∥1 ≤ ∥A − B∥1 + (q − p).
Interchanging the roles for q ≤ p, integrating, and using |p − q| ≤ ∥A − B∥1 proves (S38.8). A finite
causal continuation with a fixed policy is again a positive normalized instrument. Truncate a stopped
continuation at a deterministic horizon, retaining a distinct surviving outcome. Apply the finite
inequality, then let the horizon increase. The surviving masses vanish under both inputs by almost-
sure termination, and the sum over terminal times converges monotonically. No expected-time
assertion is deduced from these norm inequalities.                                                 □
  The upper finite-state construction never stores a normalized posterior. It uses the acquired
report and a finite decoder. Consequently stability of the raw instrument, rather than a globally
contracting posterior recursion, suffices for the singular realizations below. More general closed-loop
optimal-control claims would require their own strategy comparison and stability; they are not
consequences of comparing the same fixed policy.

          S39. Acquired geometry at regular, stratified and atomic scales
                                                                                √
  All masses in this section refer to the actually prepared pushforward σ = (f, w)# ν. They√are
not volumes on a parameter space. Conditional expectation of any feasible density given (f, w)
preserves its mass, density cap and objective for every dictionary; conversely a density on this
pushforward lifts by composition. Thus the profile can equivalently be optimized on this actual
marked law. The transfer theorem compares operating task measures with this physical acquisition
law. In the relative-order realizations it also bounds actual upper-program report measures above
and below by fixed multiples of ν. Thus the comparison remains tied to acquired mass, even though
perturbed conditional means need not coincide with f .
Proposition S39.1 (A non-power geometric comparison). For r ≥ 0 let
                     Ωm (r) =       max        ν{x : w(x) + dist(f (x), C)2 ≤ r2 }.
                                C⊂H, 1≤|C|≤m

For all b, m ≥ 1,
                       sup r2 [1 − bΩm (r)]+ ≤ Qm (b) ≤ inf{r2 : Ωm (r) ≥ 1/b}.                (S39.1)
                       r≥0
In particular a verified estimate bΩm (r− ) ≤ 1/2 and a verified covering by m distortion sublevel sets
of total acquired mass at least 1/b at radius r+ imply r− 2 /2 ≤ Q (b) ≤ r 2 . The assertion requires
                                                                   m        +
no integer local dimension or isotropic geometry.
Proof. For a fixed dictionary and a density cap b, at most bΩm (r) of the selected mass lies in its
radius-r sublevel set. The remaining mass costs at least r2 , proving the lower. To see that the
maximum defining Ωm is attained, use an ordered tuple in H m . For every report, the indicator
of the closed sublevel condition is upper semicontinuous in that tuple. Reverse Fatou for these
bounded indicators makes its integral upper semicontinuous. Compactness gives a maximizing tuple.
If the corresponding sublevel set has mass v ≥ 1/b, the density 1D /v is feasible and has loss at
most r2 . Taking the infimum over such radii gives the upper. This proof keeps the density cap and
actual mass; a cover of the formal reachable set alone provides neither.                         □
   The proposition can be used with anisotropic boxes, different local scales, or non-power small-ball
laws, provided both the upper mass estimate and the required acquired cover are verified. It is a
finite-scale statement, not a claim that an unspecified anisotropic dimension determines risk. We
give explicit closed curves for a useful class and its finite stratifications.
   Assume σ is supported on K × [0, 1], where K ⊂ H is compact, and for some d, α > 0, 0 < r0 ≤ 1
and c, C > 0,
               crd sα ≤ σ(B(x, r) × [0, s]) ≤ Crd sα   (x ∈ K, 0 < r ≤ r0 , 0 < s ≤ 1).        (S39.2)
These are global estimates on the stated component, including small reliability mass. Singular
Ahlfors-regular location measures of noninteger d are permitted.
Proposition S39.2 (The marked acquired profile). Under (S39.2), uniformly for b ≥ 1 and integer
m ≥ 1,
                            Qm (b) ≍ F (b, m) := max{b−2/α , (bm)−2/(d+α) }.                (S39.3)
The constants depend only on the displayed data and the bounded task carrier. This geometric
calculation is inherited from the stopped-experiment and predictable-geometry work retained in [21];
its proof is included for independence of the central theorem.
Proof. Cover K by finitely many balls of radius r0 /2. Their upper bounds yield σ(K × [0, s]) ≤ C0 sα .
Under density cap b, choose s = c0 b−1/α sufficiently small so that at most half the selected mass
has reliability at most s. This gives a risk lower s2 /2.
  For a dictionary with m centers, its distortion sublevel at radius r lies in the union of m location
balls of radius r with reliability at most r. A center outside K whose ball intersects K can, for this
154                                                 QIAN QI


mass bound, be replaced by one intersection point and the radius doubled. For 2r ≤ r0 , the union
has acquired mass at most C1 mrd+α . Set r = c1 (bm)−1/(d+α) with one sufficiently small constant c1
so that the restriction and the half-mass bound both hold for all b, m ≥ 1. Proposition S39.1 gives
the other lower in (S39.3).
   For the upper, the location marginal has two-sided d-dimensional ball bounds by taking s = 1.
A maximal 4r-separated set covers K by 4r-balls. The marginal upper bound then forces at least
c2 r−d points for small r. Its radius-r balls are disjoint. Choose constants c3 > 0 small and C2 large
such that, for all sufficiently large b, the integer
                         j = min{m, ⌊c3 bd/α ⌋} ≥ 1,           r = C2 (bj)−1/(d+α)
satisfies r ≤ min(r0 /8, 1), j ≤ c2 r−d , and cjrd+α ≥ 1/b. The last condition fixes a sufficiently
large C2 ; after fixing it, a sufficiently small c3 enforces the packing condition because jrd =
C2d j α/(d+α) b−d/(d+α) . Increasing the lower threshold for b then enforces the radius condition and
j ≥ 1. Use j separated centers. The disjoint rectangles B(xi , r) × [0, r] have union mass at least 1/b
and distortion to those centers at most 2r2 . Selecting a unit mass under cap b from that union proves
the upper, and substitution of j gives (S39.3). For b in the remaining bounded interval, always
accept and use one center. Its bounded loss is controlled by a fixed multiple of b−2/α , uniformly
over arbitrarily large m. This proves all claimed uniformities.                                      □
Corollary S39.3 (All-scale resource curves under fixed relative calibration). Suppose (S39.2) and
the relative hypotheses of Corollary 10.2 hold. On a set of feasible budgets suppose the positive effort
ratio κ+ /κ− and positive width ratio m− /m+ are bounded above and below by fixed constants. Then
                                      RiΘ (N, M ) ≍ F (N/κ− , m− (M ))                          (S39.4)
throughout that set, including arbitrarily fine resolution. The constants may depend on l± , u± , but
these factors need not approach one as N, M grow.
   More generally the same conclusion holds with absolute defects if AN ≤ A0 < 1, b+ a+ ≤ A1 < ∞,
EN ≤ c0 F (b− , m− ) and b+ e+ ≤ C0 F (b− , m− ), for a sufficiently small c0 > 0 depending on the fixed
geometric and order constants.
Proof. For pure relative order and l+ b+ ≥ 1, both effective budgets and widths in (10.5) differ from
b− , m− by bounded positive factors. Apply Proposition S39.2. If 1 ≤ b+ < 1/l+ , then b− lies in a
bounded interval. The first-attempt upper is bounded, while the lower and F (b− , m− ) are bounded
below by a fixed positive constant independently of m− . This treats the boundary regime. With
absolute defects, tN is bounded below by (1 − A0 )/u− and the corrected upper budget differs by
fixed factors. The geometric lower therefore exceeds cF before subtracting EN . Take c0 ≤ c/2. The
upper defect has the stated order, yielding the result. A fixed positive absolute defect need not
satisfy these scale conditions; relative order and absolute proximity are distinct hypotheses.      □

   For overlapping abstract components, the Radon–Nikodym selection used in this proof is an ideal
Borel construction. A physical finite classifier for it requires the separate effective partition and
integration data; it does not follow merely from the mixture decomposition.
   The mixture theorem permits different local dimensions and reliability exponents, including
noninteger singular dimensions. It is more than a statement that strata may be supplied through
an interface: it gives their coupled acquisition–memory optimization and proves both sides with the
original masses. Proposition S39.1 remains available when an anisotropic component has no single
power-law description.
Proposition S39.5 (An acquired zero-loss atom). Suppose σ = λδ(x∗ ,0) + (1 − λ)σ0 , 0 < λ < 1,
where σ0 has no mass at that atom. Then Qm (b) = 0 for every m ≥ 1 if bλ ≥ 1. If s = 1 − bλ > 0,
then
                             b(1 − λ)                        b(1 − λ)
                                                                   
                     sQ0m               ≤ Qm (b) ≤ sQ0m−1                 (m ≥ 2).              (S39.6)
                                 s                               s
For m = 1 only the displayed lower applies; a valid upper fixes its center at x∗ and optimizes the
remaining selected mass with that center. If w > 0 almost surely on the remainder, then every finite-
width profile is positive below threshold. Under pure relative transfer and the additional condition
w > 0 almost surely on the remainder, zero risk is impossible when u− b− λ < 1. Without that
additional condition, the displayed atom still supplies the sufficient zero-risk bound when l+ b+ λ ≥ 1
and the upper width is feasible, but other zero-loss atoms may lower the true threshold. If w > 0
almost surely on the remainder, the exact matched threshold is N = κ/λ. The complete finite-width
threshold without this restriction is Theorem S34.2.
Proof. Above threshold accept only the atom and decode x∗ . Below threshold at least mass s
must come from the remainder. Scale any selected remainder measure down to mass s; it remains
dominated by b(1 − λ)σ0 and nonnegative loss cannot increase. Normalize it to obtain the lower in
(S39.6). For the upper retain all available atom mass bλ, allocate one center to it, and optimize the
mass-s remainder with m − 1 centers. With one center there is no zero-center optimization, which
explains the separate statement. If the remainder has w > 0 almost surely, any positive selected
156                                                    QIAN QI


probability has positive residual loss; attainment from Lemma 11.1 makes the profile minimum
strictly positive. Finally substitute the atom criterion into the two effective caps of (10.5). Relative
calibration preserves zero tasks but may change the required acquisition threshold by the stated
factors; it does not preserve the numerical threshold without exact matching.                         □

Nonuniform defects and metastable preparation.
Lemma S39.6 (Killed-potential occupation bound). Let a nonnegative predictable defect di be
charged at raw call i, and suppose, for every legal command before commitment, nonnegative
integrable potentials obey
                        di + E∗ [Vi 1{i<τ } | Fi−1 , ai ] ≤ Vi−1 + γE∗ [ci | Fi−1 , ai ].
Here V0 is integrable, γP≥ 0, and after commitment the potential is killed. Then every budget-N
competitor satisfies E∗ i≤τ di ≤ E∗ V0 + γN . Potentials may depend on the hidden physical state;
this is an analytic certificate, not uncharged observer memory.
Proof. Multiply by the predictable indicator of making call i, integrate and sum to a deterministic
horizon n. All interior potentials cancel. The remaining final potential is nonnegative and may be
dropped. Bound the expected partial cost by N , then use monotone convergence for the nonnegative
defect sum. Randomized commands are handled by integrating the commandwise inequality. No
optional stopping with unverified uniform integrability is needed.                               □
Corollary S39.7 (Relative order with rare defective modes). Suppose relative order holds on good
raw histories, while visible conditional additive defects are bounded by the conditional expectations
of ab 1{Di−1 =1} and eb 1{Di−1 =1} for a hidden mode D ∈ {0, 1}. At every legal call, given the physical
past, assume
                 P∗ (Di = 1 | Di−1 = 0, ·) ≤ u,           P∗ (Di = 0 | Di−1 = 1, ·) ≥ v > 0.
Let π0 = P∗ (D0 = 1) be the actual prepared mass and ci ≥ c∗ > 0. The central theorem applies with
                                 ab      ab u             eb      eb u
                           AN = π0 +          N,   EN = π0 +           N.
                                 v       vc∗               v      vc∗
These quantities may replace rowwise worst-case defects even if bad-mode errors are not small and
the residence time 1/v is large.
Proof. Use V (D) = (ab /v)1{D=1} . In a good mode its expected increase is at most ab u/v; in a
bad mode its expected decrease is at least ab and pays for the defect. Killing at commitment
only improves the inequality. Since each raw call costs at least c∗ , Lemma S39.6 gives the first
bound. Counting only productive calls makes it smaller. Replace ab by eb for the task bound
and use conditional expectation to pass from physical modes to the visible-history defects. This
retains actual preparation and selection-biased residence, rather than substituting an invariant-mode
distribution.                                                                                      □

               S40. Classical and quantum realizations from raw kernels
   The two realizations below are verified after the general theorem. Recurrent witness preparation,
when present, belongs to the charged productive primitive; it is not a reset made freely available
to other models. Neither supplies a premise used to prove it. Their conditional physical evolution
between calls may be noncontracting, and their acquired laws may be singular. Both tasks score
the post-command audit. A target which must remain unchanged is a different experiment; the
nonintervening directional result is retained in the companion.
158                                                QIAN QI

               1

                   1             2                    5              10                  30

       Figure 1. The exact minimizer of the displayed comparator, not a fitted risk curve.
       Vertical lines mark the five algebraic transition budgets. The two crossover intervals
       are part of the optimum; smoothing a maximum before differentiation would miss

A common paid physical interface. The gate port G costs one per call and has success
probability at least p0 > 0, conditional on every physical and visible past. A success enables exactly
one productive call at port A, also of cost one. Commitment is forbidden at G and allowed after a
productive report. Rejection at A returns the protocol to G; it need not reset the hidden state. The
two ports have disjoint physical command domains and no port tag is a free intercall input to an
autonomous observer. Suppose the actual lower witness has gate success probability at most p1 ≤ 1.
Then
                            κ− = 1 + 1/p1 ,     κ+ = 1 + 1/p0 ,        B0 = B = 2.              (S40.1)
Let Gτ and Aτ count the gate and productive calls through commitment. Every productive call
consumes a gate success. A terminating path has no unconsumed success. Predictable counting
gives E∗ Aτ ≤ p1 E∗ Gτ , so E∗ Cτ ≥ κ− E∗ Aτ . The probability of j failures after any gate start is at
most (1 − p0 )j , giving conditional expected gate count at most 1/p0 and whole-attempt cost at most
κ+ . Both mandatory ports are visited before commitment, and Lemma 11.4 gives two live roles.
Consequently the construction requires at least two event labels or three state labels when it has
one output. If the witness has constant success p0 = p1 , then E∗ Cτ = κE∗ Aτ with κ = 1 + 1/p0 . In
that matched case budget feasibility is exactly N ≥ κ with the respective state thresholds. Even
when p0 = 1, the cost is two, not one.

Theorem S40.1 (A relatively calibrated classical realization). Let a hidden symbol E take values
in a finite set of size q. A finite group G acts transitively on that set. The allowed productive
commands apply g ∈ G and then, within the same call, a fixed positive normalized joint report–audit
kernel JgE (dy, dz) with bounded audit in H. There is no unknown intervening operation between
that command and its audit instrument. Evolution between port interactions is otherwise arbitrary
and causal.
   The genuinely prepared comparison has uniform input and joint law K0 = q −1 e Je . Its full

   For any fixed incoming symbol, uniform averaging over a transitive group gives the uniform
distribution of its image: each image has the same number of preimages in the group. Averaging also

Proposition S39.5. A biased fresh witness and imperfect command probabilities satisfy (S40.2) with
fixed nonzero constants. On T = 0 the task is exactly deterministic even under these errors. On
T > 0 the two-point audit moment matrix has rank two, while at T = 0 it has rank one. This is an
actual changing-rank task measure, not a formal limiting parameter set.
   The command group and its bit-level sampler are part of the program description and workspace
account. A high-cardinality group does not provide free randomization or a free persistent seed. In
the binary realization a single fresh sign coin suffices within a productive call. Conditional posterior
continuity of the classical raw kernels is governed by Proposition S38.4, including likelihood zeros.

   The qubit is part of the physical experiment, not a quantum memory register owned by the finite
classical observer. The pulse and instrument form one productive call in this convention; their
duration, finite sampling precision and arithmetic are separately charged. An interface requiring a
separate pulse call must add its cost and any intervening routing state. Both realizations retain a
strictly positive paid gate floor p0 .

Predictive realization rather than an assumed parameter manifold. For the finite classical
kernels and finite-dimensional quantum instruments whose entries depend continuously on compact
report coordinates, the primitive continuity criterion in Proposition S42.2 supplies compact positive-
evidence history patches. Their executable predictive quotient is therefore Borel by Theorem S42.1.
Fixed continuous causal evolutions between calls are included and need not contract. For a completely
arbitrary merely Borel hidden evolution, the risk theorem still uses the stated raw certificates, but
we do not assert the compact-exhaustion quotient theorem without its regularity hypotheses. The
unknown model’s family of posteriors is never stored by the common upper program.

              S41. Finite computation, calibration and causal composition
  The inequalities proved so far concern Borel existence with a declared physical acquisition program.
A finite source description and an executable precision certificate are additional mathematical data.
We state exactly where they enter.
Proposition S41.1 (Numerical implementation at a restored physical budget). Suppose a chosen
ideal density and dictionary at cap β have a finite implementation whose acceptance probability, as
a function of the current virtual report, differs pointwise from h/β by at most η ≥ 0. Suppose its
actual exported decoder values differ from the ideal exported values by at most δnum , with no larger
output width. These finite operations use the same legal acquisition program and counted live roles.
Put b = N/κ+ and
                                                  l+ b
                                         β=                 ≥ 1.
                                             1 + b(a+ + η)
If the chosen ideal pair has objective at most Qm (β) + ξ, the implemented program terminates almost
surely at actual expected core cost at most N and risk at most
                    "s                                                         #2
                         u+ (1 + b(a+ + η))
                                            (Qm (β) + ξ) + b(e+ + Lη) + δnum        .          (S41.1)
                                 l+
Here ξ is a certified design/integration suboptimality, not a local optimizer’s unverified gap. A
uniform bound Tattempt on conditional expected physical and arithmetic time of a whole attempt
yields expected time at most bTattempt . Program bits and scratch are separately those of the certified

Proof. Acceptance rounding reduces the conditional hazard by at most η. The chosen cap gives
l+ /β −a+ −η = 1/b. The same conditional-survival summation as in the main theorem yields at most
b attempts and cost at most N . Before perturbing decoder values, the one-attempt unnormalized
loss changes by at most Lη under the actual joint law, because loss is bounded by L. Its other
contribution is at most u+ (Qm (β) + ξ)/β + e+ . Summation gives the expression under the square
root. At the actual committed report, the implemented and ideal exported values are within δnum ;
Minkowski’s inequality in the actual stopped L2 law adds this distance to root-mean-square loss. It
does not multiply the decoder error by the waiting time. The time claim is a separate predictable
sum of the stated whole-attempt bound.                                                           □
   In the preceding display Tattempt denotes a declared time quantity, not a free clock input to
the observer. An implementation based on m fixed centers in a k-dimensional bounded box, p-bit
coordinate certificates and sequential squared-distance comparisons has sufficient read-only data
O(mkp) bits, within-call scratch O(kp + log m) bits and O(mkp2 ) bit operations per report, in
addition to its primitive measurement and coordinate-evaluation costs. These are upper accounts,
not optimal computational lower bounds. General Borel classifiers need not have such certificates.
The complete dyadic implementation in the companion gives explicit rational thresholds, separate
read-only/scratch counts and an independently proved profile lower.
162                                              QIAN QI


Corollary S41.2 (Finite-description matching upper under effective primitives). In the pure relative
regular class, suppose additionally that the actual upper-program report laws are bounded above by ūν
on every surviving history. Assume effective compactness of the dictionary carrier and certified finite
approximations of exported readouts inside H. Assume the calibrated report law has an effective
sequence of finite physical partitions which approximates bounded finite decision vectors in L1 (ν),
with certified integration of their masses and bounded task losses. Primitive classifiers and their
finite costs are supplied as part of this effective interface. Then a finite-description upper with the
same live and terminal role counts has risk at most a fixed multiple of F (b, m) at actual expected cost
at most κ+ b, for all feasible b, m. Every such construction has finite, separately charged program,
scratch and execution accounts. No polynomial-time discovery or matching bit-complexity lower is
asserted.
Proof. For b ≥ 4/l+ choose the ideal cap β = l+ b/2. Its conditional acceptance is at least 2/b. By
Proposition S39.2, its profile is at most a fixed multiple of F (b, m). Represent its finite decision as
functions qc (x), one for each dictionary value, whose sum is the acceptance probability. Approximate
the vector on one finite physical partition by rational vectors in the subprobability simplex. The
assumed effective approximation makes the sum of its L1 (ν) coordinate errors arbitrarily small, say
at most γ. Domination of the actual report law bounds its actual conditional decision discrepancy
by ūγ on every history, and its unnormalized loss discrepancy by Lūγ. Approximate the actual
finite exported values inside H as well.
p Choose γ so small that ūγ < 1/(2b) and bLūγ ≤ F (b, m), and choose export error at most
   F (b, m). The conditional acceptance still exceeds 1/b, so the expected cost is at most κ+ b.
Summing loss and applying the stopped L2 readout estimate gives a fixed multiple of F (b, m). For
1 ≤ b < 4/l+ , accept the first attempt and use one finitely described value in H. Bounded loss is a
fixed multiple of the strictly positive lower bound for b−2/α on this interval, uniformly in m.
   The existence argument can be effected by finite enumeration using the supplied integration
certificates and effective dictionary nets: the above choices leave strict mass and loss margins, so
a candidate with certified acceptable intervals is eventually found. This need not be an efficient
search. Current partition indices and rational randomization are charged within-call work; they do
not create new intercall roles. All chosen descriptions and precision parameters are finite. Exact
zero risk on an atom requires an exactly implementable atom classifier and readout; otherwise
Proposition S41.1, not an unqualified finite-code zero claim, governs that component.                 □
Proposition S41.3 (Resource-certified causal morphisms). A causal simulator must specify its
parameter-independent strategy lift, legal command map, report transformation, internal state, clock
and terminal task readout. Suppose a target program has ℓ live and t terminal roles. A compatible
simulator has at most K continuing internal states, including every persistent phase, coin and clock,
and at most E terminal export classes sufficient for its final decoder. Then a source implementation
uses at most ℓK + tE state-stopped labels or max(ℓK, tE) event-stopped labels. Without a terminal
sufficient-class certificate, retaining the whole simulator state gives the safe product bound.
   If the complete stopped scored transcript, including mapped stopping time and premature commit-
ment, is within total variation ε of the target law uniformly over the declared models and policies,
then the bounded common-task risk changes by at most Lε. Two compatible simulators multiply their
continuing and terminal resource factors and add their deficiencies. Raw-call count and physical-time
transfer require separate actual cost or killed-potential certificates. Reverse risk inequalities require a
reverse simulator or an actual lower witness; terminal-law proximity alone does not provide them.
Proof. Before commitment retain the pair of target live role and continuing simulator state. After
commitment retain the pair of target output role and sufficient export class. Compatibility supplies
legal transitions and commands without an uncharged port or phase. Lemma 11.4 gives the two
packing counts. Serial composition retains triples; rebracketing yields the products K1 K2 and E1 E2 .
For a loss in [0, L], integration of its superlevel probabilities gives |EP ℓ − EQ ℓ| ≤ L∥P − Q∥TV . Total
                                   CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         163


variation contracts under a common Markov kernel, so the triangle inequality adds complete-law
deficiencies. Costs are generally unbounded functions of stopped paths and are not controlled by
this bounded-loss argument; sum their certified increments or use Lemma S39.6 instead.        □
  Combining Propositions S41.1 and S41.3 adds Lε to (S41.1) while charging the simulator’s states
and actual cost map. This composes approximation and simulation in one task metric. A bound on
an allowed error is not, without attained alternatives, an unavoidable lower bound. The following
construction supplies such alternatives on a single task.

A finite calibration acquisition with guarded stopping. The reference and task-order cer-
tificates above are not inferred automatically from finite data. Here is one precise finite-sample
certificate and its cost when the unknown quantities are the fixed probabilities of a physical command
sampler. It also explains why high confidence alone cannot certify almost-sure stopping.
Proposition S41.5 (Finite command calibration and a counted guard). Suppose a physical sampler
has a fixed unknown distribution (λj )Jj=1 throughout calibration and deployment, its draws are
independent, and deployment draws are independent of the current hidden input. The known target
probabilities are 1/J. Observe n independent calibration draws at cost ccal each, and let λ
                                                                                          b j be their
frequencies. Fix 0 < ζ < 1, and choose a certified rational bound
                                                   s
                                                       log(2J/ζ)
                                            rn ≥                 .
                                                           2n
Declare a relative certificate with parameter η < 1 only when all intervals [λ   b j − rn , λ
                                                                                            b j + rn ] lie in
[(1 − η)/J, (1 + η)/J]. The probability of a falsely declared certificate is at most ζ.
   On certificate failure, commit by a bounded-loss first-attempt fallback. On certificate success,
deploy the corresponding upper of Theorem 10.1, with design hazard 1/b, but force commitment
with a fixed decoder after at most T ≥ 1 productive attempts. The whole-attempt cost bound κ+ is
required whether or not the certificate is true. If the true sampler lies in a tighter relative band
η0 < η and 2rn ≤ (η − η0 )/J, then the entire guarded procedure commits almost surely and has
                                      ECtotal ≤ nccal + κ+ (b + ζT ),                                (S41.4)
                                                                 −T /b 
                                       Rtotal ≤ U + L 2ζ + e               ,                         (S41.5)
where U is the certified core upper. The calibration phase can be implemented with at most (n + 1)J
count-vector labels. Deployment uses a counted attempt index, multiplying its live-role bound by at
most T ; fallback and terminal roles are additional explicit roles. These resources may not be omitted
from an autonomous budget.
Proof. For a Bernoulli variable X, the log moment-generating function of X − EX has second
derivative at most 1/4 under every exponential tilt, and value and derivative zero at the origin.
Twice integrating gives log Eet(X−EX) ≤ t2 /8. Independence, the exponential Markov inequality
and optimization in t imply P(|λ  b j − λj | > r) ≤ 2e−2nr2 . A union bound proves that all confidence
intervals contain the true probabilities except on an event of probability at most ζ. Hence a false
certificate has probability at most ζ. If the tighter-band condition holds, this same simultaneous
event puts each empirical interval within the declared wider band, so rejection of a true certificate
also has probability at most ζ.
    On a true certificate, the conditional hazard proof bounds survival through T attempts by
(1 − 1/b)T ≤ e−T /b . The loss contributed by earlier acceptances is at most U ; the forced last
commitment adds at most Le−T /b . Expected attempts on this branch are at most b. On a certificate
failure the first-attempt fallback costs at most κ+ and loses at most L. On a falsely accepted
certificate the deterministic guard uses at most T attempts and has loss at most L, even if the
unguarded acceptance set has zero actual mass. Every attempt terminates almost surely by its finite
whole-attempt cost bound. Combining these cases gives (S41.4) and (S41.5), with the non-false
cases bounded by b ≥ 1 attempts and the false-acceptance case by ζT additional attempts.
   A count vector stores every calibration total, including the number of draws already taken, and
has at most (n + 1)J possible values. At total n, the threshold comparison may be performed in
charged scratch using a fixed certified finite arithmetic implementation; the resulting branch choice is
encoded in the selected live role. The counts can then be discarded because the deployed dictionary
is fixed for the nominal law and declared band, not fitted to the realized frequencies. The deployment
guard requires its own retained index with T values. This gives the asserted conservative finite-state
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       165


accounts. Without the tighter-band assumption one still has the false-certificate probability bound
and guarded termination, but no small rejection-risk bound; we do not omit that distinction. □
   This result controls one genuinely acquired calibration quantity, not arbitrary unknown-kernel
learning. It counts pilot observations, a physical fallback, the stopping guard and its memory.
It prevents a rare incorrect calibration certificate from being hidden inside an unjustified robust
almost-sure stopping assertion.

      S42. A predictive quotient constructed from positive-evidence histories
   The squared-task coordinates in (2.1) need not separate all future experiments. We therefore
give the predictive realization independently of those coordinates, for a fixed prepared law with its
declared legal test class. The theorem is a sufficient realization result and a detailed application of
compact selection; predictive minimality itself has classical antecedents [4, 5].
   Let H be the standard Borel space of finite visible histories on which specified conditional versions
are defined, embedded in a metrizable history carrier. Reports and resource records are included in
the history. Actions belong to a finite set, with Borel legal domains. Histories with different legal
continuations must be separated. Let qj (h) ∈ [0, 1] be a countable determining list of conditional
response coordinates for all finite open-loop future report words and bounded tests. Include legal-
domain indicators and the coordinates necessary to determine report and cost-record laws. The
list is closed in the following determining sense: the one-call prefixes of these future tests, localized
by a countable measure-determining family of current-report functions, are determined by the list.
This closure is a property of the declared executable tests, not a finite-moment assertion. Set
q(h) = (qj (h))j≥1 in [0, 1]N .
   The realization theorem in this appendix concerns one fixed prepared law, not an unknown-model
quotient oracle. Its compact-fiber selection step is a concrete instance of classical measurable
selection [32]; the explicit construction below checks its needed measurable domains instead of
assuming a selector.
                                                                                               S
Theorem S42.1 (Compact-exhausted executable predictive realization). Suppose H = n≥1 Cn
with Cn compact in its metrizable carrier and every qj continuous on each Cn . Assume the specified
response coordinates are the consistent conditional laws of the raw causal kernels and satisfy the
determining and continuation conditions above. Then the actual image S = q(H) is Borel and has a
Borel section s : S → H. Equality of q is exactly equality of all declared finite executable future laws,
including legal domains. The report and next-quotient law factor through q as a Borel kernel. This
factorization is modulo the common current-report null sets of each compared pair of histories; it is
not a pointwise claim on impossible reports.
   If another Borel statistic T determines the response coordinates by Borel maps qj = gj ◦ T , then
q = g ◦ T measurably, where g = (gj )j≥1 on the realized range. Thus q is minimal for these executable
tests. Equality extends to bounded stopped tests which terminate almost surely under both compared
histories, by finite truncation. Unbounded costs and infinite-horizon limits require their separate
integrability or convergence hypotheses.
Proof. The countable product is compact metrizable,
                                               S
                                                       and coordinate continuity makes q continuous
on every Cn . Thus q(Cn ) is compact and S = n q(Cn ) is Borel. A section can be constructed rather
than assumed. Given x ∈ S, first choose the least n for which x ∈ q(Cn ), a Borel decision. For
this compact Cn , choose finite closed covers at each scale, refining each previously chosen compact
cell, with diameters tending to zero. Empty cells are discarded. At each level choose the first child
compact cell D for which x ∈ q(D). Such a child exists because its parent covers the nonempty fiber;
the test is Borel because q(D) is compact. The chosen cells are nested compact sets with diameters
tending to zero and each intersects the fiber. Choose a fixed representative in each possible cell.
Their sequence is Borel in x and converges to a unique point of Cn . Continuity of q on Cn shows
166                                              QIAN QI


that this point maps to x. The countable piecewise construction in the least n gives the required
Borel section.
  Suppose q(h) = q(h′ ). The legal-domain coordinates give the same permitted next commands.
The determining current-report coordinates give the same next-report law Ra for a common legal
command a. For a future response coordinate qj and a member g of the determining current-report
function family, continuation consistency gives a conditional joint-test value
                                         Z
                                             g(y)qj (hay)Ra (dy).

The prefix closure and equality of q(h), q(h′ ) imply equality of this integral with the expression
using h′ ay. Uniqueness of finite measures on a determining family implies qj (hay) = qj (h′ ay) for
Ra -almost every y. Intersecting these sets over the countable coordinates gives q(hay) = q(h′ ay)
almost surely. Concatenations outside the positive-evidence history domain may be completed
arbitrarily, because they have zero report mass from the histories in question.
   Define the quotient report/update kernel by running the raw row from s(x) and pushing the
continued history through q. The section, the raw kernel and concatenation map are Borel, so this is
a Borel kernel. The preceding almost-sure equality shows that it agrees with the pushforward from
every representative of x, not just from s(x). Induction gives finite-prefix equality under a common
quotient policy. More generally finite adaptive policies depending on the visible future history are
handled by the same raw conditional continuation laws: with finitely many actions, sum over action
words and multiply their open-loop report laws by the measurable feedback probabilities of the
policy. Equality extends from bounded determining functions to Borel sets by the monotone-class
argument. The converse follows since each listed response is itself a declared test coordinate.
   For another statistic with the stated Borel response maps, the product map g is Borel and satisfies
q = g ◦ T ; the asserted minimality is precisely this factorization, not a finite-state claim. For a
stopped test truncate at time n with a distinguished surviving outcome. Its finite law is determined.
Almost-sure termination under both histories makes both surviving probabilities tend to zero, so
the bounded stopped law agrees. This limit does not control expected stopping cost without an
additional bound.                                                                                   □
Proposition S42.2 (Primitive positive-evidence patches). The continuity and exhaustion conditions
of Theorem S42.1 hold for the following raw class. Hidden-state and report coordinates lie in compact
metric spaces, the action set is finite, and primitive transition–report kernels have bounded continuous
densities with respect to fixed finite reference measures; the preparation is fixed. A finite-dimensional
positive instrument whose matrix entries depend continuously on compact report coordinates is
another such class. Finite physical phases are included as discrete coordinates, with continuous
legal-domain indicators on the corresponding patches. Only histories of positive prepared likelihood
are included.
Proof. Fix a finite action word and history length. Iterated integration of products of bounded
continuous primitive densities gives a continuous nonnegative history likelihood p(h). The same
statement holds for the numerator of a conditional finite future test whose report test function
is continuous; the integration domains are fixed compact spaces with finite measures. Uniform
boundedness on each finite product and dominated convergence justify continuity. In the matrix case
these quantities are traces of products of continuous instrument maps followed by finite integration,
with the same conclusion.
   The sets where p(h) ≥ 1/n are compact and exhaust the positive-likelihood histories for this
action word. On each, division by p(h) is continuous and all conditional response coordinates are
continuous. Countably many lengths, finitely many action words per length, and countably many
n give the compact exhaustion. On a compact metric report space there is a countable uniformly
dense family of continuous functions; include their finite products and all finite action words. Their
integrals determine finite report laws, and adjoining finite prefixes gives the required determining
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                      167


closure. Costs and legal phases must be included among the stipulated continuous or discrete
observable coordinates. The probability of a zero-likelihood report history is zero under the prepared
law. The construction does not claim that the likelihood has a uniform positive lower bound over
the whole experiment.                                                                               □
   The proposition is about continuity of raw finite-word responses. It does not assume a pre-existing
posterior manifold or a covariance geometry, and it does not give a finite algorithm for discovering
all quotient classes. An unknown-model family of separate posterior laws is not automatically one
executable posterior. Applying this appendix uniformly to a model family would require a common
determining response construction and compatible null-set/regularity conditions; the finite-state
upper in the main theorem instead obtains its uniformity from the explicitly checked task-order
program and stores no such family.

                     S43. Sharp boundaries of the proved comparisons
  The following witnesses isolate distinct hypotheses. They are mathematical counterexamples to
specific uncharged inferences, not a replacement of the main theorem by an impossibility statement.
Proposition S43.1 (Absolute task error can be amplified by selection). For every b ≥ 1 and
0 ≤ e ≤ 1/b, there are positive prepared and actual joint laws with identical report marginals and
one-call absolute squared-task defect at most e, whose optimal stopped risks at expected call budget b
differ by be, even with one output value. The difference occurs in either direction. A fixed relative
task order is therefore strictly stronger than a small absolute task defect near a zero-loss component.
Proof. Draw an independent uniform report x ∈ [0, 1] at every call and let D have mass 1/b. All
audits have conditional mean zero. Off D, both laws  √ have a fresh fair-sign audit of magnitude one.
On D, the comparison has a fair sign of magnitude be and the actual law has audit zero. R    For every
q ∈ [0, 1] and finite-image readout r(x), their unnormalized squared losses differ by be D q(x)dx ≤ e.
The comparison residual variance is everywhere at least be, so every committing strategy has risk at
least be; selecting D and outputting zero attains it with expected cost b. The actual law attains zero
by the same procedure. Reversing the laws proves the other direction. A single event-stopped label
can reject at itself and commit with a final event; a state-stopped realization uses an additional live
label. On D a task zero under one law is positive under the other, so no two-sided relative order
with a positive lower factor can hold.                                                               □
   A report-only comparison also fails: a constant mark can have the same marginal under two laws
while an additional visible bit reveals a fair audit sign. A two-output decoder then has zero loss,
although a profile formed from the constant mark alone has residual variance one. The full-report
conditional moments in (2.3) exclude this omission.
   There is also an all-program witness for the necessity of more than report balance. Take the
three admitted models θ ∈ {−1, 0, 1}, let every precommit report be the same constant, and let
the unseen audit at commitment be Z = θ. There is one legal productive command, costing one,
and no command changes θ. The actual comparison member θ = 0 has Qm (b) = 0. Nevertheless
the distribution of every committed readout R is identical under θ = ±1, because all precommit
laws, including stopping and coins, are identical. The average of their risks is 1 + ER2 ≥ 1, and
output zero attains worst-case risk one. This holds for every almost-surely terminating policy, at
every feasible budget and every dictionary size. Report masses match exactly; the missing first task
moment is the obstruction. It is not repaired by choosing a different causal rejection rule.
   A live-state count alone cannot justify a terminal-width subtraction. In the two-port interface,
use labels 0, 1 with live commands gate and productive respectively, and decoder values −1, +1.
Gate failure continues at 0, success at 1; a rejected productive report returns to 0. A productive
commitment emits a final event with label 0 or 1 according to its chosen output. Two live and two
168                                            QIAN QI


output roles have used only two labels. The state-stopped model cannot share those roles because
its halt map is intrinsic. This is the exact semantic repair retained from the earlier work.
   Finally, bounded-law discrepancy is not a cost certificate. A source procedure can agree with a
target terminal output with probability one while performing an independent preliminary waiting
time of arbitrarily large finite mean, or of infinite mean. Terminal total variation is then zero and
expected cost is uncontrolled. Our simulator definition includes the scored stopping information
when its law is compared, and still requires a separate expected-cost certificate because time is
unbounded. Likewise, neither fixed-time equivalence nor finite-dimensional positive matrix identities
imply large-deviation equivalence, unrestricted optimal-control equivalence, or closure of unbounded
operators.

What is and is not generalized. The central theorem generalizes exact and absolute-calibrated
causal profile transfer to a common affine task order and derives its all-scale relative and stratified
consequences. The positive-matrix characterization concerns localized task order; the quotient
construction concerns compact-exhausted continuous-response experiments; the pilot concerns
a fixed independent command sampler. These results do not establish necessity of a common
acquisition program for every causal experiment, nor the minimal possible simulator state under
all implementations, nor matching lower bounds for program length, scratch and arithmetic time.
Those stronger mathematical questions are distinct from the fully stated sufficient-class theorem
proved here. The general theorem does not depend on a conclusion taken from either realization.
The retained nonintervening sensing, occupation-flow, exact role and absolute-defect results remain
substantive independent parts of the complete companion.

                        S44. The scope of the strengthened theory
   The boundaries in Appendix S43 concern the inherited causal transfer theorem. The present
synthesis theorems add necessary-and-sufficient conclusions on explicitly stated classes; they do not
turn that sufficient transfer theorem into a classification of all standard Borel causal experiments.
   The task-defect theorem is exact for a fixed selected loss family, its declared decision carrier, and
one global finite decoder. Its occupation version holds for an actual occupation measure supplied by
a competitor. Neither assertion manufactures that measure or an acquisition program. The scalar
squared-task order is strictly weaker than the affine-square matrix order; the latter remains an
exact characterization of its own larger test family. The Bregman comparison preserves squared-loss
exponents only under the stated curvature bounds.
   The finite-obstruction hierarchy characterizes one common full-history program for finitely
branching instruments, bounded terminal scores and a strictly positive call-cost floor. It permits
arbitrarily many operating models, but finite testing of an arbitrary uncountable family is not a
decision procedure for its infinite synthesis problem. Compactness produces a policy, not a uniformly
bounded number of live labels. Its finite dual concerns one specified horizon and finite model
set. Fallback completion requires a genuinely legal common continuation and charges its cost and
memory. The current supplement treats deadlocks, restricted preparation faces and the distinction
between small survival mass and small expected cost tails.
   The finite-memory certificate is intrinsic to a fixed finite-label observer on a declared time-
homogeneous interface. Its projections and potential matrices are proof certificates, not stored
observer variables. The finite algebraic decision conclusion requires finitely many models, algebraic
primitive data and a semialgebraic decision carrier. It does not assert efficient synthesis, rational
optimal probabilities, an exact finite-bit implementation of arbitrary algebraic randomization, or
optimal program, scratch and arithmetic lower bounds. Exact initial-state uniformity does not
replace a missing certificate at later roots of a history-dependent experiment. Returning from a
balancing subroutine must be compiled at the current productive report before its prospective audit,
as specified in the supplement.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                        169


   The two-detector theorem has no balancing group and no free reset. It characterizes all integer
classical live-memory budgets for two projectors, unit call costs, first-success commitment and
no other physical command. The maximum principal-angle cosine is the worst spectral block of
that model, not a universal rank or memory exponent. The initial-state saddle at unrestricted
memory is proved from the physical instrument. The exact two-label minimax gap shows that a
single preparation need not supply a sharp robust converse after imposing a nonconvex controller
restriction. It does not imply such a gap for every memory-constrained experiment. Randomized
initial labels are a separately stated interface, not an uncharged repair of a fixed-start observer.
   All inherited valid results remain either in the native main inputs, the complete preceding
companion, or their pinned independent realization lineages. In particular, the ordered-measurement
local geometry and allocation theory, exact two-stage reset and message-discrimination theory, rank
constraints and spectral variational values are not asserted to follow from the detector example.
Conversely the current article does not abandon them because its general structural results have
become central. The accompanying preservation and dependency maps identify their locations and
prevent a change of research identity from being hidden in a reorganization.
   Open mathematical goals remain: an intrinsic classification beyond finite branching; minimal
memory under arbitrary resource-certified simulators; a broad nonintervening controlled-sensing
application beyond the retained directional model; matching computational-resource lower bounds;
and the independent unbounded-operator, large-deviation and wider A1/A2 pipeline targets. These
are not hypotheses standing in for a missing proof of a theorem stated here. The present proofs
establish their displayed conclusions with their displayed quantifiers. Finite diagnostics test particular
algebraic identities, boundary cases and implementations; they are not formal proofs, independent
expert assessment or an editorial decision on novelty and significance.
   The report-resolved continuation theorem is exact for linear marked task equalities of a supplied
time-homogeneous finite-return routine. It does not identify the reachable positive cone with every
positive matrix on its support, nor replace a one-sided reachable cost bound by an unsupported
operator inequality. The reset-instrument classification applies to failure branches which physically
prepare known states and to first-success commitment. It includes arbitrary finite rank-one families,
but not arbitrary quantum instruments or an arbitrary-rank projection without input-erasing failure.
The zero-risk spectrum is exact for the acquired profile; its physical threshold has the same converse
and implementation hypotheses as the exact transfer theorem. These distinctions separate the new
structural results from the independent wider research goals.
   The primitive finite-model route in Section S25 removes the reset and reference assumptions on
its own declared interface. It supplies a common fallback, monotone two-sided approximations to
the least feasible cost, and bounded-risk approximations at every strictly interior feasible budget
with counted finite implementations. It does not replace the preparation-free all-initial-state
quantifier by an arbitrary restricted preparation set, and it assumes finite models, finite globally
legal commands/reports, time homogeneity and bounded positive call costs. It is not unknown-kernel
statistical learning, a universal closed formula for the comparison profile, or optimal computational
complexity. The six-regime diagram solves one explicit mixed-strata comparator with its original
asymptotic qualification intact.
   The compact-family route extends the primitive finite-model risk approximation to a continuously
parameterized compact uncertainty class on a common finite globally legal physical interface. It
controls model error and restores the same budget at every N > Nmin . Its algebraic specialization is
a terminating approximate synthesis result for supplied exact primitive data, not learning from finite
data, polynomial-time synthesis or a solution of the boundary finite-memory problem. The model
stays fixed during a run. Exact geometric reference-profile realization, arbitrary unknown-model
quotient construction and minimal computational resources remain separate mathematical questions.
The examples showing boundary discontinuity and failure of vertex-only certification delimit the
positive theorem rather than replace it.
170                                               QIAN QI


   The current optimality layer distinguishes three assertions. Boundary attainment and complete
performed-law compactness use the shared finite-branching tree, positive cost floor and bounded
terminal loss, with a fixed dictionary for total variation. Finite spectral witnesses additionally
require the compact continuous primitive family and strict prefix feasibility. Same-budget finite-
label approximation uses a real common fallback with strict cost slack. Hard optimized output
width is not convexified, and several expected resources require a jointly safe fallback. The timing
application assumes a stateless single-command device with no free clock; its exact attainment count
is not a universal lower for devices that report time. None of these theorems supplies an arbitrary
unknown-model quotient or a general polynomial-time synthesis method.

One may choose Pe = P . On that least support, finite-cost proper operation from the preparation
