trace by max(|αj |, |βj |) Pr(τ > n), which vanishes. Its partial sum contains the actual disjoint
terminal-score contributions. Thus the initial compressed block is exactly Aj .
   Conversely the cost bound on every initial density in the face gives k≥0 ⟨g, T k X0 ⟩ ≤ N rank Q.
                                                                        P

The same series starting at T n X0 is a nonnegative tail of that series. Summing for 0 ≤ n < D
yields a finite bound DN rank Q on the series starting at R. Since R is strictly positive on its
support, every positive supported matrix is bounded by a finite multiple of R. Thus the increasing
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       171


partial cost potentials on that support have bounded norm and converge. Their limit H min solves
H min − TP∗ H min = P gP and has the required compressed initial budget.
   The floor implies norm convergence of k (TP∗ )k P . Positivity bounds the iterates of any supported
                                          P

Hermitian matrix by its norm times the iterates of P , so every such iterate tends to zero. The
same holds for all complex matrices by decomposing into Hermitian parts. In finite dimension this
forces every eigenvalue of TP∗ to have modulus less than one, proving transience and the Neumann
formula. The bounded-score series also converges. Its normalization bounds follow from telescoping
the terminal effect P − TP∗ P , whose series sums to P . The initial face identity follows from the
assumed terminal scores. Finally iteration shows that every positive superpotential dominates every
partial exact cost potential, hence their limit.                                                    □
   An arbitrary preparation set is not automatically its whole support face. For equality of task
expectations, its linear span, not its support face, controls what is required. The main manuscript’s
report-resolved continuation theorem treats that distinction. Extending a preparation family to its
full face strengthens the quantifier and must be declared explicitly.

                        S46. Legal physical domains and deadlocks
   A physical command can have a restricted domain. A time-homogeneous face-valued domain
is represented by projections Da on the physical space: command a is permitted only on states
supported on Da . The controller is not given that support or a free physical-port tag. Let Ps be
the controller’s least reached physical support at label s, computed from the allowed preparations
and the actual continuing instrument.
Proposition S46.1 (Fixed-row legality on reached supports). A command row ps,a is legal at every
reached history if and only if
                                 ps,a > 0 =⇒ Ps ≼ Da
for every label and command. Equivalently ps,a (I − Da )Ps = 0. For a finite algebraic model family
with face domains, these are polynomial conditions on the shared row probabilities and modelwise
support projections. They may be added to the finite-memory potential system without giving the
controller an extra domain input.
Proof. If the support inclusion holds, every reached positive state at label s is supported on Da , so
the command is legal. Conversely, legality at every reached positive state annihilates all of them by
I − Da whenever ps,a > 0. Their positive sum over a spanning finite set of reached supports has
support Ps ; hence I − Da also annihilates Ps . This gives the inclusion. The scalar multiplication
formula handles zero row probabilities without introducing a division or a nonclosed strict-positivity
condition. It is polynomial in real and imaginary matrix coordinates. Apply it separately in each
model, retaining the same row probabilities.                                                        □
   This proposition concerns declared support-face domains. A legal domain specified by a different
predicate on states need not admit this polynomial encoding. If a protocol port is already a
deterministic function of the visible history, a full-history policy can recover it, but a finite-label
controller must store enough information to choose a legal row without a free port input.
   For the full-history finite-obstruction hierarchy, the main manuscript assumes a nonempty legal
command set at every open decision node. Deadlocks can instead be admitted explicitly as forbidden
nonproductive leaves. A node with no legal decision must carry zero reached mass. For a finite set
of models and finite horizon, impose the corresponding positive-operator equality for each such leaf
on the admitted preparation face. In the common realization plan it is the equality
                                               ∗
                                       Qθ γ(h)Iθ,h (I) Qθ = 0.
Here γ(h) excludes physical report probabilities, as in the main manuscript.
172                                             QIAN QI


Proposition S46.2 (Deadlock-aware finite hierarchy). With finitely branching legal histories, a
common initial preparation face in each finite-dimensional model, a positive call-cost floor and
bounded terminal scores, the finite-intersection proof of the causal synthesis hierarchy remains valid
after adding the deadlock zero-mass constraints. Constraints at depth T include every deadlocked
leaf reached by that depth. An open nondeadlocked leaf still owes at least one further paid call. A
deadlocked leaf cannot be used as a fictitious terminal output or a free fallback.
Proof. Give an empty decision node a singleton dummy coordinate solely to make the product of
nodewise controller coordinate spaces compact. This coordinate causes no physical action and is
constrained to have zero reached mass in each admitted model. It is not a legal executed command.
All finite-prefix mass, cost and score expressions remain continuous in the product coordinates.
Deadlock constraints are closed and remain true under extension to later horizons. Restricting a later
feasible prefix therefore preserves them and the usual cost reserve and score intervals. Conversely,
every proper legal policy satisfies them because it never reaches a deadlock with positive probability.
   For any finite collection of model, horizon and deadlock constraints, choose its largest horizon and
union of model indices. Feasibility at that level gives one common prefix satisfying the collection.
Compactness gives an infinite common coordinate assignment satisfying all levels. No dummy
decision is executed with positive probability. The same positive-cost survival bound tends to
zero, so the resulting process commits almost surely without reaching a deadlock, and monotone
convergence supplies its budget. Bounded terminal-score tails recover its targets. This repeats the
hierarchy argument with the missing legal-boundary event explicitly accounted for.                    □

              S47. Prospective audits and post-report return compilation
   A routine’s logical return and the experiment’s irrevocable halt are different events. Let its final
productive call produce a visible report y. At this report the routine selects a return mark x and a
finite exported decoder choice, using current coins. Its prospective audit instrument defines the
score that would be obtained if the outer caller accepted. Those scores do not require executing the
audit on a rejected branch.
Proposition S47.1 (Compilation without a free audit or hidden mode). Suppose the caller’s
acceptance probability and decoder are functions only of the current finite return mark and the
existing finite decoder dictionary. Suppose the physical rejection operation is legal and either executes
within the current productive call or has an explicitly counted finite-state return subroutine. Then
the routine can be compiled into an event-stopped observer by replacing each logical return transition
by its acceptance and rejection branches. On acceptance it emits the outer halt event and retains
the decoder label; on rejection it executes the declared return and reenters the existing start label.
Prospective audit statistics on accepted branches, complete physical paths and charged costs agree
with this construction. The state-stopped implementation uses disjoint live and final-output types
instead.
Proof. At the current productive report, first sample exactly the routine’s original return choice.
Sample the caller’s independent acceptance coin next. On acceptance, emit the outer commitment
event, store its dictionary value and only then perform the declared scored audit. On rejection
do not perform that audit; follow the declared rejection instrument and reenter the start. This
reproduces each branch’s raw physical instrument and its unnormalized accepted audit measure.
Iteration gives equality of every finite stopped prefix and every raw path cost. Properness is supplied
by the positive conditional-hazard argument, not by the compilation alone.
   All information used to decide the branch is available during the current call. No value of the
future audit is read. If the return needs another physical call, its phase persists and is stored in
the added return-subroutine labels; its cost is included. Otherwise the existing start label suffices
for continuation, and the event-stopped live/output packing introduces no continuing mode bit. A
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                     173


state-stopped implementation sends accepted branches to disjoint halt-typed labels. This proves the
assertions while keeping the different observer models distinct.                                 □

   A synthesized terminal score list alone is not enough to prove this proposition’s hypotheses. In
particular, a score obtained only by actually measuring and revealing the audit cannot be treated
as an unobserved prospective score on rejection. Likewise a kernel identity at the first routine root
cannot substitute for its report-resolved continuation certificate. The current main manuscript
supplies that additional finite test for time-homogeneous finite-return routines.


                        S48. Survival mass is not expected cost tail
Proposition S48.1 (The exact limitation of a mean-cost bound). For a positive-integer stopping
time with Eτ ≤ b, one has
                             Pr(τ > T ) ≤ min{1, (b − 1)/T }     (T ≥ 1).
This does not imply a uniform vanishing bound on E[(τ − T )+ ] over the same budget class. In
particular the main manuscript’s score-tail convergence, which uses bounded scores, cannot be
repurposed as an expected cost-tail convergence statement.

Proof. Pointwise τ ≥ 1 + T 1{τ >T } , which gives the probability bound. Fix b > 1. For an integer
K ≥ b, set τ = K with probability (b − 1)/(K − 1) and τ = 1 otherwise. This is a finite-branching,
unit-call-cost terminating experiment and Eτ = b. At a fixed cutoff T < K,
                                                 K −T
                       E[(τ − T )+ ] = (b − 1)        −→ b − 1    (K −→ ∞).
                                                 K −1
Thus its supremum over the budget class never tends to zero with T . The survival probability at
that same cutoff can tend to zero. Bounded terminal scores are controlled by the latter probability;
unbounded remaining call cost is not.                                                             □

  The finite-prefix fallback theorem uses a conditional cost bound on an actual fallback, not
an unproved uniform tail-cost estimate. For a fixed finite-state transient instrument its own
supported resolvent gives a finite tail-cost bound, but its constants need not be uniform over a
family approaching the boundary of transience.


                   S49. Correction boundary and preserved companion
   The single-atom proposition inherited by the current manuscript is corrected only in its necessary-
threshold quantifier. The statement that its specified atom yields zero loss when bλ ≥ 1 remains
valid, as do its lower and upper comparisons with the remainder profile. Necessity based on that
atom alone requires positive residual variance almost everywhere on the remainder, or another
condition excluding all other finite-dictionary zero-loss mass. The current main theorem gives the
complete criterion through the largest atomic masses of the zero-residual predictive measure, and
an explicit two-atom counterexample shows why the qualification matters.
   The byte-preserved R58 native companion has its own older statements and provenance. Retention
is not a claim that its historical wording supersedes a correction in the current manuscript. The
preservation map identifies the current controlling result for every corrected claim. The original
acquired-law geometry, absolute-calibration proofs, nonintervening Gaussian sensing and exact stop-
ping constructions remain available in that companion, and the independent ordered-measurement,
reset-communication and rank-constrained discrimination developments remain at their pinned
source identities.
174                                              QIAN QI


             S50. The primitive frontier: budget mixing and limiting scopes
   This section supports the current primitive-instrument theorem. Its baseline is not a hidden-state
reset model: a reported state may retain all dependence on the unknown input. The fixed fallback
must be valid from every initial state in every one of the finitely many declared models.

Lemma S50.1 (A finite mixture without an uncounted persistent seed). Let two finite-label observers
share the same physical command/report interface and fixed terminal dictionary. If their live-label
sets have sizes n1 , n2 , a mixture chosen with one fixed probability at initialization is realizable with
at most 1 + n1 + n2 live labels and the same terminal dictionary. Its expected cost and terminal-loss
operators are the corresponding convex combinations in every model. Event stopping permits the
usual live/output reuse; state stopping uses disjoint output labels.

Proof. Use an initial label whose command row mixes the two initial command rows with the
prescribed probability. At this first call sample the branch and command jointly using fresh private
randomness. On the current report, either commit according to the sampled branch or enter its
tagged continuing label. Subsequently all transitions stay inside that branch’s counted live-label
set. If a transition is specified only by a kernel conditioned on the executed command and report,
integrate out the branch coin: for a fixed executed command its posterior branch weights depend
on the two command probabilities, but not on the physical state, since the report instrument for
that command is identical in both branches. Zero command probabilities are assigned arbitrary
unused conditional rows. This is a legal post-report kernel, not a persistent external random tape.
Coupling with the explicit within-call coin proves equality of every path law and its nonnegative
costs. The same retained decoder is used in both branches, and the halt convention gives the two
total-label counts.                                                                               □

Proposition S50.2 (Why the uniform qualifications cannot be omitted). Modelwise properness does
not imply a uniform finite-cost fallback in an infinite uncertainty class, even in physical dimension
one. A robustly minimax initial command row also need not be pointwise optimal for each known
initial state.

Proof. For the first claim take one command and models indexed by integers n ≥ 1, with productive
probability 1/n and unit call cost. Every individual model terminates almost surely at finite mean
n, but every legal observer must wait for the first productive event or longer. Its worst-model mean
is infinite. The finite-model theorem instead takes a finite maximum of strictly positive termination
margins.
   For the second claim take the two orthogonal rank-one failure projections P = |0⟩⟨0| and Q = |1⟩⟨1|
with unit calls and immediate first-success commitment. Fair first choice followed by switching
after failure has robust value 3/2. At the known initial state |0⟩, calling the detector with failure
projection Q succeeds at cost one, while the fair-first policy has mean 3/2. Thus one uniform
minimax row is not a simultaneous pointwise optimizer. The current reset-control statement makes
this distinction explicit; the complete R61 wording remains in the retained original.               □


   The risk approximation does not bound the expected cost remaining in an arbitrary original
policy after a cutoff. It executes a different, uniformly cost-bounded fallback, and hence bounds
the new remaining cost by κ times survival mass. For budget N > κ, mixing with this fallback
restores the original budget exactly. At N = κ that mixing argument has no positive cost slack;
only the stated enlarged-budget conclusion follows unless the fallback itself costs the one-call floor.
The absence of a same-budget conclusion there is a limit of this proof, not a no-go assertion about
the optimal boundary policy.
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                         175


               S51. Finite diagnostic experiments accompanying the proof
   The retained R62 diagnostics include two separate classical finite-prefix programs. One is binary
costly observation with a hidden fixed bit and two possible noise levels, using a common decision
plan for both models and both initial bits. The other is a heralded experiment with nonproductive
reports and two possible productive-report laws. Their linear programs implement the common
sequence weights, terminal loss and open-path cost reserve directly. An intentional removal of
the reserve at one unit of budget produces a false zero-loss open prefix; the correct constraint
excludes it. Exact finite checks separately test the sharp nilpotent detection depth, a noncommuting
two-projection fallback resolvent and the budget-restoring probability. Neither the numerical linear
programs nor the finite examples prove the semidefinite statement for all dimensions.
   The six-regime phase calculation is the exact solution of the explicitly displayed comparator,
which estimates the acquired risk only up to the inherited geometric constants. Numerical checks
compare it with an independently optimized scalar convex objective. The two nonsmooth intervals
are not replaced by a differentiable surrogate. The plotted curve is not fitted to data or presented
as an exact universal physical risk curve.

         S52. Certified finite approximations for compact model uncertainty
   The current compact-family theorem compares raw report-resolved instruments, not only final-
output distributions. In this section use the same common finite interface, bounds c∗ , c∗ , L, model-
cover errors ϵI , ϵg , ϵF , horizon T and uniform fallback cost κ < N as in the main manuscript. The
fallback must work from every current physical state of every actual model. A returned numeric
optimizer value, by itself, is neither a valid all-model upper nor a proved lower value.
Proposition S52.1 (A posteriori certificate with rigorously bounded solver errors). Suppose a
normalized common finite realization plan has certified bounds
               Cϕ,T + c∗ Sϕ,T ≼ (N + s)I,       Vϕ,T ≼ (q + r)I    (ϕ ∈ F ),       s, r ≥ 0.
Its normalization and positive weights are exact; s, r may be proved interval bounds on constraint
residuals. Put
                         D = min{1, ((N + s)/c∗ − 1)/T },
                                               N +s
                         e = s + (κ − c∗ )D +        [ϵg + ϵI (c∗ (T − 1) + κ)],
                                                 c∗
                                          N +s
                         z = r + LD + L         ϵI + ϵF .
                                            c∗
Completion by the actual fallback, followed by the counted initial mixture of weight (N −κ)/(N −κ+e),
gives a proper common observer of uniform expected cost at most N and risk at most
                                   min{L, q + z + Le/(N − κ + e)}.
If a separate exact dual certificate gives a lower value q for the finite-model problem at budget N , then
q is also a lower bound for the fixed-dictionary full-family risk. For a decision net subtract its uniform
rounding modulus from that lower. A feasible approximate primal value q is not automatically such
a lower certificate.
Proof. The proved source budget N + s bounds source prefix occupation by (N + s)/c∗ and open
mass by D. Completing the same plan on the source adds at most (κ − c∗ )D to its reserved cost.
The occupation-sensitive comparison in the main manuscript bounds the discrepancy to any covered
target by the displayed instrument and cost term, so the actual completed cost is at most N + e. Its
loss is at most q + z, using the source upper q + r and at most L on the open terminal mass. Both
bounds are uniform in the initial density matrix. The fallback alone costs at most κ and has loss at
176                                            QIAN QI


most L. Its stated initial mixture therefore costs at most N ; the same convex calculation gives the
risk upper. All calls, return phases and branch memory are those of the compiled observer.
   For the lower, every full-family budget-N policy restricts to a feasible finite-model prefix with
no greater nonnegative loss. Weak duality for an exactly feasible finite dual therefore gives q no
greater than every such policy’s worst loss. The rounding argument for one common decoder net
adds only the modulus at its actual single terminal event. Nothing in this proof converts numerical
feasibility tolerances into exact probability normalization or a numerical objective into a rigorous
dual certificate.                                                                                 □

S52.1. Size of one certified construction. Write b = ay for the maximum command–report
branching factor and assume b ≥ 2. At horizon T , the number of pre-call node roles is at most

scalar weights. Apart from normalization and nonnegativity, the finite cover problem uses two

a data-fitted unknown uniformity claim.
   For the compact algebraic class, the main manuscript instead supplies a fully quantified polynomial
procedure. Common finite-prefix probabilities and one decoder tuple are variables; model parameters
are universally quantified in the cost and loss inequalities. Real-algebraic elimination can produce
the values and witnesses, and the explicit cutoff error tells when to stop. This does not turn a
quantified problem into an ordinary finite-grid semidefinite problem. The endpoint N = Nmin need
not be decided by the strict-feasibility search. Algebraic probabilities are exact descriptions in the
stated ideal sampler model, not proofs of a bounded-bit physical randomization implementation or
optimal arithmetic cost.

S52.2. Independent finite diagnostics for the compact theorem. The new diagnostic program
builds four common finite-prefix linear programs for a noisy observation of an unknown fixed bit,
with three noise models and both initial bits. After numerical optimization, it recovers rational
primal and dual vectors and checks their feasibility and equality of objective values using exact
rational arithmetic. The same recovered plan is completed and budget-mixed at finitely many
off-grid rational noise parameters. The analytic cover radius and the main theorem, not the sampled
off-grid points, supply the continuum implication.
   A separate check constructs normalized noncommuting qubit instruments and compares inde-
pendently computed forward path sums with backward cost, survival and loss operators. The
occupation-sensitive inequalities are tested in operator order on these finite examples, not only at one
pure input. Exact negative controls cover boundary risk discontinuity, the required remaining-horizon
                                      CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                     177


cost term, failure of vertex-only certification even for affine primitive data, and the distinction
between a fixed unknown model and a switching adversary. These diagnostics cannot prove the
theorems in all dimensions, but their failures would expose implementation or formula inconsistencies.
Their actual counts and environment are emitted by the executable, rather than inserted into the
mathematical proof.

   We give the finite recursion used by the main article’s primal–dual theorem. Its cost atoms are

At a compulsory terminal report omit the continuation alternative. At an impossible word all

Proposition S53.1 (Exact slack accounting for a common finite tree). Let γ(h, a) be a plan’s
sequence weight before the physical report and let γ(h, a, y, r) and γ(h, a, y, c) be its commitment and
continuation weights. The difference between its scalarized reserved objective and U (∅) − λN equals
the sum of its nonnegative local Bellman slacks, weighted by these sequence weights. In particular
every positive-weight alternative must have zero slack at equality. The matrices in the recursion are
analytic data used to choose a single common tree, not an observer-owned model register.
Proof. For a command choice the slack is a(h, a) + y W (h, a, y) − U (h) ≥ 0. For a productive
                                                                 P

commitment it is b(h, a, y, r) − W (h, a, y) ≥ 0, and for continuation it is U (hay) − W (h, a, y) ≥ 0;
the latter is zero when continuation is forced. Multiply by the corresponding sequence weights
and sum over the tree. The common flow equalities cancel every internal occurrence of U and W .
At the root its incoming weight is one. At a depth-T open leaf the remaining term is exactly the
reserved cost; at a terminal leaf it is exactly its weighted task loss. Thus the uncancelled terms are
the claimed objective difference. Finiteness makes all sums literal finite identities. A finite sum of
nonnegative terms vanishes exactly when every term with positive coefficient vanishes.               □
   If several physical atoms assign zero probability to a reached word, no division by those probabilities
is taken. This is why a common unnormalized recursion remains valid with changing supports. A
dual-minimizing deterministic tree may have large cost or risk in an individual model. It is not a
178                                            QIAN QI


primal optimal strategy unless the original robust constraints and complementary slackness are also
verified.

                  S54. Conic compression and what a certificate proves
  The sparse bound uses affine coordinates on the plan polytope, not the number of tree variables
before flow reduction. At an optimal feasible x∗ , each active risk gradient is augmented by 1 and
each active cost gradient by 0. Their conic combination is (g, 1), with g in the negative normal
cone.
P
         If the positive support has more than k + 1 generators, choose a nonzero linear dependence
  i ti a i = 0. Reverse its sign if necessary so some ti > 0, and replace positive coefficients wi by
                                                               !
                                                          wj
                                       wi′ = wi −   min            ti .
                                                    tj >0 tj

All new coefficients are nonnegative and at least one vanishes. The augmented vector is unchanged,
so the risk mass remains one. There is no requirement to preserve the old total cost-multiplier mass:
all cost atoms are active at x∗ , and the strict feasible point bounds the new mass by L/(N − κ).
Iteration proves finite support. This is the classical conic Carathéodory mechanism, applied to the
specified common-plan constraints.
   The certificate therefore has four logically separate components: a robustly feasible plan, active
physical risk and cost preparations, a common-tree minimum of the scalarized expression, and a
paid completion if the object being certified is an infinite stopped experiment. Exact arithmetic
may verify each finite identity. A numerical optimizer which only reports convergence verifies none
of the universal model inequalities by that fact alone. For a semialgebraic compact model set, the
universal inequalities have the real-algebraic description given in the main article; a numerical grid
is not substituted for them.

       S55. Boundary compactness is not compactness of occupation measures
   The common infinite-plan space is compact in its countable product coordinates. The bounded-
cost subset is closed because every finite cost reserve is continuous modelwise. The terminal loss is
bounded, so its tail is uniformly at most L(N/c∗ − 1)/T . These facts prove boundary attainment
and increasing finite lower convergence. They do not prove continuity of total cost in that topology.
The earlier example with a small probability of a very long run has bounded mean while retaining a
nonvanishing expected cost tail. Thus the map from a strategic plan to its full occupation measure
need not be continuous. Occupation-measure compactness and uniform absorption have their own
hypotheses in the existing theory; the argument here neither assumes them silently nor claims to
improve that theory in arbitrary Borel control spaces.
   For the boundary theorem, each finite tree has finitely many actions and reports. Its countable
product is compact metrizable, which is why a subsequence suffices in that proof. This is not a
replacement of the older profile’s weak-star compactness argument by an unjustified sequential
argument. Optimized output tuples lie in compact metric Dm and use a common decision modulus.
A hard output-width class is not convex in general; randomizing two dictionaries does not preserve
its width.

   The new weighted theorem applies beyond model-independent report        √ words.

For a fixed prepared state the maximum cost occurs at the upper-right corner, where every finite
unary word has positive mass. Thus the cost-support coverage holds. This example verifies the
enlarged interface directly and does not replace its theorem by a simulation.
   For a prescribed stochastic observer with transitions Qy , its block killed map retains the same total
physical failure mass. Indeed summing the output memory blocks removes Qy by row normalization.
Iteration proves the uniform survival bound on positive block inputs. Its score potential is therefore
the unique bounded solution of the main article’s equation (S27.7); the first block evaluated on
the prepared density is the actual performed risk. In contrast, a finite Hankel factorization with
negative transition entries has no such probability interpretation.

                   S57. Scope of exact algebraic memory certification
   The finite test in Proposition S27.7 fixes a desired probability response, not merely finitely many
empirical samples. Its linear representation is an input identity valid for all words. Equality at
words through length M + r − 1 implies equality everywhere by the invariant-span argument; finite
tests on arbitrary fitted data do not carry that conclusion. The number of tested words is at most
PM +r−1
  j=0     |Y |j , each coordinate equality has polynomial degree at most M + r, and the unknown
controller has |Y |M 2 + M |C| entries before row-sum elimination. These are finite syntactic bounds,
not an efficient bound for quantifier elimination.
   For the damped qubit the polygon construction supplies a finite search bound, with algebraic
vertices and algebraic barycentric weights for algebraic α. Real-algebraic feasibility can therefore
return an algebraic minimal controller. Its transition-table description, the encodings of those
algebraic numbers, and the resources of exact sampling are additional to ⌈log2 M ⌉ retained-state
bits. In the protected classical chain example the displayed rational probabilities already give an
exact k-label controller, and the probability-valued Hankel rank proves that this label count is
minimal. Neither proof gives a matching universal program or working-storage lower bound.
   The classical chain and the quantum calibration problem score replication of a terminal probability,
not recovery of the realized hidden state. The observer’s private random chain is independent of the
protected chain; only their terminal output laws agree. This distinction makes clear why no extra
observer-owned quantum memory or hidden-state oracle has entered the construction.

             S58. A nonreset, noncommuting exact instrument realization
  Here is an explicit small realization of the branch-interiority theorem. Let the core vertices be
the six Pauli eigenstates
                                 τj,± = (I ± σj )/2,    j = x, y, z.
Their convex hull is the Bloch octahedron.  For any positive matrix B with trace t and unnormalized
Bloch coordinates bj = tr(σj B), if j |bj | ≤ t, then
                                    P

                       B=         wj,± τj,± ,   wj,± =                      + max{±bj , 0}.       (S58.1)

                                                              6

All coefficients are nonnegative, their sum is t, and their signed coordinate differences are bj , proving
the identity directly. If B = 0 every coefficient is zero. Thus this formula uses no undefined
zero-report posterior.
   Take any qubit instrument and, after each reported branch, physically apply D(B) = B/4 +
3 tr(B)I/8. Its normalized√output has Bloch length at most 1/4; by Cauchy–Schwarz its Bloch
coordinate sum is at most 3/4 < 1. Formula (S58.1) gives exact branch rows with six core states
for any finite command family. A separately specified initial state can be retained as one extra
anchor. This seven-state upper is better for this example than the general dimension-only bound; it
is not asserted minimal.

  The instruments used in the exact diagnostics include amplitude damping with Kraus matrices
                                                                    
                                                           0 4/5
                              A0 = diag(1, 3/5),      A1 =       ,
                                                           0 0
and its conjugate by the Hadamard unitary. Their report probabilities depend on the input state
and may vanish. After the declared depolarization their unconditional channels have distinct fixed
Bloch vectors (0, 0, 16/91) and (16/91, 0, 0), so there is no common stationary density matrix. Their
report branches are not all replacement maps: in particular the invertible branch retains a nonzero
dependence on the input. The main theorem therefore has content beyond a shared fixed point or a
reset model.
   Two additional weak-measurement commands, with Kraus matrices diag(3/5, 4/5) and
diag(4/5, 3/5) and their Hadamard conjugates, have both report effects bounded away from
zero. A two-label observer alternates these commands after report zero and stops at report
one. Its physical block cost and score potentials are computed independently of the seven-state
simulator’s fourteen-state joint killed matrix. Exact rational inversion gives the same initial cost
and squared-audit risk. This is a diagnostic of the all-policy interpolation proof, not its substitute.

               S59. Interiority can first occur at a genuine second call
  Let one command have the single reported channel Φ(B) = (HBH ∗ + SBS ∗ )/2, where H is
Hadamard and S = diag(1, i). The Kraus choice is not a recorded report. On Bloch vectors its
matrix is
                                       0 −1/2 1/2
                                                      

                                A = 1/2 −1/2 0  .
                                      1/2     0    1/2
The largest singular value of A is one, so no positive uniform one-call floor holds. But ∥A2 ∥2F =
5/8 < 49/64, hence every output of Φ2 has Bloch length at most 7/8 and spectral floor at least
I/16. Thus the block theorem applies with L = 2 and η = 1/8. The realization still exposes both
actual calls and allows stopping after the first. Treating the two unrecorded Kraus choices as if they
were available reports would be a different instrument.
   The average-interiority counterexample in the main article goes in the opposite direction: the
random unitary choice is actually recorded, so its branch states remain pure and its record cannot
be averaged away. These two examples fix exactly which information is erased and which remains
in the causal interface.

       S60. Positive coordinates, arbitrary effects and a forbidden inference
   A state-positive simulator has a linear reconstruction map p 7→ i pi τi from its simplex. It does
                                                                    P

not have a positive affine inverse defined on every quantum state. If such an inverse E : Dd → ∆M
reproduced every density matrix exactly, then every pure state would be a convex combination of the
fixed τi . Extremality would force that pure state to be one of the finitely many τi , an impossibility
for d ≥ 2. Our construction instead fixes finitely many initial preparations and uses the strict branch
margin after the first call. It is not an entanglement-preserving channel simulation.
   Theorems S22.2 and S22.4 use positive density coordinates. The lower count for uniform noisy
states therefore cannot be imported into the general optimal-face realization problem without
checking that its generator decoders have this structure. Conversely the fixed-angle spectral
exclusion in Corollary S22.8 requires only a finite stochastic matrix and a probability decoder, so it
does not have that extra restriction. Its asymptotic lower rate is much weaker than the state-positive
noise exponent and is not claimed sharp.
   Fanizza, Lumbreras and Winter’s Theorem 2 [50] bounds the maximal positive-realization
dimension across a varying phase family in a different noisy quantum process. It includes an inverse-
square-root noise dependence in that worst-phase statement. The present fixed-angle rational
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       181


exclusion should not be presented as an improvement to it. The new uniform upper instead supplies
a constructive finite realization from a condition on every actual report branch, and its same-policy
corollary keeps the simulator hidden under control and stopping.

          S61. Leakage boundaries and exact bounded-decoder conventions
   The transient-core lemma in the main article uses a nonzero response tending to zero, a prescribed
initial label and a decoder in [0, 1]. The assumptions ensure that the initial label belongs to the
output-visible core even when its immediate output is zero. The positive path argument tests every
active entry, not only the norm of one arbitrarily chosen physical state. The appended zero-output
label remains live until the productive herald. Removing it by declaring a hidden leakage event
terminal would change the experiment.
Proposition S61.1 (Degenerate leakage parameters). For un = λn [1+γαn cos(nϑ+φ)]/2, interpret
the initial term separately at zero parameters. With 0 < λ < 1, zero contrast gives least live memory
two. At α = 0 the least size is two if γ cos φ = 0, and three otherwise. At λ = 0 it is one when
u0 = 0 and two otherwise. At λ = 1 there is no mandatory leakage label: the inherited unattenuated
theorem applies in its stated nonreal-mode case. Background η = 1 gives the constant response 1/2
and needs one label.
Proof. For zero contrast, the response is λn /2. One live label cannot change its output; a transient
label of retention λ and output 1/2, followed by an absorbing zero-output label, realizes it with two.
   At α = 0, let a = u0 = (1 + γ cos φ)/2. The subsequent terms are λn /2, n ≥ 1. A three-label
construction has a starting label of output a, leading deterministically on its first failure to a
transient label with retention λ and output λ/2, followed by a zero-output label. Its response is
exactly the required one. A two-label realization has, by Lemma S20.1, a one-dimensional active
core, so its entire response must be arn . Matching the positive terms at n = 1, 2 forces r = λ and
a = 1/2. If a = 0, nonzero later output already excludes a one-dimensional core. Thus two are
possible precisely in the stated case.
   At λ = 0 only the initial output can be nonzero. A starting label of that output followed by
a zero-output label suffices; one label is possible precisely when all terms are zero. At λ = 1 the
output no longer has the decay hypothesis used to force a zero-output label. The remaining claims
follow directly from the constant response or the inherited theorem.                                □

  The theorem’s nonreal-mode assumption also matters. If ϑ is a multiple of π, exponential modes
can coincide or cancel. One must minimize the resulting actual response rather than apply a nonreal
spectral count. No claim about those coincidences is hidden in the four-mode Hankel statement.

S61.1. The physical audit is unchanged by the realization argument. For the six-dimensional
squared embedding, the herald probability is p in every model; it is not the leakage probability.
Its first compulsory call and every failed call cost one. Hence the least cost is 1/p and every
boundary-feasible policy accepts the first herald. The flag parameter t changes an unobserved flag
channel, not the herald hazard. The two signs are physical audit-model parameters with the same
fixed loss (r − z)2 , not a choice of loss after observing the answer. The three effects in the inherited
construction have positive mass and sum to the identity. Their computed moments give
                                                        X
                             Et,σ (r − Z)2 = 2 + 2εσp       (qt)n (bn − un ).
                                                        n≥0

Averaging the two signs gives two for every rule. Thus the target response attains two even on the
finite witness family, while exact finite-memory equality requires both opposed inequalities at every
test parameter. The hidden sink is never supplied as an observer report.
182                                             QIAN QI


S61.2. What the finite test certifies. The generating function of an M -label stochastic decoder
has denominator det(I − zQ) and numerator degree at most M − 1. The determinant is nonzero
throughout [0, q t̄] because q t̄ < 1. The target’s denominator is likewise nonzero there. Consequently
the M + r-point theorem is a polynomial identity argument with a known degree bound. It does
not certify an arbitrary fitted function from finitely many samples, nor identify an unrestricted
full-history rule from those finitely many equations.
   For the leaking irrational-phase example, the exact fixed-M optimization can be encoded with
variables Q, d, vj , w and the inequalities
          Q ≥ 0, Q1 = 1,      0 ≤ d ≤ 1,    (I − qtj Q)vj = d,   −w ≤ (vj )1 − U (qtj ) ≤ w.
The minimizing w exists and is strictly positive. Quantifier elimination over this compact graph
computes it as an algebraic number and returns a minimizing controller. The proof supplies this
terminating procedure for every prescribed size; the finite diagnostics do not claim to have run a
global elimination for all sizes. The number of model pairs grows with M , which is essential to the
scope of the finite witness assertion.
Proposition S61.2 (An exact finite-witness one-label value). In Theorem S20.6, take M = 1, the
four parameters t = 0, 1/6, 1/3, 1/2, and both audit signs. The least one-live-label risk is
                                                  331
                                             2+         ,
                                                 13573
attained by the constant probability d = 12249/13573 of decoder +1.
Proof. The generating target is
                           −8z 2 + 28z − 40
               U (z) =                           ,     U (0) = 1, U (1/4) = 2144/1939.
                        5z 3 − 22z 2 + 44z − 40
Every one-label rule is a constant d ∈ [0, 1]. Its uniform resolvent error is at least max{1 − d, 4d/3 −
2144/1939} from the two endpoint tests. Their intersection gives d∗ = 12249/13573 and lower
error 1324/13573. Direct rational substitution at z = 1/12 and z = 1/6 gives errors respectively
−465602268/9412509029 and 6704202/487474295, whose absolute values are strictly below this
lower error. Thus all four tests are bounded by the endpoint value at d∗ . Finally 2εp = 1/4 converts
resolvent error to the stated excess squared risk. This proves optimality over all stochastic one-label
decoders; no local numerical optimizer is used.                                                        □
S61.3. Background, initial mixing and precision. The background effect Eη = ηI/2 + (1 − η)E
is a valid actual effect on the qutrit, with eigenvalues in [η/2, 1 − η/2]. Its positive floor changes
which terminal responses must vanish on the stationary face. It does not add a report, a reset, an
observed physical state or a free random seed.
   The construction stores the T exceptional prefix values. Only its final failure row chooses one
of two disjoint suffix machines, and that choice is subsequently represented by the retained label.
Their average realizes the complete suffix from its first step. This is why the stated count is
T + 2 + m(λα, ϑ), with no additional initialization label and no uncounted intercall mixture memory.
   For algebraic input the background target has an algebraic linear representation of dimension at
most four. A rationally certified polygon or the existing spectral size theorem supplies a finite size
upper. The prescribed-size algebraic equality test then terminates a minimal-size search. Exact
algebraic encodings and their sampling cost are separate from the live-label count. The upper
logarithmic dependence on 1/η is not a matched precision or program-bit lower bound.

                   S62. Arithmetic synthesis and diagnostic boundaries
   The main arithmetic-envelope theorem constructs a stochastic matrix, not merely a rational angle
close to a target. If a/q is the chosen approximation, pass to the explicitly counted multiple l of q.
The target eigenvalue belongs to the triangle with vertices zero and the two adjacent lth roots of
                                 CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       183


unity. If its radial boundary is r, the two chord coefficients give that boundary point; scaling them
by α/r and assigning the remaining weight to J = 11T /l gives a stochastic circulant matrix. Its
row from the prescribed initial vertex produces the complete response from time zero. This explains
why passing to a multiple costs labels but no physical invocation.
  The arithmetic envelope itself also admits finite certified bounds. For any integer B ≥ 1, write
                                   AB = min max{3, q, ex (q)/ϵ}.
                                         1≤q≤B

Then
                                   min{AB , B + 1} ≤ Ax (ϵ) ≤ AB .                              (S62.1)
Indeed every omitted integer denominator is at least B + 1. In particular, once B + 1 ≥ AB ,
enumeration gives the exact envelope if its finitely many arithmetic comparisons are exact. For
a computable irrational phase, rational interval enclosures instead give certified upper and lower
values by replacing each error with its interval bounds. Precision is increased whenever a comparison
or a polygon margin remains unresolved. A supplied strictly feasible polygon upper is a finite exact
construction; this does not assert decidability of arbitrary equality cases from finite-precision phase
samples.
   The two noise sequences used in the main proof have different functions. For a convergent
denominator Q with error ex (Q), noise ex (Q)/Q is a low-memory scale. For the next denominator
Q′ , noise 1/[Q(Q′ )2 ] is a crossover scale at which every candidate denominator below Q′ /2 has
excessive error. Both statements use all candidate rationals in their lower arguments. Restricting a
numerical search to convergents alone is not the proof of the universal envelope or of the crossover
exclusion.

S62.1. Finite computations supplied with this revision. The R71 diagnostic program sep-
arately records exact rational checks and floating matrix probes. The exact part tests finite
continued-fraction identities, all denominators below the tested convergent bound, the rational
separation used at crossover scales, complete finite envelope enumeration and its certified stopping
condition. Rational finite tails are deliberately identified as rational test data. Their finite check
results are not used to infer an irrationality exponent of a limiting number.
   The floating part independently constructs the full circulant transition matrix and compares
its forward probabilities with raw qubit iteration and with the target scalar formula. It checks
bounded decoders, row normalization, nonnegative mixture weights, spectral eigenvectors, the
envelope constants and the power-sector obstruction on finite examples. A separate sequence of
large partial quotients probes the two distinct noise scales in the fluctuation proof; it is not labeled
a numerical proof of the limiting exponents. Exact special cases and deliberately invalid shortcuts
provide negative controls.

S62.2. Resources and unresolved sharp constants. The upper construction may use more
states than a minimal stochastic realization by the universal factor in Theorem S18.1. Its main
purpose is a uniform matching law and exact realization, not a claim that the polygon is optimal
at every phase. At algebraic eigenvalues, the inherited finite spectral-membership test can search
the actual minimal size below a supplied upper. For general computable phases, finite interval
arithmetic can certify strict feasible constructions without deciding all exact spectral boundary
equalities.
   The growth exponents concern log M/ log(1/ϵ) for the least live-label count. In a finite mixture,
taking a maximum before a lower limit is essential; no simultaneous Diophantine property is supplied
by separate one-dimensional irrationality exponents. Classical rational approximation, stochastic
spectral geometry, positive realization and the physical squared embedding retain their distinct
roles in this proof chain. No formal proof-assistant check or independent human priority assessment
is claimed by the finite diagnostics or the native build.
184                                              QIAN QI


            S63. Verification coordinates for common reversible responses
   In the compact-group argument the map from hidden rows to response functions is defined only on
the reachable row span R. A whole realizing simplex need not lie in that span. Thus the admissible
polytope is the image of R ∩ ∆M , not an unspecified positive cone on the minimal physical support.
The support-count estimate uses the fact that two distinct extreme points of the section cannot
have exactly the same positive coordinates. It is only a size certificate for a supplied realization, not
a constructive upper on minimal stochastic memory.
   The quantum specialization removes the common kernel of all commanded terminal tests. Each
unitary conjugation restricts to an isometry on the finite-dimensional reachable Hermitian span.
Forward invariance and finite dimension imply backward invariance, both for that span and the
invisible kernel. The quotient therefore has a common invariant inner product. No controllability or
informational completeness of the terminal measurement is assumed; a constant effect has a trivial
observable group even if the full physical group is infinite.
   For the two reflections the physical chronological convention is Φan · · · Φa1 ρ, while the observer row
is eT
    1 Qa1 · · · Qan . The intertwining equations use row vertices and the transpose of the physical Bloch
map. This is why the word (01)n corresponds to powers of Q0 Q1 and to the rotation produced by the
physical map S1 S0 . The explicit diagnostic uses both equations and a separate raw density-matrix
evolution. Reversing only one convention would break the mixed-word identity.

             S64. Partition synthesis, anchors and physical interpretation
   The initialization row in the periodic construction chooses its recurrent block only after the first
actual transition. At time zero it uses a separately assigned decoder b0 . Entering phase one, rather
than phase zero, ensures the first observed response is correct. The lower’s extra label is not an
arbitrary implementation preference: two reachable closed recurrent classes cannot contain the
same prescribed initial state. A model permitting an initially mixed state is a different resource
convention and can remove that label.
   In the decay-normalized lower, the full nonnegative core need not be irreducible, row stochastic
or similar by one strictly positive global Perron vector to a stochastic matrix. Its powers are
bounded because every active entry is bounded by a fixed shifted target-output path. Perron scaling
is performed only on a critical irreducible diagonal block. The no-critical-chain lemma forbids
recovering an initialization label by sending a critical class into another required critical class. The
proof counts nonnegative paths, so cancellation cannot conceal the resulting quadratic cumulative
growth.
   The periodic response has a prepared block-qubit realization and a fixed terminal effect. The
established flag embedding realizes the associated exact-response requirement using one fixed squared
loss and three actual audit outcomes at the original first-herald budget. Internal damping, physical
leakage and an observed terminal event are different operations. The new finite-memory laws
concern exact replication of the terminal probability, not predicting the realized hidden bit or the
distinct squared-sign decision task. They do not give the protected system to the observer.

                  S65. Independent diagnostics for the present revision
   The new program compares subset dynamic programming with an independent enumeration of
all set partitions on finite input lists. Rational and algebraic examples check row normalization,
bounded decoders, the initialization phase, exact damped and leaked coefficients and random-horizon
resolvents. A positive substochastic two-block example shows exactly how linking critical classes
yields linearly unbounded rescaled powers. These checks do not replace the class-decomposition
lower proofs.
   For the controlled theorem, a shared three-vertex algebraic construction verifies both reflection
matrices and their intertwining identities exactly. Every tested mixed word is independently evolved
                                  CURRENT TECHNICAL SUPPLEMENT                                       185


through the raw qubit channels. Additional numeric polygon constructions check longer binary
words, both physical maps, their pairwise product and phased periodic mixtures. They establish
finite implementation consistency only. A finite angular or continued-fraction sample is never used
to infer an irrationality exponent or uniform asymptotic class.


                                     S66. Verification status
   The accompanying scripts compare finite algebraic examples, solve common finite-prefix control
problems and small finite reset-control instances independently of the manuscript’s closed formulas,
check report-resolved span counterexamples, test boundary resource values, and compile the complete
native manuscript, this supplement and the retained companion. Exact rational checks and floating-
point residual checks are separately counted. Neither kind proves an unbounded theorem by sampling.
Native LaTeX compilation verifies source completeness and references, not mathematical correctness.
No formal proof assistant, newly commissioned independent human referee or editorial acceptance
is claimed.


         S67. Response degeneracies and the stochastic spectral certificate
  The exact spectral criterion in the main article deliberately assumes a nonreal mode and nonzero
contrast. The following closes its real and zero-mode boundaries, without applying a three-mode
rank argument where the modes coalesce.

Proposition S67.1 (Complete scalar boundary cases). Let bn = 12 (1 + aλn ), with |a| ≤ 1,
−1 ≤ λ ≤ 1, and λ0 = 1. Its least stochastic live memory with a prescribed initial label is one if
a = 0 or λ = 1, and otherwise two. In particular this covers the original sinusoidal family at phases
0 and π with a = γ cos φ, and at α = 0 with λ = 0.

Proof. In the constant cases a single label with the constant output probability suffices. In every
other case b0 ̸= b1 , whereas a one-label stochastic machine has a constant output probability. For
an upper construction use
                                  1 1+λ 1−λ                    1 1+a
                                                                     
                            Q=              ,             d=         .
                                  2 1−λ 1+λ                    2 1−a

The constant vector is fixed, and (1, −1)T is an eigenvector at λ. Starting in the first label gives the
required response. This includes negative real eigenvalues and both unit-circle endpoints. At α = 0
the formula gives its one exceptional initial output followed by a constant half output.              □

S67.1. Polynomial verification without an initial randomization. For z = x + iy, y ̸= 0, the
real variables of (S19.3) encode a complex eigenvector v = a + ic. The equations Qa = xa − yc,
Qc = ya + xc imply Qv = zv, while v1 = 1 prevents a zero witness. The inequalities a2j + c2j ≤ 1
ensure legal decoder probabilities for every 0 < γ ≤ 1 and every fixed phase. Conversely a maximal-
modulus eigenvector coordinate can always be normalized this way. Thus the certificate does not
assume a special circulant form, a full-support stationary distribution, a reversible chain or a hidden
preparation mixture.
   With algebraic x, y, γ, cos φ, sin φ, every nonempty fixed-size certificate has an algebraic sample
by real-closed-field elimination. A dense transition table has M 2 entries and a binary decoder has M
probabilities, in addition to the retained ⌈log2 M ⌉ state bits. Algebraic-number representations and
exact sampling have their own costs. The cubic-root theorem does not silently grant a polynomial
bit implementation of an arbitrary real Diophantine rotation.
186                                             QIAN QI


S67.2. Constructing a directional circulant. Let q ≥ 3 and place ϑ between adjacent root
angles 2πj/q and 2π(j + 1)/q, choosing a representative on the circle. Write β = 2π/q and
ψ = ϑ − 2πj/q ∈ [0, β]. If α ≤ Rq (ϑ), set
                              sin(β − ψ)             sin ψ
                        a=α              ,     b=α         ,     c = 1 − a − b.
                                 sin β               sin β
All three are nonnegative. The matrix Q = aCqj + bCqj+1 + cJ is stochastic and has eigenvector
vl = e2πil/q at z = αeiϑ . Its decoder is dl = (1 + γ cos(φ + 2πl/q))/2. This is a directly specified
program at a fixed phase, not an abstract existence proof requiring the physical state to be observed.
A root-angle endpoint is permitted and may reduce the actual minimal size. The construction is
sufficient; it is not asserted to describe every part of the Karpelevič boundary.
   The cyclic eigenvector gives the all-time certificate Qv = zv. Checking a finite list of output
probabilities without that identity would not certify exact realization. The new diagnostic program
tests both the intertwining identity and independent raw qubit evolution, and labels the latter tests
as finite probes.

S67.3. Constants and quantifiers in the arithmetic theorem. For h ≤ π/3 and 0 ≤ δ ≤ h,
the inequalities used in the lower proof are
                3                   1
         sin δ ≥ δ,      1 − cos δ ≤ δ 2 ,     tan h ≥ h,      cos δ + sin δ tan h ≤ sec h ≤ 2.
                4                   2
                                                                               √
The first follows, for example, from concavity of sine and sin(π/3)/(π/3) = 3 3/(2π) > 3/4. Thus
there is no numerical fitting in the constant 16 of the cubic-root lower. The exact first-sector law
proves the worst-phase lower, not a relaxation to a larger convex hull of all low-order roots. In
contrast, the fixed-phase upper uses a polygon whose size is a Diophantine denominator; the two
constructions have different quantifiers. √
   For the golden quadratic phase x = (3− 5)/2, the integer-polynomial proof in the main text gives
a global constant c = 1/3, valid at every positive denominator. Inspecting a finite continued-fraction
prefix would not give that global conclusion. For (3 + 4i)/5 the Gaussian-integer argument gives
the displayed logarithmic lower but not a badly-approximable certificate. These facts prevent
transferring the cubic-root exponent to a different phase by numerical resemblance.
   The exact-memory count is discontinuous at vanishing contrast: all nonzero contrasts have the
same spectral obstruction, but zero contrast is constant. This statement concerns equality of every
response coefficient. Approximate risk at a fixed tolerance can of course become insensitive to small
contrast; that is a different optimization problem and is not contradicted by the exact criterion.

S67.4. Relation to classical results and to other resource curves. The stochastic sector
inequality, Karpelevič regions and first-sector lazy-cycle classification are classical; their pertinent
statements are credited in the main text and proved there in the form used. The noise-dependent
classical-memory bounds in Fanizza–Lumbreras–Winter concern processes and quantifiers distinct
from the current one-response squared task. The present argument neither claims priority for the
unit-circle obstruction nor uses it as a substitute for the matched arithmetic rate.
   The protected system in the squared embedding has dimension four, independent of the observer
labels. The instrument-level state-positive simulator of Section S22 is a separate construction: it
preserves every declared terminal effect and adaptive experiment, whereas the current stochastic
response realization preserves only the fixed optimal task kernel. The old two-label squared-sign
frontier and delayed-release timing frontier likewise have different objective functions. Their proofs
remain current and no general-M risk formula for either is inferred from the new exact-response
law.
